Parker tests three readings—expansive gameplay as metaphor for self-fashioning, as a genuine practice of freedom when players problematize Halo's militarist hero worship (the Pacifist Run), and finally as a simulation of rule-based systems letting players rehearse self-fashioning at a safe scale, with the gloomy alternative that it merely replaces real practices of freedom.
- ch. 27 "Would Cortana Pass the Turing Test" **·** Alan Turing, John Searle — The Turing Test; The Chinese Room — Sherol Chen asks whether Cortana would pass the Turing Test and answers that games need believability of behavior, not constitution of mind. Turing's imitation game sets the target; Searle's Chinese Room shows that performance without understanding suffices for the player's experience of Covenant AI and Cortana alike, while French's seagull test and associative-priming objection show the Turing Test really measures human-style intelligence—limits Halo happily ignores. Surveying game-storytelling workarounds (amnesia openings, Planescape's immortal death, Bioshock's 'Would you kindly'), she argues designers convert paidia's limits into narrative features, and Cortana would pass here and now—though in her own era newer tests would expose new limits.
- ch. 28 "UNSC Debriefing" *(no index entry yet)* — Roger Ngim's closing piece traces how First Person Shooters changed gamespace: Halloween's killer-POV shots first placed him inside a filmic world, and Doom added the startling awareness of a 'behind me'—space that exists even when unrendered on screen, populated by rules and code rather than photographed sets. He punctures 'immersion' as a marketing term, contrasting the honest low-res reductionism of Atari-era games with film and games converging on the holodeck dream and 3D cinema. The upshot is phenomenological: FPS worlds are places to be rather than things to behold—and something may still be sneaking up behind you.
- ch. 29 "UNSC Personnel" *(notes)*
- ch. 30 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 31 "Halo and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Hannibal Lecter and Philosophy - The Heart of the Matter

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(no index entry yet)* — This opening page is the book's front matter of advance-praise blurbs (Mikita Brottman, Sara Waller, Cynthia Freeland, Harold Schechter and others), not an essay. The blurbs preview the volume's themes - the morality of eating people, Lecter's psychiatry and empathy, aesthetics and haute cuisine, friendship, evil, and why we are drawn to a cultured cannibal across Harris's novels, the five films, and the television series - while dropping the names of Plato, Augustine, Nietzsche, and Foucault without developing any of them. No philosophical argument is made here, so no slugs apply.
- ch. 3 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "vi" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Le Menu" *(no index entry yet)* — Le Menu is the book's own table of contents, styled as a multi-course dinner menu running from Apéritif (Westfall's 'Hello, Dr. Lecter') through Café ('Doctor, Heal Thyself'), with the six parts titled by Lecter quotations such as 'Having an Old Friend for Dinner' and 'I Gave You a Rare Gift, but You Didn't Want It'. It lists the eighteen essays and their authors plus the back-matter resources and index, and argues nothing itself.
- ch. 8 "Hello, Dr. Lecter" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Socrates — The Übermensch; The Unexamined Life — Westfall's introduction sorts Lecter's incarnations into four presentations: the psychopath (Brian Cox's 'Lecktor' in Manhunter, modeled on Harris's real-life Mexican prison doctor 'Dr. Salazar'), the anti-hero avenging Mischa in Hannibal Rising, the vampire (Hopkins's Silence of the Lambs Lecter as a Dracula figure who feeds from a sense of innate superiority), and the devil (Mikkelsen's fallen angel, Romula's 'Shaitan', wielder of the church-collapse argument that God enjoys killing). What unites them is a Nietzschean self-conception - Lecter as an Übermensch to whom ordinary law and morality do not apply - and a Socratic function: like Socrates in the Phaedrus, he goads Will, Clarice, and the audience into examining what we already are, posing questions he never resolves because each of us must take the 'rare gift' for ourselves.
- ch. 9 "I. Having an Old Friend for Dinner" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "1. Cosmopolitan Cannibal" **·** Anthropocentrism; Food Ethics — Staged as a fictional interview over herring and aquavit in Copenhagen, Wong lets Lecter wield philosophical critique like an 'autopsy saw' - he invokes Schopenhauer's condemnation of anthropophagy and Montaigne's noble-savage view only to have the interviewer refuse both, and 'creams Aristotle' in a debate about categories - before she adopts Agamben's distinction between sovereigns and bare life. Lecter's 'we are elaborations of carbon' de-anthropocentrism, his killing of the rude, and the parallel cases of shipwrecked sailors who ate shipmates, Guantánamo detainees, and drone casualties all serve her argument that every political order rests on someone arbitrarily deciding which lives are mere killable matter, and that Lecter horrifies because he shows every human is also nonhuman - Us and Them at once.
- ch. 11 "2. I, Cannibal" **·** Michel de Montaigne — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Food Ethics — Westfall sorts cannibalism into starvation types (the Donner Party, the Andes rugby team, Alferd Packer, and Grutas's crew killing Mischa - 'We have to eat or die'), cultural types (Aztec ritual eating, the prisoner-eating tribes of Montaigne's essay, whom Montaigne judged no worse than the French torturing their prisoners alive, and Neanderthal nutritional eating), and hedonistic types (paraphilic, sadistic, and recreational). Using Irvine's active/passive distinction he argues that it is the killing, not the eating, that makes cannibalism wrong - passive consumption of corpses is at worst distasteful - while Lecter emerges as a purely recreational cannibal who eats 'because he likes it', and the TV series' dinner parties turn Jack and Will from unwitting 'benign cannibals' into morally responsible passive ones.
- ch. 12 "3. What’s So Bad about Eating People" **·** Immanuel Kant — Virtue Ethics; Consequentialism; Humanity as an End in Itself; Natural Law — McCraw imagines Lecter defending each class of victim with a different ethical theory: the Momund and Dortlich kills through Natural Law Theory plus lex talionis retribution (Dortlich ate Mischa, so he is eaten; Momund treated the aunt as meat, so he is treated as meat), the 'Sorbet' Rolodex dinner-party victims through a perverted Kantianism that swaps politeness for rationality so the rude forfeit humanity and corpses have no moral standing, and Krendler, Raspail, and Chilton through consequentialist accounting of worlds improved by their deaths. He concludes that only virtue ethics resists such distortion: Lecter's cold, callous, manipulative character and his isolated, paranoid, friendless life show that no possessor of good character could eat people, so cannibalism really is bad even when the murders are bracketed.
- ch. 13 "II. What Does He Do, This Man You Seek" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "4. Acts of God" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The Problem of Evil; Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; The Best of All Possible Worlds — McCrossin reads Lecter's parable of the church collapses - 'It must feel good to God - He does it all the time, and are we not made in His image?' - as Lecter's solution to the problem of evil in both its theological (Job) and secular (Neiman) forms: a malevolent deity of the kind de Sade's Saint-Fond describes, doing God's work becomes Lecter's justification, and killing feels good because 'if one does what God does enough times, one will become as God is'. Following Neiman, he sets Rousseau's tradition (evil must be made intelligible - 'All is well leaving the hands of the author of things') against Voltaire's Candide refusal of theodicy, showing that Will and Starling live in Rousseau's camp except when facing Lecter himself, and that the Hannibal/Hannibal Rising revelation that Mischa's murder 'happened' to him radically revises his earlier claim that 'we don't invent our natures', with Starling's reason finally making 'a place for Mischa' in Lecter and Starling together.
- ch. 15 "5. Office Hours Are for Patients" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Autonomy of the Will; Deontology — Alloy argues that the psychiatric code of ethics - confidentiality with Franklyn ('It would be unethical to approach a patient'), the formal boundaries Lecter scrupulously observes once Will becomes an official patient, and even the oath-violating concealment of Will's encephalitis with Dr. Sutcliffe - is the closest Hannibal can come to friendship, because professional rules artificially manufacture the trust that friends enjoy organically. Working through informed consent, placebo deception, paternalism, and psychiatry's extraordinary power to declare patients incompetent to exercise autonomy at all, he shows the Will-Hannibal relationship cycling through four stages between professional ethics and friendship - 'Office hours are for patients. My kitchen is always open to friends' - while never achieving either, since every display of trust is a manipulation and the power asymmetry never disappears.
- ch. 16 "6. The Psychiatrist as Sociopathic God" **·** Aristotle, Augustine of Hippo, Friedrich Nietzsche — The Problem of Evil; Potentiality and Actuality; Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — Hassert takes Lecter's 'First principles, Clarice' at its Aristotelian word - nature is revealed through capacities and action, as an acorn's telos is not pumpkins and a kitten will never be a Great Dane - to explain Lecter's psychiatry: he reads each patient's essence and potential, prescribing Mason Verger's face-peeling so appearance finally matches essence, and force-raising Will toward equality so that Aristotelian perfect friendship between the alike-in-virtue (which Franklyn's flattery can never reach) might become possible. Reading God through Augustine's account of evil as the destruction of a preexisting good and Mischa's murder, the chapter argues that Lecter emulates a capricious deity - 'Typhoid and swans - it all comes from the same place' - and, unlike Nietzsche, does not pronounce God dead but merely misconceptualized, while his friendships fail because he must corrupt the other into his own image rather than love an equal.
- ch. 17 "III. They Say He’s a Psychopath" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "7. Psychopaths, Outlaws, and Us" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Autonomy of the Will — McClelland argues Lecter is neither psychopath nor sociopath, marshaling his empathic childhood (softening a bread crust for Mischa, polishing an eggplant because she loved purple, sensing Lady Murasaki's fatigue), his genuinely compassionate psychiatric work with Margot Verger, and his chess-master planning as evidence that both cognitive and emotional empathy operate normally - unlike Grutas, 'here's your real psychopath'. Reading Lecter's preternatural calm, mutism, and suppressed memories as PTSD and resilience after Mischa's death, and his fundamental project as the pursuit of autonomy (with Hirschman's exit-voice-loyalty framework explaining his contempt for declining institutions like the FBI), he reclassifies Lecter as an outlaw - one placed beyond the law's protection yet living inside civil society - who kills instrumentally the way evolutionary psychology says humans do, concluding that 'there is an outlaw in all of us'.
- ch. 19 "8. Consuming Homicidal Art" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Humanity as an End in Itself; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — McAteer uses Bullough's aesthetic distance to test whether the psychopath Hannibal can have genuine aesthetic experience, arguing against Maibom and Harold that egocentric pleasure in an artwork is compatible with aesthetic distancing - so a Lecter who loves Bach and Italian architecture (unlike Cleckley's unmoved psychopath and the Tooth Fairy's graceless 'performances') could really exist. But since psychopaths can value nothing as an end in itself - they treat persons and artworks merely as means - homicidal art like the Muralist's corpse cathedral and Hannibal's gourmet cannibalism is the logical extreme of Wildean aestheticism, proving Wittgenstein's 'Aesthetics and ethics are one' false; yet in homicidal art Hannibal's moral deficit becomes an aesthetic deficit too, because anyone who can see beauty in human bodies as mere material is over-distanced - the bodies, like any sculpture's material, matter.
- ch. 20 "9. Not Knowing Serial Killers with Hannibal Lecktor" **·** Power/Knowledge; Epistemic Injustice — Davis reads Manhunter's Lecktor through agnotology - Robert Proctor's study of how ignorance is made - arguing that Lecktor runs a 'knowing game' with Will Graham, strategically withholding what he knows (the Pilgrim's lunar cycle, his fan mail from the Tooth Fairy) so that Graham must do his own dangerous 'field work' and risk becoming killer or bait, a playbook he delivers as an invented Rumsfeldian riff on known knowns and unknown unknowns. The same lens then exposes the real world: the FBI's post-Silence serial-killer panic (Jenkins's evidence that the BSU's statistics were false, capped by the USS Iowa profiling fiasco) as institutional ignorance-making that inflated the FBI's power, and Egger's 'less-dead' - marginalized victims like Kendall Francois's Poughkeepsie sex workers whom media footprints render invisible, 'the missing missing' - as the human cost of our fascination with a killer who never existed.
- ch. 21 "IV. I Gave You a Rare Gift, but You Didn’t Want It" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "10. The Light from Friendship" **·** Aristotle, Friedrich Nietzsche — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); The Übermensch; The Death of God — Pavelich sets Aristotle's account of friendship - recognized and reciprocated goodwill between equals, perfected in the friendship of the good who 'share our souls', a standard met only by Jack and Bella Crawford - against Nietzsche's friendship as striving between overmen, who honor 'one's best enemy' in the friend and grow through conflict. He reads Hannibal's treatment of Will (hiding the encephalitis, framing him, serving the Nietzschean trout) as Nietzschean friend-making - Lies that are supposed to spur Will's growth, consistent with an Übermensch who tells Will 'Killing must feel good to God' and says 'I don't hide from God' - while Will's masquerade of friendship is betrayal in both senses, since Will seeks an Aristotelian soulmate and finds a killer he must stop. Because each betrays the other, neither Aristotelian nor Nietzschean friendship survives, though the moments before the Mizumono bloodbath - Hannibal inviting Will to just walk away - glimpse a real friendship already made impossible.
- ch. 23 "11. A Rare Gift" **·** Michel Foucault — Power/Knowledge — Breikss reads the Hannibal-Will bond as Sedgwick-style homosociality filtered through gift theory: Mauss and Bataille explain how Hannibal's lavish dinner parties and mentorship function as gifts that assert dominance, overwhelming Will until his inner voice becomes Hannibal's. Girard's mimetic desire then maps the rivalry's escalation — Alana as the shared object, Will's proxy murder attempt via Matthew Brown, the killing of Randall Tier that merges the two men into the 'Willdigo', and Mason Verger as the scapegoat whose death would have purged the conflict. Foucault's reading of Bosch's 'Cutting the Stone' casts Will as the patient and Hannibal as the doctor 'far more insane than the patient', with Jack and Alana as unwitting accomplices in the psychiatric stripping of Will's life, while Bataille's Erotism frames the cannibal feasts as transgression that heightens rather than abolishes the taboo.
- ch. 24 "12. A Little Empathy for Hannibal Is a Dangerous Thing" *(no index entry yet)* — Jones argues that Hannibal turns the therapy-room virtue of empathy into a horror: Will's profiling works by inhabiting a killer's frame of reference in Carl Rogers' sense, but empathic contact with Hannibal is framed so seductively — the fungi victim of 'Amuse-Bouche', the 'Sorbet' dinner party where the camera makes the viewer share his smirk — that Will, and before him Dr. Du Maurier, cannot pull their own moral perspective back out. The chapter ends by staging a fantasy Rogerian session in which a fearless therapist uncovers Hannibal's contempt for the weak as self-hatred for the powerless boy of Hannibal Rising, yet concludes that until such therapy is really possible, Rifkin's 'global peak empathy' world is one best walked away from.
- ch. 25 "V. It’s Beautiful in Its Own Way, Giving Voice to the Unmentionable" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "13. An Aesthete par Excellence" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Holt argues that the key to Lecter is aestheticism: the Goldberg Variations scoring his nightstick killing of the Memphis guard is no camouflage, because art, cannibalism, murder and etiquette are all unified by aesthetic value prized apart from the good and the true. His 'ACME question' applies inference to the best explanation to show that a single hypothesis — the aesthetic — explains Art, Cannibalism, Murder and Etiquette together, with 'Discourtesy is unspeakably ugly to me' read as an aesthetic rather than a moral complaint. The final twist is that Lecter's psychopathic 'moral blindness' may enhance his powers of aesthetic discrimination the way literal blindness enhances hearing and touch.
- ch. 27 "14. A Funny Thing Happened on the Way to the Dinner Party" *(no index entry yet)* — Westfall sorts Lecter's humor into mockery ('I hope you're not too ugly'), expressions of power ('Are you setting a new standard of care?', delivered while hanging from a noose), and inside jokes ('I'm having an old friend for dinner', 'I wouldn't do that to the food, Tobias'), arguing against Noël Carroll that something can be frightening and funny at once because the joke is itself an exercise of power. He shows how the television series converts Harris's cruel mockery into displays of control, and how in-jokes addressed to the audience — Mikkelsen's census-taker callback to Hopkins's fava-beans line — pull viewers into complicity, a subtle fourth-wall break that is itself an expression of power.
- ch. 28 "15. The Art of Killing" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Aristotle — Zanin takes FBI profiler John Douglas's maxim 'if you want to understand the artist, you have to look at the painting' and argues, with Croce's expression theory, Novitz's everyday aesthetics and Hegel's account of art and freedom, that Lecter's murders genuinely are artworks: Paul the butcher's head arranged in a suiban vessel with a 'Momund, Boucherie de Qualite' tag, Dortlich's head on the tree stump that recalls the charcoal stump in Lecter's cell drawing, and Rinaldo Pazzi's disemboweling staged like a Deposition painting over Florence. Against Hegel's restriction of art to pleasing beauty, the chapter claims revulsion is as much an aesthetic response as glorification, so that murder can be Aristotle's techne — the skill by which Lecter lives.
- ch. 29 "VI. The Beauty and Art and Horror of Everything This World Has to Offer" *(front)*
- ch. 30 "16. Empathy for the Devil" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Will to Power; The Übermensch — Shaw explains our identification with Lecter through mirror neurons triggered by Demme's extreme close-ups, Lecter's impeccable aesthetic and culinary taste, his evil-genius plotting, and his choice of 'free range rude' victims — but the deepest ground is Nietzsche's will to power, quoting The Gay Science and The Antichrist on the feeling of power as what makes action valuable. Lecter is read as Nietzsche's Overman in complete control of himself, his environment, and his inferiors, and the TV series confirms the theory because, with the black humor and romance stripped away, Mikkelsen's Lecter out-plots Will, Jack and Alana alike and walks triumphant from the Season 2 finale, having played everyone 'like his beloved Theremin'.
- ch. 31 "17. The Beguiling Horror of Hannibal Lecter" *(no index entry yet)* — Devlin and Biderman apply Noël Carroll's account of the monster and the paradox of horror to Lecter: as with Dracula, Freddy and Jason, audiences are horrified by and yet attracted to him, paying the price of fear and disgust for the pleasure of learning about the creature. But Lecter breaks Carroll's definition because he is a scientifically possible human being — his menace comes from intelligence, charm, persuasion and heightened senses rather than supernatural impossibility — so the authors widen the definition with Cynthia Freeland's realist monster, a creature embodying moral and social perversion. His rich mythos, from Mischa's death in Hannibal Rising to his tenderness toward Starling, makes him a tragic human monster whose back-story deepens both repulsion and attraction.
- ch. 32 "18. Doctor, Heal Thyself" **·** Aristotle — The Unconscious; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — McClelland argues Lecter is a flawed healer: reading Starling's FBI disgrace as social exclusion, he shows how Lecter's hypnotic drugs and deep-hypnosis 'treatment' let her revisit and metabolize her repressed rage at her dead father — the parental imago he named back in Silence of the Lambs — producing a chrysalis-like metamorphosis completed when she defies Krendler at the dinner and asks for more of his brains. The healing is symmetric, since Starling's insight into Lecter's own Mischa fixation releases him too, and the chapter closes with an Aristotelian verdict — choice reveals character, and whether the couple's Buenos Aires partnership counts as good depends on long-term flourishing we have not yet seen.
- ch. 33 "241" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "Other Resources" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "The Psychopaths" *(notes)*
- ch. 36 "Index" *(notes)*

## Harley-Davidson and Philosophy - Full-Throttle Aristotle

- ch. 1 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Foreword by R.K. Stratman, a self-made motorcycle-race T-shirt vendor whose family business grew from iron-on patches at the races to a company of over two hundred employees. He recounts his first ride in a high-school parking lot, his collection of first-and-last engine models including the Buffalo Bike, and names the allure of the ride: freedom on the open road, escape from conformity, and the instant brotherhood of riders whose image has become a pop-culture stereotype. His only contribution to the philosophy of Harley-Davidson is his company's twenty-year slogan: 'If I have to explain, you wouldn't understand.'
- ch. 9 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Editor Bernard Rollin frames the book with his Yellowstone anecdote: dreading the 'Dreaded Question' about his profession, he confesses to an outlaw biker that he is a philosophy professor and is embraced with 'You have a steady job so you can keep the bike running!' He draws two morals: Harley riders share an instant camaraderie (the trauma surgeon who cursed him for costing him a ride) plus a quiet elitism of being privy to something most people never experience, and bikers treat everything, even careers, as ancillary to time on the motorcycle. The book's essays aim to explain this je ne sais quoi of the Harley way of life.
- ch. 11 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Dogen, The Buddha — Zen Buddhism; Non-Self (anatman); Compassion (karuna); Wu Wei (effortless action) — Priest argues that Pirsig's Zen and the Art of Motorcycle Maintenance is far more genuinely Zen than its disclaimer admits: once riding becomes effortless, the dissolution of the subject-object distinction that Dogen calls genjokoan arrives on the open road ('you ARE the driving and the road and the elements'), and the Harley's relaxed posture and low-revving thug-thug-thug rhythm make it a better vehicle for suchness than cars or whinier bikes. Even maintenance done as an end in itself is genjokoan, and because ultimate reality erases every conceptual distinction, care of self, others, and the bike amount to the same thing, the Mahayana ground of compassion; the mountain-and-water Zen saying resolves the paradox that the unconceptualized and the conceptualized are one. Suffering, on the Buddhist diagnosis Priest applies, comes from imposing conceptual grids that see the world wrong, and seeing it aright dissolves it.
- ch. 14 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger, Edmund Husserl — Authenticity; The They-Self (das Man); Existence Precedes Essence — Auxier uses the question 'Would Bruce Springsteen ride a Honda?' as an ontological probe: Honda embodies defensible middle-class values while a Harley is not primarily a machine but 'a decision about life and what makes it valuable', a suicide machine Jesus could have ridden into Jerusalem and whose absence would render Easy Rider meaningless even if Fonda rode some other Harley. His own boyhood purchase of a Honda XL 70 over the Harley Z90 becomes a lesson in Heideggerian honesty, since he knew he belonged on a Honda, while buyers who choose for appearances are consumed by das Man, the they-self that lives inauthentically through images and slogans. Using Husserl's method of imaginative variation on the question, he concludes that the Harley as cultural icon represents an existential decision and the life that follows from it.
- ch. 15 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Epicurus, Diogenes the Cynic, Pyrrho, Seneca — Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Pyrrhonian Skepticism (suspension of judgment); Cynicism (living according to nature); Stoicism — Over an evening at the Iron Post biker bar, Auxier maps four habitues onto the Hellenistic schools: Cowboy, the Vietnam chopper pilot whose bar is a Garden of modest pleasures, loyalty, and withdrawal from power games, is an Epicurean; the laconic Bear, who doubts that even possessing knowledge would make us better off and suspends judgment, is a Pyrrhonian (unlike the coffee-house Carneadean); Happy Jack, the 'true nature's child' of 'Born to Be Wild' who defies convention and keeps nothing six months, is Diogenes's Cynic; and Gary, the Vietnam-damaged recovering alcoholic, is a damaged Stoic who has not reached apatheia. He concludes that bikers are postmodern pagans whose philosophy is enacted rather than propounded, a Biker Platonism that refuses the Christian-Platonic divorce of body and spirit and lives by honor, freedom, and a pagan fatalism that accepts the consequences of one's choices.
- ch. 16 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Karl Marx — The Dialectic; Commodity Fetishism; Struggle for Recognition; Class Struggle — Goldstein applies Hegel's thesis-antithesis-synthesis and Marx's more complex, multidimensional dialectic to the splits within biker culture: bar-room stare-downs as struggles for recognition, wrenchers versus trailers, RUBs versus working-class riders, helmet disputes, and one-percenters versus the rest. He traces the collapse of brotherhood (the clenched fist of solidarity giving way to the selective wave) to the commodification of the motorcycle during the Japanese invasion and to Harley's own yuppie-era Willie G. marketing, arguing via commodity fetishism that bikers misread economic forces between manufacturers as personal differences. His conclusion is that the seeds of unity lie within the divisions, and that Harley-Davidson Motor Co. will not save the culture, since its task is profit, not tradition.
- ch. 17 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** John Dewey, Martin Heidegger, Gilles Deleuze — Experience and Education; Being-Toward-Death; Authenticity — Responding to the Guggenheim's record-breaking 'The Art of the Motorcycle' show, Rollin argues that Dickie's institutional theory explains how motorcycles became art but not why people lined up, and offers three aesthetic lenses: Marinetti's Futurist celebration of machine speed, danger, and rebellion; Dewey's 'an experience', which every ride exemplifies, from his two-week trips with his son to the blown engine rescued by a ranch truck and the canned-spaghetti feast; and the Bauhaus doctrine that form follows function, making customized Harleys industrial sculpture that appreciates in value. He closes with Heidegger: riding's regular face-to-face with 'the possibility of the impossibility of one's being' is a call to authenticity and the final warrant for calling the motorcycle a fountain of aesthetic experience. American Indians revering Harleys as 'iron horses' illustrate the freedom and speed at the core of the bike's aesthetic pull.
- ch. 19 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Albert Camus, Sigmund Freud — Rebellion and Revolt; The Absurd; Civilization and Its Discontents — Pratt reads the outlaw biker's FTW (Fuck the World) posture as pop-culture nihilism: Nietzsche's 'danger of dangers' and Camus's answer of scorn to an absurd universe ('there is no fate that cannot be surmounted by scorn') are embodied by Brando's Johnny in The Wild One, Sonny Barger's Hells Angels, and the wanton Born to Lose club of The Born Losers, whose smashing matches Pisarev's Russian nihilism. He then shows how Willie G. Davidson's 1977 Low Rider and events like Daytona's Bike Week sanitized and marketed the rebellion, reducing skulls and DILLIGAF patches to ubiquitous fashion worth $340 million a week. Freud supplies the psychology: the superego keeps the rest of us tame, so we voyeuristically buy a tasteful version of outlaw freedom, even though pure nihilism, denying all truth, refutes even itself.
- ch. 20 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Free Will vs Determinism; Fate and Foreknowledge — Feldman tests Harley's 'Great American Freedom Machine' slogan against each conception of freedom: metaphysical freedom (universal causation, divine foreknowledge, fate) cannot depend on your vehicle; legal freedom actually shrinks for riders through helmet mandates and extra licensing; and circumstantial freedom is gained equally by any vehicle, a wheelchair being the true freedom machine. Borrowing Socrates' comparison method from the Philebus, he argues the alleged 'feeling of freedom' is really a feeling of happiness or exhilaration combined with the false belief that one is freer than a Camry driver. What really binds him to his 1986 FXRT is fun, the thrill of leaning to the edge, and the sensory and historical pleasures of the air-cooled V-twin line back to the Silent Grey Fellow, which have nothing to do with freedom.
- ch. 21 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, David Hume — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Bourne argues that bikes like the Ducati 916 and Harley V-Rod are genuine works of art: Kantian disinterested attention is both possible and actual (he bought his Ducati Monster on styling alone), and Hume's standard of taste shows that judgments like calling a Honda Gold Wing elegant are answerable to criteria and informed comparison, so taste is not mere subjectivity. Against Wollheim he deploys Currie's account of the artwork as a type of action, so mass-produced instances are no obstacle, exactly as copies of Sartre's Nausea are the same novel; against Danto's aboutness requirement he claims motorcycles are about motorcycles, and should be evaluated like architecture, where function enters the aesthetic judgment. On Bauhaus principles the Ducati 916 expresses its own construction authentically while the V-Rod's styling compromises its ground clearance, which is what makes the Ducati, at least, high art.
- ch. 22 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Plato — Phenomena and Noumena; Transcendental Idealism; Allegory of the Cave — Harman argues the real heroes of Easy Rider are the two Harley-Davidsons: the exploding Captain America bike that drowns out its rider's corpse, the cocaine hidden in the gas tank like a prosthetic organ, and the obscured helmets and muffled dialogue reduce Fonda, Hopper, and Nicholson to 'stowaways and parasites' of their machines. He uses this reversal to attack Kant's Copernican Revolution, on which we could know only how Harleys appear to us and never the things in themselves, and to champion the Whitehead-Deleuze-Latour current in which genes, pickup trucks, bridges, and Harleys are autonomous actors no less than humans, against Plato's two-world heir of appearances and unknowable things. Billy and Captain America's 1960s transgression, he concludes, quietly ends in submission to the power, rhythm, and demands of their bikes.
- ch. 24 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Thomas Hobbes, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Karl Popper — Two Concepts of Liberty; The Noble Lie — Rollin opposes mandatory helmet laws by contrasting two conceptions of freedom: the Hobbesian absence-of-constraint view ('go to hell in my own way', from Pinocchio's 'no strings on me' to the trapped coyote) and the rational-order view running from Plato (ranked classes, noble lies, censorship, contempt for democracy) through Rousseau's 'forced to be free', which Popper already identified as the enemy of the open society. Since, as the obesity-as-disease example and Robert Paul Wolff's expert-government argument show, we cannot claim certainty about value judgments the way we can about fractures, paternalism fails: if danger justified coercion we would have to ban motorcycles, skiing, parachuting, and bull-riding alike. Helmet laws in effect claim society knows what you ought to want better than you do, and Rollin's answer to his British colleague's 'what will you do if you injure your brain?' is to join the Department of Food Science and Nutrition.
- ch. 25 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The State of Nature; Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; The Social Contract — Alford reads the biker image, born at Hollister in 1947 and fixed by The Wild One, through two opposed accounts of presocial man: the badass on his tricked-out Harley is Hobbes's natural man, all vanity, domination, and fear merely redirected to government, praising Chuck Zito's 'most primitive philosophy'; the club run with bonfire, brothers, old ladies, and Steppenwolf re-enacts Rousseau's golden age between the stupidity of brutes and the fatal enlightenment of civil man, before the first enclosed field ('this is mine') bred property, vanity, and misery. The paradox of a biker who is simultaneously threat and salvation dissolves once we see that 'primitive' and 'civilized' are mutually defining: suburbanites and bikers alike live both Hobbesian individualism and Rousseauvian community, each defining themselves against the other.
- ch. 26 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Sigmund Freud, Karl Marx — Commodity Fetishism — Ferriss explains biker leather through the two forms of fetishism: Freud's fetish object, by which the black jacket augments masculine potency against castration anxiety (while women riders negotiate pink leather, rhinestones, and the dominatrix option, as Dot Robinson of the Motor Maids did with her lipstick holder), and Marx's commodity fetishism, in which Capital's dancing wooden table becomes the H-D-branded jacket whose exchange value carries Americana and rebellion to dentists, Vogue spreads, Harley Ken dolls, and the Phoenix Art Museum. From Brando's promotional still and Girl on a Motorcycle's 'black bull' to Kenneth Anger's gay Nazi bikers in Scorpio Rising, leather's erotic charge is fashion, a language of gender that changes, and it is now fading as motorcycles themselves become mere accessories in deodorant ads.
- ch. 28 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault — Power/Knowledge; Objectification — Kieffner applies Foucault's deployment of sexuality to show how power centers constructed biker sexual stereotypes: the psychiatric profession behind Time's 1970 'Motorcycle Syndrome', the press that sexualized the Van Buren sisters and filled Easyriders with centerfolds, police 'gauntlets' that pull over bikes but not cars (and ask Raw Thunder's Char Zack if she is a lesbian), and even Harley-Davidson's own 'mystique' video deploying eroticism to sell bikes and HOG memberships. Following Foucault's rule of the tactical polyvalence of discourse, he ends with resistance within the same power structure: riders' rights organizations videotaping police encounters, and the tactical discourses of queer, lesbian, and female riders, from Evelyn Greenway's 1921 'Do it with a motorcycle' onward, who refuse the labels of sexual outlaw and gender traitor.
- ch. 29 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" **·** Zhuangzi, Heraclitus, Jean-Paul Sartre, Plato — Wu Wei (effortless action); The Tao (the Way); Yin and Yang; Li (ritual propriety) — Jones narrates a day riding a customized Sportster around O'ahu as a lived Daoism that heals the Platonic-Cartesian mind-body split: the ride enacts Zhuangzi's 'rambling without destination' and Cook Ding's effortless wu-wei, rider li (the low hand-greeting among riders) is Confucian ritual propriety, and the island's dry and wet ranges embody yin and yang through whose flow the rider blends. Heraclitus's 'expect the unexpected' and 'the sun is new every day' are vindicated when a container truck stops short and only the disciplined, ego-less low-side, dropping the beloved bike while riding the skid, saves the rider's life, sealing the day's lesson that there is never freedom without discipline. Sartre's hell of other people appears in the cagers' compartmentalized inattention, and the chapter closes with Zhuangzi: 'The way is made by walking it.'
- ch. 30 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 31 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "Harley-Davidson and Philosophy" *(front)*

## Harry Potter and Philosophy - If Aristotle Ran Hogwarts

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Unknown" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "The Blackwell Philosophy and Pop Culture Series" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright Page" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Foreword" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Acknowledgments" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "Introduction" *(no index entry yet)* — The editor, invoking Plato's claim that philosophy begins in wonder, argues that Rowling like Tolkien and Lewis deliberately poses serious philosophical questions for young readers. He frames the volume's agenda through the books' own material: whether love is the most powerful magic, whether souls exist and can be split by Voldemort's Horcruxes, what Animagi and boggarts teach about personal identity, whether a love potion could ever be ethically used, whether Kingsley's 'every human life is worth the same' is true, and whether Dumbledore is right that something can be real even if it exists only inside one's head. It explains the book covers the complete seven-novel saga with emphasis on the final two volumes.
- ch. 10 "PART ONE - THE HORCRUX OF THE MATTER DESTINY, IDENTITY, AND THE SOUL" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "Philosophical Conceptions of the Soul" **·** Immortality and the Soul — Scott Sehon opens his chapter by cataloguing how central souls are to the plot: Harry, Sirius, and Dudley narrowly escape dementors while Barty Crouch Jr. does not, and Voldemort's Horcruxes split his soul into eight parts that must all be destroyed before he can die. He sets up the guiding question of what the soul is in Rowling's world, where souls generally survive bodily death, and announces a survey of philosophical accounts to test whether her picture is plausible.
- ch. 12 "Unknown" *(interlude)*
- ch. 13 "Unknown" **·** Aristotle on the Soul — Presents the life-source view held by some ancient Greek philosophers, on which the soul simply accounts for life itself, so that all living things, plants and animals alike, are ensouled. It applies the view to Rowling's world by noting that Blast-Ended Skrewts and even gillyweed would then have souls, though few philosophers today accept this conception.
- ch. 14 "Unknown" *(no index entry yet)* — Presents the sentience view, on which the soul is responsible for the capacity to feel pleasure and pain and for all higher-level thought, excluding plants but not sentient animals. It notes the view would oddly grant souls even to the Whomping Willow within Rowling's universe, since magical plants there display direct sensation.
- ch. 15 "Unknown" **·** René Descartes — Mind–Body Dualism — Explains Descartes' narrowing of the soul to higher cognition: sensation can be accounted for materially, but language and complex belief require an immaterial soul, so nonhuman animals are soulless. It shows how magical creatures complicate this in Rowling's world, since owls seem to understand speech and Crookshanks seems abnormally intelligent, and notes that the first three views all treat the soul as an immaterial substance able to survive death, motivating the materialist alternative.
- ch. 16 "Unknown" *(no index entry yet)* — Defines materialism as the view that nothing exists but matter and physical forces, so all mental life is brain process, there is no soul, and death is simply the end. This is the position the chapter will measure against the Potter world, where souls demonstrably survive bodily death.
- ch. 17 "Ghosts and “Going On”" *(no index entry yet)* — Distinguishes the everyday sentimental use of 'soul', from Hoagy Carmichael's 'Heart and Soul' to soul mates and soulful art, arguing such talk is metaphorical commentary on our deepest emotions, love, and conscience that even materialists need not renounce. It foreshadows the chapter's conclusion that Rowling's conception of the soul mixes this sentimental view with a metaphysics drawn from the sentience and Cartesian views.
- ch. 18 "The Dementor’s Kiss" **·** Immortality and the Soul — Argues materialism is straightforwardly false within the Potter universe, using Hermione's claim that a sword through the body leaves the soul untouched, and then taxonomizes the forms surviving souls take: Nearly Headless Nick's explanation that ghosts are imprints of wizards who refused to 'go on', Voldemort's bodiless state as 'less than the meanest ghost' after his curse rebounded on baby Harry, the graveyard echo in Goblet of Fire where Cedric and Harry's parents emerge 'more solid' than ghosts, and the Resurrection Stone in Deathly Hallows returning Sirius, Lupin, and his parents as 'neither ghost nor truly flesh'.
- ch. 19 "Horcruxes" **·** René Descartes — Mind–Body Dualism; Personal Identity — Uses Lupin's account of the Dementor's Kiss, whose victims like Barty Crouch Jr. go on existing as empty shells without memory or self, to eliminate the leading views of the soul: the soul cannot be the source of life, of sentience, or even of Cartesian higher thought, since the soulless victim may still sense and think dimly. The chapter compares the Kiss's aftermath to severe dementia or to Lockhart after his backfired memory charm, raising the question of whether the person rather than just the body survives.
- ch. 20 "A Plausible View" **·** René Descartes — Immortality and the Soul; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Reconstructs Slughorn's lesson to young Tom Riddle, that murder rips the soul and the torn piece can be hidden in a Horcrux, an act 'against nature', and argues that because Voldemort's intellect and magical powers remain intact while his capacity to charm gives way to rule by fear and his body becomes snake-like, Rowling's soul must be the sentimental one: our humanity, love, and moral conscience. On this view the soul is damaged by supreme evil and can be repaired only through remorse, which is exactly what Harry urges on Voldemort ('be a man... try for some remorse') before the final killing curse rebounds.
- ch. 21 "NOTES" **·** Mind–Body Dualism; Immortality and the Soul — Steps outside the fiction to ask whether Rowling's hybrid soul theory is actually plausible, answering probably not: neuroscience shows no brain events lacking physical cause, mind-body interaction remains the classic obstacle for any dualism, and it is doubly implausible that an immaterial substance would be responsible only for our deepest emotions and conscience. The differing physical powers of ghosts, Resurrection Stone shades, and Horcrux-souls make the metaphysics even harder, though the chapter concedes that a fictional soul picture that vividly dramatizes what we care about needs no truth to make a terrific story.
- ch. 22 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 23 "Mind-Body Distinctions" **·** Personal Identity — Eric Saidel launches his chapter from a puzzle: why would Sirius, outside for the first time in months, celebrate by chasing his tail in Order of the Phoenix? He asks whether an Animagus is a man in a dog's body, a dog in a man's body, or a third thing, and extends the question to Pettigrew, Rita Skeeter, McGonagall, werewolf Lupin, boggarts, and Polyjuice transformations such as Barty Crouch Jr.'s eerily effective impersonation of Moody.
- ch. 24 "Whose Reasons" **·** René Descartes — Mind–Body Dualism; Personal Identity — Tests the Cartesian division of the transformed Padfoot into mind plus body across four possible combinations and rejects them all: Sirius's human body cannot plausibly slip 'into non-being' during transformation (against McGonagall's quip), yet a Sirius-mind cannot explain tail-chasing and a dog-mind cannot explain Padfoot standing on his hind legs to embrace Harry at King's Cross. Mind-body dualism thus fails as an account of what the transformed person is.
- ch. 25 "A Step in the Right Direction" *(no index entry yet)* — Recasts the puzzle as a question of whose reasons cause the transformed person's actions, then shows this framing fails too: Polyjuice victims act on their own reasons, since Harry-as-Runcorn warns Arthur Weasley and strips Umbridge's door while Ron-as-Cattermole ignores the wife his host would protect, dogs chase tails because it feels good rather than for reasons, and werewolf Lupin attacks from instinct rather than acquiring a reason to harm his students.
- ch. 26 "A Unified Self" **·** Mind–Body Dualism — Proposes that some of Padfoot's behavior is explained by his dog's body rather than Sirius's mind, imagined through a counterfactual in which Sirius became a bear instead and would never have chased his tail. But the strategy generates absurd costs: if tail-chasing feels good, it is unclear whether the feeling belongs to Sirius's mind or to the dog's body, forcing the strange conclusion that a person's body might have reasons of its own that clash with the person's will.
- ch. 27 "NOTES" **·** René Descartes — Mind–Body Dualism; Personal Identity — Concludes that transformed persons are unified mind-body wholes rather than pilot-and-ship pairings, enlisting Descartes himself, who insisted he is 'not only lodged in my body, like a pilot in his ship' but compounded with it into a single whole. The evidence is textual: Polyjuiced Harry naturally 'thunders' with Runcorn's powerful frame while acting for Harry's reasons, Luna recognizes Harry through Barny Weasley's facial expression, and Crouch-as-Moody makes sense as an indivisible embodiment, so Padfoot is neither man nor dog but a third kind of being, a man-dog.
- ch. 28 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 29 "Varieties of Prophecy" *(no index entry yet)* — Jeremy Pierce contrasts Trelawney's fraudulent fortune-telling, tea leaves, tarot, crystal balls, and her endlessly repeated predictions of Harry's death, with the two 'real predictions' even Dumbledore certifies. Using McGonagall's dismissal of divination and Firenze's distinction between Seers and 'the self-flattering nonsense humans call fortune-telling', the section poses the chapter's question: what makes a prophecy genuine?
- ch. 30 "Fallible Prophecies" **·** Aristotle — Fate and Foreknowledge — Credits Aristotle with first raising whether the future is fixed and builds a sevenfold taxonomy of how a Seer might access the future, from fallible sensory prediction like Trelawney's and Muggle weather reports, through glimpses of likely or possible futures as Dumbledore describes, to infallible access to a deterministic or actual future. The section then asks which kind Trelawney's two genuine prophecies are.
- ch. 31 "Self-Fulfilling Prophecies" **·** Fate and Foreknowledge — Reads Dumbledore's gloss on the prophecy, 'you are free to choose your way, quite free to turn your back on the prophecy', as treating even real prophecies as non-compelling, and argues the two true prophecies cannot be mere likelihoods because they are involuntary trances with a magical source. Trelawney's card-reading episodes in Half-Blood Prince, accurate while she is oblivious to Harry behind the statue and to the lightning-struck tower, hint at inconsistent unconscious access to the future.
- ch. 32 "Destiny" **·** Fate and Foreknowledge — Examines Dumbledore's suggestion that the first prophecy was self-fulfilling: it did not originally mean Harry rather than Neville, but Voldemort's choice of the half-blood he saw himself in marked Harry as his equal, and Snape's ejection from the interview left Voldemort ignorant of the risk that attacking Harry would transfer power to him. Invoking Alexander of Aphrodisias's discussion of the Oedipus prophecy, the section argues a genuine prophecy reveals rather than causes the future, so self-fulfillment cannot distinguish Trelawney's real predictions, since the prophecy never made Voldemort do anything.
- ch. 33 "A Rodent’s Destiny" **·** Compatibilism; Fate and Foreknowledge — Takes Rowling's 2007 remark that there is 'no such thing as destiny' and maps the logical space it could occupy: compatibilist freedom within a predetermined course, libertarian freedom with a fixed future of true statements about what will happen, or an open future where claims about free choices are neither true nor false until made. The section asks whether Dumbledore means prophecies do not compel anyone or that their content is merely one of several possible futures.
- ch. 34 "Time Travel and Fixed Time" **·** Fate and Foreknowledge — Argues Trelawney's second prophecy, that the servant will break free and rejoin the Dark Lord that very night, was far too improbable to be a merely likely prediction given how easily Wormtail's escape could have failed. Likewise the chain running from the first prophecy through Wormtail becoming Secret-Keeper to Lily's protective sacrifice shows the 'real' prophecies need a stronger connection to the actual future than probability.
- ch. 35 "NOTES" **·** The Metaphysics of Time Travel; Fate and Foreknowledge; Free Will vs Determinism — Defends a fixed-time theory from the Prisoner of Azkaban time-travel episode, where later-Harry casts the stag Patronus that saved earlier-Harry and Buckbeak and Sirius are saved without anything changing, while acknowledging Hermione's warnings about wizards killing their past selves complicate the picture, and adds special relativity's absence of an absolute present as further support. The section then argues that the sheer improbability of the luck fulfilling the prophecies, the Myrtle's-bathroom Polyjuice chain, Felix Felicis, the capture that delivers Griphook, and Harry's arrival at the Shrieking Shack, makes better sense with something like divine providence guiding events than with pure chance.
- ch. 36 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 37 "PART TWO - THE MOST POWERFUL MAGIC OF ALL" *(front)*
- ch. 38 "Snape and the Many-Splendored Thing" **·** Philosophy of Love — Catherine Jack Deavel and David Paul Deavel open with the puzzle of Dumbledore's 'ironclad' certainty about Snape, maintained even after Snape kills him and attested by McGonagall's report that his repentance was 'absolutely genuine'. Their answer is Snape's unrequited love for Lily Potter, and they set the chapter's task: to examine, against the suspicion that redemptive love-talk is mere sentimentality, what philosophers have said about love's nature and its power to transform and to mislead judgment.
- ch. 39 "The Abandoned Boys" **·** Plato — Philosophy of Love; Platonic Love (the ladder of love) — Surveys the classical triad of eros, philia, and agape from Plato's Symposium onward and maps it onto the series: the Weasleys, Ron and Hermione, and Harry and Ginny exemplify romantic love, Dumbledore's remark that Voldemort never had a friend dramatizes the absence of philia, and Lily's self-giving love that repels Quirrell and Voldemort is agape. Because traditional accounts treat love as an act of the will rather than a feeling, Snape's persistent dislikes cannot disprove that his love for Lily has transformed him.
- ch. 40 "Snape the Occlumens" *(no index entry yet)* — Traces how Snape shares the abandoned-boys origin of both Harry and Voldemort, a mixed bloodline and a feuding home that made Hogwarts the first real home for all three, 'the Half-Blood Prince' title itself an attempt to bury his Muggle ancestry. Where Voldemort chose power, selfishness, and a fragmented psyche and Harry opened himself to friends, the section frames the question of what makes the third lost boy, Dumbledore's double agent and killer, tick.
- ch. 41 "Snape’s Choice" **·** Aristotle, Thomas Aquinas — Philosophy of Love; Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Defines love, with Aristotle, Aquinas, and M. Scott Peck, as willing the good of the other for the other's own sake, and reads Snape's double-agent life as love expressed in action despite real hatred: he protects Harry's enemies at constant risk while disliking most of them. His Occlumency, the skill to lie to Voldemort's face, reveals both strength and weakness, for rather than renouncing love as Voldemort did he only conceals it, and his dying gift of memories to Harry, revealing that Harry is a Horcrux, must be freely offered because his thoughts can never be taken.
- ch. 42 "A Work in Progress" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Platonic Love (the ladder of love); Philosophy of Love — Reads Snape's love for Lily through Plato's Symposium: his childhood watching of Lily with 'undisguised greed', his failure to ask Voldemort to spare her husband and child, 'You disgust me', shows a love that desires Lily's good only as it relates to him, falling short of Aristotle's beloved as 'second self'. Diotima's 'possession of the good forever' and 'giving birth in beauty' describe how his love matures into genuine virtue, a deepening Pope Benedict XVI's picture of love as 'ecstasy', an exodus out of the closed self toward self-giving, completes.
- ch. 43 "NOTES" **·** Aristotle — Philosophy of Love; Virtue Ethics (arete); Forgiveness — Argues Snape's redemption is gradual virtue-building rather than instant transformation, since vice and virtue are habits built up over time and his resentments persist even as his actions serve the good. His doe Patronus, matching Lily's, manifests Aristotle's beloved as a second self, his remorse, an emotion he shares with Harry but Voldemort cannot feel, begins to knit his damaged soul whole, and Harry's forgiveness, naming his son Albus Severus for 'the bravest man I ever knew', completes the arc love began.
- ch. 44 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 45 "Violently Pink Products" **·** Philosophy of Love — Gregory Bassham opens by contrasting Muggle spending on attractiveness with the wizarding world's far more reliable magical love potions, and poses the ethical problem of their nonconsensual use. The chapter's test cases are the two key episodes: Merope Gaunt's use of a love potion to ensnare Tom Riddle Sr., Voldemort's father, and her eventual choice to stop dosing him at great cost to herself and her unborn son.
- ch. 46 "Little Hangleton" **·** Philosophy of Love — Catalogues what Half-Blood Prince establishes about love potions: Fred and George sell them legally at Weasleys' Wizard Wheezes, Hermione identifies Amortentia by its mother-of-pearl sheen and personal smell, and Slughorn warns it 'doesn't really create love... It is impossible to manufacture or imitate love', producing only obsessive infatuation, as Ron's Romilda Vane episode demonstrates. The section ends by introducing Voldemort's mother as the person who actually used one.
- ch. 47 "Real Love or Mere Infatuation" **·** Philosophy of Love — Retells Dumbledore's reconstruction of Merope Gaunt's ensnarement of Tom Riddle Sr.: Harry correctly narrows the cause of the squire's son's behavior to the Imperius Curse or a love potion, and Dumbledore concludes she dosed him, then chose to stop, wrongly hoping he had come to love her or would stay for the baby. The section closes on Dumbledore's insistence that this backstory 'has everything to do with the prophecy' about Harry and Voldemort.
- ch. 48 "Not His Mother’s Son" **·** Harry Frankfurt — Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism; Philosophy of Love — Argues Merope did not truly love Riddle because the potion robbed him of the freedom to do otherwise, invoking Harry Frankfurt's denial that alternative possibilities are necessary for freedom and noting that even compatibilists deny potion-induced affection is free, since Tom would not have wanted to love her under magical inducement. Real love respects the beloved's true interests and creates a relationship in which both grow, and even self-administered potions wrong the user by forfeiting the moral growth that only uncoerced commitment makes possible.
- ch. 49 "NOTES" **·** Aristotle — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Free Will vs Determinism; Philosophy of Love — Contends that Voldemort was not his mother's son: what defines a person is the habitual pattern of choices, as in Aristotle's 'we are what we consistently do', and Merope, who freed Riddle at the cost of her own life and renounced magic entirely, is a genuine though partial redemption, whereas Voldemort followed Marvolo Gaunt and Salazar Slytherin instead. Quoting William Hasker on how love potions trap both parties because love must be freely bestowed, the section argues character is destiny only if forged by free choice, and that Harry, protected by his mother's sacrificial love, shows what Voldemort's rejection of love and vulnerability destroyed.
- ch. 50 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 51 "The Feminist Debate So Far" **·** Feminism — Anne Collins Smith announces the chapter's thesis: in Rowling's universe love crosses hierarchical boundaries and defeats evil most effectively precisely when it does not compete, as self-sacrifice and kindness overwhelm greed and ambition without fighting them on their own terms. She claims this pattern resonates with radical feminism, and sets up a survey of feminist scholarship on Harry Potter before examining love's role in the books.
- ch. 52 "Radical vs. Liberal Feminism" **·** Feminism — Surveys the divided feminist reception of the series: Schoefer, Heilman, and Dresang charge it with reinforcing gender stereotypes, from giggling girls to the absence of any woman as heroic as Harry or wise as Dumbledore, while Kern, Zettel, and Gladstein defend it on contextual grounds, Lavender's silliness mirroring Dean and Seamus, Trelawney mirroring Lockhart, and Umbridge and Bellatrix commanding as much fear as any male Death Eater. The giggling debate, with Zettel's refusal 'to tell girls that the only way to be valid human beings is to turn themselves into boys', stages the disagreement the chapter will recast as liberal versus radical feminism.
- ch. 53 "More Wonderful and More Terrible than Death" **·** Mary Wollstonecraft, John Stuart Mill — Feminism — Distinguishes liberal feminism, historically grounded in Wollstonecraft, Harriet Taylor Mill, and John Stuart Mill, which holds women are autonomous persons who succeed on equal but masculine-coded terms, as in Gladstein's praise of McGonagall and Hermione, from radical feminism's analysis of the sex/gender system that forces masculine and feminine traits into rigid, ranked pairs. It reads Zettel's defense of Molly Weasley's mothering and Gallardo-C. and Smith's 'Cinderfella' interpretation, evil as 'phallic power and ambition' even in Umbridge, good as feminine-coded kindness and selflessness in Harry, as radical-feminist readings, noting thinkers from St. Gertrude to Gandhi and C.S. Lewis have likewise insisted love and compassion belong to full humanity.
- ch. 54 "The Triumph of Love" **·** Feminism; Philosophy of Love; Forgiveness — Tracks love's escalation across the series as a force that overwhelms evil without competing: Lily's skin-deep sacrificial love repels Quirrell in Sorcerer's Stone, Harry spares Pettigrew out of love for Lupin and Sirius, his love for Sirius expels Voldemort's possession at the Ministry, and Dumbledore names it 'a force that is at once more wonderful and more terrible than death'. In Deathly Hallows love extends to Kreacher, Griphook, and Dobby, and culminates in Harry's astonishing plea that Voldemort 'try for some remorse', his redefinition of manhood in 'be a man', and victory through willing sacrifice and a single defensive spell rather than a duel, vindicating traditionally feminine values over hypermasculine domination.
- ch. 55 "NOTES" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 56 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 57 "PART THREE - POTTERWATCH FREEDOM AND POLITICS" *(front)*
- ch. 58 "The Dangers of Patriotism" *(no index entry yet)* — Andrew P. Mills opens his essay by asking whether the loyalty Harry, Ron, and Hermione show toward Gryffindor - cheering its Quidditch team, earning House points, and refusing to help Slytherins like Crabbe and Goyle - is really a virtue. He observes that we call being a 'patriotic Gryffindor' one of their virtues and demand patriotism of political leaders, yet previews the disturbing argument that preferring one's own group makes us morally similar to Voldemort and the Death Eaters.
- ch. 59 "Death Eaters and Discrimination" *(no index entry yet)* — Mills develops the case against patriotism through Tolstoy's definition of patriotism as 'the desire for the exclusive good of one's own nation' and Emma Goldman's charge that patriotism breeds conceit and arrogance. Just as Hermione should prefer Gryffindors and 'buy American' urges favoring the U.S. economy, the patriot gives her country's interests priority over all others - and this preference, the section argues, mirrors the Death Eaters' sense of pureblood superiority, forcing other nations into line 'at the tip of a wand' just as at the barrel of a gun.
- ch. 60 "The Sorting Hat Speaks Division and Divisiveness" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill — Humanity as an End in Itself; Utilitarianism — This section argues that patriotism violates the principle of equal moral worth shared by utilitarians and Kantians alike: Bentham's 'each to count for one, and none for more than one' means the Minister of Magic's pain counts no more than a house-elf's, while Kant's formula forbids treating anyone (Muggle or wizard) merely as a means. It reads Death Eater pureblood ideology as ordinary racism and sexism, cites Potterwatch's 'every human life is worth the same, and worth saving' and Hermione's S.P.E.W. campaign against 'slave labor,' and asks how preferring countrymen over foreigners differs from preferring people of one's race or gender, since neither is something a person controls.
- ch. 61 "Patriotism and Global Conflict" *(no index entry yet)* — Mills's second objection is that patriotism divides people who should cooperate, quoting the Sorting Hat's fifth-year song warning that Hogwarts must 'unite inside her or we'll crumble from within.' He reads the Triwizard Tournament through Ron's 'It's about winning!' outburst at Hermione's friendship with Viktor Krum, against Dumbledore's insistence that the tournament exists to build international ties and that 'we are only as strong as we are united' - the same unity global crises like warming and oil wars demand. Dumbledore's Army, drawing members from every House but Slytherin, shows that external threats make House and national divisions irrelevant.
- ch. 62 "Patriotism Restored" *(no index entry yet)* — The section adds a resource-based objection: if loving your country means wanting your countrymen to enjoy the middle-class good life, maintaining that lifestyle seems to require exploiting poorer countries' cheap labor or consuming more than a fair share of scarce resources - presupposing that compatriots matter more. Mills illustrates with the Hogwarts house-elves whose enslaved labor underwrites the students' comfort, and concludes that defending patriotism requires reconciling it with the equal moral worth of all people.
- ch. 63 "The Importance of Community" **·** Martha Nussbaum — Cosmopolitanism — Mills rehabilitates patriotism through Martha Nussbaum's cosmopolitanism, which justifies special care for one's own in universalist terms. He models this on the Hogwarts Houses: sorting students into small, loyal Houses is the most effective way to give every student an equally good education, so Hermione's preference for Gryffindors is justified not by their superiority but because everyone prospers when each member gives special care - just as parents rightly love their own children more without thinking them morally more important. Patriotism is thus a virtue when it serves the interests of all countries' citizens and a vice when it fosters injustice.
- ch. 64 "Human Flourishing and the Preservation of Dying Cultures" **·** Aristotle — Communitarianism; Man as Political Animal — This section examines communitarianism - the view, drawing on Aristotle's claim that humans are political animals, that identity, values, and flourishing require membership in a tradition-bound community - and reads the Death Eaters' blood-purity mantra as its violent extreme, noting Voldemort's murder of the Muggle Studies teacher for advocating cultural mixing. Harry's identity as 'the Boy Who Lived' and his misery living 'under the stairs' outside the wizarding community illustrate how selves are community-constructed, while debates over the treatment of women in traditional cultures expose the tension between preserving a culture and violating the liberty of its members.
- ch. 65 "NOTES" **·** Cosmopolitanism; Communitarianism — The concluding section weighs communitarian rootedness against Jeremy Waldron's cosmopolitan claim that the hybrid, culturally mobile life is the only fitting response to the modern world. It asks whether centaurs like Firenze should be kept in the Forbidden Forest or absorbed into wizarding society, and argues the books endorse both cosmopolitanisms: equal moral worth of all persons (against Voldemort's racism) and flourishing through intercultural fluency, embodied in Harry's ease among Muggles, goblins, and house-elves and in part-this part-that characters like Hagrid, Lupin, Sirius, Fleur Delacour, and Firenze.
- ch. 66 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 67 "Is Dumbledore a Libertarian" **·** The Minimal State — Beth Admiraal and Regan Lance Reitsma open by defining political libertarianism - the only just state is a minimal 'nightwatchman' state protecting citizens from force, fraud, and theft - and report that commentators Travis Prinzi and law professor Benjamin Barton read Dumbledore and the series as libertarian. They object that the books never champion free markets, antiwelfarism, or national sovereignty, and that Dumbledore, despite holding the posts of Headmaster, Supreme Mugwump, and Chief Warlock, never advocates any general political philosophy at all, so they ask on what evidence the libertarian reading actually rests.
- ch. 68 "Barton’s Libertarian Interpretation of the Potter Series" **·** The Minimal State — The authors rebut Prinzi's three arguments that Dumbledore is libertarian-minded. Distrust of political power (the Ministry's suspension of Dumbledore, Buckbeak's condemnation, Harry's underage-magic trial) is a non sequitur since non-libertarians limit power through checks and balances too; his defense of house-elves, the giants, and despised-minority teachers like Hagrid, Lupin, and Firenze shows a devotion to liberty liberals share, not the libertarian's distinctively robust right (no paternalism, no victimless-crime laws, no redistribution); and his hands-off management - letting Snape bully Neville, standing apart from Harry's fights - cannot transfer from school to state, and even a libertarian headmaster would be obligated to stop teacher abuse and require professors to teach.
- ch. 69 "NOTES" **·** The Minimal State — This section takes up Barton's claim that the Ministry's tyranny - Azkaban, trialless death sentences, surveillance, a controlled press - makes the series libertarian invective. The authors grant the Ministry is 'a lousy bureaucracy' but note its honest officials like Arthur Weasley, the Wizengamot's majority acquitting Harry, and sensible laws such as the self-defense exception to underage wizardry, and argue the inference from bad governance to a minimal nightwatchman state is a non sequitur: corrupt Supreme Court justices are replaced, not answered by a two-justice court, so the antidote is better governance. Rowling's own politics - welfare, a 1 million pound Labour donation, support for Obama - undercuts any libertarian agenda.
- ch. 70 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 71 "Plato and Dumbledore Separated at Birth" **·** Plato — The Philosopher King — David Lay Williams and Alan J. Kellner open their essay with paired epigraphs from Dumbledore ('Those who are best suited to power are those who have never sought it') and Plato ('A city in which those who are going to rule are least eager to rule is necessarily best'). Against Lord Acton's dictum that power corrupts, they present Plato's ingeniously simple solution - power must never reside with those who lust for it, since disinterest paradoxically makes the best rulers - and announce that this lesson is central to the climax of the entire Harry Potter series.
- ch. 72 "Fudge and Umbridge The Lessons of Obviously Unfit Power-Brokers" **·** Plato — The Philosopher King; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?) — The section draws a biography-by-biography parallel between Plato and Dumbledore: each lived through upheaval (the Peloponnesian War, the Thirty, and Socrates' execution; Grindelwald and Voldemort), each renounced a political career after disillusion, and each turned from politics to education, founding the Academy and leading Hogwarts. It explains the Republic's philosopher-rulers and their four cardinal virtues, argues that intelligence alone (Lex Luthor, Hannibal Lecter) plus excessive self-love disqualifies a ruler, and introduces Glaucon's Ring of Gyges as Plato's test of incorruptibility - a legend it says Rowling revives in Harry's invisibility cloak.
- ch. 73 "Voldemort and Dumbledore Two Tempted by Power" **·** Plato — The Philosopher King — The authors run Plato's criteria on the wizarding world's obviously unfit power-brokers. Umbridge fails as a ruler: her 'I must not tell lies' blood-quill detentions and multiplying Educational Decrees expose a lust for power that grows with her authority, and she lacks all the cardinal virtues, especially self-control. Fudge's insecurity proves equally corrupting - his denial of Voldemort's return, smearing Harry in the press, and tilting Harry's Wizengamot hearing (moving its time to exclude Dumbledore) all serve only to keep him in office, so both, like Plato's tyrants, lose the power they clutch at.
- ch. 74 "Harry’s Cloak, the Ring of Gyges, and the Temptations of Power" **·** Plato, Immanuel Kant, Socrates — The Philosopher King; Humanity as an End in Itself; The Unexamined Life — This section contrasts Voldemort and Dumbledore as two figures tempted by power. Tom Riddle's egocentrism - frightening and controlling other children at the orphanage, treating his 'friends' as mere followers, killing Lily, and splintering his soul for Horcruxes - violates Kant's dictum never to use persons merely as means and fits Plato's category of the least trustworthy rulers. Dumbledore, though tempted in youth by Grindelwald's 'for the greater good' schemes and later by Marvolo Gaunt's ring, is venerable for Socratic self-knowledge: knowing 'I was not to be trusted with power,' he repeatedly refused the Ministry and so promoted justice by teaching.
- ch. 75 "NOTES" **·** Plato — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); The Philosopher King — The concluding section argues that Harry passes Plato's Ring of Gyges test, which Rowling rebuilds around the Invisibility Cloak: unlike Gyges, Harry never uses invisibility for selfish gain, refuses Scrimgeour's offer of his dream job, hunts Horcruxes rather than making them, and at eleven pulls the Sorcerer's Stone from his pocket precisely because Dumbledore's enchantment lets only someone who wants to find but not use it do so. This indifference to the lure of power, joined to courage, justice, and self-restraint, is the Platonic quality that makes Harry fit to rule.
- ch. 76 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 77 "PART FOUR - THE ROOM OF REQUIREMENT A POTTER POTPOURRI" *(front)*
- ch. 78 "Truth in Fiction" *(no index entry yet)* — Tamar Szabo Gendler uses Rowling's 2007 Carnegie Hall announcement that Dumbledore is gay to pose the philosophical problem of truth in fiction: what makes something a fact about a story's world? She catalogs three fan reactions - delight, dismay, and, most interesting philosophically, challenges to Rowling's authority to add facts after the series ended - and notes the puzzling asymmetry that nobody objected to her other revelations, like Neville marrying Hannah Abbott or Lupin's impoverished life, before asking whether canon, readers, authorial thought, or genre conventions determine fictional truth.
- ch. 79 "So, Is Dumbledore Gay" **·** David Lewis, Aristotle — Possible Worlds — Gendler tests whether fictional truth works like historical truth and finds it fails: whereas a biographer discovers facts about the one actual world, a storyteller chooses among infinitely many imaginary worlds - David Lewis's point that 'every way that a world could possibly be is a way that some world is' - so storytelling mixes creation with discovery. She distinguishes the 'primary truths' stated on the Potter novels' 4,100 pages (discounting trivial inconsistencies like Percy's badge color and Moaning Myrtle's S-bend versus U-bend) from unstated 'secondary truths' like Hermione having ten fingers, rejects a maximal inclusiveness principle that would import every actual-world fact into the story, and closes with Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics advice to seek only the exactness a subject allows.
- ch. 80 "Closing Speculations Genre" *(no index entry yet)* — This section weighs the first two criteria for whether it is true in the Potter world that Dumbledore is gay. Textually the claim appears nowhere in the canon but is arguably implied by Dumbledore's intense bond with Grindelwald ('you cannot imagine how his ideas... inflamed me') and the absence of any heterosexual interest; anti-intentionalist theorists (Wimsatt, Beardsley, Gadamer, Barthes, Foucault, Derrida) hold that meaning is public and plural, so Rowling cannot legislate new facts and there are as many Potter worlds as readers, while intentionalists like E. D. Hirsch counter that authors fix meaning - and most fans treat Rowling's announcements as an oral appendix consistent with her hundreds of interviews.
- ch. 81 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — The final section asks whether genre rules out Dumbledore's homosexuality on the ground that children's stories exclude adult sexuality, but answers that Potter is young-adult fiction where adult desires matter (Hagrid and Madame Maxime's flirtation, Snape's unrequited love for Lily), and that adults' desires being invisible to youthful protagonists actually explains the text's silence. It argues Rowling's metaphorical treatment of minority identity - Tonks marrying werewolf Lupin, Hermione's elf liberation, Mudblood and pureblood rhetoric - catches readers in 'an act of unwitting toleration,' and that Dumbledore's love for Grindelwald does real plot work, perhaps reflecting that Rowling 'could not conceive of there being a woman who was his intellectual equal.'
- ch. 82 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 83 "Choices" *(no index entry yet)* — Gregory Bassham opens with Socrates' 'know thyself' and reads the series as Harry's journey from appearance to reality, centered on the end of Chamber of Secrets: alarmed that he speaks Parseltongue like Voldemort and was nearly Sorted into Slytherin, Harry is reassured by Dumbledore's claim that 'It is our choices, Harry, that show us what we truly are, far more than our abilities.' Bassham asks what choices are, how they differ from abilities, and whether Dumbledore's claim - which Tom Morris calls one of the books' most important philosophical insights - is actually true.
- ch. 84 "How Revealing Are Our Choices" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — The section builds on Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics, which treats choice (prohairesis) as 'deliberative desire' for things within our power and a better test of character than actions - possibly the source of Dumbledore's remark. It then distinguishes three senses of choice: internal-choice (Draco choosing to kill Dumbledore but being unable to do it), act-choice (Dumbledore's poor youthful choices), and motive-choice, illustrated by Kant's two storekeepers who both refuse to cheat customers, one from fear of jail and one because it is right, so that only knowing a person's motive reveals what choice was truly made.
- ch. 85 "Abilities" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — Virtue Ethics; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Bassham tests each sense of choice against the novels: internal-choices are uninformative (Dudley resolving to give up sweets), act-choices mislead since character is settled habit - Hermione's occasional rule-breaking for Dumbledore's Army doesn't make her a rebel - and overt acts cannot reveal motives, as Snape's apparent Death Eater loyalty conceals devotion to Dumbledore and Lily. Motive-choices like Harry's decision to enter the Chamber to save Ginny are most revealing, but self-deception (Vernon Dursley's rationalizations) and wizarding complications - Harry's vision of attacking Arthur Weasley, Voldemort's implanted false memory in Morfin Gaunt, the Imperius Curse, Polyjuice Potion, and a Priori Incantatem thought experiment - show that in Rowling's world choices may be unknowable even to their maker.
- ch. 86 "How Revealing Are Our Abilities" *(no index entry yet)* — This section defines abilities as powers or capacities and shows why the concept is 'fuzzy': they come in degrees and vary with circumstance (speaking seventy words of Mermish, conjuring a Patronus only when relaxed).
