## Heroes and Philosophy - Buy the Book, Save the World

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Heroes and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "The Blackwell Philosophy and Pop Culture Series" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright Page" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Acknowledgements" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — The editor's introduction argues that Heroes is more reflective than average television, opening from Mohinder's 'Genesis' voice-over about life's mysteries and claiming that we are all philosophers seeking answers to the same fundamental questions. It frames narrative (from Aesop's fables to the Bible) as a powerful vehicle for moral lessons and previews the book's questions: is the Company good or evil, does Hiro really have a destiny, could mind reading be a power we already have, is time travel possible, what does Peter owe Nathan, and is it okay to lie to hide one's powers? It closes with John McCain's SNL joke defending Heroes against its competition.
- ch. 8 "PART ONE - HEROIC OBLIGATIONS" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "NOTES" **·** Thomas Hobbes, John Rawls, John Locke, Plato — The Social Contract; The State of Nature; The Veil of Ignorance; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?) — Sharp argues that superpowered individuals break the equality assumption on which social contract theory rests: since Claire cannot be killed, D.L. can walk out of any jail, and Matt can sway juries, Hobbes's fear-of-death rationale for the contract seems not to apply to them. He then answers that the heroes remain effectively equal (Claire can still be killed by a wood splinter, Adam by decapitation) and shares Locke's and Rawls's reasons for contract, yet shows Rawls's difference principle would force the powered to serve society while the liberty principle forbids it, and that Sylar could not even be morally condemned under a pure Hobbesian reading. Cases like Claude's invisible petty theft (Glaucon's Ring of Gyges made real), the 'Five Years Gone' registered-superhumans future, and Linderman's plot to reunite society through the New York explosion lead to the conclusion that superpowers really would break society.
- ch. 10 "NOTES" **·** Peter Singer, Immanuel Kant — The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); Deontology — Miles asks whether extraordinary power generates extraordinary obligations, testing Spider-Man's 'with great power comes great responsibility' against Claude the invisible thief and the heroes' reluctance. He uses Singer's principle of minimal obligation (Micah diverting the train from the infant; Claire's negligible risk in burning-building rescues; Hiro teleporting across distance) and Kant's perfect/imperfect obligation distinction (Niki choosing between saving Monica and mothering Micah; Noah Bennet lying to protect Claire) before deploying Fishkin's three 'explosion' objections: too many endangered people, too many capable heroes, and too many repeated rescues dilute obligation. He concludes the heroes went beyond the call of duty in saving New York, so saving the world could legitimately be their paid job, like lifeguards or soldiers.
- ch. 11 "NOTES" **·** Michael Sandel — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Utilitarianism — Robichaud evaluates the morally gray Company: its goal of mastering the evolutionary forces behind powers is tested against eugenics worries, the 'playing God' objection that natural processes have intrinsic value, and Sandel's Case against Perfection (that mastery breeds inflated responsibility, eroded community, and rigidity), each of which he finds inconclusive or outweighed by lives saved from people like Ted Sprague. He then turns to blame for the bag-and-tag program, applying Dennis Thompson's criterion to hold the founders plus hands-on agents like Bennet and the Haitian responsible while excusing accountants like Malsky. Finally he sets conditions for when an insider such as Bennet or Mohinder may blow the whistle, ruling out Bennet's murderous take-down-from-within scheme.
- ch. 12 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — Southworth defends Tim Kring against comic fans' plagiarism charges (Heroes versus X-Men's Rogue and Wolverine, Rising Stars' identical plot), arguing plagiarism requires intending to pass off known others' ideas as one's own, which Kring's ignorance of the genre (he asked 'Who's Magneto?') rules out, even accidentally. But he then convicts Kring of failing his epistemic responsibility, applying Montmarquet's virtues: Kring lacked epistemic responsibility (not reading the Rising Stars Lindelof gave him), impartiality (deliberately refusing to learn the genre), and sobriety (pressing on despite Loeb's warning that everything he pitched had been done). Parallel cases from the show include Mohinder's early dismissal of his father's research and Niki's obligation to learn about Jessica.
- ch. 13 "PART TWO - SUPERMEN, SAMURAI, AND INVISIBLE MEN" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "NOTES" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Eternal Recurrence; Amor Fati (love of fate); Fate and Foreknowledge; The Meaning of Life — Shores reads Volume 1 through Nietzsche's eternal recurrence as a self-test of life-affirmation: Adam sealed in a coffin embodies recurrence in its 'most terrible form,' Future Hiro's string map dramatizes Nietzsche's claim that every action is indissolubly tied to all others, and Linderman's speech divorcing happiness from meaning is answered by Nietzsche's demand to crave nothing more fervently than the eternal return. The chapter stresses where the show diverges: Claire insists 'the future is not written in stone' and the heroes rewrite destiny, while Arthur Petrelli ironically holds a copy of Thus Spoke Zarathustra while contradicting it. Hiro's failed attempts to save Charlie and his father (Kaito's 'we cannot play God') frame the amor fati question of whether free will means changing events or willing them eternally.
- ch. 15 "NOTES" **·** Carl Jung — Archetypes; The Collective Unconscious; Zen Buddhism — Baldwin argues the popular view of Bushido as a moral code (Nitobe, Star Wars, Kurosawa, Campbell's Jungian hero archetype) is historically wrong: by the Hagakure, Bushido is a death-ready 'mode of action' so morally neutral that even villains like Takezo Kensei or Sylar can practice it. He traces Hiro's evolution from comic-book and video-game hero-worship, through the Yaeko betrayal that turned Kensei into Adam Monroe, toward the fearless Future Hiro ideal of 'no-mind' readiness for death (echoing D.T. Suzuki's Zen reading). Because the Meiji reforms abolished the samurai class, Hiro can never literally be a samurai, only follow Bushido analogically, which lets him remain both superhero and samurai.
- ch. 16 "NOTES" **·** Plato — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Tripartite Soul; Justice in the Republic — Adams uses Plato's Ring of Gyges to ask whether superpowered people have reason to be moral, answering through the tripartite soul: Claire declining credit for the fire rescue shows well-governed thumos, her calculated revenge on Brody shows logistics ruling spirit, while Sylar's uncontrollable hunger for powers is textbook epithumetikon and intemperance that makes his life spin 'super' out of control. Cases like Micah's ATM thefts and Hiro's Vegas cheating (punished by a desert beating) illustrate that vice harms the agent's own soul, since justice is the soul's health. Claire's hospital apology to Brody is read through the Pagan Golden Rule and Sophocles' Ajax: enemies can become friends, so virtue, not mere conformity, is 'only natural' for heroes and ordinary people alike.
- ch. 17 "PART THREE - METAPHYSICS, REGULAR PHYSICS, AND HEROIC TIME TRAVEL" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "NOTES" **·** Aristotle, Boethius — Fate and Foreknowledge; Free Will vs Determinism — Johnson asks whether Isaac's paintings and Hiro's visions make the future fated, running Aristotle's sea-battle argument: if propositions about the future are already true (by bivalence and the correspondence theory of a 'written timeline'), then Sylar's exploding New York is inevitable and no one is free. He extends the problem to infallible foreknowledge via Future Hiro as a stand-in for God, considers and rejects Boethius's timeless-knowledge solution, and uses open theism plus a probability calculation about Ando's waffles order to show probabilistic foreknowledge is useless. He concludes most of Isaac's 'prophecies' are really self-fulfilling, as when Noah's efforts to dodge the eighth painting cause his shooting, and that the Heroes universe leaves room to deny fate.
- ch. 19 "NOTES" **·** David Lewis — The Metaphysics of Time Travel; Possible Worlds — Luck argues backward time travel is nearly useless for a hero: after the grandfather paradox (Hiro hearing of Kaito's murder only because Kaito died), he shows Lewis's linear time travel makes Hiro necessarily fail to save Kaito, Charlie, or Isaac, yet cannot explain Hiro meeting Ando two days after a five-week absence or New York being spared. Branching time travel, where each trip into the past spawns an alternate universe, fits Volume 1 coherently by building a Heroes multiverse of Hiro-1 through Hiro-4 and explaining Future Hiro's subway appearance as a 'brute fact' copied into branching universes. The price of coherence is that Hiro only ever saves other universes' New Yorks, never his own.
- ch. 20 "NOTES" **·** Thomas Aquinas — The Metaphysics of Time Travel — Faraci asks why Hiro may prevent the nuclear future ('save the cheerleader') but comes to agree it would be wrong to save Kaito from the past ('we cannot play God'). Using thought experiments (a variant where Hiro learns of his father's death in the future) he isolates the past/future asymmetry as the candidate morally relevant factor, then tests justifications: Aquinas's natural law fails because observing the past would be equally 'unnatural,' and a duty to keep the timeline intact would also forbid changing the future, which is just as 'settled' to a time traveler. Since every theory of where Hiro's 'present' lies fails or collapses into subjectivity, he concludes there is no good reason to treat altering the past differently from altering the future, while demonstrating how normative ethics evaluates moral intuitions.
- ch. 21 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — Tests the science behind the powers: Claire's and Adam Monroe's rapid healing is deemed barely possible (retained telomeres, error-free cell replication) but far too fast to be realistic, while Nathan's flight survives neither rocket-propellant nor magnetic-levitation explanations, leaving undirected manipulation of gravity as the only option. Hiro's teleportation and time travel are analyzed with Minkowski diagrams, worldlines, and light cones — his rescue of Claire from Sylar and Elle in 'The Eclipse: Part II' puts his broken worldline outside every light cone — and wormholes, quantum entanglement, and quantum tunneling are each rejected as workable mechanisms. The chapter then shows Darwinian evolution cannot produce Dr. Suresh's 'evolutionary jump': even Adam Monroe fathering children across the globe for four hundred years could not spread and diversify a power gene that fast, concluding that only design (God, aliens, or destiny) could explain the show's powers.
- ch. 22 "NOTES" **·** Thomas Kuhn, William of Ockham — Paradigm Shifts; Ockham's Razor; Falsificationism — Asks how to tell genuine science from pseudoscience using Chandra Suresh's rejected Activating Evolution theory, Sheldrake's morphic fields, alchemy, and Behe's intelligent design. Kuhn's picture of normal science, anomalies, and revolutions explains why Suresh would need a full-blown crisis in evolutionary theory — such as people actually flying — before the community accepts him, and why time rather than mockery settles a theory's fate. Ockham's razor condemns the unnecessary organizing principles in both Sheldrake's and Suresh's theories, and intelligent design is exposed as bad method because, like creationism, it was devised to protect a prior commitment from disconfirming fossil and cosmological evidence.
- ch. 23 "PART FOUR - THE MINDS OF HEROES" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "NOTE" **·** John Locke, Thomas Reid, Derek Parfit, Bernard Williams — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Ship of Theseus; Teleporter Paradox (personal fission) — Asks whether amnesia preserves numerical identity: is the Peter who wakes in the shipping crate still Volume-1 Peter, are Niki and Jessica one person, and is the Sylar whom Matt rewrites with Nathan's memories now Nathan? The chapter builds Locke's memory criterion, breaks it with Reid's transitivity objection run through Mohinder's timeline in 'Five Years Gone,' repairs it with Grice's and Perry's memory-ancestor chains, then destroys the repair with Arthur Petrelli's wipe of Hiro (Forgetful Hiro vs. Stabby Hiro), Bernard Williams's torture thought experiment, Parfit's psychological spectrum, and the graphic novel's branching self-copier Evan. It concludes that no memory criterion can define personal identity, yet memory still matters practically: Claire must act as if she persists over time to keep her promise to Zach and to bother finishing the manatee assignment.
- ch. 25 "NOTES" **·** René Descartes, David Chalmers — Philosophical Zombies; The Cogito; Methodological Doubt (evil demon) — Distinguishes Matt Parkman's supernatural telepathy from the 'mundane mind reading' everyone performs, then argues even Matt's power cannot solve the problem of other minds: the suicidal woman's thoughts he hears in the bar ('One Giant Leap') could be part of a Maury-Parkman-style illusion, and a brain-to-brain link could never rule out that others are Chalmers-style philosophical zombies. After grounding the skepticism in Descartes' cogito and method of doubt, it defends our everyday attribution of minds by analogy (Audrey Hanson reading Claire's insincerity) on pragmatic grounds, and maps Matt's progression from hearing thoughts to nightmare manipulation and compulsion onto the four adaptive functions philosophers assign to mind reading: interpretation, prediction, coordination, and influence. It closes by showing why neuroimaging-based technological mind reading remains a pipe dream, since brain recordings are quantitative syntax without semantics.
- ch. 26 "NOTES" **·** David Hume, Adam Smith — The Impartial Spectator; Reason Is the Slave of the Passions — Takes Claude's label of Peter as an 'empath' seriously and tests three accounts of empathy against his power: Hume's emotional resonance (rejected because Peter keeps powers after the donor leaves), Scheler's absorption into another's identity, and Hume's conversion of ideas into vivid impressions, settling on the mirror-neuron thought that Peter literally replicates other people's brain structures — which explains why he copies Isaac's painting skill and even Sylar's anger in 'Kindred.' It then asks whether empathy makes you moral and answers no — Elle enjoys Peter's pain and Mohinder feels his experimental subjects' terror — arguing instead that empathy needs a filter: Hume's general point of view and Adam Smith's impartial spectator, illustrated by Hiro's imagined Kensei ideal and by Nathan serving as Peter's emotional filter until his death leaves Peter without one.
- ch. 27 "PART FIVE - VILLAINS, FAMILY, AND LYING" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "NOTES" **·** Carol Gilligan, Socrates, Aristotle, Friedrich Nietzsche, Augustine of Hippo — The Ethics of Care; The Übermensch; The Golden Mean; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know) — Reads the show's eclipse icon against Plato's sun of the good — goodness has become hard to discern — and tests four theories of evil on the characters: Socrates' claim that no one errs knowingly is half-confirmed since villains like Angela and Nathan always take themselves to be doing right; Aristotle's akrasia and vice as an excess or deficiency of disposition fit Sylar's blood-scrawled 'Forgive me' apartment and his uncontrolled hunger; and Nietzsche's Übermensch and pre-Christian master morality raise the uncomfortable question of why Sylar preying on inferiors is not heroic like Achilles. The chapter's endorsed answer is Gilligan's ethics of care: the heroes are good because they care — Matt for Molly and Daphne, Bennet for Claire — while evil is the absence of care, though Bennet's authoritarian, family-only care shows that care itself must be both widened and restrained.
- ch. 29 "NOTES" **·** Confucius, Socrates — The Ethics of Care; Xiao (filial piety) — Explains the seemingly incomprehensible things Heroes characters do for family by sorting their motives into three theories of family obligation: universal justice (framed by Socrates confronting Euthyphro for prosecuting his own father), care ethics' partiality to loved ones, and Confucian role-based duty with its asymmetrical parent–child, husband–wife, and sibling obligations. Noah Bennet turns out to be Confucian in practice ('I'm your father, Claire Bear') despite his universalist Company rhetoric, Hiro defies Kaito's Confucianism for the greater good while Kaito still teaches him the sword because that is a father's role, Niki and Janice act from care while D.L. and Micah act from roles, Mohinder's duty is Confucian sonship to his dead father, and the Haitian kills his own brother Baron precisely because stopping him is his brotherly role.
- ch. 30 "NOTES" **·** Immanuel Kant, Plato, Niccolò Machiavelli — The Categorical Imperative; The Noble Lie; Utilitarianism; The Ends Justify the Means — First defines lying — distinguishing it from mere error (Angela believing Peter dead), from the Haitian's non-verbal memory-wipe deception, and from Hiro's silent staging of Ando's fake death — then tests moral theories against the show's liars. Kant's categorical imperative bans all lying but is abandoned when Sylar demands a friend's hiding place; utilitarianism vindicates Victoria Pratt's lie about the virus vial yet also licenses Linderman's .07% explosion and Plato's noble lie of the earthborn metals, which the chapter rejects. Machiavelli's advice that princes may lie to keep power is played out by 'President Sylar' in 'Five Years Gone,' and the chapter finally endorses Sissela Bok's presumption against lying — a public-reasoning procedure modeled by Bob Bishop and Mohinder before Monica's virus injection.
- ch. 31 "CONTRIBUTORS" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "CHANDRA SURESH’S LIST" *(front)*
- ch. 33 "INDEX" *(notes)*

## Hip-Hop and Philosophy - Rhyme 2 Reason

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Hip-Hop and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Epigraph" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Popular Culture and Philosophy™" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Foreword" **·** Socrates, Plato, William James — Practical Wisdom (phronesis); The Unexamined Life — Cornel West argues that Plato's dialogues invented philosophy as a Socratic genre of cross-examination and bold frank speech (parrhesia) about ordinary life, and that hip-hop music - like the spirituals, blues, and jazz - enacts that same parrhesia against conventional morality and entrenched power. He claims reading hip hop philosophically demands metaphilosophical subtlety, showing how paideia, phronesis, and parrhesia operate in the work of cultural producers under free-market fundamentalism and authoritarianism. Because the racial dimension of hip hop is inescapable, he says philosophers must break their discipline's silence on race and music (neglected figures like Du Bois and Alain Locke alongside Aristotle, Kant, and Rorty), closing with Cicero's Socrates who turned philosophy from the heavens to earth and William James's pragmatism that took it from the academy to the streets.
- ch. 8 "Shout Outs" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "From Rhyme to Reason This Shit Ain’t Easy" **·** Socrates, Plato — Socratic Method (elenchus); The Unexamined Life; The Philosopher King — The editors' introduction argues that hip hop and philosophy are both street-born examinations of life in pursuit of wisdom: Socrates's elenchus is likened to MC lyrical battles and call-and-response (his interrogations compared to Rakim's technique, his execution for corrupting the youth to the fate of a controversial rapper), while Plato's philosopher-king parallels Kurtis Blow and Run-D.M.C. claiming thrones. After a Bronx hip-hop history sketch (Kool Herc, BDP's "The Bridge is Over"), they frame the volume around hip hop's suppressed fifth element of knowledge and preview its pairings: Aquinas and Rakim on God, Plato and Lauryn Hill on love, Sartre and Lil' Kim's gaze, Kant and Ice Cube on punishment, Hegel and Nas, Hobbes and Dead Prez, Mill and Chappelle on epithets. Record companies don't own hip hop and universities don't own philosophy, they conclude - both belong to whoever understands, loves, and respects them.
- ch. 10 "Disk 1 - Da Mysteries God, Love, and Knowledge" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "Ain’t No Mystery" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Socrates, René Descartes — Framed by Rakim's "The Mystery" and Five-Percenter knowledge-of-self, Darby asks who God is and attacks divine omnipotence with the "paradox of the blunt" and "paradox of the glock" - can God roll a blunt so strong even he can't hit it? - then defends Aquinas's Summa Theologica restriction of God's power to the logically possible, against Descartes's claim that God can do even the impossible. He presses further with Tupac's jailbreak paradox from a Vibe interview ("If you God, then pop the fuckin' gate") and Aquinas's paradox of sin, resolving them via Samuel Clarke's natural/moral contradiction distinction and Socrates's Crito arguments for why a perfectly good person refuses escape and accepts the state's verdict.
- ch. 12 "The Mysteries of Love Revealed" **·** Plato — Platonic Love (the ladder of love); Philosophy of Love — Shelby stages Plato's Symposium as a cipher of raps on love to answer hip-hop "love haters" like Jay-Z ("I got ninety-nine problems but a bitch ain't one"): Pausanias's vulgar versus spiritual love, Aristophanes's myth of split halves read through Method Man and Mary J. Blige's "You're All I Need" and Biggie's "Me & My Bitch", and Socrates's ladder from beautiful bodies to Beauty itself, with Lauryn Hill's "Zion" as birth of beautiful offspring. He objects that Socrates makes the beloved a replaceable means to the good, and argues that Alcibiades's drunken confession supplies what is missing - love's possession, exclusivity, reciprocity, and drama (The Roots: "lies come in, that's where the drama begins") - concluding that love ain't just 'bout da booty but demands a vulnerability the love hater rightly fears.
- ch. 13 "Proper Use of the Mind" **·** René Descartes — Green reads Del tha Funkee Homosapien's line on Gorillaz's "Clint Eastwood" ("you don't see with your eye, you perceive with your mind") alongside Descartes's Meditations window passage - judging men in the square from hats and coats - to argue against the sense-data picture that perception is mind-guided, interest-driven interpretation rather than passive reception. He then deploys the "new" adaptive unconscious (the cocktail party effect, the love-on-the-bridge misattribution of arousal, unconscious racism in housing audits, lottery winners no happier - "mo money, mo problems") to show we are strangers to ourselves, so the proper use of the mind means standing back from one's patterns, as 2Pac's "Letter 2 My Unborn" and KRS-One's "The Mind" urge.
- ch. 14 "Disk 2 - What’s Beef Ruminations on Violence" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "Hip Hop and “This Makin’ Dollars Shit”" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, W.E.B. Du Bois, Karl Marx — Struggle for Recognition; Master–Slave Dialectic; Class Struggle; The Dialectic — Pittman argues the rap battle - UTFO's "Roxanne, Roxanne" answered by Roxanne Shanté's "Roxanne's Revenge", Run-D.M.C.'s "Sucker MC's", Jay-Z's "Takeover" versus Nas's "Ether" - is hip hop's enactment of Hegel's master-slave struggle for recognition, where self-consciousness proves itself only through an all-or-nothing contest ending in domination rather than destruction. Gangsta rap (N.W.A's "Gangsta Gangsta", Ice Cube's fantasy raid on the suburbs) radicalizes the battle into a collective demand that America recognize black urban conditions, and the chapter turns to Du Bois's race-centered and Marx's class-centered revisions of Hegel's history to read "this makin' dollars shit" - Wu-Tang's "C.R.E.A.M.", Talib Kweli on capitalism and crack, Mos Def's "The Rape Over" - as the dialectic's capture of the cipher by corporate beef.
- ch. 16 "(Positive) Violence of Self-Discipline" **·** Plato, Aristotle, John Dewey, William James — Pragmatism — Shusterman opens with Plato and Socrates' ancient quarrel against poetry - banning the artists from the Republic, much as rap is demonized today - and rejects Aristotle's catharsis strategy of quarantining art from life, applying pragmatist aesthetics (Dewey, James, and the Du Bois-Alain Locke lineage) to argue that rap's aesthetic violence of beat, scratch, and "Bring the Noise" is life-enhancing force, with reality rap's metaphysics of mix and flow against Plato's changeless Forms. Reading KRS-One's "Stop the Violence" movement born from Scott LaRock's killing, Guru's "Choice of Weapons" (a mic or a gun), and Jeru the Damaja's command to "kill in thyself", he concludes rap overcomes bad violence not by abolishing violence but by channeling it into lyrical battle and the positive violence of self-discipline.
- ch. 17 "Resistance Raps" **·** Thomas Hobbes — The Social Contract; The State of Nature — James reads hardcore and underground rap - N.W.A's "F*** Tha Police", Ice-T's "Cop Killer", Dead Prez's "Police State" sampling Omali Yeshitela, Tupac's "Dear Mama" with its Panther 21/Cointelpro footage, Michael Franti's "Crime to Be Broke in America" - through Hobbes's Leviathan to argue that for criminalized black communities the social contract never held, so policing is not aberrational protection but the foundation of a police state. She distinguishes rebellion from revolution, argues capital converts the underground's insurgence into marketable alienation, faults hardcore's misogyny and policing of women and queers as counter-revolutionary, and concludes that a Leviathan which is the law cannot cohabit with democracy.
- ch. 18 "Disk 3 - That’s How I’m Livin’ Authenticity, Blackness, and Sexuality" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "No Love (for Hip Hop), No problem" **·** Cornel West — Communitarianism; The Culture Industry — Taylor starts from his own lack of passion for hip hop as a black man and the "Eminem enigma" of the respected white rapper to ask whether hip hop belongs to black people by nature, using Dre's sellout dilemma in Brown Sugar to examine the communitarian language of roots, authenticity, and alienation. Following Cornel West's "racialist reasoning", he argues classical racialism is false, and that even critical racialism - races as socially constructed groups - plus the survival, duty, and cultural-preservation arguments (hip hop as "the black CNN", the ecosystem argument for reclaiming hip hop from the culture industry) fail to obligate blacks to embrace it, so no love for hip hop, no problem.
- ch. 20 "The Possibility of Authenticity The Life We Choose" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Judith Butler, bell hooks — The Look (hell is other people); Gender Performativity; Objectification; Authenticity — Gines reads hip hop's sexual imagery through Black feminist thought - Tupac's feud with C. Delores Tucker over "Wonder Why They Call U Bitch", Patricia Hill Collins's controlling images of the jezebel and welfare mother, bell hooks on the slave-rooted devaluation of Black womanhood, Nelly's "Tip Drill" video with a credit card swiped between a woman's buttocks, Jay-Z's "Big Pimpin'". Applying Sartre's keyhole gaze from Being and Nothingness and his Anti-Semite and Jew point about hierarchical societies, she argues the gaze under racism and sexism is non-reciprocal - women are seen without seeing - and that Lil' Kim's reversal of it reinforces rather than subverts the stereotypes; Butler's gender performativity then frames the closing Sartrean choice of authenticity or inauthenticity, Nas's "I Can" and "the life we choose."
- ch. 21 "The Hunger for More Than Serious Play" **·** Frantz Fanon, Friedrich Nietzsche, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, W.E.B. Du Bois — Black Skin, White Masks — Gordon argues hip hop is in practice black adolescent culture wrongly elevated to authentic blackness, and reads the problem through Fanon's Black Skin, White Masks: the white world's sociogenic construction le nègre denies black adulthood, freezing black people in perpetual childhood so there is no coherent black adult. Using Nietzsche's Birth of Tragedy account of health as creative confrontation with suffering, Rousseau's critique of modernity's underside, and Du Bois on being made "problem people", he separates hip hop's postmodern unruly form (off-key singing, beat over song) from its often conventional content (Dead Prez's nostalgic revolutionary imagery), and finds mature alternatives in the Philly sound - Me'Shell Ndegeocello, Angie Stone, the Roots - whose adult play knows death, the hunger for more than serious play.
- ch. 22 "Disk 4 - Word Up! Language, Meaning, and Ethics" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "Hip-Hop Lyrics No Black CNN and No Art of Storytelling" *(no index entry yet)* — Thompson pits Chuck D's "black CNN" communicative-message model of lyrical meaning - lyrics tell truths rooted in the lyricist's real life - against Young Buck's narrative-story model - lyrics tell as-if truths from adopted personae like Slim Shady, Snoop Dogg, and Lil' Kim. Working through Kelis's "Milkshake" via Jerrold Levinson's theory of what an artwork says, Eminem's "Stan", the Ja Rule versus 50 Cent beef, Mario Winans's "bull shoes", Lil' Flip's freestyling, and Lauryn Hill's Miseducation persona-shuttling, he argues no synthesis survives because intentions split across insider and outsider audiences: lyrical meaning teeters on personae that remain genuine extensions of lived lives, so hip hop is neither black CNN nor pure storytelling, and that undecidability is what keeps listeners involved.
- ch. 24 "Check Out Time" **·** John Stuart Mill, Immanuel Kant, David Hume — The Harm Principle; Kingdom of Ends; Utilitarianism — McGrath and Tilahun ask whether hip hop is bad for women, setting protest and sex-positive tracks (Tupac's "Brenda's Got a Baby", Missy's "One Minute Man", Queen Latifah's "Ladies First", Ms. Dynamite) against endorsements of violence (N.W.A's "One Less Bitch", Snoop's "Can You Control Yo Ho?", 50 Cent's "P.I.M.P"). They apply Mill's harm principle - offend yes, harm no - Hume's counterfactual analysis of causation (the Derek Fisher shot case) to show that whether lyrics cause violence is an unsettled empirical question, Austin and Rae Langton's speech-act theory to argue rappers' cultural authority may let pimp lyrics subordinate women in a way pornography cannot, and Kant's kingdom of ends to charge misogynist lyrics with treating women as mere means.
- ch. 25 "Final Skit Paris’s Field Nigga Boogie" **·** John Stuart Mill — The Harm Principle — Corlett evaluates the ethics of "bitch" and "nigga" in hip hop through Dave Chappelle's comedy, using the use-mention distinction and speaker intention to argue Chappelle's gender-neutral, affectionate, equivocating uses ("now that's a nigger who's one crazy nigger", the blind Black Klansman of "Frontline: Black KKK", "Reparations 2003") are racial rather than racist language. Invoking Mill's harm principle - words should be legally barred only when they harm, as with the KKK cross-burning example, not merely offend - he defends in-group reclamation, then extends the analysis to Paris's "Field Nigga Boogie" and "AWOL", whose epithets carry a progressive, Malcolm X-style challenge to unjust wars and state evil.
- ch. 26 "Disk 5 - Fight the Power Political Philosophy’n the Hood" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "Post-Civil Rights Music" **·** John Locke, Charles W. Mills, Cornel West — The Social Contract; The Racial Contract; Natural Rights — Lawson argues political rap - Grandmaster Flash's "The Message", Public Enemy's "911 Is a Joke", Tupac's "Trapped", Dead Prez's "They Schools", Naughty By Nature, Ice Cube's "I Wanna Kill Sam" - challenges liberal political philosophy by claiming the social contract between blacks and the United States is broken: using Locke's Second Treatise account of state protection of life and property, he contends the state's failure of economic and physical protection voids blacks' political obligations. Drawing on Cornel West's diagnosis of black nihilism and Charles Mills's racial contract - a contract constructed for white supremacy - he reads conscious hip hop as post-civil-rights music continuing the black freedom struggle in a new voice, with KRS-One asking "Who protects us from you?"
- ch. 28 "Reality versus Revolution" **·** Thomas Hobbes, John Rawls — The State of Nature; The Veil of Ignorance; Communitarianism; The Social Contract — McPherson argues political rap has never been politically revolutionary, sorting it into three schools: the amoral "gangsta Hobbes" ethic (Mobb Deep's "Survival of the Fittest", 50 Cent's "get rich or die tryin'") that sees the hood as Hobbes's state of nature where no social contract with blacks ever existed; Afrocentric communitarianism, split between nationalist separatists (Brand Nubian, X-Clan, Dead Prez) and the more cosmopolitan Native Tongues (De La Soul, A Tribe Called Quest); and a radical liberalism closer to Rawls than its adherents admit. Against Chuck D's "What's a smilin' face when the whole state's racist?", he argues Rawls's original position, veil of ignorance, and difference principle - applied with real-world knowledge of racism - support rather than oppose Public Enemy's demands for racial justice.
- ch. 29 "Prison Ghetto" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham, Michel Foucault — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Discipline and Docile Bodies — Kelly takes gangsta rap's payback attitude (50 Cent, Nas, Dr. Dre, OutKast) as the starting point for the philosophy of punishment: rappers doubt retribution because police, courts, and prisons lack legitimate authority over them - Public Enemy's "Black Steel in the Hour of Chaos" rejects the government's letter. Weighing Kantian and Nozickian retributivism against Bentham's consequentialist rationales, she argues desert, proportionality, and authority all fail in a racist system - racial profiling, the crack-versus-powder sentencing disparity, Wacquant's prison-ghetto thesis, Goodie Mob's "Cell Therapy", Dead Prez's "Behind Enemy Lines", KRS-One's officer-overseer analogy - leaving incapacitation as racialized social control rather than justice.
- ch. 30 "Not Just a Black Thing" **·** John Locke — Natural Rights — Roberts uses hip hop's reparations consciousness - Chuck D's "Kill Em Live" and "Who Stole the Soul?", KRS-One's "Sound of Da Police" overseer-officer analogy, Ice Cube's "F*** Tha Police", the Amadou Diallo shooting and its mural, Spike Lee's Do the Right Thing, Chappelle's "Reparations 2003" - to introduce rectificatory justice: punishment of perpetrators, compensation for victims' unjust losses, and apology that reaffirms victims' moral standing. Arguing that ideal theory reflects the experience of the privileged and neglects injustice, and that "forty acres and a mule" was never rectification (hence H.R. 40), he closes with Ice-T's "Straight Up Nigga" and the Hawaiian Kingdom apology to show rectifying injustice is "not just a black thing".
- ch. 31 "After . . . Word!" **·** Michel Foucault, Cornel West — Power/Knowledge; Cultural Hegemony; The Public Sphere — Marcyliena Morgan's afterword traces hip-hop battling to the philosophical houses and crews of Saturday kung-fu cinema (Wu-Tang's obsession included), reading hip hop's South Bronx birth through Victor Turner's ritual threshold state and defining "flow" as conscious movement within chaos to create balance and collective identity. The mantra represent-recognize-come correct, shout outs as a system of indexicality, and hip hop's counterpublic sphere function, she argues, to expose hegemony and contest Foucault's technologies of power - answering Cornel West's "ignoble paradox of modernity" - so that hip hop battles through the theoretical houses of Foucault, Bakhtin, Butler, Gramsci, and Habermas, where "there is no such thing as a dead philosopher - just one that has not been resurrected yet."
- ch. 32 "Beats & Rhymes!" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "The Crew" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "The Hip-Hop Head Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "ALSO FROM OPEN COURT" *(front)*
- ch. 36 "Hip-Hop and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 37 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Hitchcock and Philosophy - Dial M for Metaphysics

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Hitchcock and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Popular Culture and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Previews and Opening Credits" **·** Existentialism; Authenticity — The editors' introduction argues that Hitchcock is a profound artist whose classic films are organized around ideas, citing Chabrol and Rohmer's claim that each film rests on a 'formal postulate,' so that philosophical issues arise directly from his cinematic motifs. It previews four features of Hitchcock's vision the volume will explore: an anti-utopian outlook on a fallen world perpetually at risk of chaos, an existentialist affirmation of self-definition through choice and commitment, a construal of spectatorship as flight from commitment and authenticity, and an anticipation of later thinking on the social roles of women. It closes with acknowledgments to the contributors, series editor Bill Irwin, and Open Court.
- ch. 7 "I - The Mystery of Everyday Life" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Violence Against Utopia" **·** Plato — Utopia and Dystopia; The Ethics of Political Violence — Drumin reads Sabotage as Hitchcock's anti-utopian statement: against perfectible societies imagined from Plato's Republic to Skinner, Freud and Dostoyevsky show that the psyche's attraction to chaos cannot be planned or conditioned away. In four scenes, the aquarium meeting where 'monsters from the Id' watch over Verloc's bomb deal, the bird shop where explosives sit among a child's toys, the conspiracy hatched behind a movie screen whose audience laughs at on-screen chaos, and Stevie's death on the bus, the film shows a quiet family man rationalizing mass murder by 'abstracting' its victims into a mere list of names. Hitchcock punishes the audience too, implicating its own safe enjoyment of represented violence, and concludes that only constant individual vigilance, never social engineering, keeps the potential for chaos in check.
- ch. 9 "Plato’s Cave Again" **·** Plato, Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, William James — Allegory of the Cave; The Categorical Imperative; Utilitarianism; The Ethics of Care — Curran uses Shadow of a Doubt to teach epistemology: Charlie's claim to 'know' her uncle distinguishes knowledge by acquaintance from propositional knowledge as justified true belief, with the permanently stuck 'Til Two clock showing a true belief that is not knowledge because its warrant is unreliable. Coherence (Hegel, Blanshard), pragmatist (James, Dewey), and correspondence theories of truth are tested against the townspeople's false but convenient beliefs about Uncle Charlie, and only correspondence survives. Kant's absolute duty not to lie is weighed against Mill's act and rule utilitarianism for the detectives' deceptions, and a feminist care-ethics perspective assesses Charlie's shielding her mother from the truth. Plato's cave frames the cinema itself: film can deceive like shadows on a wall, yet the detectives' unused photograph shows the camera can also reveal truth, so movies help us face facts, at the price, as Charlie pays, of lost innocence.
- ch. 10 "Rupert’s Response and Responsibility" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; Will to Power; The Death of God — Biderman and Jacobowitz read Rope through Nietzsche: Brandon and Philip strangle their classmate David as would-be Nietzschean supermen, acting on mentor Rupert Cadell's cocktail-party theory that murder can be an art for a superior few. The chapter explains the will to power, the Ubermensch, and the death of God, then argues Brandon is no superman: he kills from weakness and a bully's need for an audience, mistaking crass destruction for the refined self-creation Nietzsche demanded, just as the fascist appropriation of Nietzsche fails. Rupert's horrified final speech, renouncing 'superior or inferior beings' and insisting each of us is 'a separate human being,' raises whether a teacher can disavow responsibility when students act out his provocative ideas: ideas have consequences, and the only true test of a philosophy is whether we can live by it.
- ch. 11 "II - Horrors Without End" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "A Proper Answer" **·** David Hume, Augustine of Hippo, Alvin Plantinga — The Problem of Evil — Tallon takes Psycho as a case study in the problem of evil: after Hume's Epicurean challenge, Marion's shower murder and Norman's shattered psyche exemplify Marilyn McCord Adams's 'horrendous evils,' whose grotesque disproportion between any crime and its fate threatens to destroy the positive meaning of a life. The free will defense (Plantinga, Augustine) fails because Marion's death is bad luck rather than punished choice, and Hick's soul-making theodicy fails because evil here shatters character rather than building it. Carroll's definition of monsters is revised so that monsters are beings of severe disorder and disproportion, which Norman, Dracula, and Hitler all display, and Swinburne's Principle of Honesty offers the closest answer: God allows horrors because systematically preventing them would deceive us about the truth that evil is the privation of proper order, inherently grotesque and disordered.
- ch. 13 "To Nest or Not to Nest" **·** Plato, Socrates, Martha Nussbaum — Platonic Love (the ladder of love); Theory of Forms; Philosophy of Love — Halwani and Jones read The Birds as Hitchcock's warning about romantic love: love compromises autonomy through joint decision-making, emotional dependence, and exposure to contingencies like jealousy, betrayal, sickness, and death. Plato's Symposium stages the alternatives, Socrates's ladder of love culminating in the eternal Form of Beauty versus Alcibiades's speech for the painful, contingent love of a unique person, and Nussbaum's reading underscores that these two visions of romantic love are mutually exclusive. In the film, bird attacks strike whenever Melanie, Mitch, Annie, or Lydia treat love as a game or possession, while the caged lovebirds, the only birds that never attack, show that only love sealed in a 'gilded cage' could be cost-free; lovers must instead face love's price wisely, maturely, and courageously.
- ch. 14 "“Your Heavenly Father Feeds Them”" **·** Aristotle, René Descartes, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Thomas Hobbes, Plato, Diogenes the Cynic — Aristotle on the Soul; Man as Political Animal; The State of Nature; Free Will vs Determinism — Calef takes the Tides restaurant scene ('Who are you? What are you?') and Diogenes's plucked rooster versus Plato's 'featherless biped' as a springboard for testing every classic criterion of humanity against the film. Aristotle's rational soul fails because Mrs. Bundy's 'brain pans' reasoning is wrong and some humans cannot reason; Descartes's language test fails because the myna bird can learn to talk while horrified humans go mute; Rousseau's free will fails because the birds behave unpredictably and resist instinct while humans are trapped, caged, and herd-like; Aristotle's political animal and Hobbes's social contract fail because the town's hoods and lawbreakers contrast with birds that flock together across species. Even the appeal to God's favor turns dark, as the drunk quotes scripture while Revelation's birds gather for a supper of human flesh, leaving the film a mirror in which we must examine ourselves.
- ch. 15 "Bad Faith" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Martin Buber, Martin Heidegger — Bad Faith; I–Thou Relation; Authenticity — Lee reads the 'Breakdown' episode of Alfred Hitchcock Presents existentially: when the executive Callou coldly fires Humka and hangs up on his despair, he treats a person as a thing, Buber's 'I-It' pseudo-listening where Humka sought an 'I-Thou' dialogue with the godlike source of authority in his world. Heidegger's account of inauthenticity explains the mask of rational control behind which Callou hides, and Sartre's bad faith names the self-deception by which he pretends his godlike management of others is justifiable. Paralyzed and taken for a corpse after a car crash, Callou can be saved only when rational strategy fails and he finally weeps: the single tear reveals his caring nature, Jessie addresses him person-to-person, and, vulnerable before God and others, he becomes an authentic self, Hitchcock's pointed moral for bosses who give an employee the sack.
- ch. 16 "III - The Reeling Mind" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Knots" **·** Aristotle, Plato — Philosophy of Love — Carroll argues that Vertigo rewards repeated viewing not because of its wildly improbable crime plot but because its double love story is a contribution to the philosophy of love: citing Aristotle's Poetics on drama as a 'concrete universal,' he treats Scottie and Judy as ideal character types, the projective fantasist and the enabler, whose sequential romances invite viewers to compare them and so discover love's inherent fault line, fantasy. Fantasy legitimately fuels infatuation, courtship, idealization, and the imagined future lovers share, but Scottie's remodeling of Judy (the grey suit, the hair, the necklace) becomes blinding projection that denies the beloved's irreplaceable particularity despite her plea to be loved 'just the way I am.' Judy's need to be loved makes her complicit in her own erasure, a Laingian 'knot' in which acting on her motive defeats it, while the film maieutically draws this analysis from the viewer, as Socrates drew geometry from the slave in the Meno.
- ch. 18 "Vertigo and Epistemological Vertigo" **·** David Hume, Aristotle, Thomas Kuhn, René Descartes, David Chalmers — Reason Is the Slave of the Passions; Paradigm Shifts; Philosophical Skepticism — Flory reads Vertigo as an epistemological parable: Scottie's 'hardheaded Scot' method, collecting facts and deducing from universal principles, fails three times (the step-stool 'cure' for his acrophobia, explaining away Madeleine's 'possession' with the horse statue, forcing Judy up the bell tower for confirmation), delivering an implicit critique of Vienna Circle positivism in the manner of Carnap and Ayer. Against Scottie, the chapter mobilizes Kuhn's theory-laden facts, Aristotle's point that human conduct resists codification by exceptionless laws, and Hume's 'slave of the passions,' with Descartes's automata and Chalmers's philosophical zombies framing the problem of other minds. Midge, who knows people emotionally as well as factually, models the alternative: philosophers who fear 'epistemological vertigo' should give up epistemological training wheels and accept that minds, especially emotions, escape law-like rules.
- ch. 19 "Identities, Selves, and Others" **·** John Locke, Derek Parfit, Jean-Paul Sartre, Aristotle — Personal Identity (memory theory); Personal Identity; Existence Precedes Essence — Patterson uses Thornhill's being mistaken for the fictitious George Kaplan to test theories of personal identity: the body theory fails a CIA thought experiment in which a Kaplan double is slowly rebuilt from Thornhill's own replaced cells; Locke's memory theory fails on amnesia scenarios and circularity, since one cannot have memories of a self unless one already has a self; and Parfit's psychological continuity survives, with Thornhill drifting along a 'Psychological Spectrum' toward 'one hundred percent Kaplan' as he plays the spy. The chapter then turns existentialist: with Sartre, no essential nature fixes who Thornhill is, so he is totally responsible for the self his choices create, from 'Expedient Exaggerations' to risking death on Mount Rushmore. Charles Taylor's claim that a self exists only in dialogue with a community explains why Eve and others literally help make Thornhill into the person he becomes.
- ch. 20 "Turning the Tables" **·** The Ethics of Care — Wartenberg argues that North by Northwest pits two defective ethical stances against each other: the Professor's 'zealous patriotism,' exposed as callous in the USIA scene where he lets the innocent Thornhill be killed as the Kaplan decoy and spends Eve in the line of danger ('War is hell, Mr. Thornhill, even when it's a cold one'), and Thornhill's 'ethical particularism,' akin to the feminist ethics of care, exposed as headstrong when love-driven rashness leaves both lovers dangling on Mount Rushmore and only the Professor's intervention saves them. Yet the ending's match cut from Rushmore to the train berth reveals Hitchcock's real McGuffin: the Professor fails less as a moralist than as a bad director whose staged deceptions misfire, and the film's true philosophical claim is self-reflexive, that only film has the power to reconcile patriotism and romantic love, an optimistic world existing only in the artifice of the camera.
- ch. 21 "IV - Hitchcock’s Ethical Dilemmas" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 22 "E Pluribus Unum" **·** Ethical Egoism; Democracy; The State of Nature — Auxier frames Hitchcock's Lifeboat against Garrett Hardin's 'lifeboat ethics' essay, arguing the lifeboat converts political questions into ethical ones by stranding the survivors outside law and politics ('We're on our own here. We can make our own law'). The survivors are ethical pluralists - Rittenhouse lives for money, Kovac for equality, Constance for fame, Joe for God, MacKenzie for care - and their clinging to private versions of the Good lets Nazi captain Willy, whose only principle is survival, manipulate them all. Using Maslow's hierarchy of needs, Auxier shows the boat descending to bare survival, yet argues the mob killing of Willy when he hoards water is moral outrage rather than survival-driven, and that the final rescue scene replays the opening dispute with nobody having grown - a vindication of pluralistic democracy capable of unified action.
- ch. 23 "Why Be Moral" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle, Thomas Hobbes, James Rachels, Plato — The Categorical Imperative; Eudaimonia (flourishing); Ethical Egoism; The Social Contract — Sanders uses Bruno Anthony's 'criss-cross' murder-swap proposal to Guy Haines in Strangers on a Train to pose the classic question 'Why be moral?' to the charming amoralist. He tests three answers: Kant's categorical imperative fails because Bruno's egoist maxim ('remove obstacles to your happiness') can apparently be universalized; Hobbesian contractarian 'morality pays' fails because Bruno free-rides on others' restraint; and the Rachels/Nelson/Thomas psychological-health answer assumes rather than proves that Bruno should care about comparable suffering. A thought experiment comparing the moral life with the Bruno-state from an impartial standpoint leads to Aristotle's eudaimonia: morality is constitutive of human flourishing, so Bruno enjoys only a counterfeit happiness.
- ch. 24 "Curtain Down" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Allegory of the Cave; Theory of Forms — Silberstein reads Rear Window as a self-reflexive film about film-watching: the shades rise like a curtain, the courtyard apartments play like frames on a filmstrip, and wheelchair-bound Jeff with his binoculars is the movie spectator - the courtyard is Plato's cave and we are the chained prisoners watching shadows (copies of copies of the forms). The chapter stages the Plato-versus-Aristotle debate over imitative art from Republic Book X and the Poetics, then argues the film vindicates both: Jeff's voyeurism breeds detachment and misjudgment (he misreads Lisa, Miss Torso, the composer and the newlyweds), yet the same watching awakens - the song 'Lisa' saves Miss Lonelyhearts from suicide, Thorwald is brought to justice, and Jeff and the audience leave the theater wiser about fantasy, boredom and their own lives.
- ch. 25 "Doing Things in Private" **·** Consequentialism; Utilitarianism; The Ends Justify the Means — Skoble asks exactly what Jeff does wrong in Rear Window and whether his illicit snooping can generate genuine duties. Against a purely consequentialist verdict ('it all worked out okay'), he argues that knowledge creates conditional obligations - given what he has seen of Thorwald, Jeff's intervention is obligatory rather than supererogatory, by 'ought implies can' - and he weighs autonomy-respecting limits on intervening in Miss Lonelyhearts' despair. He then analyzes privacy as a moral right grounded in reciprocal legitimate expectations, concluding that Jeff violated no such right since everything he watched was on public display, so the ends-justify-the-means charge mostly dissolves.
- ch. 26 "V - What’s It All About, Alfred" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "Lessons to Take Away" **·** The Watchmaker Argument (design) — Kinghorn asks what makes Hitchcock's trademark plot twists improbable, distinguishing physical, statistical and evidential probability and illustrating each with the fraying jacket seam in Saboteur, the sea of identical umbrellas in Foreign Correspondent, the porter uniforms in North by Northwest, and Manny Balestrero's uncanny double in The Wrong Man. The core of the chapter is a three-question method for assessing evidence - does the evidence fit the theory, does it fit rival theories too, how likely was the theory beforehand - worked out through Jeff and Doyle's debates over Thorwald's guilt in Rear Window, where Jeff turns out to be a methodologically sound amateur sleuth despite his bias against marriage. A closing lesson defends design-argument-style reasoning about God from a confused objection that demands statistics where only evidential probability is at issue.
- ch. 28 "Da-dum, Da-da-da-da Dum-da-dum" *(no index entry yet)* — Holt uses Hitchcock's cameo appearances - the Reduco newspaper ad in Lifeboat, the double-bass in Strangers on a Train, the wheelchair in Topaz - as a case study in theories of artistic interpretation. Joke and allusion cameos support Intentionalism, but the cameos began as a casting expediency in The Lodger and their significance arose before Hitchcock envisioned it, which troubles Intentionalism; the Where's-Waldo spotting game and the cameos' mundanity, which adds a touch of realism to the fictional world, give ground to New Criticism and a limited Subjectivism. He concludes that all three theories hold within their respective domains and that 'the' meaning of art is a false ideal - plural, constrained interpretation is the truth of the cameos.
- ch. 29 "Questioning Everything The Birds" **·** Aristotle, David Hume — Virtue Ethics (arete); Problem of Induction — The Deavels argue that Hitchcock's suspense is fundamentally epistemological. In the social world, heroines in love - Constance in Spellbound, Lina in Suspicion, Rose in The Wrong Man - must weigh mounting evidence of their beloveds' guilt against the Aristotelian premise that character is consistent because virtue and vice are habits: Constance rightly bets that J.B.'s gentleness with her proves he is no murderer, Lina reasonably concludes Johnnie may poison her, and Rose survives only by inventing a persecutory 'they'. With The Birds the question widens to nature itself: Mrs. Bundy's ornithology assumes past bird behavior predicts future behavior, and the film's terror is Hume's worry that causal order may be an unfounded projection, leaving Mitch to stake his family's escape on the humble observed pattern that attacks come in waves.
- ch. 30 "Marnie An Enigma to Herself" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, René Descartes — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Philosophical Skepticism — Yanal sets Descartes's two claims about mind - certainty about one's own thoughts (via the method of doubt and the deceiving-evil-genius possibility) and skepticism about other minds (hats and coats that may cover automata) - against Wittgenstein's counters, then reads Shadow of a Doubt and Marnie as Hitchcock's counterexamples. In Shadow of a Doubt the two Charlies, twins in blood and in the film's doubling motifs, are 'transparent' to each other - Young Charlie alone knows her uncle is the Merry Widow Murderer - refuting other-minds skepticism; in Marnie, repressed memory of killing her mother's sailor client leaves Marnie ignorant of her own mind until Mark's amateur therapy recovers it, refuting Cartesian self-certainty. Both films thus side with Wittgenstein: one person can know another's mind, and one can be an enigma to oneself.
- ch. 31 "Cast and Crew" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "ALSO FROM OPEN COURT" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Hitchcock and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Homeland and Philosophy - For Your Minds Only

- ch. 1 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Euthyphro Dilemma; The Problem of Evil; Faith and Reason — Robert Arp's introduction explains that Homeland's jihadist material (Nazir, Hamid, Brody, 9/11) drew him in and previews the book's philosophizing with a sample argument: whether morality needs God. He argues jihadists ground ethics in Allah's commands, but since faith defies rational proof, competing conceptions of God conflict, and scripture (Abraham and Isaac, the Flood, the Egyptian firstborn, the genocide commands in 1 Samuel and Hosea) has God commanding atrocities, either the terrorists' God is evil or morality is better grounded in reason without God. The dilemma he poses - a good God would not command 9/11, so the command conception or God's goodness must go - is the Euthyphro structure applied to terrorism.
- ch. 8 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory) — Jason Iuliano asks whether the Brody who lied to Helen Walker about Tom Walker's death is the same person as the one who beat Walker, and whether 2011 Brody is responsible for 2003 Brody's acts. He contrasts the biological criterion (same human animal), the psychological criterion of overlapping memories and intentions (so Brody remembering his family remains the same person), and the narrative identity theory Carrie invokes in 'Q&A' when she says Nazir pulled Brody apart and put him back together as someone else. A Carrie thought experiment about ECT outcomes and a Nazir mind-swap of Brody's and Walker's psychologies argue that only narrative coherence captures whom we should praise, blame, and anticipate surviving.
- ch. 10 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Christine Korsgaard, Søren Kierkegaard, Jean-Paul Sartre — Kantian Constructivism; Bad Faith; Existence Precedes Essence — Talia Morag reads Brody's colliding roles - Marine, congressman, father, Muslim, follower of Abu Nazir - and Carrie's unstable ones (CIA agent, bipolar patient, lover) through Korsgaard's notion of practical identities, whose contradictory obligations (kill and not kill) demand reconciliation. Following Kierkegaard's advice to rethink labels like 'Christian', Brody reinterprets his suicide tape as acting 'as a Marine' against domestic enemies - a rationalizing move the chapter treats as bordering on bad faith - while Dana's phone call in 'Marine One' reasserts the father identity and stops the bombing. Carrie's 'Q&A' re-description of Brody as a good man and future hero shows identities are constructed through choices, Sartre's point that we are not summaries of our past.
- ch. 11 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** John Locke — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory) — Laszlo Kajtar uses Brody's Complex PTSD - torture, forced killing of Walker, intrusive nightmares attacking Jessica - to test theories of personal identity. Animalism says Brody is the same biological organism before and after captivity, while the psychological approach, sourced in Locke's memory criterion, traces continuity of beliefs, desires, and intentions and finds it disrupted by trauma and Nazir's brainwashing, which filled the hole trauma left after Issa's death. Kajtar argues both miss the first-personal 'sense of self': like many PTSD sufferers, Brody feels he is not the person he was, and 'It's like she doesn't know who I actually am now' shows identity shattered from the inside.
- ch. 12 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Seneca, Peter Singer, Aristotle — Stoicism; Humanity as an End in Itself; Utilitarianism — John Karavitis evaluates four suicide events: Afsal Hamid's suicide in CIA custody, Aileen Morgan's cutting her throat with a lens, Brody's aborted suicide bombing, and Carrie's ECT as a 'suicide' of her old self. Kant's absolute ban on suicide (it denies your humanity) clashes with Seneca's Stoic counsel that a life of miserable captivity need not be continued, and with Singer's utilitarian permission of euthanasia that reduces suffering - under both, Hamid's and Aileen's suicides are defensible while Brody's emotionally driven bombing is not. Dana's plea that her father is needed recasts Aristotle's point that suicide wrongs the community, and Sloterdijk's analysis of rage turned on oneself frames Carrie's amoral self-destruction.
- ch. 14 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Philosophy of Love; Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — Brittany Lorenz and Adam Barkman apply the Greek four-loves framework (storge, eros, philia, agape) to Homeland's relationships. Terrorism breaks storge by destroying comfort in familiar places; broken eros appears in the Brody-Jessica-Mike-Carrie square and Mira's lover who loves 'making love' rather than her; philia falters because Brody hides his Islam from Jessica, and Abu Nazir's bond with Brody is Aristotle's friendship of utility, dissolving when the advantage ceases. Brody's agape for country is broken because fighting abroad violates his prior duties as husband and father, so the show offers only glimpses of loves that might be redeemed.
- ch. 15 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** John Locke — Free Will vs Determinism; Moral Luck; Consequentialism — Dan Burkett defends Brody against blame for flipping the suicide-vest switch in 'Marine One' by arguing he was not free. On the desire-satisfaction view Brody freely avenge Issa, but via Locke's locked-room thought experiment (Carrie contentedly 'freely' staying in a house she cannot leave) the chapter motivates the libertarian requirement that one could have done otherwise, which Nazir's years of torture and brainwashing may have removed. The malfunctioning vest then raises moral luck - Brody is no better a person because the bomb failed - and since Dana's plea restricts his options just as coercively, the best defense grants Brody freedom throughout, making the switch blameworthy and his free choice to come home genuinely redemptive.
- ch. 17 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault, Aristotle, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Jeremy Bentham — The Panopticon (surveillance); Virtue Ethics (arete); The General Will — April Marratto and Adam Barkman use Foucault's appropriation of Bentham's panopticon - surveillance whose mere threat disciplines - to read Carrie's illegal watches on the Brody house, the family's stay in the safe house, and Dana's discovery that speech about her father's Islam is policed. Against Foucault's claim that freedom is an illusion maintained by power, the chapter sets Aristotle's view that freedom is a self-directed will choosing rightly (Dana making amends for the hit-and-run) and Rousseau's social contract, under which citizens trade equal freedoms for equal protection. Both would endorse Saul forcing Brody's detox for the country's sake, concluding absolute freedom is neither possible nor desirable.
- ch. 18 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** John Locke — The Ethics of Political Violence — Roberto Sirvent and Ian Diorio argue, following William Cavanaugh's 'myth of religious violence', that the assumption that religion is uniquely irrational, absolutist, and divisive blinds Americans to their own fanaticism. Homeland's blind spots - Carrie suspecting the Muslim Galvez, Dana's classmate nuking Tehran, 'Infinite Justice' naming, Guantanamo torture flashed right after Carrie's 'We don't do that here' - show the religious/secular split is a construct (traced to Locke's privatizing of faith and Augustine's broad religio) that legitimizes state violence while deeming identical acts crazy when done by the religious. Nazir's 'Who is the terrorist?' drone question and 'We only begin as enemies because that's what others told us to be' seal the point.
- ch. 20 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli, Michael Walzer, Adam Smith, Immanuel Kant — The Ends Justify the Means; The Invisible Hand; Deontology; Consequentialism — Christophe Porot and Charles Taliaferro ask whether Homeland's pervasive hypocrisy and deceit can be justified, starting from Walden's cover-up of the drone strike and Brody's heroic persona hiding a terrorist plot. Machiavelli's Prince supplies the case that political relationships with allies (Saul's 'Us and Them' speech to Carrie) require hypocrisy, while the Smith-inspired 'honest politicking' model - trading openly on self-interest, as with Carrie blackmailing al Sahrani - fails because politics should secure the public good, though la Rochefoucauld's 'homage vice pays virtue' explains Saul extorting the judge into doing right. Weighing Kant's unconditional duty not to lie (Carrie's fatal lie to Lynne Reed) against consequentialism, the chapter lands on the Walzer Dirty Hands view: some acts stay wrong yet necessary, and honest agents accept the guilt.
- ch. 21 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Just War Theory; The Ethics of Political Violence — Jai Galliott argues Homeland's drone strike that killed Issa is not a one-off plot device but mirrors real US drone warfare that breeds blowback: strikes strengthen al-Qaeda's brand and radicalize the disaffected, from Brody to the Boston bombers. He analyzes the asymmetry problem - the weak side comingles with civilians, straining the just war principles of discrimination and proportionality, while drone operators living among US suburbs become contested targets - and rejects giving weaker states a 'wild card' of lowered ethical standards. His proposal is that the technologically superior side bind itself to stricter just war compliance and policing-like restraint, since foreseeable terrorist reciprocity can tip proportionality against the war itself.
- ch. 23 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; Utilitarianism; Just War Theory — Edwin Jacob contrasts the utilitarian calculus behind Walden's drone strike - Estes's eighty percent estimate, 'potential collateral damage falls within current matrix parameters', eighty-three children killed - with Kant's three formulations of the categorical imperative applied to that decision. On the universal-law formula Walden cannot will a world of strikes on one's own soil, on the humanity formula the schoolchildren are used merely as means, and the kingdom-of-ends formula condemns treating foreign lives as quantities in a matrix. The chapter adds that drones, like Brody's suicide vest, are asymmetric terror at a distance, and that blowback (Shahzad's Times Square attempt) shows the strategy fails even consequentialist test.
- ch. 24 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** René Descartes, David Hume, Charles Sanders Peirce, Bertrand Russell — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Philosophical Skepticism — Don Fallis frames Homeland as a world of real epistemic warfare - unlike Descartes' hypothetical malicious demon, terrorists and the CIA actively engineer false appearances (Brody the war hero, Carrie the support-group veteran, Saul's memory-card trick). He analyzes lying versus true-but-misleading statements, uses Russell's barber paradox to define double agents without contradiction, and shows how Carrie, Dana, and Lauder each detect an inconsistency (Yorkshire Gold tea, the locked bedroom, Walker's three missed shots). Following Hume on testimony and Peirce's abduction - inference to the best explanation - Dana concludes her father is a terrorist, and Coady's defense of conspiracy theories vindicates Lauder's Brody-Walker hypothesis.
- ch. 26 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** John Locke, John Stuart Mill, Immanuel Kant — Natural Rights; The Harm Principle; Respect for Persons; Utilitarianism — Christian Cotton asks whether Carrie's clandestine surveillance of the Brody home, though illegal, is morally wrong, distinguishing legality from morality. Locke's state of nature grounds natural rights to life, liberty, property - and by extension privacy - that government exists to protect and that no national-security goal automatically overrides; Mill's utilitarianism would justify violating one family's privacy to prevent an attack, but Mill's own harm principle and 'tyranny of the majority' caution blocks it when Carrie has only a hunch. Kant's respect for persons as ends in themselves condemns Saul's blackmail of Judge Turner as using a man merely as means, so the Right constrains pursuit of the Good.
- ch. 27 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Boethius — The Problem of Evil; The Consolation of Philosophy; Fate and Foreknowledge; Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society — Trip McCrossin reads Carrie's 'I missed something once before... everyone's not me' as the modern, secular problem of evil: as Susan Neiman argues, unreasonable suffering threatens reason itself, and the Rousseau-Voltaire split defines the responses. Carrie takes Rousseau's side - 'All is good leaving the hands of the author of things' degenerates in human hands, so evil is intelligible and redemption possible, the chance at redemption she offers Brody and even Nazir - against Voltaire's Candide-style tending of the garden. The finale deepens this with Boethius's Consolation: Carrie's 'I believe one of the reasons I was put on this Earth was for our paths to cross' fuses fate with providence, and she accepts Brody's execution as a redemptive end 'more so if he dies'.
- ch. 29 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, David Hume — Personal Identity; Bundle Theory of Self — Frank Scalambrino probes Carrie's 'personality paradox' through the 'You can't change your soul!' / 'Yes you can' exchange. Aristotle's Metaphysics offers one pole: only generation and corruption are substantial change, so Carrie persists essentially as human however her mania, job, and loves shift. Hume's rejoinder - sameness of kind is not given in sense experience but added by interpretation - opens the question of whether Carrie's identity is determined by intrinsic factors (love, soul, truth) or extrinsic ones (the CIA's rules, religious affiliation), with Saul representing extrinsic determination and Carrie privileging the intrinsic, while Brody's conversion shows a soul can change and re-order which factors govern a self.
- ch. 30 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, Aristotle — Deontology; Utilitarianism; Virtue Ethics — Robert Arp uses Saul's interrogation of Afsal Hamid (sleep-deprivation via cold, strobe lights, heavy metal) and the good cop/bad cop dynamics of Brody's 'Q&A' interrogation to examine deception in interrogations, including the nine-step Reid technique and its role in false confessions documented by the Innocence Project. He then tests the routine practice of lying to sources against three frameworks: Kant's absolute duty not to lie, Mill-inspired rule utilitarianism, which permits lies when telling truth regularly harms but lets interrogation exceptions serve the greater good, and Aristotle's virtue theory, on which habitual lying makes the agent vicious. Saul's lie about Hamid's family shows why the utilitarian exception dominates in the war on terror.
- ch. 32 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — Utilitarianism; Deontology; Humanity as an End in Itself; The Male Gaze — Dona Cayetana and Nathan Everson argue that 'enhanced interrogation techniques' is a euphemism - with Zizek and Badiou they show relabeling torture as what the West does while 'torture' is what Muslims do conceals identity between the acts, as 'Blind Spot''s safe-house interrogation of Hamid demonstrates. Utilitarianism justifies Saul's fatiguing of Hamid by its results, while the Kantian view that persons must never be mere means condemns it categorically; the chapter contends the show's framing nudges viewers to accept torture as a necessary evil. Carrie's gendered role as Zizek's 'new face of torture' - a pretty white woman whose desirability and perceived vulnerability become interrogation tools, from sex with Brody to 'You broke my heart' - feminizes and normalizes the spectacle.
- ch. 33 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** John Rawls, Elizabeth Anscombe, John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham — Just War Theory; The Veil of Ignorance; Deontology; Utilitarianism — John Fitzpatrick asks whether anything goes in war, using the FBI's killing of two innocents in the mosque and the ensuing official lie, plus Walden's strike on Issa's school. Truman's Hiroshima utilitarianism (saving lives overall) meets Anscombe's deontology - 'to choose to kill the innocent as a means to their ends is always murder' - before Rawls's social contract supplies a middle path: rules rational parties would agree to behind a veil of ignorance ('we won't kill your children if you don't kill ours'), which condemns both Nazir and Walden since neither faces an 'extreme crisis' like 1940 Britain. Brody's turn is itself Rawlsian: what breaks him is the unfairness of killing children as collateral damage.
- ch. 35 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** The Ethics of Political Violence; Just War Theory; Deontology; Utilitarianism — William Rodriguez traces terrorism's evolving meaning from the Jacobins' state Terror through anarchist 'propaganda by deed' (Nechayev, Bakunin) to non-state groups, then tests whether Abu Nazir's campaign, grounded in bin Laden's 1998 fatwa grievances and the greater/lesser jihad distinction, can be morally justified. On a utilitarian defense terrorism could be last-resort self-defense against a collectively guilty population, but the just war criteria of competent authority, right intention, last resort, discrimination, and proportionality all fail for al-Qaeda, since civilians killed on 9/11 were neither combatants nor guilty. Deontologically, targeting civilians violates the right to life and human dignity, so Nazir's attacks on innocents are murder, however real the grievances.
- ch. 36 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** Alasdair MacIntyre, Martin Heidegger, Augustine of Hippo, Thomas Hobbes — The Social Contract; The Two Cities; Thrownness (Geworfenheit) — Roberto Sirvent and Ian Diorio ask what the state really asks soldiers to die for: not Hobbes-and-Locke's rational social contract, since we are Heideggerian thrown into the wreckage of democracy rather than a pure founding state, but a nation that makes itself sacred. MacIntyre's charge that killing for the nation-state is 'killing for the telephone company' frames the analysis of America's civil liturgies - oaths (semper fidelis), flag ritual mirrored eerily in Brody burying Jessica's thrown Quran, saints, martyrs, and Saul pitting belief in America against belief in Allah. Augustine's civitas dei names the result: America as chosen city of God, and Islam terrifies the West because its creed claims devotion above any national flag.
- ch. 37 "Homeland and Philosophy" **·** The Ethics of Political Violence; Anarchism — This standalone section traces terrorism's history and definition, from the French Revolution's Reign of Terror when the state was 'le terroriste' (Robespierre's 'terror is nothing other than justice that is prompt, severe, and inflexible') to the term's reversal onto non-state actors via Russian anarchists like Nechayev. It offers a seven-feature characterization (violence, destruction, indiscriminate harm, psychological trauma, propaganda, policy change, non-state perpetration) matching the UN's 2004 definition, then catalogs often-forgotten American terrorism: the Boston Tea Party reframed as a news report, the Ku Klux Klan's campaign, the Haymarket bombing, the Los Angeles Times and Wall Street bombings by labor radicals and anarchists, the Bath School massacre, the Mad Bomber, the Weather Underground, anti-abortion Army of God killings, and the Unabomber.
- ch. 38 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Homeland and Philosophy" *(notes)*
