## House and Philosophy - Everybody Lies

- ch. 1 "Series" *(front)*
- ch. 2 "Title page" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Table of Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Introduction Contemplating a House of Cards" **·** Plato, Aristotle — The introduction reads Frank Underwood's opening mercy-killing of the injured dog and his unpunished rise from scorned congressman to president ('Democracy is so overrated') as a picture of politics where people do not reap what they sow. It argues the show channels real American anxieties — economic stagnation, the unfulfilled postracial promise, corporate money, inequality — so the titular house of cards may actually tumble. It frames the book's project as the contemplative life: withdrawing from politics to think before returning to action, invoking Plato's and Aristotle's claim that wonder is the beginning of philosophy and that wonder begets courage.
- ch. 6 "PART I SOCRATES, PLATO, AND FRANK" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Notes" **·** Plato, Socrates — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Justice in the Republic — Argues House of Cards is a modern version of the Sophistical challenge in Books 1-2 of Plato's Republic: Thrasymachus' claim that justice is the advantage of the stronger and the unjust life is preferable. It maps Frank's proprietary 'my people' attitude and his shepherd-and-flock use of Russo, Stamper, Sharp, Meechum and his Gaffney constituents onto Thrasymachus' reversal of Socrates' shepherd analogy, and reads the Secretary of State snub, Blythe's naivety and Dunbar's descent into dirty tricks as proofs that the just are dupes. Frank's Sentinel ring and 'craft' make him Glaucon's perfectly unjust man of the Ring of Gyges story — unjust while seeming just — while the framed Lucas Goodwin plays the perfectly just man punished for a reputation for injustice. The chapter ends by arguing Frank falls short of the ideal tyrant (his conscience, his church visits asking 'what Justice is') and is coming to live the Book 9 tyrant's friendless 'worst sort of life' as Claire and the others abandon him.
- ch. 8 "Notes" **·** Socrates, Plato, Niccolò Machiavelli — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); The Unexamined Life; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know) — Argues that Underwood's fourth-wall asides are the show's device for dramatizing the Platonic being-versus-seeming distinction: his public performances (reassuring Blythe over the leaked education bill, the confessional letter typed on the Underwood typewriter to destroy Walker, goading the President into appointing a special prosecutor) are contradicted point by point by what he tells the camera. The chapter reads Frank as a Ring of Gyges success story — the show so far teaches it is better to be the bad guy who seems good — while Socrates' examined life from the Apology and Crito shows Frank never asks why he wants power, and his cockiness is the opposite of Socratic ignorance. It closes with the Gorgias' contrast of Sophistic rhetoric (producing belief, not knowledge) and Machiavelli's advice to feign, casting Frank as the manipulator of opinion Socrates called a tyrant's politics.
- ch. 9 "PART II IMAGINING POSSIBILITIES AMERICAN IDEALS IN HOUSE OF CARDS" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Notes" **·** John Rawls, David Hume, Adam Smith — Justice as Fairness; The Invisible Hand — Argues that Rawls' ideal theory rests on a 'strict compliance' assumption — citizens and politicians who pursue justice rather than self-interest — which makes his ideal society look like The West Wing, while House of Cards depicts the real one: teachers' unions fighting performance standards, Senator Haas blocking the omnibus bill for electoral fear, Jackie Sharp trading favors, and SanCorp's Remy Danton buying votes against Russo's watershed bill. The chapter traces the competing 'every man a knave' tradition — Smith's invisible hand converting the butcher's self-love into our dinner, Hume's maxim for constitution-designers, Madison's 'ambition counteract ambition', and Buchanan's public choice revival — and uses the theory of the second best to argue we must design institutions (publicly financed elections, balanced-budget and inflation amendments, a generality constraint) that assume Underwoods run for Congress.
- ch. 11 "Notes" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Ressentiment; Will to Power — Argues the Underwoods act out historian James Truslow Adams' warning that the American dream, severed from the question of values, degenerates into a means-first pursuit of power — 'burning the barn to find our penny in the hay'. Using Nietzsche's ressentiment and above all Max Scheler's account of its systematic repression of emotion, the chapter reads Claire's repressed longings for motherhood (the 'What will we leave behind?' scene), integrity and love erupting as spite against those who embody what she sacrificed: firing and persecuting the pregnant Gillian Cole, destroying Adam Galloway, and abandoning Megan Hennessey. Michael Corrigan's suicide in his Russian cell — an integrity she cannot drag down to her level — finally shatters her ressentiment, and the chapter ends with her walking out of the White House as the escape Adams' question demands.
- ch. 12 "PART III CHARACTERIZING FRANK ÜBERMENSCH OR THE PRINCE" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Notes" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; Will to Power; The Last Man; Master and Slave Morality — Argues Frank is a Nietzschean Übermensch figure whose power play is staged through the show's postmodern techniques: the wave and the asides as metafiction that destroys our suspension of disbelief, self-referentiality in the mirror monologue ('Hunt or be hunted'), and a flattening of affect that lets us watch Zoe's murder at the train station with numbed indifference. Zarathustra's fearless overman is set against the risk-averse last man and the lambs-and-birds-of-prey passage against Christian slave morality, mapping onto Frank's breaking of Russo at the shipyard, the martyr's-sword maneuver on Blythe, and his spitting on the crucifix before taking 'God's ear'. The chapter reads Frank's ascent as will to power in both the political and personal Nietzschean sense, while warning that by Season 3 his hunger has cost him Jackie, Remy and Claire.
- ch. 14 "Notes" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; Master and Slave Morality; Amor Fati (love of fate) — Argues against the title claim of the previous chapter: Frank is a Nietzschean master type — he defines good as good-for-him, embraces pain as strength, and keeps no allegiances once Walker and Vasquez break their promise — while Peter Russo is the paradigmatic reactive slave who cannot command himself and blames others, exactly Nietzsche's 'he who cannot command himself is commanded'. But the overman requires self-overcoming, the sublimation of drives into creation, and amor fati, and Frank only dominates others for vengeful ends: his whole scheme grows from ressentiment over the Secretary of State snub, he affirms other wills merely as investments, and in Season 3 he becomes a slave to his own position. The chapter uses the Cesare Borgia misreading (better a Borgia than a Parsifal as raw material, but Borgia was no overman), Napoleon's corruption, and Goethe's creative wholeness to conclude that Frank remains 'all-too-human' — destruction without creation.
- ch. 15 "Notes" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — The Ends Justify the Means; Virtù and Fortuna — Argues Frank's rise is a genuinely successful illustration of The Prince: Machiavelli's advice to be prepared not to be virtuous shows in his lying (feigning loyalty to Walker while engineering his impeachment, promising Russo the governorship), his strategic murders of Russo and Zoe, his audacity in making his own luck ('turn over the table'), and his cultivation of a pious family-man image while privately spitting on the crucifix. The chapter also lets Machiavelli criticize Frank — revenge on Tusk (better to pamper or crush), Claire's fruitless generosity at CWI, alliances with the stronger, and being feared to the point of being hated, which costs him Sharp, Remy and Claire. Finally it tests The Prince against modern democracy — party discipline, sex-scandal politics, Obama's donated Nobel money, birther smears — concluding that since Machiavellians will always adapt to whatever system exists, citizens must reward results and stop judging by image.
- ch. 16 "Notes" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The Ends Justify the Means — Runs through everything Frank does for a single project — the education reform bill: leaking Blythe's draft, gutting collective bargaining after promising Spinella ('I revised the parameters of my promise'), the Rasmussen speakership coup he then 'exposes', the shipyard-for-base trade, and the staged brick incident and strike-breaking — to show he endorses being 'other than good'. It then argues Machiavelli would not be impressed: Frank fails to keep up the appearance of integrity (Blythe, Feng and Vasquez all stop trusting him), inspires hatred rather than fear alone, and seeks power purely for its own sake where Machiavelli wanted it for the state's stability and liberty — the BBC's Francis Urquhart, who read The Prince and serves England, is the better Machiavellian. The chapter closes by weighing ironic and exposé readings of The Prince (Benner's critique, Rousseau's view that Machiavelli was unmasking princes, Dietz's sabotaging-gift reading) and concludes Frank fails even on the technique, not just the morality.
- ch. 17 "Notes" **·** Michael Walzer, Jean-Paul Sartre, Niccolò Machiavelli — Utilitarianism; Deontology — Uses Sartre's play Dirty Hands and Walzer's 'Political Action: The Problem of Dirty Hands' to ask whether Frank's willingness to do the 'necessary thing' — from the opening mercy-killing of the dog to the Season 3 drone strike — could ever count in favor of an Underwood presidency, with utilitarians (nothing wrong with dirty hands) and absolutists like the bishop ('Love God, and love each other. Period.') dissolving the problem from opposite sides. Walzer's later 'supreme emergency' standard and threshold deontology frame the Jordan Valley staring contest with Petrov and the resulting deal as the one case where Frank's capability might genuinely matter, while Heather Dunbar's reluctance (her refusal of Stamper's offer of Claire's diary, then her reversal) tests the ideal of a leader both capable of and averse to dirtying her hands. The verdict: Frank's only advantage over Dunbar is his capacity for dirty hands, and even that fails because he lacks the scruples to keep them clean when it is not necessary.
- ch. 18 "PART IV CLASSICAL LIBERALISM AND DEMOCRACY" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Notes" **·** Thomas Hobbes — The Social Contract; The State of Nature — Casts Frank as the 'Foole' of Leviathan chapter 15 — the man who grants that covenants exist but thinks injustice can 'stand with reason' — and documents his befriend-then-betray pattern: Russo groomed for governor then gassed in his car, Zoe fed stories then pushed under a train, Walker befriended, helped and maneuvered into resigning, all to reap the contract's benefits while breaking it for 'the getting of a kingdom'. The chapter then defends Hobbes' reply via Kavka: the Foole's vainglory makes him overestimate his ability to deceive, and a detailed audit of Frank's two murders (fingerprints, witnesses, electronic traces, forensic evidence, alibis, high-stakes deception 'tells') shows them contingency-riddled and irrational. Even Frank's success is no defense — his presidency rests on others' errors and luck (Skorsy, Orsay, Tusk, Stamper, and above all Claire all remain loose ends), so he exemplifies the vainglorious Foole acting against the reason of his preservation rather than a rational violator of the social contract.
- ch. 20 "Notes" **·** Thomas Hobbes — The State of Nature; Democracy — Argues Frank is 'Hobbesian to the core': for him democracy is just the state of nature with elections, which is why it produces mediocre, fearful presidents like Walker (a Xanax-taking incumbent undone by a mere legislator) while a real sovereign like Frank — or the autocrat Petrov, with whom Frank shares cigars precisely because no common power enforces rules between them — gets things done. The chapter reads Frank's appetites (ribs at Freddy's, power over money against Remy and Tusk, contempt for sleep and death) through Hobbes' pleasure-and-pain psychology and his account of conflict arising when equals want the same thing, which finally drives Claire from the White House. Frank's church scenes ('I pray to myself, for myself') illustrate Hobbes' subordination of religion to civil power, the Kate Baldwin 'he is the tyrant' charge illustrates that a tyrant is only a king disliked, and the closing appeal is to Madison's Federalist 51: only the ambition of others can contain this one.
- ch. 21 "Notes" **·** Plato, Thomas Hobbes, G.W.F. Hegel, Karl Marx, John Stuart Mill — The Philosopher King; Tripartite Soul; Historical Materialism; Democracy — Argues the Underwoods' success is not far-fetched fiction but the structural outcome four critics of democracy predict. Plato's Republic supplies the spine: the kallipolis of philosopher-guardians decays through timocracy and oligarchy into democracy, whose love of freedom invites the tyrant — Peter Russo with his random desires is the democratic soul, Frank the tyrannical soul ruled by one illicit appetite, doomed to the fear and friendlessness Book 9 describes (which Season 3 delivers). Hobbes and Hegel then explain why monarchy may beat popular rule (the monarch's private interest aligns with the public's; the monarch lets a people will as one), and Marx reframes the whole show economically — SanCorp, Tusk and Feng as capitalists, the laid-off shipyard workers as proletariat, and AmWorks as self-interested politicians flirting with expropriation. Against all four, Mill's Considerations on Representative Government gives the democratic reply — elections force responsiveness, participation educates citizens — so the burden falls on viewers to keep elections fair, the press free, and checks on unscrupulous politicians effective.
- ch. 22 "Notes" **·** Karl Marx — Historical Materialism; Class Struggle — Byron and Wood use Marx's claim that the modern state is 'a committee for managing the common affairs of the bourgeoisie' to read House of Cards: Raymond Tusk secretly dictates President Walker's policies, SanCorp buys influence over Frank through Claire's water-filters favor, and Frank's rise to the vice presidency is engineered by capitalists to protect their market interests. Even Frank's seemingly pro-worker America Works plan serves capital, since gutting Social Security, Medicare, and Medicaid to subsidize $45,000 hiring costs boosts business profit, and the Dunbar-Walmart dispute shows the state arbitrating between conflicting capitalists rather than aiding workers. The chapter concludes that worker victories like a Dunbar minimum-wage win would only be the bourgeoisie losing a battle while winning the class war, confirming Frank's line that 'democracy is so overrated.'
- ch. 23 "Notes" **·** Michel Foucault, Jeremy Bentham — The Panopticon (surveillance); Democracy — Sirvent and Diorio apply Foucault's panopticon (built on Bentham's prison design) to the show's web of watching: Frank manipulates Zoe through leaked stories, Doug keeps Rachel permanently locatable by phone, and Claire must demand the bug be removed from Michael Corrigan's Russian cell, showing watched subjects paralyzed by fear. Against Bauman and Lyon's 'liquid surveillance,' the chapter argues democracy is an anti-institutional force requiring free dissent and a press at arm's length from power, and that surveillance collapses the Greek public/private distinction so citizens fear punishment for questioning rulers. Frank's 'Nobody can hear you. Nobody cares about you' to Zoe captures how the surveillance state turns criticism into danger, leaving a society that is no longer a democracy.
- ch. 24 "PART V INTRAPERSONAL RELATIONSHIPS, SEXUALITY, AND RACE IN HOUSE OF CARDS" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Notes" **·** Philosophy of Love — Southworth and Tallman defend the Underwood marriage by way of Richard Wasserstrom's three grounds for condemning adultery (promise breaking, deception, pain): Frank and Claire's openly negotiated nonmonogamy - Frank disclosing the Zoe affair over breakfast, the Meechum threesome, Claire's nights with Adam Galloway - breaks no promise and involves minimal deception, so it is morally permissible. They reject Vincent Punzo's dualist claim that sex must merge souls, arguing that view rests on unverifiable theology and sets people up for guilt. The marriage's real strength is its egalitarian mutuality - Claire's sabotage of the watershed bill works as tit-for-tat strategy that restores the partnership - and agreed nonmonogamy suits Frank's bisexuality better than monogamy, which would force him to give up part of what it means to be Frank.
- ch. 26 "Notes" **·** bell hooks — Objectification — Rivera Berruz argues that Freddy Hayes's storyline - the BBQ joint whose franchise design keeps linoleum peeling so 'white folks feel like they slumming,' his son's parole violation destroying the deal, and Frank publicly dropping him when the bad press lands - commodifies black male life as consumable 'spice' for a predominantly white show. Using bell hooks's account of ethnicity as seasoning in commodity culture, Tommy Curry and Michelle Alexander on blackness coded as criminality, and Nancy Leong's racial capitalism, the chapter shows Freddy's value is entirely derivative of his usefulness to Underwood. It closes by adopting Derrick Bell's racial realism ('I live to harass white folks') as the proper stance: the critique cannot dismantle racial capitalism but can harass the representation so viewers stop assuming the stereotype is 'just the way things are.'
- ch. 27 "Notes" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Aristotle — The General Will; Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society — Jackson reads the show through Rousseau's contrast between the general will and a pathological 'corporate will': Frank discards Freddy the moment his criminal past goes viral, Tusk and Walker's old friendship curdles into mutual blackmail, and Remy and Jackie trade intimacy for career protection - all reducing Aristotle's true friendship to friendships of utility and pleasure subordinated to institutions that treat persons as means. Rousseau's account of civilization degrading natural self-love (amour de soi) into vanity (amour-propre) explains why Washington's corporate ethos leaves no friends, only 'loose ends' to be eliminated, from Zoe pushed onto the platform to Doug hunting Rachel. The chapter ends with 'Man is born free, but is everywhere in chains' as the verdict on corporate personhood's conquest of human bonds.
- ch. 28 "PART VI EXISTENTIAL REALITIES SELF-LOVE AND FREEDOM" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "Notes" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli, Augustine of Hippo, Plato, Aristotle — Virtù and Fortuna; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); The Two Cities; The Ends Justify the Means — Cooper reads Frank's one prayer - kneeling to pray 'to myself, for myself' - as a self-love he calls autoeroticism, built on metaphysical atheism, ruthless pragmatism, and happiness as power. Like Glaucon's Ring of Gyges, Frank's atheism removes divine punishment from the moral equation, so the murders of Peter Russo and Zoe Barnes incur only human risk, while his feigned piety at the Peachoid girl's vigil, broken promises to Tusk, and 'cruelty well used' toward Russo exemplify Machiavellian virtu taming fortuna. Against this the chapter sets Augustine's critique of the libido dominandi: Frank's remorse after betraying Freddy ('Do you think I'm a hypocrite?') shows the lust for domination enslaves rather than liberates, the way of the City of Man against the Augustinian freedom of self-giving love.
- ch. 30 "Notes" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, Jean-Paul Sartre, Plato — The Ethics of Ambiguity; Ethical Egoism; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?) — Hackett contrasts Frank's ethical egoism - acting like an invisible Ring of Gyges wearer who pushes Zoe in front of a train and kills Peter once they cannot be controlled - with Beauvoir's existentialist ethics, in which consciousness is a 'nothing' whose freedom is real only when it wills and sustains the freedom of others. Peter's drunken enslavement and Zoe's freely chosen deal with Frank illustrate the ambiguity of a freedom that is still causally situated, and Frank's Season 3 miscalculations with Jackie, Remy, and Claire show egoism's inability to weigh others' self-interest. Claire's sympathy for Michael Corrigan and her final walkout dramatize her learning Beauvoir's lesson that promoting others' freedom is the condition of one's own, the difference between self-interest and existential freedom.
- ch. 31 "PART VII LET ME BE FRANK WITH YOU AGENCY, AESTHETICS, AND INTENTION" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "Notes" *(no index entry yet)* — Kajtar asks why viewers root for a double-murdering politician, framing the show through Tamar Gendler's puzzle of imaginative resistance: we would refuse to imagine Zoe's murder narrated as 'brave and noble,' yet House of Cards dissolves that resistance. Using Jenefer Robinson's claim that emotional engagement is necessary for understanding, the chapter shows how the Greenville first-pitch embarrassment, the Fort Sumter swim confession, and above all Frank's fourth-wall asides (telling us the real Gaffney eulogy, admitting 'I despise children') grant voyeuristic intimacy that humanizes him bite by bite - 'that's how you devour a whale.' By the time Frank frames Lucas Goodwin and Corrigan dies, we have adopted the morally deviant perspective, and the chapter warns this is exactly how politicians and artists seduce audiences into values we would otherwise reject.
- ch. 33 "Notes" *(no index entry yet)* — Kaufmann tests Donald Davidson's reductionism (intentions as belief-desire pairs) against Michael Bratman's planning theory through Frank's scheming: Frank can simultaneously desire revenge on Walker and desire to stay near power, but he cannot intend both, so intention's distinctive consistency, partiality, and control explain why he swallows the snub, delivers the education bill, and adjusts when Claire derails Peter's water bill. Tusk's maxim 'Decisions made on emotions aren't decisions at all' and Frank's patience in destroying Tusk across time illustrate Bratman's point that intentions coordinate temporally extended, cross-temporal agency that mere beliefs and desires cannot. The chapter closes on the Iowa victory speech intercut with Claire closing her bedroom door - the first sign her plans have diverged from Frank's.
- ch. 34 "Notes" **·** Martin Heidegger — Dressen and Taliaferro treat Frank's asides as a 'magical mystery tour' of concealment and disclosure, reading truth in Heidegger's fashion as disclosure rather than correspondence: the opening strangling of the dying dog, the Gaffney eulogy where Frank publicly mourns the father he privately calls 'quiet, timid, almost invisible,' and the tombstone urination ('It makes me seem more human') all play presence against absence to alienate Frank from responsibility. A close reading of five maxims - treading water, being a rock, loathing sleep, loving Claire 'more than sharks love blood,' and 'seem more human' - argues Frank consistently styles himself as barely human, the presidency rather than the person. Iris Murdoch's warning that art makes goodness seem dull and relaxes our moral guard explains why viewers find this fascinating rather than condemning.
- ch. 35 "PART VIII VIRTUE AND CHARACTER IN HOUSE OF CARDS" *(front)*
- ch. 36 "Notes" **·** Aristotle — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Eudaimonia (flourishing); Virtue Ethics (arete) — Johnson sorts Frank's six friendships through Aristotle's three kinds: Freddy and Meechum are friendships of pleasure (ribs and candor; loyalty and sex), while Doug, Cathy Durant, Tusk, and Jackie Sharp are friendships of utility that dissolve when usefulness ends - Jackie endorsing Dunbar being the proof. Even Frank's marriage fails the test of true friendship, since Aristotle holds that only good people can mirror each other's character, and badness corrupts the self-knowledge friendship provides. Because Aristotle deems friendship 'most indispensable for life' and eudaimonia requires virtuous activity joined to good fortune, the chapter ends hoping Frank can still choose the good for its own sake - a hope Claire's walkout after Iowa badly dents.
- ch. 37 "Notes" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Democracy; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Auxier asks who is morally worse, judging Frank and Claire by past deeds (Frank's murders of Zoe and Peter), future consequences (a president with the nuclear button), and the subjunctive (what each would do), while tracing Claire's lineage from Lady Macbeth and Elizabeth Urquhart, who assassinates Francis in the British series. Plato's and Aristotle's warning that democracy degenerates into tyranny frames the answer: Frank symbolizes the House, Claire the Senate, both coveting executive power, and Meechum - 'utterly loyal, utterly deadly' - is the military they seduce in the menage a trois and will underestimate. Niebuhr's 'moral mediocrity' of nations, from McCarthyism and the Joe Welch moment to mass incarceration, explains the verdict that neither individual is worse alone: 'they are far worse together,' and the union, not the person, has no decency.
- ch. 38 "President Frank Underwood's White House Staff (Contributors)" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "EULA" *(front)*

## House of Cards and Philosophy - Underwood's Republic

- ch. 1 "Series" *(front)*
- ch. 2 "Title page" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Table of Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Introduction Contemplating a House of Cards" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli, Plato, Aristotle — The editor's introduction frames House of Cards as dramatizing the anxiety that politics cannot deliver justice: Frank's cold-blooded mercy killing of the injured dog in the pilot, his devouring of ribs at Freddy's like a lion among lamb-politicians, and his impunity-ridden rise ('Democracy is so overrated') all transgress our moral commitments. It argues the show taps real worries — inequality, corporate influence, the unfulfilled postracial promise — and that the book answers them with the contemplative life, withdrawing from action to think before returning. It closes on Plato and Aristotle's claim that philosophy begins in wonder, and that 'wonder begets courage.'
- ch. 6 "PART I SOCRATES, PLATO, AND FRANK" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Notes" **·** Plato, Socrates — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Justice in the Republic — Ketchen and Yeo read the show's cynicism as Thrasymachus's challenge in Books 1-2 of the Republic — 'justice is the advantage of the stronger' — under which the just (Blythe, Lucas Goodwin) are dupes while the crafty win. Frank's proprietary 'my people' politics replays the shepherd-sheep exchange: Russo, Stamper, Sharp and the Gaffney constituents are a flock groomed, fleeced, and slaughtered (the naval base closure), just as Thrasymachus says shepherds fatten sheep for their masters. Frank's Sentinel ring makes him Glaucon's Ring of Gyges craftsman — perfectly unjust with a reputation for justice from the library dedication — while Lucas Goodwin is the just man made to seem unjust and destroyed. Yet against Petrov, Frank shows a conscience and tortured questions about justice in church, so the chapter ends with Republic Book 9: the tyrant, isolated and abandoned by Season 3's close, lives 'the worst sort of life.'
- ch. 8 "Notes" **·** Socrates, Plato, Niccolò Machiavelli — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); The Unexamined Life; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know) — Gray argues Frank's fourth-wall asides dramatize Plato's being-versus-seeming distinction: the camera confidences reveal the true motive behind the public performance, as in the Blythe education-bill martyrdom gambit, the deliberately failed persuasion of Walker over the special prosecutor, and the seduction letter typed on the Underwood typewriter. The chapter reads Frank as winning the Ring of Gyges bet — enjoying adultery, corruption, and murder while seeming good — exactly the outcome the Republic sets out to refute. Against this it sets Socrates' examined life and Apology profession of ignorance: Frank pursues power without asking to what end, and his mastery of rhetoric over knowledge makes him the Sophist of the Gorgias rather than Plato's true politician, with Machiavelli's dissembling prince as his model.
- ch. 9 "PART II IMAGINING POSSIBILITIES AMERICAN IDEALS IN HOUSE OF CARDS" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Notes" **·** John Rawls, David Hume, Adam Smith — Justice as Fairness; The Invisible Hand — Kogelmann argues that political philosophers' ideal theory rests on Rawls's 'strict compliance' assumption — the West Wing picture where citizens and politicians pursue the public good — while House of Cards depicts the reality: Russo's zoning constituent, the teachers' union fighting performance standards, Senator Haas and Sharp's colleagues trading votes for self-interest. By the theory of the second best, ideals designed for selfless politicians misfire in a world with Frank and Remy Danton in it, so he recruits the older 'every man a knave' tradition — Adam Smith's self-love turned to public benefit, Hume's maxim, Madison's ambition-countering-ambition, Buchanan's 'politics without romance' — to redesign institutions against rent-seeking (SanCorp's purchase of votes on Russo's watershed bill) and politicians' short election-cycle horizons (Claire courting Senator Mendoza), via public financing and balanced-budget and inflation rules.
- ch. 11 "Notes" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Ressentiment; Master and Slave Morality; Will to Power — Palm and Stikkers read the Underwoods through James Truslow Adams's American dream as a question of values gone rotten: lacking ends, they pursue power for its own sake while repressed longings (Frank's Sentinel camaraderie, song, and friendship; Claire's motherhood — 'What will we leave behind?') erupt as the ressentiment Nietzsche introduced and Max Scheler systematized, hating those who embody the sacrificed values. Claire's hatred thus targets Gillian Cole's pregnancy and integrity (fired, then threatened with letting her child 'wither and die') and Adam Galloway's freedom and love — Scheler's pedestal-smashing pattern — after the slave-morality-like repression of her normal human affects. The ressentiment finally shatters when Michael Corrigan refuses to betray himself and hangs himself in Petrov's cell: Claire tells the cameras the truth and walks out on Frank at the end of Season 3, free of the resentment.
- ch. 12 "PART III CHARACTERIZING FRANK ÜBERMENSCH OR THE PRINCE" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Notes" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; Will to Power; Master and Slave Morality; The Last Man — Aarons argues Frank is a Nietzschean Übermensch figure delivered through postmodern technique: the metafictional wave in 'Chapter 1,' self-referential mirror monologues ('Did you think I've forgotten you?'), and the flattening of affect in Zoe's death at the train station. Zarathustra's fearless overman, contrasted with the comfort-loving last man, maps onto Frank's 'hunt or be hunted' creed and his 'money is the McMansion in Sarasota... power is the old stone building' will-to-power, played out on Russo, Zoe, and Walker. The Genealogy's birds of prey and lambs frame Russo's Christian hypocrisy, and The Antichrist's equation of good with heightened power explains the church scene where Frank spits on the crucifix and leaves with the statue's ear — 'I've got God's ear now.'
- ch. 14 "Notes" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; Master and Slave Morality; Amor Fati (love of fate); Will to Power — Meyer mounts the counter-case: Frank exemplifies Nietzsche's master type (good as good-for-him, 'no longer are we bound by allegiances') and Russo the slave type ('he who cannot command himself is commanded'), but the overman is a synthesis of neither. He corrects the Leopold-and-Loeb 'above the law' reading and the Cesare Borgia identification: the genuine overman sublimates strong drives into creation — Goethe's self-discipline, Beethoven's new norms — and affirms other wills in themselves rather than as instruments, which Frank never does with Zoe, Peter, or Claire. Frank's revenge-driven scheming is slave-like psychological warfare, ruling others is the 'pettiest' form of power, and by Season 3 he is the slave of his own presidency — destructive without creating, and lacking even amor fati.
- ch. 15 "Notes" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — The Ends Justify the Means; Virtù and Fortuna — Littmann tests Frank against The Prince and largely confirms the 'Machiavellian' label: his lies (playing the loyal confidant while engineering Walker's fall), his strategic murders of Russo and Zoe, his preference for weaker allies, and his manufactured piety and father stories all enact Machiavelli's advice that a prince must learn 'not to be good' while seeming merciful, faithful, and religious. But the debts run both ways: Claire's vindictive 'let's make him suffer' offends Machiavelli's pamper-or-crush rule, and modern democratic image politics (Obama donating his Nobel money, the Obama-as-Muslim smear) outperforms The Prince where voters punish fear and reward a generosity Machiavelli thought ruinous. The moral is not despair but vigilance: since Underwoods will always rise, citizens must reward genuine results rather than respond to images.
- ch. 16 "Notes" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The Ends Justify the Means; Virtù and Fortuna — Fallis walks through the education-reform arc — leaking Blythe's draft, gutting collective bargaining ('I revised the parameters of my promise'), framing Rasmussen in the speakership coup, the staged brick incident, and goading Spinella into felony assault — to show Frank follows Machiavelli's counsel to be 'other than good.' Yet Frank fails at Machiavelli's how and why: he never cultivates the appearance of integrity (Blythe, Feng, and Vasquez refuse to deal with a known liar), he inspires hatred as well as fear, and he seeks power for its own sake rather than the stability of the state, which makes the BBC's Francis Urquhart the superior Machiavellian. Even the revisionist readings of The Prince — Benner's irony, Rousseau's claim that Machiavelli was exposing princes' methods like an investigative reporter, Dietz's deliberate-sabotage thesis — would not rescue Frank, since he bungles the techniques regardless of their intent.
- ch. 17 "Notes" **·** Michael Walzer, Jean-Paul Sartre, Niccolò Machiavelli — Utilitarianism; Just War Theory — Perry frames Frank through Walzer's problem of dirty hands, taking its name from Sartre's play ('I have dirty hands right up to the elbows... Do you think you can govern innocently?') and Machiavelli's advice to 'enter into evil when forced by necessity' — the pilot's mercy killing of the dog and 'just shy of treason, which is politics' being the show's emblems. He maps the field onto the show: utilitarians for whom breaking rules can be right, absolutists like the bishop ('Love God, and love each other. Period.'), and Walzer's later 'supreme emergency' threshold deontology applied to Frank's child-killing drone strike and his desperate deal with Petrov in the Jordan Valley. The verdict: Frank's only edge over Heather Dunbar is a proven capacity to dirty his hands when needed, but he lacks the scruples that keep that capacity from becoming routine wrongdoing, so he is probably still the wrong man for the job.
- ch. 18 "PART IV CLASSICAL LIBERALISM AND DEMOCRACY" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Notes" **·** Thomas Hobbes — The State of Nature; The Social Contract — Courtland uses Hobbes's 'Foole' from Leviathan — who grants that covenants exist but insists injustice 'may sometimes stand with reason' — as the template for Frank, who enjoys the benefits of the social contract (security, cooperation, even Freddy's BBQ) while befriending and betraying Russo, Zoe, and Walker up to outright murder. Applying Hobbes's reply, the chapter audits both murders as contingency-ridden blunders — fingerprints, witnesses, cameras, forensic evidence, and the 'tells' of high-stakes deception — and diagnoses Frank's confidence as the vainglory behind Kavka's 'Foole's gold.' Even his success proves nothing: in the Latin Leviathan a fortunate outcome of reckless action is still imprudence, and his 'kingdom' rests on loose ends (Skorsky, Orsay, Tusk, Stamper, and Claire) that could bring the house of cards down.
- ch. 20 "Notes" **·** Thomas Hobbes — The State of Nature; The Social Contract; Democracy — Michels reads Frank as 'Hobbesian to the core' and democracy as 'the state of nature with elections': Walker, Matthews, and Vasquez show that elections elevate mediocrities, while Frank's America Works and his FEMA/Stafford Act end-run around Congress display the energetic sovereign for whom 'a tyrant is only a king disliked.' Hobbes's pleasure-pain psychology of appetite and aversion explains the cast — Frank's ribs, PlayStation, and pure taste for power; Russo's indiscriminate addictions; Doug's serial ones — while Hobbes's competition for gain, fear, and glory structures the Tusk money-versus-power war whose casualties include Freddy. With Hobbes's subordination of religion to civil power, Frank's crucifix-spitting 'I pray to myself, for myself' caps the case that truth only obstructs sovereignty, leaving Madison's 'ambition must be made to counteract ambition' as the lone check.
- ch. 21 "Notes" **·** Plato, Thomas Hobbes, Karl Marx, G.W.F. Hegel, John Stuart Mill — Democracy; Historical Materialism; The Philosopher King — Shea argues the Underwoods' rise is structurally guaranteed rather than far-fetched, surveying democracy's critics: Plato's Republic Books 8-9 has the kallipolis of philosopher-guardians decay through timocracy and oligarchy into democracy, where a 'protector'-tyrant like Frank exploits the drones and the people's love of freedom (Russo as the prototypical democratic soul, Tusk as the oligarch-in-fact), while the tyrant ends fear-ridden and friendless — precisely Frank's Season 3. Hobbes and Hegel then reverse the verdict, arguing monarchy aligns the ruler's private interest with the public good and lets a people will as one instead of a formless mass — the insight Tom Yates has about voters identifying with the Underwoods as people — and Marx reframes it economically: SanCorp, Tusk, and Feng are the capitalist structure, and Frank's AmWorks, entitlement cuts, and nationalization threats show self-interested politicians siding with a growing proletariat. Against all four, Mill's Considerations on Representative Government supplies the democratic reply — responsiveness, better information, and citizen participation mean an Underwood cannot last if we keep fair elections, a free press, and real checks.
- ch. 22 "Notes" **·** Karl Marx — Historical Materialism; Class Struggle — Argues that Marx's claim that the modern state is 'a committee for managing the common affairs of the bourgeoisie' explains House of Cards better than Frank's own power-over-money creed: Raymond Tusk and SanCorp dictate President Walker's policies and cost Frank the Secretary of State post, and even Frank's signature America Works jobs bill functions as a capitalist subsidy (government-paid wages, gutted entitlements), while the Dunbar-versus-Walmart fight over Medicaid shows the state arbitrating among capitalists. The authors conclude that even a worker victory like a Dunbar minimum-wage raise would only be a concession in an ongoing class conflict, so 'democracy is so overrated' because capital, not Frank, is really in charge of the White House.
- ch. 23 "Notes" **·** Michel Foucault, Jeremy Bentham — The Panopticon (surveillance); Democracy — Uses Foucault's panopticon (built on Bentham's prison design) and Bauman's 'liquid surveillance' to read Frank's Washington as a surveillance society: Zoe's archived texts, Doug's inescapable tracking of Rachel, and the bugged cell where Claire visits Michael Corrigan all show how being watched destroys privacy and paralyzes dissent. It argues that democracy needs free spaces in which citizens can question power without later punishment, so the culture of fear Frank rules through-'you are a rock, you absorb nothing, you say nothing'-turns checks and balances into instruments of control even inside an elected government.
- ch. 24 "PART V INTRAPERSONAL RELATIONSHIPS, SEXUALITY, AND RACE IN HOUSE OF CARDS" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Notes" **·** Philosophy of Love — Defends the Underwoods' open marriage by applying Wasserstrom's account of adultery (wrong via promise-breaking, deception, and pain): Frank and Claire's mutually negotiated nonmonogamy-frank transparency with Claire about Zoe, Claire rushing home to Frank from Adam's-apartment avoids all three wrongs, and Punzo's dualist claim that sex merges souls is rejected as unverifiable and damaging. It reads the marriage as an egalitarian partnership whose tit-for-tat renegotiation after Claire's watershed-bill sabotage and their agreed childlessness ('I promise you freedom from that') show that nonmonogamy strengthens rather than corrodes their bond.
- ch. 26 "Notes" **·** bell hooks — Argues that Freddy Hayes's storyline-Frank slumming at the BBQ joint, the franchise plan designed so white customers 'feel like they slumming', and Frank cutting Freddy loose when his son's gun photo goes viral-commodifies black male life as 'spice' for a white narrative (bell hooks) and exemplifies Nancy Leong's racial capitalism, where blackness has value only insofar as it benefits white institutions. Drawing on Tommy Curry, Michelle Alexander, and Derrick Bell's racial realism ('I live to harass white folks'), the chapter claims the show reinforces rather than interrupts the stereotype equating blackness with criminality, poverty, and death.
- ch. 27 "Notes" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Aristotle — The General Will; Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society — Reads the show through Rousseau's contrast between the general will and a pathological 'corporate will': governments and corporations are sociopathic artificial persons that depersonalize their agents, degrading natural self-love (amour de soi) into status-hungry amour-propre. On this account Frank's break with Freddy ('just a good customer'), the Tusk-Walker alliance, and Remy and Jackie's bought loyalties are Aristotelian friendships of utility or pleasure crushed under the corporate will, never true friendship, leaving Washington with 'honor without virtue' and men 'everywhere in chains'.
- ch. 28 "PART VI EXISTENTIAL REALITIES SELF-LOVE AND FREEDOM" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "Notes" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli, Augustine of Hippo, Plato — Virtù and Fortuna; The Two Cities; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?) — Analyzes Frank's kneeling 'I pray to myself, for myself' as an ethics of autoeroticism built on metaphysical atheism, ruthless pragmatism, and happiness-as-power: like Glaucon's Ring of Gyges, removing God from the equation makes the murders of Zoe and Russo cost only human, never eternal, judgment. The chapter details three exercises of Machiavellian virtu-feigned piety at the Peachoid vigil, fox-like promise-breaking with Tusk, and 'cruelty well-used' toward Russo-before closing on Augustine's critique of the libido dominandi: Frank's betrayal of Freddy, met with a flash of remorse, shows the lust for domination enslaves rather than liberates, the way of the City of Man.
- ch. 30 "Notes" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, Jean-Paul Sartre, Plato — The Ethics of Ambiguity; Ethical Egoism; Radical Freedom and Responsibility — Contrasts Frank's ethical egoism with Beauvoir's existential ethics: Frank moves like an invisible Ring-of-Gyges wearer-murdering Zoe, reading everyone's self-interest-yet because he treats others purely as instruments his freedom is never 'prolonged through the freedom of others', and it fails in Season 3 when he miscalculates Jackie, Remy, and Claire. Claire's awakening through Michael Corrigan and her final walkout dramatize the Beauvoirian lesson that consciousness is freedom-as-nothingness whose values we remain collectively responsible for, not a license to will anything.
- ch. 31 "PART VII LET ME BE FRANK WITH YOU AGENCY, AESTHETICS, AND INTENTION" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "Notes" *(no index entry yet)* — Takes up the puzzle of imaginative resistance (Gendler): while viewers would refuse to imagine that Frank's murder of Zoe was 'brave and noble', we end up rooting for his double murder and abuses of power. It explains this lack of resistance through the Greenville first-pitch confession, Frank's fourth-wall asides that grant us secret intimacy ('I despise children'), and Robinson's account of emotion-driven understanding-an engineered emotional seduction that doubles as a lesson in how politicians and artists manipulate audiences into morally deviant points of view.
- ch. 33 "Notes" *(no index entry yet)* — Defends Bratman's planning theory of intention against Davidson's reductionism using Frank's schemes: his intention to become President is a distinct commitment-involving plan-state that imposes consistency and control-forcing him to postpone revenge on Walker, readjust when Claire derails Peter's water bill, and coordinate cross-temporally with Claire-where mere beliefs and desires cannot. Tusk's 'decisions made on emotions aren't decisions at all' and Frank's handling of the Corrigan backlash illustrate why intentions, not passions, are the inputs that keep his long-run plans on track.
- ch. 34 "Notes" **·** Martin Heidegger — Authenticity; The Problem of Evil — Treats Frank's soliloquies-strangling the dog's 'useless pain', the Peachoid eulogy that goads a grieving congregation toward hating God, urinating on his father's grave 'to seem more human'-as a Heideggerian tour of concealment and disclosure, where truth is disclosure rather than correspondence and Frank alienates himself from responsibility and even from being human. Quoting Iris Murdoch on how fiction makes goodness seem dull and invoking Roth's 'theodicy of protest', the chapter warns that Frank's shuffling of morality into maneuver relaxes our moral guard about blame, God, and evil.
- ch. 35 "PART VIII VIRTUE AND CHARACTER IN HOUSE OF CARDS" *(front)*
- ch. 36 "Notes" **·** Aristotle — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Sorts Frank's six friendships using Aristotle's three types: Freddy and Meechum are friendships of pleasure, Doug, Durant, Tusk, and Jackie friendships of utility, while Claire-despite 'I love that woman more than sharks love blood'-cannot be a true friend of the good because true friendship requires two people of good character seeing themselves in each other. Since Aristotle ties friendship to flourishing, the chapter ends hoping that Frank, who has good fortune but neither good character nor true friends, may yet choose virtue and the good life.
- ch. 37 "Notes" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Democracy; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Asks who is morally worse by three standards-what each has done, is doing, and would do in the future subjunctive-and answers that Frank and Claire are far worse together, read symbolically as the US Constitution itself: Frank is the mercurial House, Claire the deliberate, more ambitious Senate, and Meechum the loyal military they seduce and underestimate at the republic's peril. Working from Plato's and Aristotle's claim that democracy declines into tyranny, Niebuhr's 'moral mediocrity' of nations, and the Welch-McCarthy 'have you no decency' moment, it asks whether a system that produced them is one 'we the people' deserve.
- ch. 38 "President Frank Underwood's White House Staff (Contributors)" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "EULA" *(notes)*

## How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy - Being and Awesomeness

- ch. 1 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** Adam Smith — The chapter poses the 'Barney Paradox': viewers identify and empathize with a character who is utterly awful (selling a woman, the Scuba Diver con that dupes all his friends), even though we would shun him in real life. Nanay tests and finds insufficient several explanations - Barney is just immature, we only feel schadenfreude at his slap-bet punishments, or his emotions are simply contagious - since none explains why Barney's badness increases our engagement. Using Hitchcock's bomb-under-the-table example and Adam Smith's idea of imagining ourselves in another's situation, he argues identification targets the character's situation, which is not itself evil. The appeal is vicarious badness: fiction lets us be 'virtual tourists' in immorality we would never choose for real.
- ch. 9 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** William James, Jean-Paul Sartre — The chapter takes Barney's 'when I get sad, I stop being sad and be awesome instead' as a serious thesis about emotional control. Goffin surveys somatic theories (William James: control the body, fake it till you make it), cognitivist judgment theories (Sartre and Robert Solomon: Ted's rage at Barney for sleeping with Robin is a judgment of betrayal), and Jesse Prinz's 'embodied appraisal' synthesis, concluding emotions are two-stage processes of direct appraisal plus narrative monitoring. Because we partly author the narratives wrapping our appraisals - as Barney did when reinventing himself after Shannon dumped him, and as Lily does by owning her failure as a painter - we can responsibly 'awesomize' our emotions.
- ch. 10 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Ethical Egoism; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — The chapter argues Barney is best understood as a committed ethical egoist, distinguishing egoism from egotism, immoralism, and moral skepticism through the Playbook's Scuba Diver scam. Ainsworth rejects psychological egoism (Joey's view in Friends) using evolution and apparent counterexamples like Marshall's Christmas Eve deliveries, then uses Glaucon's ring of Gyges from the Republic to ask whether anyone would resist injustice with guaranteed impunity. The friendship objection is dissolved via Aristotle's 'a friend is another self': once friends are included in the ego, saving Marshall's relationship or rushing to Ted's hospital bed counts as self-interest, constrained like chess by absolute rules such as the Bro Code's Article 150.
- ch. 11 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** Carol Gilligan — Feminism — The chapter reads Robin's series-long arc as an argument for non-essentialist feminism against Carol Gilligan's suggestion that all women share fundamental traits and rules. Caught between Lily's Dreamhouse-Barbie domesticity and the Career-Barbie ideal her father forcibly raised her into - mocked by kindergartners at career day, 'called slut' by Marshall for a one-night stand in 'The Naked Man' - Robin keeps trying to live one prescribed path entirely and is miserable both times. Only when she stops choosing between the boxes, marrying Barney while still never wanting children, does the show endorse the non-essentialist feminism of Deborah Rhode and Drucilla Cornell: a woman may custom-tailor her own happy ending.
- ch. 12 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Bad Faith; Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Existence Precedes Essence — The chapter uses Sartre to diagnose Barney's fixed persona of suits, womanizing, and Bro Code worship as bad faith: having freely chosen to become the suited businessman who stole Shannon, Barney now acts as if those are his only options, denying his radical freedom. Hardy maps Sartre's own examples onto the show - Wendy the Waitress play-acting a stereotypical waitress, the woman who ignores the knee-touch, Barney tuning out criticism with 'I'm awesome' - and reads emotions, via Sartre's Sketch for a Theory of the Emotions, as magical transformations of the world like Marshall's 'best burger' quest and the Bob Barker paternity story. Barney's love for Robin and his secret reunion of Marshall and Lily are the moments he breaks out of bad faith and chooses freely against his persona.
- ch. 13 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault, Aristotle — Cultural Hegemony; Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Feminism — The chapter argues the Bro Code's real value lies not in getting laid but in structuring homosocial bonds, listing its best articles (loyalty, equality, generosity) alongside its most sexist ones (the Hot/Crazy Scale, Article 101). Karioris explains men's competitive relations through hegemonic masculinity - the school-bully dominance that forces all masculinities to compete and pushes women out - and invokes Foucault's 'Friendship as a Way of Life' for how bonds between men can escape taboo and emotional distance. Robin as wingwoman ('a better Ted than Ted') and the gang's gender transgressions show the Code can be amended toward equality, with Aristotle's friendship of mutual cooperation and benefit, 'quid pro bro', as the standard.
- ch. 15 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — History Has a Direction — The chapter reads Future Ted's narration as Hegel's philosophy of history scaled down to one love life: like Hegel's development of freedom, Ted's dating disasters are necessary stages whose rational order appears only retroactively, when the owl of Minerva flies at dusk. Against Marshall and Lily's 'let the universe decide' readings of doppelgangers and apartment signs, Ted's line that 'we only see what we want to see when we're ready to see it' captures belated necessity: the Lucky Penny chain and the Right Place, Right Time walk become one necessary story only through reflection. Meeting the Mother, the final end of Ted's history, redeems the goat beating, the broken engagement, and the lost Chicago interview as contributions to the narrative.
- ch. 16 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** David Lewis — The chapter asks what is true in the fiction of HIMYM, distinguishing explicit 'primitive' truths (Robin is Canadian) from 'derivative' truths (Barney's conquests do not pop in and out of existence; his awesome gland is not real even in the story). Testing candidate accounts - carry over real-world truths, then restrict to shared community beliefs - against the college sub that is obviously a joint, the authors deploy David Lewis's pretense analysis of truth in fiction and Gregory Currie's fictional narrator, which the show's fallible, lying narrator Ted makes both nearly indispensable and insufficient. The upshot: ordinary viewers know exactly what is true in the show (they were smoking dope), while the theories strain to keep up - 'yeah, not so quick.'
- ch. 17 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — Humanity as an End in Itself; The Categorical Imperative; Respect for Persons — The chapter evaluates Marshall and Lily's promise to tell each other everything through Kant: lying violates the universalizable duty of truthfulness and treats the deceived as a mere means, as with Lily's hidden credit card debt in 'Dowisetrepla', the realtor's sewage-plant omission, and Barney's fully staged seduction of Meg that blocks her informed choice. Even Marshall's kindly lie about the doppelganger fails Kant's test, alongside his infamous refusal to lie to the murderer at the door. Yet Kant's letters on reticence in friendship and his grim view of marriage as shared ends where spouses may use each other 'as if' they were things suggest that telling each other everything may itself corrode respect - the tension Lily's never-opened bathroom door dramatizes.
- ch. 18 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** David Hume, Adam Smith — Reason Is the Slave of the Passions; The Invisible Hand; The Golden Rule — The chapter presents Barney as a Humean moral philosopher: reason is the slave of the passions, and norms like the Bro Code are invented conventions that evolve, Adam Smith's invisible-hand style, rather than being designed, revealed, or deduced. The Platinum Rule ('never, ever love thy neighbor') is grounded in Humean reciprocity: the eight-stage arc of attraction to fallout in Barney's Wendy the Waitress disaster, the Gerards' brunch tyranny, and Robin's romance with co-worker Curt shows the retaliation costs that make the norm a rational solution to coordination failures. Against the Golden Rule's painterly elegance, the anatomist's cautious observation of behavior explains why a norm banning neighborly love emerges even in a society of non-saints.
- ch. 20 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** Carol Gilligan, Aristotle — The Ethics of Care; Eudaimonia (flourishing); The Golden Mean; Feminism — The chapter argues HIMYM offers two complementary feminist icons: Robin embodies personal autonomy and independence - fighting from 'but, um' early-morning shifts to lead anchor, and grieving infertility as stolen autonomy rather than lost motherhood - while Lily, and even more Marshall, exemplify Carol Gilligan's feminist care ethics with its emphasis on emotion over impartiality, cooperation over competition, and partiality toward loved ones. Aristotle's golden mean supplies the test Robin passes by balancing career and love instead of pushing one to an extreme. The twist that a male character best exemplifies care ethics, with Lily bridging the two moral outlooks, is offered as the show's progressive complexity about gender.
- ch. 21 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger, Søren Kierkegaard — Authenticity; The They-Self (das Man); Thrownness (Geworfenheit); The Leap of Faith — The chapter diagnoses the show's identity crises through Heidegger's Being and Time: Marshall's Goliath National Bank drudgery is anxiety over thrownness - the rent, the bills, the situation he never chose - while Lily's prom-night bolt to San Francisco is anxiety over freedom and possibility, and both fail anticipatory resoluteness, the commitment to be yourself while shouldering what you did not choose. das Man ('everybody's doing it') explains the gang's absorbed everydayness. Kierkegaard supplies the problem of time (life understood backwards but lived forwards) and Fear and Trembling's contrast between Abraham's leap of faith into the absurd and the 'demonic': Barney courts legendary moments yet manipulates uncertainty so he never becomes anyone new - the identity crisis that never ends.
- ch. 22 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The chapter models Ted's annual Slutty Pumpkin vigil as a decision-theory problem: multiplying Ted's subjective probability (0.1) by his utility (9) yields an expected utility of 0.9, beaten by the Victoria's Secret party's 2.5, and two further irrationalities are added - misjudging the objective probability and acting on harmful desires. Yet Ted's impulse purchase of the derelict house in 'Home Wreckers', which becomes his future family home, shows expected-utility reasoning failing when unknowable scenarios multiply. The authors conclude that when the future becomes incalculable, gut feelings are the only remaining and legitimate guides - sometimes it is better to be a happy lunatic waiting on the roof than a rational but unhappy sage.
- ch. 23 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** William James — Truth as What Works; The Will to Believe; Pragmatism — The chapter compares Barney's 'True story' pronouncements with William James's pragmatism, using James's squirrel anecdote and the 'Zoo or False' monkey-mugging to ask when an evidentially undecidable dispute becomes a live practical question. Like James's pragmatic cases for free will and religious comfort, Barney's monkey story and the twenty-eight-year-old 'with some sun damage' are beliefs made true by their practical (mostly entertainment) value, then replaced like outdated theories when new evidence arrives, as James's consistency requirement demands. The divergence: the Barney Stinson (BS) theory of truth is self-serving, engineering unverifiable stories others cannot disprove, while James weighs what works for everyone's experience.
- ch. 25 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** David Hume, G.E. Moore, Aristotle — Is–Ought Problem; The Naturalistic Fallacy; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Framed as a sports broadcast with Aristotle as commentator, the chapter tests an evolutionary ethics ('BEES': the good is the naturally desirable, so the Pleistocene Stinson strategy of spreading genes grounds Barney's womanizing) against its classic critics. Hume's is-ought gap and Moore's open question argument are deployed against deriving Barney's right to pursue tail from human nature, while William Casebeer's evolutionary ethics replies that 'good' can be defined by present reproductive success - under which lying and eternal bachelorhood fail, since commitment is what reproduces genomes in urban New York ('BIRDS'). The chapter concludes that evolutionary ethics can support the Bro Code only by contestable redefinition, and the lying objection stands.
- ch. 26 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger — Personal Identity — The chapter reads the show's storytelling as identity construction through Paul Ricoeur's narrative identity, built on Heidegger's insight that human being is fundamentally temporal: we live in memories and future possibilities, so 'damn you, past Ted!' collisions between selves are structural. Ricoeur's two times - linear cosmological time versus fragmented phenomenological time - explain the show's looped, goat-intruding storytelling, while Giddens's reflexive project of the self (dubbed 'reflexibility') and Turkle's new-media multiple windows explain our fragmented, self-designed identities. Goffman's dramaturgy completes the picture: Barney's womanizer persona is a Goffmanesque masquerade that collapses, the yips, when his first lover reveals the founding story was false.
- ch. 27 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, G.W.F. Hegel — The Dialectic — The chapter defends Barney's 'possimpible' as real philosophy. Aristotle's three formulations of the law of non-contradiction (from the Metaphysics) and Duns Scotus's ex falso quodlibet explosion argument show why classical logic must ban the possible and impossible becoming one - from one true contradiction, 'bimbos love studying formal logic' follows. Hegel's Science of Logic (the limit of the beer, the finite that is itself only by passing into its not-being) and Graham Priest's dialetheism from In Contradiction then argue limits are inherently contradictory, so the possimpible names the real turning point where opposites become each other - lived, the chapter claims, in Barney's transformation from lame hippie into suited bro.
- ch. 28 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 29 "How I Met Your Mother and Philosophy" *(notes)*
