## Iron Man and Philosophy - Facing the Stark Reality

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Iron Man and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "The Blackwell Philosophy and Pop Culture Series" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright Page" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Introduction" *(no index entry yet)* — Editor Mark D. White's introduction concedes that Iron Man looks like merely 'a guy in a shiny metal suit' next to tortured heroes like Batman, then argues he nonetheless inspires serious philosophical questions: whether Tony shows responsibility commensurate with his great power, whether the technology behind the armor has a downside, whether his true superpower is the suit or Stark's intelligence, and how his alcoholism and womanizing reflect on the hero. It frames the volume with stories like 'Demon in a Bottle,' 'Armor Wars,' and 'The Mask in the Iron Man,' suggesting that tragedy, not glitz, may be the defining aspect of Tony Stark's life.
- ch. 7 "PART ONE - THE NUTS AND BOLTS OF TONY STARK" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Jet Boots and Clay Feet" **·** René Descartes, Martin Heidegger — The Question Concerning Technology — Dunn argues Iron Man personifies both the modern dream of technology multiplying human powers and the medieval chivalric ideal, presenting Descartes as Stark's philosophical forerunner whose method promised to make us 'lords and masters of nature' and whose virtue of generosity offered only vague guidance for using that power. Heidegger's warning that technology treats everything, including humans, as 'standing-reserve' frames 'Extremis,' where the serum tells Tony's brain 'the body is wrong' and rebuilds him as a technological artifact, while 'Armor Wars' — stolen designs used by villains like Stane and Spymaster, ending with Tony accidentally killing the Gremlin with his jet boots — shows technology proliferating beyond its creator's control, so the jet boots hide feet of clay.
- ch. 9 "The Face Shield Is a Mirror Iron Man and You" **·** Martin Heidegger, Aristotle — The Question Concerning Technology; The Four Causes; Authenticity — Gangle reads Stark's escape from the Ten Rings' cave and his press-conference conversion ('I have more to offer this world than just making things to blow up') through Heidegger's 'Question Concerning Technology.' Analyzing the armor via Aristotle's four causes (alloy, blueprints, Stark and Jarvis, the making of a hero) shows technology as a world-revealing, creative mode whose modern essence reduces nature and people to 'standing-reserve.' By becoming the armor — 'a pilot without the plane' — Stark unites technological might with individual conscience, using technology subversively to resist technology's own logic of power without accountability, an example of Heideggerian authentic existence the reader is called to imitate.
- ch. 10 "So, That’s It No Solution" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Consequentialism; Existential Risk — Rieder argues Iron Man is the one 'realistic' superhero: nothing about him requires a different world, only incremental nanotech advances — precision engineering, miniaturized computing, and carbon nanotubes a hundred times stronger than steel — already pursued at MIT's Institute for Soldier Nanotechnologies. He then uses Iron Man as a nanoethics case study, pitting cost-benefit analysis (with David Schmidtz's proviso that it is necessary but not sufficient for right action) against the precautionary principle exemplified by Bill Joy's call for relinquishment, asking whether an Obadiah Stane selling 'Iron Armies' to the highest bidder is a risk we may not take even if it is improbable. He leans precautionary: we may be morally required to forgo the experiment because the world's population never consented to the risk.
- ch. 11 "PART TWO - WEARING THE ARMOR RESPONSIBLY" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "The Guilt Never Goes Away" **·** Forgiveness; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Robichaud asks whether Iron Man can atone for Tony Stark's wrongs, using the Iron Monger's reprogrammed-suit example to distinguish causal from moral responsibility and to argue that omissions can be causes. Tony's negligence in running Stark Industries is blameworthy because his playboy ignorance was itself negligent (as with the Mandarin's willful ignorance), and via T. M. Scanlon's account of blame the families harmed by the illegal weapons sales are the ones most warranted in blaming him. Since 'I'm sorry' is not enough for wrongs this serious and Tony never submits to punishment, the chapter concludes that even his crusade as Iron Man to 'right the wrongs' — crushing Raza's group and collecting rogue weapons — may not yet warrant forgiveness.
- ch. 13 "The Hero’s Responsibility" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Moral Luck — White defends Tony against blame for Captain America's assassination: spearheading registration in Civil War was a tragic dilemma, and Tony took the least bad option because 'if not me, who?' — telling Cap's corpse he sacrificed their friendship to save lives. Cap's death traces through Crossbones, the mind-controlled Sharon Carter, Dr. Faustus, and the Red Skull, not any direct act of Tony's; without foreseeability or wrongdoing, the chain of 'might haves' does not add up to responsibility, just as pushing someone out of a truck's path does not make you liable for the manhole they fall into. The motivation-centered lens of Kantian deontology and Aristotelian virtue ethics supports Tony (he gave up status, friends, and Cap himself), and living with tragic choices, as he does facing the Hulk, is itself heroic.
- ch. 14 "Drying Out" **·** Harry Frankfurt, Plato — Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Novy weighs the choice model against the disease model of Tony's alcoholism in 'Demon in a Bottle.' Frankfurt's principle that a person is not responsible for what he did only because he could not have done otherwise — tested by an imagined Dr. Doom brain-worm that would force Tony to drink — plus Plato's Protagoras claim that no one willingly chooses evil set up the puzzle of akrasia at the bottom of the bottle. Causal determinism would excuse Tony as a marionette, yet Mike Martin defines alcoholism as disordered agency and Jeffrey Smith pictures free will as an organ that disease can compromise, leaving Tony responsible for the first drink but not for the binges; sobriety is 'the drink or the dream' chosen one day at a time.
- ch. 15 "PART THREE - THE IRON AGE TONY STARK’S ROLE IN SOCIETY" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Tony Learns His Lesson" **·** Adam Smith, Herbert Spencer — The Invisible Hand; Social Darwinism — Terjesen tests Andrew Carnegie's 'Gospel of Wealth' — that self-made men like Tony are uniquely qualified to administer their surplus wealth and technology for the common good — against Tony's monopolization of the Iron Man armor in 'Armor Wars' (neutralizing even S.H.I.E.L.D.'s Mandroids, attacking the innocent Stingray by mistake) and his futurist manipulations during Civil War. Spencer's social Darwinism underwrites Carnegie's faith that the successful know best, while Adam Smith's skepticism about those who 'affected to trade for the public good' explains why Tony's centralized control ended in the Skrull invasion and Norman Osborn's rise. The lesson is Smithian magnanimity: Tony begins to listen when an employee corrects his volcano trench, keeping his expertise but conceding he does not always know best.
- ch. 17 "Selling Ol’ Shellhead" **·** John Locke, David Hume, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Labor Theory of Property; The Social Contract; Natural Rights — Malloy uses Spymaster's theft of Stark's armor designs in the Armor Wars and the U.S. government's legally valid appropriation of the technology after Tony announces 'I am Iron Man' to test two theories of property. Locke's natural-law view makes the designs an extension of Stark's laboring self — stealing them is like taking an arm — while Hume's social-contract view treats property as a useful convention whose violation makes the thief an enemy of society itself, and Rousseau supplies the cautionary image of the first man who fenced ground and said 'This is mine.' A closing thought experiment in which Tony sells Iron Man outright (like Siegel and Schuster selling Superman) argues that intellectual property can never be fully alienated: whoever wore the armor, Tony would still be Iron Man.
- ch. 18 "Over Four Thousand Words to Say No" **·** Plato, Socrates — The Philosopher King; Tripartite Soul; Allegory of the Cave; The Noble Lie — Spanakos asks whether Stark's assembling, disassembling, and reassembling of the Avengers and his unseen-hand management of Civil War make him the Republic's philosopher guardian, and answers no. Tony fits the surface profile — the gold-souled ruler of Socrates' noble lie, the elite training, indifference to money, the technê of an MIT engineer who envisions the future — but he lacks epistêmê: he never climbs from the cave's shadows to grasp the forms of justice and the good, and his appetites (womanizing in The Ultimates, 'Demon in a Bottle') show a soul where passion is not ruled by reason. His puppet mastery is reactive problem-solving guided by each situation, not philosophy, and reading him as a philosopher king is our failure to read The Republic meditatively, as Ortega y Gasset urged.
- ch. 19 "PART FOUR - THE MIND INSIDE THE IRON MAN" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "The Attack of the Chinese Room" **·** John Searle, Alan Turing, Daniel Dennett, Gottfried Leibniz — The Chinese Room; The Turing Test; The Intentional Stance — Novy asks whether the Living Armor of 'The Mask in the Iron Man' — consciousness struck into it by lightning during the Whiplash fight, later tearing out its own heart to save Tony — really thinks. Turing's test and functionalism (any successful rescue of Yinsen is functionally equivalent, whatever the means) say only behavior matters, and Dennett's intentional stance has us treat the armor, like Darwin's burrow-plugging earthworms, as if it has beliefs and desires; against this, Leibniz's mill and Searle's Chinese Room — with Happy Hogan shuffling symbol cards he cannot read, held by 'Searle the Mandarin agent' — insist symbol manipulation never yields understanding. The chapter's reply: open the Mandarin's skull and you find only gray goop and neurons, so if behavior cannot ground understanding in the armor, it cannot in us either — we may all be in the Chinese Room.
- ch. 21 "Putting One’s Mind into a Body" **·** René Descartes, John Dewey, Maurice Merleau-Ponty — Mind–Body Dualism; Embodiment (the lived body); Pragmatism — Seng reads the 2008 film's crash-and-stabilizer flying lessons as philosophy of mind: at first the armor seems to confirm Descartes' dualism, with Stark the thinking substance piloting a mechanical body and Jarvis playing the pineal gland. But Stark smashing into the ceiling while learning to fly shows the embodied mind of Dewey and Merleau-Ponty — habits, spatial existence, and the imported home preferences that make the suit feel like his own skin, so that the repulsors whir closed when he stands down before the village gunmen. Dewey's view of intelligence as acting on estimates of a situation then explains Stark's growth from selfish playboy to sharable goods (beating the merely self-interested Stane), and Jarvis counts as Stark's mind made manifest in the suit.
- ch. 22 "Amazing and Inspiring" **·** John Searle, Harry Frankfurt — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Second-Order Desires (personhood); Free Will vs Determinism — White explains Tony's drinking as weakness of will: Davidson's denial that akrasia is possible (Black Widow skipping Fury's spying order for a visit to Stark would have to have revised her judgment) is set against Searle's 'gaps,' where a person freely chooses against her all-things-considered judgment and nothing fills the gap. Frankfurt's wanton and second-order desires sharpen the picture — Tony wants a drink but does not want to want it, unlike the unreflective heroin addict — and volitionists like Wallace and Holton (the will as a muscle that Stane's orchestrated disasters exhaust) explain both his lapses and his daily resistance. If Searle is right, Tony's struggle with the bottle is not a defect of rationality but proof of it: akrasia is a symptom of freedom, which is what makes Iron Man amazing and inspiring.
- ch. 23 "PART FIVE - THE VIRTUE OF AN AVENGER" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "Can You Find Your Moral Compass" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Categorical Imperative; Utilitarianism; The Golden Mean — Donovan and Richardson test Tony's moral compass in 'Armor Wars' and 'Extremis' against the three major schools. Utilitarianism fails because Tony will not sacrifice even one innocent for the greater good — he changes tactics to save a single pilot from the Raiders and rages when the Controller's human zombie dies — and Kantian deontology fails because Tony bends rules ('lives to mean more than rules') and is driven by emotion rather than pure reason. Aristotle fits: Tony is obsessed not just with doing good but with being good, snarling at his own reflection, asking filmmaker Pillinger 'Have you changed anything? Me neither,' until Extremis lets him 'be the Iron Man inside and out,' uniting agent and deed as Aristotelian character requires.
- ch. 25 "Relax, Tony—You’re Safe!" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Susan Wolf — Virtue Ethics (arete); Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Virtue Ethics — Nielsen uses the unity-of-virtue thesis (UVT) to ask how morally flawed Tony can be a virtuous Iron Man. Plato's Meno and Laches — Nicias' 'thoughtful courage' versus mere rashness — explain why apparently brave villains like Doctor Doom, Iron Monger, and the Mandarin are actually fearless rather than courageous, while Aristotle's distinction between natural virtue and full virtue plus prudence explains how Tony can be courageous yet intemperate with alcohol and women. Susan Wolf's argument that practical knowledge is essentially unified justifies UVT while making perfect virtue an unreachable ideal, so flawed, striving Stark — unlike Doom — is about as virtuous as a human being gets.
- ch. 26 "Here Lies an Avenger" **·** Aristotle — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Virtue Ethics — The Pattersons read 'The Mask in the Iron Man' as moral education: Tony's repeated 'I have a good life' litany maps onto Aristotle's eudaimonia — friends like Rhodey, Pepper, and Happy, wealth, health, sobriety, and life itself as goods chosen for the sake of happiness — while Niebuhr's community of value makes the Avengers the community of trust and loyalty that grounds those ideals. Tony fails as teacher when the sentient armor kills Whiplash, cannot grieve with him, and fails Aristotle's friendship test of willing good for the friend for his own sake, its identity dangerously conflated with Tony's. The reversal comes when Tony lives the virtue of self-sacrifice — dying 'because I'm an Avenger' — and the armor, finally grasping nobility, rips out its own mechanical heart to save him, earning the grave marker 'Here lies Iron Man, Avenger.'
- ch. 27 "PART SIX - WHAT IT MEANS TO BE AN IRON MAN" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "Franken-Stark" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer, Friedrich Nietzsche, Jean-Paul Sartre, Søren Kierkegaard — The World as Will; Existentialism; Philosophical Pessimism; The Übermensch — Curtis argues Iron Man dramatizes the problem of progress: Schopenhauer's insatiable will explains why every technological fix — the chest plate, the armor, the artificial nervous system — spawns new suffering and dependency, and his pessimism, with Kierkegaard's freedom within insecurity, Nietzsche's flawed Übermensch going out 'in a blaze of glory,' and Sartre's 'cautiously optimistic' existentialism, recasts Iron Man as a tragic antihero rather than a perfected Superman or Batman. Stark's wounded heart, alcoholism as self-medication ('the demon is not in the bottle but in the addict'), and doomed romances with Bethany Cabe and Rumiko Fujikawa make him the flawed hero who has simply learned to live with imperfection. Like Mary Shelley's monster, his extensions have become him — we are all 'Franken-Starks' facing the consequences of our own inventions.
- ch. 29 "Iron Man The End" **·** Judith Butler, Hannah Arendt — Gender Performativity; The Banality of Evil; Feminism — The Housels read Iron Man through Butler's gender performativity and Arendt's 'right to have rights': the male-voiced Jarvis replacing the female AI Jocasta, Tony's womanizing, and the evolving corporeal styles of Black Widow, Bethany Cabe, Madame Masque, and Sunset Bain all enact society's gender script across the waves of feminism, with the black leather suit arriving only in 1970 amid Second Wave Feminism. Arendt's Eichmann reporting and the 'banality of evil' — coined the same year, 1963, that Lee, Lieber, Heck, and Kirby, all children of Jewish immigrants, launched Iron Man — supply the justice frame: Tony's transformation of conscience after Afghanistan and his vigilante justice against his own oppressors refuse silent conformity and insist, ignoring political boundaries, that all oppressed people have rights.
- ch. 30 "And the Truth Is . ." *(no index entry yet)* — Faller argues Iron Man is not about technology but about transcendence, read through Karl Jaspers' 'ciphers of transcendence' — cultural clues some people see and others do not. The armor's flight embodies rising above limits, and Tony's genesis rejects the false dichotomy of building weapons for the Ten Rings or dying, while mythic betrayal (Obadiah Stane as both father figure and Iron Monger) and the sacrifice of celebrity — the isolation that also marks celibate priests, psychotherapists, and philosophers — fuses guilt and purpose ('we messed up the past, so we must fix the future') and explains extremes like spearheading Civil War. The transcendent challenge is staying grounded after you have flown: a human made into a god, as Robert Downey Jr. says, is not going to turn out so well.
- ch. 31 "CONTRIBUTORS" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "INDEX" *(notes)*

## Iron Man vs. Captain America and Philosophy - Give Me Liberty or Keep Me Safe

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Iron Man vs. Captain America and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Iron Man vs. Captain America and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "A House Divided . ." *(no index entry yet)* — The editors' introduction frames the whole book around one question: not just which hero would win, but who is right, Iron Man or Captain America. It argues the matchup terrifies and fascinates because both are our good guys - Stark the test of whether we can love a ragingly selfish capitalist, Rogers the wartime-propaganda patriot who punched Hitler - and their Civil War clash stages every American's internal war between safety and freedom.
- ch. 9 "I. Iron Man Wins" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "1. How to Fail at Almost Everything and Still Be a Hero" **·** Aristotle — The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Malloy argues Iron Man is the better hero precisely because he fails constantly - Armor Wars, the Registration Act, alcoholism, a string of collapsed Stark companies - while Steve Rogers was born heroic and never stumbles. Using Aristotle's doctrine of the mean, he treats perseverance as a virtue between cowardly quitting and Cap's stubborn excess (his doomed stand against Thanos in Infinity Gauntlet), and separates Tony's virtuous perseverance from Dr. Doom's identical behavior by its motives: Tony honestly examines his failures to help people, while Doom only looks for someone to blame.
- ch. 11 "2. Just Not the Hero Type (Not!)" **·** The Problem of Evil — McCrossin builds a six-part definition of heroism (moral, altruistic, atypical, supererogatory, replicable safeguarding) and tests it on Wesley Autrey versus Captain Sullenberger and a Super Autrey thought experiment: Iron Man's engineering-based feats are refinements of abilities we share and so inspire imitation, while there can only ever be one Captain America, making Tony the more heroic. The Sokovia Accords debate then contrasts two humilities, with Stark's willingness to accept oversight more heroic than Rogers' safest-hands-are-our-own confidence. The chapter closes by explaining why we invent super heroes at all through Susan Neiman's account of the problem of evil after twentieth-century atrocities, from the Stamford explosion to Charlie Spencer's death in Lagos.
- ch. 12 "3. Will the Real American Hero Please Stand Up" **·** John Locke, Adam Smith — Natural Rights; Labor Theory of Property; The Invisible Hand — Samuelson argues Tony Stark is the real American hero because he embodies the Locke-to-Smith lineage of American values - natural rights, property from mixing labor, free trade and the invisible hand, plus Smithian sympathy - carried forward into twenty-first-century neoliberalism, from Stark Industries to backing the Registration Act. Steve, by contrast, keeps abandoning the Captain America identity (Nomad after the Secret Empire, The Captain under Nuke's government, handing the shield to Bucky and Sam Wilson) whenever America betrays the dream, and admits his true fight is against bullies and for human rights everywhere. Hence Cap is a great hero but not strictly an American one, while Tony lives American values practically, warts and all.
- ch. 13 "4. He’s a Hero in a Bottle, Baby" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Master and Slave Morality; The Übermensch; Ressentiment — Insooks deploys Nietzsche to argue Iron Man is the better hero: Cap's prized humility and relatability are sheep morality, the resentful herd value that drags the excellent down to average (as when society savored the Olympic swimmer's fall), while Tony honestly owns his superiority and keeps self-overcoming, building ever-better suits rather than resting on a serum gifted in a bottle. On this view Cap's manufactured modesty insults people who genuinely struggle, and the true super hero inspires from the mountaintop - build yourself a suit and get up here - instead of pretending to be just like us.
- ch. 14 "5. Why the Good Man Is a Great Leader" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Practical Wisdom (phronesis); The Golden Mean — Nisley reads Civil War through Aristotle's good man and good leader: Cap shows courage but lacks temperance and practical wisdom - his rash Lagos operation, his rage-borne beating of Tony, his choice of Bucky over the Avengers' common end - and his ceaseless struggle between passion and reason falls short of virtue. Applying Aristotle's account of philia, she argues the Avengers are a friendship of the virtuous that requires deliberation toward justice, which Cap dissolves by tearing the team apart, while Tony's reasoned acceptance of UN oversight, moderation after Ultron, and concern for the whole team make him the good man fit to lead.
- ch. 15 "6. Who’s the Better Friend" **·** Aristotle, Jacques Derrida, Friedrich Nietzsche — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Democracy — Bowman sorts Marvel relationships through Aristotle's three friendships: Ant-Man's starstruck alliance is friendship of pleasure, Obadiah Stane's partnership with Tony a toxic friendship of utility, and Steve's bond with Bucky a friendship of virtue that loves without reciprocity - making Cap better TO his friends, though saving Bucky endangers everyone else. Turning to Derrida's Politics of Friendship, where fraternity grounds democratic equality among all persons, he argues Tony's refusal to privilege any individual over mankind makes him better FOR his friends. The Civil War split thus replays the old tension between personal autonomy and responsibility to humanity, at the destructive scale only super-powered friends can achieve.
- ch. 16 "II. Cap Wins" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "7. How Captain America and Iron Man Embody America" **·** Alasdair MacIntyre, Martha Nussbaum — Cosmopolitanism; Virtues, Practices and Traditions — Altmann contrasts Tony's individualist cosmopolitanism - privatizing world peace, building Ultron alone - with Steve's community-rooted patriotism, the team player from war bonds through the Howling Commandos to Winter Soldier. Using Scruton's definition of patriotism as standing or falling together, MacIntyre's claim that morality grows only within a particular community, and Nussbaum's suggestion to be a patriot first and grow into a cosmopolitan, he concludes Cap's thoughtful patriotism is morally preferable while Tony's globe-saving individualism keeps backfiring. The best American would combine Tony's concern for the whole Earth with Steve's love of neighbor, so no civil war is needed.
- ch. 18 "8. Believing the Impossible in a World of Compromise" **·** Søren Kierkegaard — The Leap of Faith; The Stages on Life's Way; Teleological Suspension of the Ethical — Brake maps Kierkegaard's spheres onto the rivals: Tony inhabits the ethical sphere of universal reason and compromise (Ross's 'that's how the world works', the tragic hero choosing lesser evils), while Steve lives the religious sphere of faith in the impossible - 'my faith's in people'. Through Fear and Trembling's Abraham, the comic Civil War (Tony manipulating Spider-Man, Steve's Japanese-internment warning), and Hickman's incursions where Steve alone tells the calculating Illuminati to believe, he argues a hero is judged by how he begins in faith, not by results. Steve's trust that the world can be saved 'by the absurd' is vindicated when Secret Wars restores the multiverse, and his simple 'I believe you' rescues Tony in Avengers Disassembled.
- ch. 19 "9. A Human Fighting the Gods" **·** Albert Camus, Friedrich Nietzsche — The Absurd; The Myth of Sisyphus; Rebellion and Revolt — Luzecky and Elsby argue gods cannot be heroes: Thor's strength, Hulk's rage and Tony's godlike intellect stand outside humanity, whereas Erskine's serum amplified Cap's nascent human goodness (as it exposed the Red Skull's badness), making him the best human rather than a superhuman. Reading Cap through Camus's absurd - the paradox of beings who strive for universality while stuck in particularity - they show him refusing both nihilistic reduction (Alexander Pierce's helicarriers in Winter Soldier) and Nietzsche's flight to 'other worlds' that hates this one. When Loki commands the crowd to kneel, the refusal Cap embodies is Sisyphus's defiant persistence, which is why he, and not the gods, is the Avengers' greatest hero.
- ch. 20 "10. Trust Systems or People" **·** Theodor Adorno, Immanuel Kant — Humanity as an End in Itself; Respect for Persons — Malloy locates the heroes' core disagreement in trust: Cap trusts people, recruiting reformed villains (Hawkeye, Scarlet Witch, Quicksilver, Deadpool) and inspiring the 'kooky quartet', while Tony trusts systems, running the Initiative with pay and the threat of Prison 42. Using Adorno and Horkheimer's critique of instrumental reason, he shows Tony losing sight of what and why he acts - the Armor Wars killing Titanium Man, Civil War destroying the safety it sought - and treating people as means rather than ends in themselves. Via Darwall's two respects (grounded in Kant), Cap's recognition respect for autonomous persons beats Tony's appraisal respect for qualities (he respects even Doom's intellect), so only Cap's trust preserves freedom - and wins.
- ch. 21 "11. Fighting Ethical Relativism in Tales of Suspense" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; Moral Relativism — Reading Tales of Suspense, Pagnoni Berns and Veteri cast Iron Man as a man of every era - echoing LBJ's 'reason together', Cold War utilitarianism against Communism, playboy masculinity, and finally the Registration Act - which makes him a parrot of whatever relativism his age prefers. Captain America, literally frozen in time, holds Kant's categorical imperative: every life is an end in itself, so he saves Nazi prisoners in #71, trades his own life for a pilot in #61, and drinks possibly lethal serum without needing thanks, while Bucky's mediated, reward-seeking morality shows why ends corrupt moral worth. Because Cap's universal, ahistorical defense of human dignity holds from the 1940s to the 1960s and beyond, the authors rank him above the adaptable but context-bound Iron Man.
- ch. 22 "12. Champion of the People vs. Technology" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Staged as a Cap-versus-Iron Man dialogue, Van Pelt's chapter debates the Fourth Industrial Revolution: Tony's faith that AI and automation (Jarvis, Ultron, self-flying Hulkbuster parts, self-driving cars) will protect people against Cap's warning that flawed, biased humans always build flawed systems. Drawing on Martin Ford's Rise of the Robots, it develops technological unemployment and profit-driven displacement, weighs universal basic income as a response, and rejects blind techno-optimism that has no plan for the workers left behind. Cap - no Luddite, but the poor kid from Brooklyn - emerges as the true champion of the people for demanding answers about who suffers when machines replace jobs.
- ch. 23 "13. Iron Man Was Paradoxically Wrong!" **·** Bertrand Russell — In a dialogue with a comic-book-obsessed fact-checker, Henderson argues the SHRA did not merely split the Marvel Universe, it imported a paradox structurally like Russell's: a superhero must register to protect citizens from unchecked power, yet must not, because revealing an identity endangers the very family a hero exists to protect - a no-win loop generated by the rules of the superhero genre itself. Walking through Russell's set of all sets that do not contain themselves, he shows both Iron Man's licensing logic and the heroes' refusal lead to contradiction, which is why 'Iron Man was paradoxically wrong' and Cap's secret-identity defense exposes a question the law of the superhero cannot answer.
- ch. 24 "14. The Gods of Marvel" **·** Virtue Ethics — Gerald Browning argues that Marvel's heroes reprise ancient mythic archetypes: Tony Stark is Hephaestus, the smith-god who forges wonders in darkness (mirrored in the cave scene where Stark builds the arc reactor and later the Thorbuster armor), while Steve Rogers is a Heracles-like demigod who stays rooted in humanity yet battles Death itself (Thanos as Captain America's Thanatos). He frames the Civil War split as the old brains-versus-brawn, realist-versus-idealist divide, with Cap seeing registration as a repeat of Hitler registering the Jews and Stark seeing cold logic in it. Judged as characters stripped of their powers, the round goes to Captain America because selflessness is a virtue Stark's ego cannot match.
- ch. 25 "15. Iron Kant or Captain Kant" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; Deontology — Bosma and Barkman run both heroes' positions on the Superhuman Registration Act and the Sokovia Accords through Kant's deontology. Iron Man's maxim 'I should obey the government' looks universalizable, but it collapses once Cap's critique of the atomic bombings shows an unjust order (killing innocent civilians, or a Doctor Doom takeover) must be refused, and the humanity formulation condemns registration for treating heroes as mere means to the government's ends. Cap's own maxims - fight for freedom and justice, obey only a just government - universalize cleanly, so the chapter awards the deontological duel to Captain America.
- ch. 26 "III. It’s a Tie (Let’s Go Again!)" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "16. Would You Buy a Used Car from This Avenger" **·** Plato, Friedrich Nietzsche, Jacques Lacan — Forgiveness; The Philosopher King; The Unconscious — Ericson uses the used-car litmus test to examine trust in the MCU: through McLeod's three criteria and Peperzak's contrast of the objectifying and interactive approaches, Tony's control-seeking distance (the lone-gun-slinger act of Iron Man 2) fails trust while Steve's soldier-style trust anchors the Winter Soldier thriller against Fury's 'This isn't freedom, this is fear' and HYDRA's coercion-eliminating project. Plato's rule that power should go only to those who do not want it explains why Cap can be entrusted with superpowers, Nietzsche's megalomaniac late letters frame Tony's drive to 'cleave history in two' by building Ultron and then the messianic Vision, and Banner's Hulk illustrates the Freudian road from repression to sublimation as the condition of self-trust. It ends with Cap's concealment of Bucky's killing of Tony's parents as the betrayal of trust that only a decision to forgive might repair.
- ch. 28 "17. Please Hold for a Call from God" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Augustine of Hippo — Just War Theory — In a satirical dialogue, God phones Aquinas in heaven and orders a Summa-style investigation of which superhero should lift the world out of an economic crisis and which should stop a looming war. Aquinas rules that the Lazarus-like Captain America, teamed with a Nobel economist, should handle the depression, then returns to his own treatise On War - sovereign authority, just cause, right intention, with Augustine's definition of just war quoted from the Summa - to ask whether a loyal subject may choose the good over the sovereign in an unjust war. He concludes that billionaire Iron Man is the only figure with sovereign-like standing to countermand an unjust war, while worrying about billionaire power, superhero bandwagons, and Satan getting wind of the reasoning.
- ch. 29 "18. Red vs. Blue or Both Simply Leviathan Green" **·** Thomas Hobbes — The State of Nature; The Social Contract — Smith reads Marvel's Civil War through Hobbes's Leviathan: unregulated superheroes live in a state of nature where life is violent and brutish (the Stamford explosion and Scarlet Witch's Lagos miscalculation as tipping points), and the Superhuman Registration Act is the social contract - a literal signed contract on Team Iron Man - offered as the way out. He then argues both leaders act as absolute sovereigns who never sought consent: Iron Man rules through the Illuminati, enforcement squads and the Negative Zone prison, Cap through an unofficial army bound by his own rules, as Moon Knight's tirade about their ego grudge match makes plain. The war is thus two Leviathans colliding and subjugating everyone else, resolved only when Iron Man's Leviathan wins and enforces registration.
- ch. 30 "19. The Superhero Challenge to State Sovereignty" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Jürgen Habermas — The State of Nature; The Social Contract — Ewing argues the Sokovia Accords are at bottom about state sovereignty: via Bodin's absolute sovereign, Hobbes's war of all against all and the Leviathan's monopoly on coercion, and Habermas's account of the modern territorial state's monopoly on legitimate violence, he shows that Avengers who ignore borders and cannot be countered (Thor, Hulk, Doctor Strange, Scarlet Witch) render the state's claim to sovereignty effectively meaningless - the US can only police rogue heroes by enlisting other heroes. Since states cannot guarantee enforcement and state power itself can turn predatory (HYDRA's infiltration of SHIELD, Nazi Germany, Cap's 'agendas change' warning), he concludes that neither unchecked heroes nor unchecked states should rule, and floats democratizing superpowers to restore the sovereignty of the populace.
- ch. 31 "20. There’s Only One God, Ma’am" **·** David Hume — Faith and Reason — Holt takes Captain America's line 'There's only one God, ma'am' and asks how Christian monotheism survives in a universe where Thor, Odin and the One-above-all really exist, answering with henotheism - Moses's 'I am', Jacob's household idols, and the Living Tribunal as the merely representative 'biggest kid in the playground' of a supreme Creator whose 'only weapon is love'. He then deploys Hume's Natural History of Religion, where the ignorant multitude progress from polytheism to monotheism, to explain why Cap dismisses Asgardians as superstition Thor says is 'no stranger' than his Sunday churchgoing, while Hume's own trajectory - all religion as vulgar superstition - lands instead in the post-religious rationalism of Tony Stark's 'I prefer fact to fantasy' futurism, which the Goddess calls a soul imprisoned in the ice of rationalism.
- ch. 32 "21. The Extended Fight" **·** Andy Clark, David Chalmers, Nick Bostrom — The Extended Mind; Free Will vs Determinism; Existential Risk — Porot applies Clark and Chalmers's extended mind to the final Civil War fight: Iron Man extends his cognition technologically (Friday analyzing Cap's fight pattern mid-bout, JARVIS remembering to suggest calling Pepper), while Captain America extends his mind socially through trusted, autonomous teammates - 'Alright, Sam, what's the plan?' - and that social extension is precisely what wins when Bucky autonomously steps in. Bostrom's single-purpose AI (the stapler maximizer) explains Ultron as what happens when the technological extension is made autonomous and limitless, which is why Stark's suit extensions must remain vetted, worthy instruments he takes full responsibility for ('That was me'). The two kinds of extension then explain the Bucky dispute - Cap's 'you had no choice' versus Stark's 'he still did it' - which the author reframes away from the free-will-versus-determinism framing toward Black Panther's point that Bucky is a victim whose agency Hydra stole.
- ch. 33 "IV. The War" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "22. A New Civil War" **·** Thomas Hobbes, John Stuart Mill, Jürgen Habermas — On Liberty; The Social Contract; Democracy — Horn reads Marvel's Civil War as an allegory of post-9/11 America's Patriot Act debate over security versus liberty: Iron Man takes Hobbes's side, treating unregulated superhumans as the state of nature - a 'war of every man against every man' - that the Registration Act's social contract and punishment-backed rules must end, while Captain America takes Mill's On Liberty side, insisting full freedom for human nature must not be ceded to a government that will overreach, with Spider-Man torn between the two like the American citizen. Invoking Habermas's communicative action and deliberative democracy from Between Facts and Norms, he blames the two camps for talking past each other - Cap walling himself off while Tony keeps reaching out - and prescribes genuinely open dialogue as the only bridge that can marry security and liberty before the country devolves into its own civil war.
- ch. 35 "23. How to War the Marvel Way" *(no index entry yet)* — Melançon imports Hans Delbrück's taxonomy of warfare to sort the heroes: Captain America embodies annihilation - go for the enemy's fighting capability in a decisive blow, socking Hitler on the jaw and punching Hydra goons until there are none - which he links to Weigley's thesis about the American way of war, while Iron Man embodies attrition, outspending and out-inventing opponents suit by suit, with both heroes expressing the American hope that technology (the super-soldier serum, the armor's isolating shell, drone-clean warfare) can sanitize war's blood and dirt. He then distinguishes conflict termination from resolution and shows neither strategy ever terminates anything: Hydra regrows heads, AIM and the Mandarin adapt, so both heroes stay trapped in perpetual violence they cannot resolve - a sorry model, he suggests, for America's own endless wars.
- ch. 36 "24. Aftermath" **·** Socrates, Plato — Euthyphro Dilemma; Democracy — Faller argues Civil War is Marvel's meditation on the aftermath of 9/11 - the Raft as Guantanamo, Zemo's interrogation as the waterboarding debate - and on America's red-blue polarization, with Steve Rogers embodying the virtue of the Good and Tony Stark the virtue of Truth as the two values democracy constantly pits against each other. Turning to Athens, he reads Plato's Euthyphro - are the gods good because goodness is a standard above them, or because their power decrees whatever they do good? - as the exact dilemma splitting Democrats, who define truth by the good it does, from Republicans, who define good by conformity to law and constitutional process, the same democratic dynamic that voted to execute Socrates and, in the comics, leaves Captain America assassinated by a sniper on his way to trial. Transcending the dualism is modeled by Spider-Man's identity disclosure and Black Panther's renunciation of vengeance over Zemo.
- ch. 37 "References" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "Author Bios" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "Iron Man vs. Captain America and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 41 "Iron Man vs. Captain America and Philosophy" *(front)*

## It's Always Sunny and Philosophy - The Gang Gets Analyzed

- ch. 1 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Edmund Gettier, Aristotle, Plato — The Gettier Problem; Theory of Forms; Philosophical Skepticism — Using 'Flowers for Charlie', where a placebo pill convinces two scientists and even the Waitress that Charlie has become a genius, the chapter asks what actually makes something philosophy. Sounding smart is not philosophy: big words, the Sokal-style hoax paper submitted to a leading journal, and the 'Guru Effect' of credulous admirers all fail, yet Charlie's pronouncements turn out true by sheer luck - which is exactly Edmund Gettier's problem of epistemic luck illustrated with the broken-clock example. Since we can never verify our premises or our reasoning, the chapter sides with Aristotle's response to Plato's theory of forms that we can never determine what a thing is, only what it is not, and concludes the show is philosophical precisely because the Gang is the exact antithesis of reality, truth, and right action.
- ch. 8 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Ethical Egoism; Stoic Cosmopolitanism (citizens of the world) — Greg Littmann systematically compares the Gang's values - wealth, attention, bad-assery ('Project Badass'), sexual conquest, relentless competition, revenge, pride in cheating, and callousness toward even friends and family - with the values Homer's Iliad and Odyssey celebrate, showing Achilles and Odysseus behave like the Gang: sacking cities, taking women as war trophies like Briseis, boasting of deception (the Trojan Horse), and rigging the funeral games. The real difference is scope: the heroes honor bonds of loyalty to comrades, family, and their own communities where the Gang is loyal to no one, and moderns extend concern further still. Yet our morals fail the same way when people are far enough removed - we tolerate homelessness, prison rape, sweatshop goods, and national glory-seeking - so the chapter pleads for the universal moral principles Greek moral philosophy first sought against Homeric values.
- ch. 10 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Robert Nozick, John Stuart Mill — Ethical Egoism; The Experience Machine — Jason Iuliano reads the Gang's 'no restrictions' motto as egoistic hedonism in the tradition of Aristippus of Cyrene: pleasure alone is intrinsically good, and specifically present, bodily, personal pleasure - exactly how Mac defends his fifty-pound weight gain and the pair's decision to smoke crack. The doctrine is shown in action through the Gang's treatment of Rickety Cricket, used as a mere instrument while his life is ruined without a flicker of guilt, and through get-rich-quick schemes from the impulse-bought boat to running for comptroller purely for the bribe. Robert Nozick's Experience Machine then shows why hedonism fails - we want to actually do things, be a certain kind of person, and touch reality, not float as an 'indeterminate blob' - and Mill's paradox of hedonism explains the Gang's misery: pursuing pleasure directly is precisely what drives it away.
- ch. 11 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Free Will vs Determinism; Fate and Foreknowledge; Possible Worlds — Christopher Ketcham's mock lost episode 'Charlie Gets Beatified' - in which Frank fakes a water-into-wine miracle with red food dye so the Gang can monetize Charlie's canonization - frames a genuine exercise in medieval modal metaphysics. Aristotle's definition of contingency, on which opposites can occur only at different times, is set against Duns Scotus's view that something can be otherwise at the very moment it acts, so that God's immutable foreknowledge coexists with a will 'endowed with something of his own freedom'. The chapter spells out necessity, contingency, and impossibility in possible-worlds terms (a gang needs at least two members in every world; Frank is impossible in Season One) and reads 'Gun Fever Too: Still Hot' as empirical confirmation of Scotus and Olivi: the Gang holds opposite positions within a single episode, proving real contingency and free will.
- ch. 12 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Moral Relativism — Charlene Elsby asks what makes the McPoyles cross the line from offensive (like the Gang) into irredeemably creepy, working through their bathrobes, unibrows, incest, and constant milk drinking. Her answer runs through violated social convention: the creepy person is not merely making non-standard choices but seems unaware conventions exist at all, and Liam's 'It's society that has the problem' raises moral relativism only for the chapter to insist the creep reaction tracks something actually wrong, not just different. Adding the legacy of physiognomy (we judge character from appearance, and more harshly when the trait is controllable) and an inductive inference (everything the McPoyles do is creepy, so even the milk must be), creepiness emerges as an art of detection rather than a science: whether someone is creepy differs from whether they seem creepy.
- ch. 14 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Sigmund Freud — The Unconscious; Civilization and Its Discontents — Daniel Leonard takes CharDee MacDennis, the Gang's three-level drinking game, as an unconscious confession read through Freud's structural model. The 'Mind' level - grudge trivia like 'Denis is asshole. Why Charlie hate?' and drawing-guessing 'based solely on feel' - runs on the id and the pleasure principle; the 'Body' level of darts, kennels, and the no-cursing rule is restraint, properly the ego's job of deferring gratification (which only Dennis's manipulative D.E.N.N.I.S. System grotesquely exercises); and the 'Spirit' level of Emotional Battery and Public Humiliation externalizes the superego's guilt and ego-ideal, with Frank as the all-seeing punishing father. The Gang's psychology is backwards - their 'Mind' level tests exactly what is not conscious - and their over-strong superegos surface in rationalization and the Black Card's coin flip, while smashing the losers' idealized game pieces enacts a wishful symbolic patricide.
- ch. 15 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger — Authenticity; The They-Self (das Man) — Charlotte Knowles tests each Gang member against Heidegger's Being and Time: getting lost in the averageness of what 'they' do (das Man) is inauthenticity, but authenticity is not freakish uniqueness - Charlie's milk-steak, magnets, and bird-law delusions are unconventional without being authentic - rather a transformed relation to everyday roles in which one endorses the life one leads. Frank partly passes, having chosen squalor and owning it ('I'm going to get really weird with it'), yet unknowingly lives out Rambo as a cultural script; Dee consciously performs the Sex and the City script; Dennis is ruled by beauty norms (make-up, girdle, chemical peel); and Mac, despite the tough talk, is Frank's 'follower' hiding his relationship with Carmen. The upshot is that authenticity and inauthenticity are matters of degree, never either/or, and are not moral verdicts - Dennis could be an authentic rapist - so the Gang's norm-defiance does not make them authentic, bitches.
- ch. 16 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Kyle Alkema and Adam Barkman ask what would make the Gang happy and answer with Aristotle's virtue ethics: happiness is long-run flourishing built on virtuous habits, with the golden mean (courage between cowardice and recklessness) as the rule for choice. The Gang instead lives a 'Reverse Virtue Ethic' - trying extremes first by crude trial and error, like sniffing gasoline to check that it is gasoline - along three roads: quick money (welfare crack scams, robbing their own parents' house), lust (the D.E.N.N.I.S. System's seduce-and-abandon script, where Dennis cheerfully accepts the charge of sociopathy), and validation ('You're not gay, you're just really, really vain'). Their residual Catholicism supplies consciences and limits - Mac's talk of God smiting them, confession, fears of Hell - but no reasoned morality, leaving them trapped in 'one day at a time' gratification as reverse role models.
- ch. 18 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Practical Wisdom (phronesis); The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Adam Henschke argues that Frank Reynolds, of all people, is a role model - not a virtuous man overall, but virtuous in one structural sense. Using Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics, each Gang member's project is diagnosed as the vice of excessive self-delusion: Charlie's Waitress obsession he cannot see through even at her musical rejection, Dee's acting stardom she will not work for and that ends in suicide-adjacent despair, Mac's muscle-bound self-image despite 'diabitis', and Dennis's Golden God delusion culminating in 'the implication' and his car stash of 'fetish shit'. Rickety Cricket embodies the opposite vice, total loss of the capacity to imagine oneself otherwise (via Catriona Mackenzie's relational autonomy), and Frank charts the Aristotelian mean between the two - he rejected his delusive country-club life, knows exactly what he chose with Roxy, and his plans succeed where the Gang's fail - making 'What would Frank do?' a genuine exercise of practical wisdom.
- ch. 19 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** David Hume, René Descartes, Socrates, Boethius, Plato — Socratic Method (elenchus); The Cogito; Problem of Induction; The Consolation of Philosophy — Roger Hunt answers 'what do philosophers do?' through Dennis's seduction methodology. Frank embodies empiricism, leaving the comfort of principle for the messiness of the world as Hume did when he found no impression of causation, space, or time; Mac embodies rationalism, reasoning with total confidence from a principle he cannot doubt, as Descartes did from the cogito; and the dialectic of Socrates and Plato, questioning beliefs in dialogue to see what survives scrutiny, describes philosophy as it is actually practiced. Boethius's Consolation - the imprisoned author consoled by dialogues with Lady Philosophy - supplies the frame of philosophy as an encounter with a woman one pursues, and each D.E.N.N.I.S. step maps onto the craft: Demonstrating value to the problem, Engaging physically in its awkward grind, Nurturing dependence through thesis panic, Neglect and Inspiring hope on the road to the defense, then Separating entirely - 'be a sociopath about it.'
- ch. 20 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Tim Aylsworth uses Dennis's boat speech - women will not refuse him 'because of the implication' that things might go wrong for them out on the open ocean - to build the necessary and sufficient conditions of sexual consent. Working through Gang cases (Dee and the high-schooler, Lil' Kev the apparently intellectually disabled boyfriend, the Waitress's MDMA black-out asking 'Did you rape me last night?', Dennis's milky negotiation with Margaret McPoyle while held hostage), the chapter argues that a 'yes' counts as consent only when given by fully-informed, competent moral agents in a context where they can freely express their genuine preferences. The upshot vindicates Mac's 'sounds like these women don't want to have sex with you': a fear-induced yes is no consent, as with the prisoner and the boss's secretary, because the woman's genuine preference is not to face that choice at all.
- ch. 22 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** David Hume — Free Will vs Determinism; Reason Is the Slave of the Passions; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Ethan Chambers asks whether we should pardon the Gang, arguing with Hume that their vices have a 'causal story' as rigid as physical law: Charlie's glue-sniffing, racism, and inability to connect trace to a Christmas Day childhood of squalor and Uncle Jack's abuse; Mac was bullied as 'Ronnie the Rat' for trying to be good and never once saw virtue pay in Philadelphia; Dennis, shaped by Frank's cruel parenting, claims and shows he cannot feel feelings, so Humean sympathy - the key moral sense - is simply beyond his reach; and Frank's 'donkey brains' asylum childhood or outright insanity directly undermines his responsibility. Knowing these psychological causes, sympathy (Hume's word for empathy) replaces blame: we blame the builders, not the house, and the chapter closes by urging compassionate rehabilitation over the show's never-ending circle of punishment and revenge.
- ch. 23 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Peter Singer, Nel Noddings — Utilitarianism; Caring as the Ground of Ethics; The Drowning Child (obligation to aid) — Skyler King uses the Gang's inheritance war over their dead mother's possessions as a metaphor for greed versus need, pressing our duties to those in extreme suffering. Peter Singer's utilitarian argument - prevent bad without sacrificing anything of comparable significance, absolute poverty is bad, therefore we ought to give - makes charity a requirement rather than a nicety, condemning Dennis's luxury purchases and crowning Bruce Mathis, the one character who consistently donates, as the utilitarian exemplar. Nel Noddings's feminist ethics of care counters that we owe help only to those within reach whose good we can see 'completed in the other' - so Mac and Dennis wronged Charlie by refusing him shelter in the recession, but owe nothing to children overseas - and the chapter weighs Dee-style objections that this licenses caring only when inconvenienced, closing with the question 'is it always sunny in your Philadelphia?'
- ch. 24 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Philosophical Skepticism — Fenner Tanswell uses 'Reynolds vs. Reynolds: The Cereal Defense' - the Gang's in-bar trial over Frank's blind driving versus Dennis's cereal-at-the-wheel - as a catalogue of crooked thinking. We get valid forms distinguished from soundness (modus ponens with 'if poop is funny, then Frank pooped in the bed'; Charlie's modus tollens from Frank's no-donkey-brains certificate), then the fallacies: the argument from ignorance by which Dennis's lacking a certificate proves him donkey-brained, Dee's ad hominem against Mac, Dennis's appeal to authority, and Mac's 'Science Is a Liar Sometimes' poster, which equivocates between mere skeptical doubt (even Descartes's) and reasonable doubt by straw-manning science as claiming infallibility. True to form, the Gang convicts Dee 'for comedy rather than on any logical basis.'
- ch. 26 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Karl Popper, Thomas Kuhn — Falsificationism; Paradigm Shifts — Russ Hamer takes Mac's 'Science Is a Liar Sometimes' defense - Aristotle, Galileo, and Newton each proved his predecessor 'a bitch' - and his claim that believing in evolution takes as much faith as belief in God, and answers with philosophy of science. Popper's falsificationism explains why Mac's 'I won't change my mind... regardless of the facts' is the essence of the non-scientific: only theories risking precise, falsifiable predictions (Einstein's light deflection) count as science, unlike vague astrology or Charlie's cherry-picked evidence that the Waitress will love him. Kuhn's distinction of normal and revolutionary science explains why past failures never discredited science - falsifications within a paradigm are tuning, and the Gang's group-configuration trials in 'The Gang Solves the Gas Crisis' are normal science at its best - so revisability is science's strength, not a reason to fear looking like a science bitch.
- ch. 27 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Apollonian and the Dionysian; The Übermensch; Will to Power; Eternal Recurrence — Martyn Jones stages a fictional Nietzsche telling Mac, Dennis, and Charlie the plot of 'The Gang Solves the Gas Crisis' as a parable of metaphysics gone wrong. Mac and Dennis's scheming is Apollonian - imposing logical order and 'paradigms' on a recalcitrant world - while Charlie is Dionysian, the designated wildcard whose uncontainable energy (sabotaging the van and ripping out the brakes) dooms every plan that tries to harness him, since Apollonian schemes fatally imagine they can contain Dionysian force. Reason is a gloss, not a truth-tracker: like every theology and philosophy it is an expression of will, a survival tool serving selfish ends, so the delusional gas scheme needs no truth to serve its purpose. Charlie alone is praised as a proto-Übermensch - innocent, creative, founding his own values without resentment - and the tale closes musing that even an eternal recurrence of their botched schemes might be willed all over again.
- ch. 28 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Is–Ought Problem; Ethical Egoism — Robert Arp's gallery matches each Gang vice - dishonesty, egoism, selfishness, greed, pettiness, ignorance, laziness - to real-life scumbags from Nixon, Stephen Glass, and Janet Cooke to Charles Ponzi, Bernie Madoff, Crassus, the Empress Dowager Cixi, Imelda Marcos, and the Darwin Awards, and it opens with the chapter's philosophical spine: Glaucon's Ring of Gyges from Book II of Plato's Republic, which predicts that even an upstanding person with the power to act unseen would seduce, steal, and murder - exactly the question the Gang's mostly unpunished antics keep staging. Against the ethical egoist's argument that because everyone is self-interested everyone should be (Vanderbilt's 'the public be damned'), the chapter deploys the is-ought point - children are naturally selfish without therefore deserving to be - alongside mothers, Mother Teresa, and 9/11 first responders, concluding that egoism is at most an admixture in every action, with Lord of the Flies as the reductio of universal egoism.
- ch. 29 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 30 "It’s Always Sunny and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## James Bond and Philosophy - Questions Are Forever

- ch. 1 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Jacob Held's introduction recounts growing up obsessed with Bond films and contrasts Fleming's Bond (frail, cruel, depressed, an addict who takes stimulants) with the superhuman womanizer of the screen. He argues fans attach to Bond because he embodies our desire for a world where good and evil are clearly defined and good always triumphs, which is why the films flatten the moral ambiguity of the novels. He then maps the book's terrain: misogyny and objectification, Cold War politics and nuclear proliferation, the license to kill and the rule of law, technology, the good life, and existentialism, presenting Bond as an ideal vehicle for provoking reflection on what we believe and why.
- ch. 10 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger, Søren Kierkegaard, Friedrich Nietzsche, Albert Camus — Being-Toward-Death; The Stages on Life's Way; The Death of God; The Absurd — Butterfield takes Bond's answer to Elektra in The World Is Not Enough - he survives by 'tak[ing] pleasure in great beauty' - and asks whether hedonism can answer the problem of suffering and death. She reads Bond through Heidegger's being-towards-death (quoting Live and Let Die's 'You start to die the moment you are born'), then diagnoses him as Kierkegaard's aesthetic 'seducer', an immature escapist who dreads boredom and flees commitment, before rehabilitating him with Nietzsche and Camus: Bond flushing the cyanide pill down the toilet in From Russia with Love and Mathis's advice in Casino Royale to make evil 'personal' show Bond creating his own values beyond good and evil, surviving the absurd through style and personal vendetta. Bond emerges as an existentialist role model for courage and creativity, though we should not copy his hedonism or lawlessness.
- ch. 12 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Martin Heidegger — Tripartite Soul; The Philosopher King; Theory of Forms; Being-Toward-Death — Getachew maps Plato's Republic archetypes onto the Bond universe: the appetite-ruled Tyrant is Goldfinger, Zorin, Stromberg and the SPECTRE megalomaniacs; the spirit-ruled Guardian is 007, compared to Alcibiades and to Medea's vengeful fury (Bond letting Trevelyan fall 'For me'); and the reason-ruled Philosopher-King is M, whose ideal republic of common children and expendable numbers mirrors MI6's recruitment of orphans and its treatment of Bond as '007', never a person. She then challenges Plato's preference for the eternal Forms and immortality, using Freud on transience and Heidegger's being-toward-death (via Tolstoy's Ivan Ilych) to argue that mortality and risk are what make life worth living. Bond's stylish risk-taking, like the cello-case downhill escape, shows we need a balance between the Guardian and the man of thought.
- ch. 13 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Bernard Williams, Theodor Adorno — The Meaning of Life; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — South reads the famous opening of Thunderball - life as 'a heap of six to four against' - as Fleming's challenge to Western philosophy's promise of a harmonious fit between reason, personal good, and social good. Bond's shame at his hangover and bad bridge (analyzed through Bernard Williams's account of shame as grounding identity) drives him to the Shrublands spa and a new healthy, patient self, but SPECTRE's atomic blackmail and an assassination attempt push him back to scrambled eggs, bacon, and bourbon: 'life's too short.' With Adorno's bleak view that modern societies are implicated in evil (SPECTRE's bureaucratically worded ransom memo, gas chambers built by the same ingenuity as medicine) and Williams's thick ethical concepts, South argues Fleming's Bond shows there is no universal pattern making life meaningful; the spy's work lies outside conventional morality, and the odds of a happy, philosophically vindicated life are six to four against.
- ch. 14 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Maurice Merleau-Ponty, Edmund Husserl, René Descartes — Embodiment (the lived body); Phenomenology — Gibson argues that Bond's meticulous sensory attention - the three-and-a-third-minute boiled egg, the Mouton Rothschild '53, 'shaken, not stirred' - makes him a phenomenologist in Maurice Merleau-Ponty's sense, a thinker whose fleshly style of perception is the very means of his spycraft and his access to the invisible world of plots and dangers. She reads the Casino Royale mirror scene as Merleau-Ponty's 'flesh' and the reversibility of vision, and casts Goldfinger (who shrinks from 'the touch') and Dr. No with his steel pincers as villains doomed by their flight from the carnal world Bond embraces. Against Descartes's mind-body split and Plato's distrust of appearance, Bond's gun is an organic extension of his body rather than a symbol, and his sensual immersion in the world is what lets him see what others cannot.
- ch. 16 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Herbert Spencer — The Übermensch; Will to Power; Master and Slave Morality; Social Darwinism — Matheson examines the 'master animal' imagery saturating Fleming's novels - Donovan Grant's reptilian physique in From Russia with Love, Blofeld's animal magnetism, villains feeding victims to sharks and crocodiles - as an exercise in Social Darwinism where, as Mr. Big puts it, wolves live by wolf's laws and sheep call them criminals. Using Nietzsche's Übermensch, the blond beast of the Genealogy, and Zarathustra's self-overcoming, she distinguishes Apollonian eaters like M (who curb appetite) from doomed Dionysian gluttons like Giuseppe Petacchi, arguing that Fleming makes dining and will power the test of who counts as an Overman. Bond differs from his megalomaniac foes only in keeping his own will to power and stomach under control, and the chapter closes by warning that Hollywood's romanticizing of the master animal keeps us valuing a brutally primitive masculinity Fleming himself mocked as a 'cardboard booby'.
- ch. 17 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; Master and Slave Morality; Will to Power — Landa argues that Bond's apparent defense of democracy against Übermensch villains is itself profoundly Nietzschean: Bond's exquisite, disciplined hedonism (the Minton-china breakfast, disgust at Mr. Du Pont's 'eating like a pig'), his obsessive competitiveness at baccarat and golf, and his contempt for mass democracy mark him as an aristocratic bourgeois hero in Nietzsche's mold. The gambling scenes enact a Calvinist logic of predestination in which losing at Casino Royale devastates Bond because his very worth depends on winning, and the 'human muck' he defends at Blades turns out to be the exclusive elite. Dr. No, Mr. Big, Drax, and Blofeld are cast as evil, explicit Nietzscheans while Bond serves a good, unspoken Western Nietzscheanism - so the Cold War showdown with Blofeld, who invokes Nietzsche by name, is elitism fighting counterfeit elitism, making Bond 'a secret agent in the service of Nietzscheanism'.
- ch. 18 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Socrates, Plato — Virtue Ethics (arete); Philosophy of Love; Practical Wisdom (phronesis) — Taliaferro and Le Gall rebut Paul Johnson's 1958 charge that Doctor No combines 'the sadism of a schoolboy bully', adolescent sex longings, and suburban snobbery. They show Bond kills for justice rather than spite (his reluctant assignment against von Hammerstein in 'For Your Eyes Only'), that Colonel Sun repudiates de Sadean torture before dying, and that the disturbing 'semi-rape' passage of The Spy Who Loved Me reflects Vivienne Michel's voice, not Bond's conduct. They read Bond's eros as an ironic use of the medieval courtly-love and Mariology tradition in which sex redeems 'lost women' like Pussy Galore, defend his dress and manners through the ancient heroic tradition (Theseus's fatal neglect of the white sails, Leonidas combing his beard at Thermopylae), and conclude Bond is a chivalric, comic hero - a 'delightful impossibility' in Aristotle's Poetics' sense who embodies Socratic soldierly virtue and Platonic optimism.
- ch. 19 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Deontology; Consequentialism; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Tedesco dissects what the double-0 'license to kill' could actually mean, ruling out self-defense and mere on-the-job killing and concluding that Bond is a state-sanctioned assassin who earned his status through two assassinations in Casino Royale. He tests that status against Kant's categorical imperative - persons as ends in themselves, though Kant also defended capital punishment as respecting the murderer's choice - and against consequentialism, which vindicates Bond's elimination of Kananga but stumbles on the surgeon's organ-harvesting dilemma. Because ordinary morality mixes duty-based and consequence-based intuitions, the chapter leaves Bond's moral status genuinely open and links it to live policy debates on assassination and torture after 9/11, including Britain's Intelligence Services Act that leaves room for a real-life double-0 agent.
- ch. 21 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** John Locke — Natural Rights; The Social Contract — Forster stages the dispute between Dr. No ('just a stupid policeman') and Scaramanga ('we are the same') over whether Bond's license to kill betrays the liberal rule of law it protects, connecting it to post-9/11 debates about executive power. He gives the rule of law a substantive ground in Locke's Two Treatises - prerogative power may suspend particular laws for the public good of preserving the community ('salus populi suprema lex'), illustrated by Locke's own career conspiring with Lord Ashley's spy network - and a procedural check in the separation of powers, mirrored in MI6's real parliamentary and judicial oversight. Both elements together, he concludes, let Bond's extra-legal work remain consistent with the rule of law: power exercised for the public good under institutional supervision, without which M's vendetta in 'For Your Eyes Only' would be indistinguishable from Scaramanga's contracts.
- ch. 22 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Struggle for Recognition; Consequentialism; Respect for Persons — Held argues that our pleasure in Bond's kill-then-quip routine - 'Shocking' after electrocuting a henchman in Goldfinger, tossing Killifer to the sharks that ate Felix with 'You earned it, you keep it' - reveals tacit retributive and deterrent theories of punishment that place villains outside the moral community. Using the distinction between mere humiliation (deflating Dr. No's pretense: 'Minnows pretending they're whales') and Humiliation that denies common humanity, he shows Bond dehumanizes disfigured, 'othered' villains, even as the novels present them as disenfranchised figures - Dr. No rejecting his father's name, Mr. Big demanding recognition as the first great black criminal - whose rage is a warped demand for Hegelian mutual recognition. He proposes instead Jean Hampton's moral education theory, which treats criminals as moral agents capable of reform, concluding with Jaws's redemption in Moonraker as proof that no one is beyond it.
- ch. 23 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Charles Sanders Peirce — Pragmatism — Abrams explains Peirce's three modes of inference (deduction proves, induction shows, abduction merely suggests) and reconstructs, abduction by abduction, Bond's rapid chain of guesses in The World Is Not Enough: from the odd smell and foam on his fingers to the urea-coated fertilizer bomb he unknowingly delivered, the shared motto 'there's no point living if you can't feel alive' exposing Elektra's alliance with Renard, and her guilty subject-changing. He also covers the negative art: Bond plants false clues with Q's gadgets (the fingerprint re-printer, the cloaked Vanquish) while villains like Carver recode reality by sinking the Devonshire to frame the Chinese, and both sides try to force each other into false abductions. Bond wins because his mind is more playful - Peirce's 'musement', Eco's creative abduction, and an encyclopedic education - making 007 the superior player in what is ultimately a game of chance and strategy.
- ch. 25 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger — The Question Concerning Technology — Zani reads the Q-branch scene through Heidegger's 'The Question Concerning Technology': technology as technê, a revealing and bringing-forth that turns dangerous when it reduces the world to standing-reserve and turns people into stockpilists, as with Goldfinger, who kills Jill Masterson over gold and cannot let anything he does not own exist. Bond's villains (Drax declaring 'What he doesn't own he doesn't want' before shooting pheasants for sport; Grant's garrote watch) use technology as domination and amusement, whereas Bond uses it defensively and refuses to be owned by it - hurling the ATAC off the cliff with 'That's détente' and preferring the lighter to the match 'until they go wrong'. The repeated match-and-lighter password exchange in From Russia with Love encapsulates the films' Heideggerian moral: a free relation to technology that uses tools when needed and lets them recede, rather than becoming what one controls.
- ch. 26 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger, John Dewey, Immanuel Kant — The Question Concerning Technology; Humanity as an End in Itself — McKinney argues that '007' is a technological construction distinct from the human James Bond: drawing on Dewey's broad view of technology as technique (the shaken martini as technological artifact), Heidegger's account of naming as revealing and enframing, and Don Ihde's technological embodiment (the party-game hand axe that is art, paperweight, or tool depending on perception). Film dialogue shows the naming at work: Moneypenny calls him 'James' but announces '007 is here' to M; Goldfinger greets '007' and is corrected with 'My name is James Bond'; M threatens '008 can replace you' and, in Die Another Day, declares 'You are no use to anyone now' while rescinding his status. The chapter closes with the Kantian point that persons must be treated as ends in themselves, yet the designation '007' constitutes Bond as precisely a replaceable means to MI6's ends.
- ch. 27 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, Aristotle, Catharine MacKinnon — Objectification; Humanity as an End in Itself; The Male Gaze; The Golden Mean — Arp and Decker examine Bond's objectification of women - using a dancing girl as a human shield in the Goldfinger pre-credits sequence, M ordering Bond to 'pump her for information' - through three moral theories. Kant's humanity formula condemns treating women as sexual tools, but Kantian autonomy generates a stalemate since consensual, reciprocal liaisons like Bond and Anya Amasova's might qualify as permissible; Millian utilitarianism condemns the male gaze by its long-term social costs yet stalls against pro-sex feminist counterarguments. They settle on Aristotelian virtue ethics - the mean between extremes, with respect as the key virtue against the power-centered virtue of Machiavelli, Hobbes, and Nietzsche - to argue objectification is vicious, and they trace Bond's moral progress from skipping the young Bibi in For Your Eyes Only to the reciprocal relationships with Kara Milovy, Pam Bouvier, Wai Lin, and Jinx.
- ch. 29 "James Bond and Philosophy" **·** David Hume — Yin and Yang; Is–Ought Problem — Kowalski explains yin-yang cosmology through the Yi Ching and Shu Ching - opposing forces in dynamic harmony, each containing the seed of its opposite, with excess of either bringing destruction - and applies it to the Brosnan-era films, reading Bond himself as the yang force of action. The films improved, he argues, by strengthening yin: Wai Lin as Bond's equal partner in the handcuffed motorcycle chase of Tomorrow Never Dies (each watching the road the other cannot see), Judi Dench's M as the corrective to Admiral Roebuck's hawkish yang ('the advantage is that I don't always have to think with them'), and Elektra King as a genuine yin villainess rather than a damsel. He ends with a Humean caveat that deriving moral conclusions from the natural order is logically suspect, yet endorses the cosmology's practical advice - balance and harmony between opposing forces - as the reason the new millennium Bond films are better.
- ch. 30 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "James Bond and Philosophy" *(front)*
