## Jeopardy! and Philosophy - What Is Knowledge in the Form of a Question

- ch. 1 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** The Unexamined Life; Personal Identity — Jeopardy! champion Ken Jennings argues that the silly answer-in-the-form-of-a-question conceit is the engine of the show's appeal, because it turns every topic, even Belgium, into a fractal of surprising factual connections rather than a dull definition. He defends the old-school internalization of facts in a Google age - champions are congenitally curious 'sponges' whose stored knowledge makes them better citizens and thinkers - and speculates that what we call the Self is largely the accumulation of facts in one's head. His philosophy of the show in a nutshell: do not just answer in the form of a question, live your life in the form of a question.
- ch. 9 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 10 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Editor Shaun P. Young introduces the volume with a brief history of Jeopardy!, from Merv Griffin's 1964 answer-and-question gimmick (his wife Julann's '5,280 feet') through its record longevity, Emmy wins, pop-culture parodies, and IBM's Watson turning the show into a human-versus-machine spectacle. He frames the book's guiding questions: is Jeopardy! a good game, does it educate or merely entertain, what values does it celebrate, and what can it teach about artificial intelligence and liberal education.
- ch. 11 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Eudaimonia (flourishing); The Meaning of Life — Classicist and Jeopardy! champion Daniel Melia reads the show as a mirror of American exceptionalism, contrasting it with European hits like Big Brother: Jeopardy! is pure individual contest rewarding a faith in classless meritocracy, its Daily Doubles and come-from-behind Final Jeopardy! wagers enact the American belief in redemption and self-reinvention, and its buzzer timing and Before-and-After clues test frontier virtues of physical reflex and improvisation. He argues that its middlebrow categories (Magritte, Sibelius, Paul Revere) preserve shared American cultural capital, and that the show's free-flowing, low-context, information-sharing format answers the question 'What does it mean to be an American, Alex?' and indexes what Americans count as living the good life.
- ch. 14 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** John Rawls, Aristotle, Martha Nussbaum, John Locke — The Difference Principle; The Capabilities Approach; Justice as Fairness — Shaun Young tests Jeopardy! against equality as an 'essentially contested concept': Aristotle's proportional equality (treat like cases as like) seems to justify the winner-take-all payout as deserved, Locke's reward for the industrious explains why equal outcomes would kill incentive, and Rawls's two principles - fair equality of opportunity plus the difference principle - capture the show's identical rules and level playing field. He then raises harder challenges: Dworkin's equality of resources, Kymlicka's charge that the Americo-centric clue bank shortchanges minority cultures (with Quebec's Bill 101 as legitimate differentiated rights), and the capabilities approach of Sen and Nussbaum applied to would-be contestants who cannot operate the signaling device. Citing Isaiah Berlin's pluralism, he concludes that no final verdict on Jeopardy!'s equality is possible - and that spectators would not want one, since Jennings's seventy-four-game streak thrills precisely because of inequality between players.
- ch. 15 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Brendan Shea argues Jeopardy! is a genuinely good game, building the case on Bernard Suits's The Grasshopper: players pursue a prelusory goal (ending with the most money) through constitutive rules - answering in the form of a question, no buzzing before Trebek finishes, wagers fixed before the clue - obeyed out of a lusory attitude because participants want to play. Good games strike a balance between too easy and impossibly hard so that winning reflects skill rather than luck, which is why the original buzz-anytime rule was revised and why Watson's win impressed where a Wheel of Fortune triumph would not. Playing a good game well matters because it demands and builds real-life skills and, following Hurka and Tasioulas, constitutes an objectively valuable accomplishment animated by a good-making lusory attitude.
- ch. 16 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault, Plato, Immanuel Kant, René Descartes, Gilles Deleuze — Power/Knowledge; Phenomena and Noumena — Franklin Allaire uses Cliff Clavin's defiant 'Who are three people who've never been in my kitchen?' on Cheers to stage the fight between modern and postmodern theories of knowledge. He rehearses Plato's Theaetetus account of knowledge as justified true belief, Descartes's cogito, and Kant's thing-in-itself as modernity's quest for objective truth via universal intersubjective agreement, then argues through Foucault and Deleuze that Trebek and the judges are gatekeepers whose power controls the narrative - his Civil War renaming example ('What is the War of Northern Aggression?') shows many knowledges competing for one answer. On this view, Cliff's insistence that different questions can fit the same answer exposes the non-objectivity of Jeopardy!'s 'objective' knowledge, making the barfly a postmodern champion.
- ch. 17 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** John Dewey, Socrates — Experience and Education; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Timothy Sexton traces Jeopardy!'s format to the fallout from the 1950s quiz show scandals, when Charles Van Doren's rigged run on Twenty-One destroyed the elitist model of expert knowledge and television pivoted to anti-elitist game shows like The Price Is Right. He reads that pivot as parallel to the Cold-War abandonment of Dewey's progressive education for a utilitarian, worker-training curriculum, as documented by Diane Ravitch and Olaf Hoerschelmann. Jeopardy! endures as the 'Gnostic' compromise between quiz show and game show: it keeps elitist knowledge but demands that it be displayed in the Socratic ritual of answering in the form of a question, which lets a trivia show rise above the dumbed-down Millionaire era.
- ch. 20 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Aristotle, John Locke — The Gettier Problem — Robert Arp argues that Jeopardy! contestants display only declarative memory - the mere recalling level of Bloom's Taxonomy, which trained apes like Koko and Kanzi can approximate - rather than genuine smarts. Working through the Theaetetus analysis of knowledge as justified true belief, with the correspondence theory of truth and Aristotle's demand for explanation and evidence, he shows that a lucky Final Jeopardy! guess ('What is tree pulp?') is a true belief that falls short of knowledge because it is true only by accident. Real knowledge lives at the analysis, synthesis, and especially evaluation levels - constructing and critiquing arguments - though he concedes many contestants can probably do this off-stage, so it would be a fallacy of hasty conclusion to insist they all ain't smart.
- ch. 21 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Alan Turing, John Searle, John Locke — The Turing Test; The Chinese Room; Personal Identity (memory theory); Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Nicolas Michaud treats Watson's 2011 defeat of Jennings and Rutter as evidence for Turing's test: had Watson's answers come from behind a door we would have judged them intelligent, and its goofy misses ('What is Toronto?????') reflect missing context, not missing thought, no more damning than a child's honest error. Against Searle's Chinese Room he deploys the systems reply - you plus the Big Book of Rules might be the thing that understands - and argues that nobody, human or machine, can meet Searle's impossible burden of proving understanding, since Watson can define words, associate connotations, and pick Jeopardy! out from not-Jeopardy!. Quoting Locke's definition of a person as a thinking being that can consider itself as itself, he concludes Watson approaches personhood and that we had better teach our 'intelligent offspring' kindness and fairness before they learn anything else.
- ch. 22 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Henri Bergson — Matter and Memory; Duration (durée) — Randall Auxier explains Jeopardy!'s appeal through Bergson's Matter and Memory: memory is a relation - the past acting in the present - along a continuum of matter (repetition of what was) and memory (variation), and the show strips contestants of bodily action so that what we watch is pure 'recollection', the instantaneous summoning of subtle structures from the flux. Providing the question instead of the answer forces a tiny temporal reversal, a six-step durational epoch he calls (after Edgar Sheffield Brightman) a 'shining present', which is why Jeopardy! outshines College Bowl, why champions like Ken Jennings fascinate as savants of immediate recollection, and why the slow clock music of Final Jeopardy! is the show's most exquisite torture and catharsis.
- ch. 23 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Socrates — Theory of Forms; Allegory of the Cave; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Rick Mayock stops a fictional Jeopardy! clock to apply Plato's divided line: most contestants, like the cave prisoners rewarded for predicting shadow sequences, trade in doxa - conjecture (eikasia) and belief (pistis) - while the Meno's slave boy shows how Socratic questioning ties true opinions down into knowledge. Winning requires only dianoia, hypothetical reasoning from hypotheses and definitions, never noesis, the dialectic that ascends to the eternal Forms; the Phaedo's example of perfect 'equality' shows why the 'Aha!' of recognition is re-cognition by an immortal soul already acquainted with the Forms. Watson, lacking such a soul, can clear a buzz threshold but never have an Aha! moment - it is Daedalus's statue, lifelike but sure to run away from the truth unless tied down.
- ch. 24 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Socrates, Plato, Søren Kierkegaard — Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); The Unexamined Life; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Daniel Wanless reads Jeopardy! through the Meno: contestants can formulate the right question only because the answer is already given, just as Socrates answers Meno's paradox by claiming that all inquiry is recollection of truths the immortal soul already holds - latent knowledge that explains how Ken Jennings buzzes in before he consciously knows the response. A priori logical ability (the slave boy's doubled square, the law of non-contradiction applied to which of three cities is southernmost) guides the search for answers, while Final Jeopardy!'s blind wagering embodies Kierkegaard's 'life is only understood backwards, but it must be lived forwards.' Socrates would bet it all, Wanless concludes, because the unexamined life is not worth living: his whole life was preparation for the ultimate Final Jeopardy!, lived in the form of a question.
- ch. 27 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Five-time champion Matt Kohlstedt argues that winning Jeopardy! reflects gamesmanship and a love of learning rather than education itself, and warns against the 'Jeopardy!-ization' of schooling: high-stakes testing makes teachers assembly-line trainers and students regurgitators, and produces the on-air 'Jeopardy! effect' of choking under pressure that made him flub the number of articles in the Constitution. Drawing on his late grandfather's book on teacher education and Abraham Maslow's hierarchy of needs and peak experiences, he defends a philosophy of education aimed at self-actualized teachers who, like Alex Trebek's mid-round contestant interviews, treat students as whole human beings rather than test statistics.
- ch. 28 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Democracy; The Public Sphere — Joseph Foy argues Jeopardy! quietly serves democracy: the breadth of its categories rewards exactly the well-rounded, informed citizenry that theorists from Tocqueville to Delli Carpini and Keeter think self-government needs, and it answers Plato's Republic worry that ordinary people are unfit to rule by showing experts and stay-at-home parents alike fluent across the board. Because civic knowledge correlates with defending First Amendment freedoms, and because the show inserts the glorification of knowledge into the public sphere while bar-trivia gatherings build Putnam-style social capital, playing along at home rehearses the habits and virtues of democratic citizenship.
- ch. 29 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Charles Sanders Peirce, William James — The Pragmatic Method; Transcendental Idealism; Truth as What Works — Ellen Sorg separates knowledge from intelligence using Kant's Critique of Pure Reason: Jeopardy! winners like Ken Jennings exercise only the first knowing power, sensibility - recall of facts as they appear to us - never ascending to the intellect of the Transcendental Analytic or acting on what they know, so Jennings would likely wash out on Survivor. Pragmatism supplies the missing criterion: Peirce and James hold that thought gains its value from its practical consequences and from the modification of conduct, which is what Survivor's fire-building, shelter-making, and Kim Spradlin's 2012-winning social game actually test. Hence Jeopardy! proves its champions knowledgeable, but only Survivor proves anyone intelligent.
- ch. 30 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Bertrand Russell — Language Games — George Reisch's imagined green-room conversation and Jeopardy! match between Wittgenstein, Russell, and F.H. Bradley enacts Wittgenstein's later philosophy: a 'green room' with nothing green in it shows that meaning is use within a language game rather than correspondence to what a name names, and no sharp definition of 'game' is possible because games share only family resemblances. At Final Jeopardy! - clue: 'An activity engaged in for diversion or amusement' - Russell wins with 'What is a game?' while Wittgenstein loses by answering self-referentially ('What is a misleading answer to the question What is a game?'), because one cannot play the game and critique its suppositions at the same time. The title's liar-style paradox nods to Russell's theory of types as the proposed cure for self-reference, while Bradley defends the self-pervading Absolute Experience against the revolt against idealism that Russell led.
- ch. 31 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 36 "Jeopardy! and Philosophy" *(front)*

## Jimi Hendrix and Philosophy - Experience Required

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Johnny—James—Buster—Jimmy—Jimi . . . How Many Jimis" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — Personal Identity — Editorial introduction tracing the successive identities of Johnny Allen Hendrix, James Marshall Hendrix, Jimmy James, and Jimi — from Seattle and the chitlin' circuit with the Isley Brothers and Little Richard to Chas Chandler's London — arguing there was no single Hendrix but a series of continual transformations. It dismisses a 'Hegelian unfolding of The Absolute' that would require true blues musicians to be born in the South, and speculates that the Jimi lost in 1970 was a would-be jazz-blues synthesizer, reading 'Rainy Day, Dream Away' and 'Still Raining, Still Dreaming' as evidence and invoking the rumored Miles Davis collaboration.
- ch. 8 "I. If 6 was 9" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "1. I Stand Up Next to a Mountain" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Aristotle — The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Reads Hendrix as Joseph Campbell's hero on the monomyth of separation, initiation, and return: the mother's death and gypsy prophecy of 'Voodoo Chile' stage the separation, the Venus witch's ring the initiation, and the transcending guitar solo the return, with Prometheus as the type of the fire-bringer punished by promoters, Little Richard, and audiences demanding the hits. Through Nietzsche's Birth of Tragedy it casts the Monterey guitar-burning and the Dionysian intensity of the Experience as tragic art whose hero 'shoulders the whole Dionysiac world for us,' and closes with Aristotle's Politics on catharsis — 'Machine Gun''s sonic battle imagery as purgation that transforms violence into a cry for peace.
- ch. 10 "2. Zen and the Art of Guitar Burning" **·** The Buddha — Zen Buddhism; Impermanence (anicca) — Argues Hendrix's art worked like a Zen transmission outside words and doctrine: Nansen's cat, the Buddha's silent flower sermon, Zeami's hidden flower in Noh, and Linji burning the sutras frame the guitar-burning, playing with his teeth, and the backwards tape of 'Are You Experienced?' as skillful means that shock listeners out of dualistic discrimination into pure suchness. The Woodstock sequence — the 'Star-Spangled Banner' followed seamlessly by 'Purple Haze' and the 'Instrumental Solo' — is read as an eschatological passage from the death-symbolism of the national anthem to a Zen-like experiential beginning, with Hendrix acting as a Bodhisattva whose Stratocaster is the master's stick of compassion.
- ch. 11 "3. Mermen and Dismemberment" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer, Friedrich Nietzsche, Karl Marx, Martin Heidegger — Philosophical Pessimism; Authenticity; The Death of God — Contends that Hendrix's discord is philosophy-as-discord: 'I Don't Live Today' (the Native American dead-in-life), the Woodstock 'Star-Spangled Banner' with its bombs and sirens against sporting-event sentimentality, 'Machine Gun,' and 'House Burning Down' lacerate listeners so they actually feel war's atrocities instead of parroting patriotic clichés. Against this the chapter sets the oceanic, womb-like escape fantasies of '1983 (A Merman I Should Turn to Be)' and 'Valleys of Neptune,' treating them through Schopenhauer as metaphysical consolation, Marx's opiate, and Nietzsche's 'hangman's metaphysics,' and concludes that Hendrix finally refuses such world-denying illusions — and Heideggerian inauthentic withdrawal — in favor of existential engagement with suffering here and now.
- ch. 12 "4. Hendrix—Freedom and Love" **·** Free Will vs Determinism; Philosophy of Love — The editor asks where Hendrix's 'bestness' lies by testing whether he was ever free: his songs demand freedom ('If 6 Was 9,' 'Stone Free,' 'Freedom,' 'Ezy Ryder') but never assert that the human condition is free, and his caffeine-style appetites, exploitative contracts, and management suggest determination rather than liberty. A detour through 'Hey Joe' as premeditated murder — compared with Othello and the Ice-T 'Cop Killer' affair — argues that fabricated narrators make it impossible to glean an artist's own moral sense from songs. He concludes that Hendrix's deepest mastery was the treatment of love, from 'The Wind Cries Mary' to 'Little Wing,' making him the sixties' most powerful spokesman for love rather than freedom, per the quoted 'when the power of love overcomes the love of power.'
- ch. 13 "II. I Know I Gotta Die When It’s Time for Me to Die" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "5. Jimi, Janis, and Jim" **·** Immanuel Kant, Arthur Schopenhauer — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; The World as Will — Traces 'genius' from the indwelling genie of an art to Goethe as the first person the public called a genius, then applies Kant's doctrine — the genius gives the rule to future art, produces what no determinate rule can govern, and cannot explain or teach what he does — to Jimi (having the Experience learn 'Sgt. Pepper' on one hearing to play it back to the Beatles three days after its release), Janis ('a victim of my own insides'), and Jim. Schopenhauer's dark side of genius — more mind than character, a will that 'affirms its own life by denying the life of others' — explains why the 27 Club burned out, and why we say 'Jimi lives' but 'Jim is still alive.'
- ch. 15 "6. Quicker than the Wink of an Eye" **·** Cornel West — Authenticity — Defends Hendrix against the Allan Bloom-style charge that sixties self-expression was mere decadence by reading his life as Charles Taylor's ideal of authenticity: self-determining freedom replacing the older external goods of Plato's Good, Aristotle's polis, or Aquinas's God. The biography — the broken home behind 'Castles Made of Sand,' the rolling-stone mobility Dylan anthemed, the London synthesis of blues, rock, soul, and jazz, 'Watchtower''s 'let us not talk falsely now,' and the accusations of race desertion answered by the Watts commitment and 'House Burning Down' — becomes the development of an authentic voice, closing with Cornel West's 'tragi-comic poet' and the humanist manifesto of 'Bold as Love.'
- ch. 16 "7. A Life Along the Watchtower" **·** Martin Heidegger, Jean-Paul Sartre, Antonio Gramsci — Authenticity; Being-Toward-Death; The They-Self (das Man); Cultural Hegemony — Reads 'All Along the Watchtower' as a dialogue between the Joker's Sartrean being-and-nothingness ('none of them along the line know what any of it is worth') and the Thief's Heideggerian reply — being-toward-death, anticipatory resoluteness, care, and the fallen 'they.' It argues Hendrix lived this struggle: the mirror-smashing of 'A Room Full of Mirrors,' the unborn speaker of 'Belly Button Window' who will throw himself into the world rather than be thrown, and the 'Electric Church Music' built to cut through 'they'-thinking and return listeners to child-like authenticity. Gramsci's point that oppressor and oppressed alike internalize the oppressor's message names the hegemony Hendrix's third way — neither victim nor revolutionary — simply denies.
- ch. 17 "III. Flotation Is Groovy, Even a Jellyfish Will Tell You That" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "8. Facing Up to the Realities" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Bad Faith; Authenticity; Existence Precedes Essence; Radical Freedom and Responsibility — Argues that 'Machine Gun' and its Berkeley introduction ('people... fighting wars... within themselves, not facing up to the realities') make Hendrix an existentialist: since existence precedes essence, each of us lives multiple realities we must author responsibly. The lyric variants diagnose Sartrean bad faith twice over — 'evil man make me kill ya' is the helplessness that denies freedom, 'fight like a farmer' the blame that locates failure out there — before the reprise's acceptance ('let your bullets fly like rain') and free judgment ('I know all the time you're wrong') achieve authenticity amid the solo's sonic battlefield.
- ch. 19 "9. Are You Experienced" **·** Martin Buber — I–Thou Relation; Philosophy of Love — Proposes an 'experientialism' — subjective conscious experience as fundamental reality, defended against eliminativists who would explain experience away as illusion — and finds its moral core in Martin Buber: the title song and 'May This Be Love' call the beloved out of an I-It world ('made out of gold and can't be sold') into reciprocal I-You relation ('take hold of my hand'), while 'The Wind Cries Mary,' 'Purple Haze,' and 'Manic Depression' lament love that is absent or unreciprocated. Experiences feel most intensely real, the chapter argues, when the Platonic order of love puts what matters most at stake — which is why kissing the sky, even without being kissed back, is worth recommending.
- ch. 20 "10. Hendrix Out of the Cave" **·** Plato — Allegory of the Cave; Theory of Forms — Scores 'Are You Experienced?' and 'Purple Haze' against the Allegory of the Cave: the backwards guitar and reversed cymbals are the painful, clumsy ascent from shadows toward the Form of the Good, with Hendrix as the initiator who must first drag the prisoner violently and then gently — 'come on across to me... we'll hold hands.' 'Purple Haze' becomes the midway delirium between cave and sun ('don't know if I'm coming up or down,' 'scuse me while I kiss the sky'), and the chapter concludes that the psychedelia is philosophical — consciousness opened by liberation from received opinion, not by LSD — warning the freed prisoner not to remain a pale copy of Plato or Hendrix.
- ch. 21 "IV. Music Sweet Music" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "11. Vibrations and Echoes Long Ago" **·** Andy Clark, David Chalmers — The Extended Mind — Argues Hendrix's sound was made with — not merely on — studio technology: Eddie Kramer's Olympic Studios innovations, in the lineage of the BBC Radiophonic Workshop, used feedback, varispeed, and backwards tape as performing instruments. Andy Clark and David Chalmers's extended mind thesis (Chalmers's own example is Hendrix's mind extending into his guitar) and Chalmers on Pokémon Go frame the analog recordings as a more immersive augmented reality than digital games, while Kittler's claim that media 'determine our situation' and the time-bending processes of 'Purple Haze' and 'Are You Experienced?' explain how listening disorients and enriches our perception of time and space.
- ch. 23 "12. The Broken Pieces of Yesterday’s Life" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, René Descartes — The Private Language Argument; Personal Identity — Uses the flood of posthumous Hendrix releases — the dismal Lonnie Youngblood eight-track, 380-plus albums, the PPX litigations — to ask what we owe the dead and what access we may claim, weighing Kafka and Max Brod, Nietzsche's sister, Harper Lee's Go Set a Watchman, and Kundera's Mirek. It rejects the framing of conflicting rights: against the Cartesian picture of a hidden self the authors deploy Wittgenstein's beetle in the box — 'the human body is the best picture of the human soul' — to show that no one can fully control their public self, leaving only prudential discernment about which recordings should shape our image of the artist.
- ch. 24 "13. Did Jimi Play Rock" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Authenticity — Flips Joel Rudinow's 'Can White People Sing the Blues?' to ask whether a very black musician can play a genre that by the late sixties had become white property. With Wittgenstein's family resemblance — musical kinds lack necessary and sufficient conditions — it traces rock's migration from Chuck Berry and the Sanctified churches to the Stones and Beatles, diagnosing the 'Jimi problem': his blackness rendered his rock inauthentic (Christgau's 'psychedelic Uncle Tom,' radio bans) at the same time as his rock rendered him inauthentically black. The chapter endorses Hendrix's own refusal of labels — 'You don't need labels, man' — as a caution against unreflective categorization.
- ch. 25 "14. Hendrix from the Bottom Up" **·** Immanuel Kant — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — A rhythm-section-first ('bottom up') listening of Electric Ladyland and the Fillmore East Band of Gypsys shows Hendrix re-grounding his music in blues, R&B, and jazz time: Steve Winwood's organ anchoring 'Voodoo Chile,' Buddy Miles's shuffle freeing the vocal-like guitar of 'Rainy Day, Dream Away,' and the communally stretched measure before 'Power to Love.' Kant supplies the vocabulary — the 'mercenary art' of studio craft versus art, and the Transcendental Aesthetic and the sublime — for the claim that the Gypsys' collectively felt rift in time approaches a rock'n'roll sublime that is 'felt as exceptionally and undeniably bad ass.'
- ch. 26 "15. You Can’t Use My Name" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Recounts how Hendrix changed law twice: ExperienceHendrix v. PPX (the 'You can't use my name' tapes) imported the Wrotham Park/Blake standard so breach-of-contract damages can reflect the infringer's gains rather than the victim's losses, and Experience Hendrix v. Hendrixlicensing.com used Washington's Personality Rights Act to extend postmortem publicity rights beyond the state of domicile, raising the constitutional problems Rossi identifies. The chapter then reads the dispute over whether the Woodstock 'Star-Spangled Banner' is art or noise through legal realism versus natural law theory and legal positivism — law as what courts actually enforce — and through the Institutional Theory of Art, with a Wittgensteinian cluster definition marking the essentialist route that failed.
- ch. 27 "V. What Any of It Is Worth" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "16. Artistic Expression in the Land of the Free" **·** John Stuart Mill — The Harm Principle; On Liberty; Utilitarianism — Argues that Hendrix's Woodstock 'Star-Spangled Banner' links political and artistic freedom: Mill's On Liberty — the harm principle, liberty of tastes and pursuits, and originality against the despotism of custom — both protects and celebrates the performance, since disliking it is no ground for suppressing it. Tolstoy's What Is Art? then supplies the aesthetic verdict: art is the infection of others by sincerely, particularly, and clearly felt emotion, so the anthem, like Picasso's Guernica, is great art expressing bad feelings about Vietnam — unlike José Feliciano's sincere-but-vague ballad or Roseanne Barr's failed joke.
- ch. 29 "17. The Cry of Love" **·** Plato, Plotinus, G.W.F. Hegel — Neoplatonism (the One); Theory of Forms; Allegory of the Cave; Geist (World Spirit) — Reads Hendrix as a romantic idealist on a Platonic-Neoplatonic arc: 'Room Full of Mirrors' shatters the cave wall and its projections toward The One, whose presence is pursued through eros with the veiled Beloved of Plotinus — the semi-divine feminine of 'Little Wing,' 'One Rainy Wish,' and 'Angel.' Hegel's Zeitgeist and world-spirit then frame the planned First Rays of the New Rising Sun as music meant to carry humanity's collective evolution, with Hendrix as Hegel's 'spiritual workman' and self-described messenger ('I am only a messenger and you are a Sheep in the process of evolution').
- ch. 30 "18. Musical Order from Sonic Chaos" *(no index entry yet)* — Explains guitar feedback as a non-linear dynamical system — strings, amp, and room air feeding output back into input — so that at Woodstock the anthem's closing notes ('yet wave...') are pulled from thin air while the whammy bar works, and 'Foxey Lady''s rising feedback shows order stirring itself out of chaos. With Laplace's predictability undone by chaos theory and Lorenz's butterfly, the chapter frames Hendrix as having learned 'to play chaos,' and locates musical beauty — citing Aristotle on symmetry, Hutcheson's 'uniformity in variety,' and Wundt's arousal curve — at the unstable border between boring order and meaningless noise.
- ch. 31 "19. Elemental Jimi" **·** Heraclitus, George Berkeley — Everything Flows / Logos — Maps Alexis Korner's 'primitivistic' Hendrix onto the pre-Socratic element theories: Thales's water returns in the submarine Atlantis of '1983' and 'Valleys of Neptune,' Anaximenes's rarefaction and condensation in the flight and storm imagery, and Empedocles's Love and Strife in the Monterey burning — a sacrifice motivated by love, set against the Strife of 'Machine Gun,' 'Izabella,' and 'House Burning Down.' Heraclitus's unity of opposites and logos explain the fire-water paradox lyrics ('that crying blue rain that's burning my eyes,' 'If 6 was 9'), while Berkeley's tree in the forest shows how the audible emerges from mere vibration, as musical sound emerges from feedback and noise.
- ch. 32 "Historical Note" *(no index entry yet)* — A British music writer's memoir-historical corrective: the 'Swinging Sixties' existed only in west London while provincial Britain remained grey and make-do-and-mend, but the blues boom of Cream, Mayall, the Yardbirds, and the Animals prepared the seedbed into which Hendrix landed in 1966 — a 'quantum blues leap' that still felt familiar. With comparatively milder (if passive-aggressive) racism, Chas Chandler, Noel Redding, and Mitch Mitchell, five UK Top Twenty singles against one American hit, McCartney's Monterey recommendation, and Keith Altham's guitar-burning idea, it was Britain that set him on his way.
- ch. 33 "Bibliography" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Ezy Wryters" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "Index or Confusion" *(notes)*
- ch. 36 "260" *(front)*
- ch. 37 "261" *(front)*

## Jimmy Buffett and Philosophy - The Porpoise Driven Life

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Jimmy Buffett and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Popular Culture and Philosophy Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Jimmy Buffett, Cultural Infidel" **·** John Dewey, Plato — Pragmatism; Experience and Education; The Meaning of Life — The editors argue Buffett is a philosopher despite his own denials, in the sense of Royce and Dewey: philosophy is critical reflection on living that returns to ordinary experience, and Buffett's songs, books, and concerts pass Dewey's test of rendering everyday predicaments 'more significant, more luminous, more fruitful.' They compare 'Margaritaville' to Plato's Republic — not equal in pedigree but equal in kind as occasions for philosophical practice — to defend meaning-making as a democratic practice open to Parrotheads, not just professors. Reading the album 'Changes in Latitudes, Changes in Attitudes' as a guide, they set Buffett's 'Porpoise Driven Life,' where purpose is found by sailing your own waters in the company of friends, against Rick Warren's revelation-based 'Purpose Driven Life.'
- ch. 6 "Coral Reefer Reason" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "That’s His Story and He’s Stickin’ to It" **·** Epicurus, Diogenes the Cynic — Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Cynicism (living according to nature) — Aaron Pratt places Buffett's 'island escapism' in the lineage of the ancient 'graceful life' philosophies: like Diogenes the Cynic, Buffett flouts social convention (pirates over Civil War generals, the beach over the rat race, 'Be good and you will be lonesome'), and like Epicurus he preaches pleasure with prudence rather than mere indulgence. 'Cheeseburger in Paradise' becomes Epicurus's command to know what you want and go get it, while the hung-over confession of 'Margaritaville' — 'I know it's my own damn fault' — supplies the Epicurean proviso that overindulgence ruins the pleasure it promises. The chapter closes by arguing that counter-cultures like Parrotheadism carry built-in safety features: careers and families force Epicurean prudence, keeping escapism a periodic liberation rather than social collapse.
- ch. 8 "Too Much Tequila, Or Not Quite Enough" **·** Seneca, Ralph Waldo Emerson, Henry David Thoreau — Stoicism; The Dichotomy of Control — Doug Anderson reads Buffett as an 'American Stoic' — unlike his friend the Cynic Hunter S. Thompson, who dropped out and died, Buffett accepts his fated world of competitive capitalism and controls the one thing Stoics say we can: his own attitudes, 'changes in latitudes, changes in attitudes.' He leans on Seneca's advice to Serenus that wine and dreaming liberate the mind ('drink washes cares away') to interpret the dialectic of dreaming and drinking in 'Margaritaville,' 'Five o'Clock Somewhere,' and 'License to Chill' as Stoic self-renewal — the margarita even frees the singer to admit his own fault — with Emerson's intoxication-speaking poet as the American model. Excess is the failure mode: the expatriates of 'Banana Republics' who mistake the dream for an end state and Buffett's late-thirties hangover reckoning mark the Stoic middle path between deadening routine and 'too much tequila.'
- ch. 9 "Jimmy Buffett “Singer of Inordinate Tunes” and Heideggerian Troubadour" **·** Martin Heidegger, Emmanuel Levinas, Augustine of Hippo — Thrownness (Geworfenheit); Being-Toward-Death; Authenticity — Drew Dalton takes Buffett's claim that 'we don't just enjoy our escapism — we NEED it' as an admission that being itself is burdensome, overturning the Plato–Augustine–Leibniz consensus that it is good to be. Since 'Margaritaville is nowhere' — it is on no map and can never be reached — what we long for is not a better vacation but release from having to be at all, which is why even on holiday in Key West we still dream of escape. Heidegger's Being and Time supplies the framework: thrownness, being-toward-death, anxiety, and guilt reveal existence as a weight we never chose, so the escapist longing to be otherwise than ourselves is the 'inauthentic' flight Heidegger describes (without moral condemnation), and Levinas's On Escape confirms that 'it is precisely from all that is weighty in being that escaping sets forth.'
- ch. 10 "“Trip Around the Sun”" **·** William James, Henry David Thoreau, Bertrand Russell — Pragmatism; The Will to Believe — Phil Oliver pairs Buffett with William James: both hold that philosophy is 'not a technical matter' but each person's sense of what life deeply means, had in non-verbal moments of elevation as much as argued in words. The chapter's core is James's 'moral holiday' — a morally legitimate respite from care and struggle — naturalized in Buffett's 'License to Chill' and 'Trip Around the Sun' ('it's good to know it's out of my control'), joined by James's lightness about error from 'The Will to Believe' and Russell's and Dawkins's responses to cosmic annihilation. Against those who hear mere hedonism, Oliver insists Jamesian holidays are care-free but never care-less: like Thoreau's 'live deliberately' echoed in 'Growing Older But Not Up,' they renew us for the return to work, and James's ambivalence about alcohol in The Varieties of Religious Experience mirrors the risk of Buffett's frozen concoctions.
- ch. 11 "Parrotology" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "“Livin’ It Up”" *(no index entry yet)* — Amanda Feller reads Buffett's life and lyrics through Jack Mezirow's transformative learning theory: a disorienting dilemma (the Holiday Inn has-been singer, the 1994 seaplane crash, the near-divorce) unhinges one's compass, and growth requires disorientation, framed exploration of one's governing assumptions, and re-orientation. She reads the arc of 'Margaritaville' — 'I know it's nobody's fault' to 'hell, it could be my fault' to 'my own damn fault' — as exactly this movement, and Buffett's post-crash fly-fishing for the small bass as embodied, whole-self reflection through which he 'rediscovered his saline psyche.' The chapter's sharpest warning is that escapism is not transformation: the blogger who moved to the beach found that no matter where you go, there you are, because only introspection, not a change of venue, transforms.
- ch. 13 "“When the Coast is Clear”" **·** Immanuel Kant — Perpetual Peace; Cosmopolitanism — Celia Bardwell-Jones asks how Parrothead tourism can be ethical when mass-market tourism walls the Caribbean inside an 'environmental bubble' of consumption — the 'ugly, stupid' tourist of Jamaica Kincaid's Antigua who never encounters the toured other as a fellow agent. Kant's Perpetual Peace sets the standard of hospitality, the stranger's right not to be treated as an enemy, and the chapter asks what duties fall on the traveler in return when tourist industries pressure host cultures 'not to be so damn foreign.' Buffett's novel A Salty Piece of Land supplies the alternative: Tully Mars is saved from free-floating survival when he drops anchor in the relationship with Cleopatra Highbourne — the 'song of the ocean' meeting 'a salty piece of land' — so that escape is redeemed by home, boundary-crossing, and self-reflection with culturally different others.
- ch. 14 "Piratology" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "The Missionary" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Ludwig Wittgenstein, Jean-Paul Sartre, Karl Marx — The Death of God; Authenticity; Existence Precedes Essence; Alienation — Christopher Hoyt reads Buffett's escape from Catholicism as a case study in secular meaning-making after Nietzsche's death of God, taking the Mardi Gras ritual of Folly swatting Death out of town as Buffett's core spiritual attitude — interpreted the way Wittgenstein criticized Frazer, as an expression of our stance toward death rather than mere magic. Sartre's 'existence precedes essence' frames the post-1960s task of self-definition without God, while Marx's alienation explains what the persona of *Jimmy* refuses: the impersonal work and borrowed values that leave moderns living an alien narrative, like McGuane's character Skelton in Ninety-Two in the Shade. The twist is that *Jimmy*'s pop-culture vocabulary — Tully Mars built from Donovan's Reef and Slim Pickens, Bogart, the Brylcreem jingle — is itself authentic, and the 'semi-true story' of the myth tells deeper truths than a literal biography could, making Buffett something like a pop prophet for late modernity.
- ch. 16 "How We Still Might Manage a Smile Being Pirates and Making Meaning" **·** Albert Camus, Friedrich Nietzsche, Socrates — The Absurd; Amor Fati (love of fate); The Unexamined Life — Adam Glover reads 'A Pirate Looks at Forty' through Kristeva's intertextuality: since every text and self is a 'mosaic of quotations' pirated from pre-existing sources, Buffett's confession that he was born 'two hundred years too late' makes him an ironic pirate who can no longer believe or plunder unselfconsciously, unlike the oblivious common-sense pirate. Invoking Barthes's death of the author, he argues the pirate's life is a hard freedom to improvise multiple meanings (the ever-changing 'head uptown' variations in the song) with full responsibility for one's choices. The chapter ends weighing Camus's worry that meaninglessness makes life not worth living against the Nietzsche of The Birth of Tragedy, who answers that the meaning of life is life itself, and it sides with Buffett against Socrates's 'unexamined life' while admitting it cannot settle the question.
- ch. 17 "An Altar Boy’s Public Penance" **·** Baruch Spinoza, Friedrich Nietzsche — The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Substance Monism (Deus sive Natura); Forgiveness — Flamm and Rea take Buffett's altar-boy grudge against Catholic moralism — the 'bakers' of 'Fruitcakes' who try to cure humanity's half-baked madness — and frame it with Nietzsche's charge that 'there was only one Christian, and he died on the cross.' They then cast Buffett as a latter-day Spinoza: like the excommunicated lens-grinder he rejects the orthodox God, finds God in nature ('One Particular Harbor' as a view 'sub specie aeternitatis'), and treats wealth, honor, and sensual pleasure ('Cheeseburger in Paradise') as means toward the greatest happiness rather than sins. Nietzsche's Dionysian intoxication ('God's Own Drunk') and Shakespeare's Sir Toby versus the moralist Malvolio support embracing natural madness, and the chapter closes by arguing Buffett's confessional songs ('Coastal Confessions') make his Parrothead audience the priest who grants the altar boy's public penance and forgiveness.
- ch. 18 "Smart Women and Fruitcakes" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "“Happily Ever After, Now and Then”" **·** Mary Wollstonecraft, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Feminism; A Vindication of the Rights of Woman; Pragmatism — Erin McKenna explains how a feminist can love Buffett by reading 'Fins' and 'Why Don't We Get Drunk (and Screw)' as critiques of predatory land sharks rather than endorsements, and 'Margaritaville' as a man admitting 'it's my own damn fault.' Against Rousseau's pliable helpmate ideal of woman she sets Wollstonecraft's Vindication — equal education and personhood for women — and Charlotte Perkins Gilman's pragmatist-feminist case that women's economic, physical, and mental dependence stunts everyone. Buffett's novels then pass the Gilman test: Desdemona, Trevor Kane, Cleopatra Highbourne, and Sophie are smart, independent women, and the male leads must reform their habits to earn them, aiming at Desdemona's 'happily ever after, now and then' instead of the fairy-tale ending.
- ch. 20 "The Fruitcake as Performative Exemplar" **·** Judith Butler, Martin Heidegger, John Dewey, Michel Foucault — Gender Performativity; Heteronormativity; The They-Self (das Man) — John Kaag pairs Buffett's 'Fruitcakes' with Judith Butler's constructivism: institutions 'girl' infants through interpellation (the sonogram naming), the heterosexual imperative abjects gay, lesbian, and transgender 'nobodies,' and these excluded fruitcakes are the cosmic baker's half-baked people. The movie-theater stanza ('a twelve pound Nestle Crunch for twenty-five dollars') becomes Heidegger's das Man, others thinking and choosing for us, while Buffett's 'mea culpa' refrain and 'Religion's in the hands of some crazy-ass people' attack the guilt constitutive of Christian subjecthood, with Dewey's broad, doctrine-free religious experience offered as common ground. Buffett concerts themselves are read as performative exemplars — drag-show-like borderland communities where four generations act out the 'little bit of fruitcake' in everyone as a quiet political protest against enforced normalcy.
- ch. 21 "Margarita Metaphysics" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Wonder Why We Ever Go Home" **·** Communitarianism; Pragmatism; The Golden Rule; The Land Ethic — Duke Richey maps Buffett's West — 'Ringling, Ringling,' the Rancho Deluxe soundtrack, Tully Mars — onto Josiah Royce's philosophy of community: only people who root in a place, face its history ('wise provincialism'), and sacrifice selfish desires for the whole can build Beloved Communities, a lesson enacted when cattle rustlers Jack and Cecil accept community sanction and find belonging working the Rancho Deluxe. Wallace Stegner's coral-reef metaphor from Angle of Repose supplies the image of communities built up millimeter by millimeter yet still vulnerable to pollution and over-fishing, with Royce's environmental regret (covering 'the wounds' of the landscape with gardens) anticipating the chapter's sustainability ethic. 'Wonder Why We Ever Go Home' thus asks what we really seek when we seek grace through community: not the nomadic Pickup Truck Death of every-man-for-himself, but cooperation that makes a society to match the scenery.
- ch. 23 "“Where Did All the Good Songs Go ”" **·** John Dewey — Experience and Education; Pragmatism — Scott Pratt answers whether 'Margaritaville' is art by rejecting object-based theories (Kivy's score-compliance, the abstract perfect performance, the unrecorded instance that vanishes) for Dewey's Art as Experience: art is 'an experience,' complete and self-sufficient, followed by a reflective, evaluative second phase. He reads 'Makin' Music for the Money' as moving from solitary self-interested music-making to a community of musicians, and the Port of Spain steel-drum rehearsal of 'King of Somewhere Hot' that carried forward into the Miami concert as Dewey's two phases in action. Buffett's fly-fishing stories — the lost blue marlin that became 'a story,' the released Long Island bass that 'replenished his saline psyche' — supply Haeckel's ecological model in which music, like fishing, is an organism-environment interaction, making songs 'homemade' eco-music that belongs to listeners in its own ecosystem.
- ch. 24 "“Livin’ and Dyin’ in Three-Quarter Time”" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli, Aristotle — Virtù and Fortuna; Virtue Ethics (arete); The Wheel of Fortune; Fate and Foreknowledge — Randall Auxier uses Buffett's catalog to think about fortune and fate, reading Buffett's bold career moves (walking out on Nashville for the beach, later annexing it via 'It's Five o'Clock Somewhere') through Machiavelli's advice to marry Fortuna and master her tricks with virtù. Against Aristotle's demanding answer that only aretē, moral excellence, defends happiness against bad luck, he takes Vico's middle path: natural causes, moral causes, and the occasions of fortune are gathered under providence, so Buffett's seaplane crash ('God, Buddha, St. Christopher, my guardian angel... luck, and the United States Navy water-survival training') shows any fate can be turned to good ends for others. Sorting Buffett's songs into places, times, meetings, sentiments, and thoughts — the key sentiment being the wistful — the chapter lands on contentment as gratitude: being 'livin' and dyin' in three-quarter time.'
- ch. 25 "Songs Referred to in This Book" *(notes)*
- ch. 26 "Pirates in Paradise" *(notes)*
- ch. 27 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 28 "Jimmy Buffett and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 29 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Johnny Cash and Philosophy - The Burning Ring of Truth

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Johnny Cash and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Popular Culture and Philosophy Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Introduction" **·** Socrates — The Problem of Evil; Personal Identity — The editors' introduction opens with Socrates on trial for corrupting the youth of Athens and quips that on stage before the youth of Athens, Georgia, Cash could claim he was only asking "What is truth?" It frames Cash as Kris Kristofferson's "walking contradiction" whose paradoxes generate the book's questions: how evil fits a world rated by a benevolent God, where the enduring "I" hides while Cash insisted he was always changing, and what makes a law unjust. It also presents the Man in Black as social critic lobbying officials at San Quentin and Folsom for prison reform and raising money for reservation schools, before welcoming readers with the 1969 Arthur, Illinois concert where hippies and Amish rubbed shoulders.
- ch. 7 "PART I - Self" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Authentic Cash" **·** Authenticity; Personal Identity — Butler rehearses how the Johnny Cash persona was socially manufactured: Sam Phillips coined the name "Johnny Cash" for its rebellious marketing value, "Folsom Prison Blues" narrates a prison term Cash never served (and borrowed from Jimmie Rodgers and Gordon Jenkins), and the Man in Black image began as an accident of black shirts at a Memphis church performance. Against Jung's persona-as-mask and Dr. Phil's "authentic core self," and armed with George Herbert Mead's social theory of the self, he argues authenticity is not fidelity to a hidden inner essence but active engagement with the social processes shaping one's identity, the way paper money gains "cash value" through use. Cash invested the imposed persona with value by sincerely living it (for example, writing "Man in Black" to give the black clothes his own meaning), so the construct became genuinely his identity.
- ch. 9 "A Modest Conclusion" **·** Personal Identity; Ship of Theseus — Lindahl-Urben and Taliaferro stage the diachronic-versus-episodic debate about the self (Galen Strawson's episodic view included) against Leigh Edwards's "no single Johnny Cash": the hotel-room-wrecking addict and the Billy Graham touring Christian do not fit one biography. They argue the private J.R. was a third option: on stage he becomes wholly absorbed in story characters (the Folsom narrator, the man who shot a man in Reno) while a self not defined by time slips in and out of the persona, evidenced by his account of gradually shedding "Johnny Cash" after shows. Johnny Cash the public quilt is compared to the Ship of Theseus, every original piece of J.R. replaced yet still owned by him, and the modest conclusion is that J.R., like anyone making a free choice, stood above the timeline of instants and could inhabit both the episodes and the grand narrative.
- ch. 10 "I Still Miss Someone The Self and Its Travels Along the Way" **·** Zhuangzi, Laozi, Confucius — The Tao (the Way); Yin and Yang; Taoism (Daoism); Li (ritual propriety) — Geisz explains the Confucian social dao of ritual (li), humaneness (ren), filial piety and the junzi, the Daodejing's metaphysical Dao with its unity of opposites, and Zhuangzi's tales of free wandering, then maps all three onto Cash's songs. Zhuangzi's "Free and Easy Wandering" answers Cash's road songs ("I've Been Everywhere," "The Wanderer"), Cook Ding's effortless carving answers John Henry's hammering in "The Legend of John Henry's Hammer," and the scrambled countdown of "Twenty-Five Minutes to Go" echoes Zhuangzi's logic-bending humor. The payoff is Cash's own country Yin-Yang of interdependent opposites, freedom chained to imprisonment ("Folsom Prison Blues"), sin twinned with redemption ("I Hung My Head"), fidelity with betrayal ("Jackson," "Cocaine Blues"), and a self that, unlike Zhuangzi's self-forgetting sage, must travel a temporal path carrying its guilt, yet stays on what Geisz calls a very wide superhighway of a Dao.
- ch. 11 "PART II - Life" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "If I Were a Carpenter" **·** John Rawls, Immanuel Kant, Hannah Arendt, Aristotle, Socrates — The Veil of Ignorance; The Categorical Imperative; The Golden Rule — Butler asks what makes an activity work, testing the market definition against Socrates' refusal of fees, Aristotle's Politics, Arendt's labor/work/action distinction, Eva Kittay's care work, and the split between extrinsic and intrinsic value. He reads Cash's catalog through it: "One Piece at a Time" (the Detroit line worker stealing a Cadillac he builds but cannot afford), "Get Rhythm" (the shoeshine boy singing through menial labor), "I Never Picked Cotton" (escaping work into an empty identity), "Let Him Roll" and "Southern Accents," and the San Quentin monologue after "Wreck of the Ol' 97" where Cash refuses to be told what to play before inmates. The chapter closes by lining up Kant's categorical imperative, Jesus' Golden Rule, and Rawls's veil of ignorance: nobody would choose a society of company-store wage slavery without knowing they were not the worker, and Cash, knowing he stood on the stage rather than in the cellblock, used his freedom to side with prisoners and carpenters anyway.
- ch. 13 "What Is Truth" **·** John Locke, Aristotle — Democracy — Lavaque-Manty and Mickey define populism by distinguishing its content from its discourse (via Michael Kazin), tracing the 1892 Populist Party platform and southern race-baiting demagogues like Vardaman and Huey Long, then locate Cash in that Dixie soil, his family rescued by the patronizing New Deal Dyess Colony that fed his populist distrust of elites. Cash's populism is anti-racist and pro-prisoner ("The Ballad of Ira Hayes," his empathy for convicts against tough-on-crime politics), his patriotism borders on kitsch ("Ragged Old Flag"), and "What Is Truth?" argues the young may be right, breaking with populist certainty about the obvious. The closing worry is the deep tension they pose with Locke's "the people be the judge" against democracy as "government by discussion": a Cash concert, like a mass rally, may stir people following rather than thinking, so populism's ring of truth is not yet truth.
- ch. 14 "Love Gone Good" **·** Paul Tillich, Jean-Paul Sartre, Socrates, Friedrich Nietzsche — Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Philosophy of Love — Johnson reads Cash as practicing "philosophy as a way of life" rather than theorizing: his love-gone-bad songs ("Cocaine Blues," "I shot a man in Reno," the addictions he chose) honor the descent into darkness, aligned with Socrates going down to Piraeus in the Republic, Nietzsche's Zarathustra descending into the depths, and biblical descents from Jacob at the Jabbok to Jonah to Christ, while his love-gone-good songs ("Daddy Sang Bass," "Man in Black") show the turn outward through which love interrupts darkness without erasing it. He frames this with Gabriel Marcel's "creative fidelity" (being at the disposal of others), Paul Tillich's courage to be, in which love is stronger than the nonbeing of death, and Sartre's Existentialism Is a Humanism, where wanting my freedom obligates me to want everyone's. The Man in Black thus carries "a little darkness" on his back while insisting "all God's children ain't free," a lived, political-ethical philosophy rather than a doctrine.
- ch. 15 "John Henry, the Steam-Engine, and Folsom Prisoners" **·** John Stuart Mill — Higher and Lower Pleasures; The Greatest Happiness Principle; On Liberty; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Mulnix pairs Cash with John Stuart Mill on the priority of quality of life over mere life: Mill's 1868 Parliament speech defends capital punishment as more humane than the "living tomb" of life imprisonment, and Cash agrees in the Folsom liner notes (the prisoner envying the cockroach that can leave) and in "The Mercy Seat," where the electric chair becomes both mercy and the throne of God. Mill's higher and lower pleasures, competent judges, and On Liberty's individuality illuminate Cash's gallery of fools, the "Folsom Prison Blues" narrator who shot a man just to watch him die, the mother-defying Billy Joe of "Don't Take Your Guns to Town," characters whose unreflective rebellion squanders rational freedom. John Henry, by contrast, exercised his higher faculties against the steam-engine and so died happy, proving the chapter's Millian thesis that freedom matters as a means to self-directed, deliberated happiness.
- ch. 16 "PART III - Love" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Conclusion June Love Beyond the Jordan" **·** Harry Frankfurt, Immanuel Kant — Philosophy of Love; Humanity as an End in Itself — Sennett builds "June Love" out of June Carter Cash's documented love, from the "Hurt" video's staircase glance to Rosanne Cash's eulogy that June saw your potential and "lifted them up" for you to see, and the love that steadied John through addictions and infidelity for thirty-five years. He stages the philosophy-of-love dilemma between appraisal theories (Velleman, grounding love in Kantian dignity that demands respect but cannot justify loving John rather than anyone) and bestowal theories (Irving Singer, on which love projects value and so cannot be justified at all), with Frankfurt's volitional account as a side note, and proposes June Love, loving the beloved's potentiality, as the hybrid that is both objective and active and can justify loving this person. He also distinguishes June Love from codependence: enabling entrenches the addict's destruction, whereas June's confronting-and-encouraging love, echoed in "Ring of Fire" and "Far Side Banks of Jordan," called Cash to transformation while leaving him himself.
- ch. 18 "And Beware of the Beast in Me" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Barnes takes Cash's infidelity despite the pledged fidelity of "I Walk the Line" and refuses the easy diagnoses of hypocrisy or lying: "Guess Things Happen that Way" voices a good will overcome by weakness, a frail human nature anyone shares. He supports this with the situationist experiments, the dime in the pay-phone slot, the Princeton seminarians in a hurry, Milgram's obedient schoolteachers, and the Stanford prison simulation that turned a pacifist student into an abusive guard, showing situation outweighs character. From Cash's songs he draws two moral lessons: charity toward wrongdoers, since the man in "I Never Picked Cotton" or the drunkard of "Kneeling Drunkard's Plea" might be us under the same circumstances, and prudence about compromising situations, as when Billy Joe's fatal pride in "Don't Take Your Guns to Town" proves his mother right.
- ch. 19 "The Doors Are Never Locked" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Augustine of Hippo — Forgiveness; Eternal Recurrence; Master and Slave Morality; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Held reads Cash's Walk the Line retort "Well, they're not Christians then" as a thesis about redemption: since, with Augustine, we rejoice most at the sinner saved, and since the Sermon on the Mount forbids judging what we cannot know (anyone's deep character), humility demands withholding condemnation and hoping even the worst can change. He contrasts Christian redemption, a revaluation of the person that needs outside grace (Cash needed June and the Carters; nobody is self-made), with Nietzsche's attack on redemption as slave-morality self-hatred, granting that Nietzsche's eternal recurrence also revalues the past but objecting that self-revaluation collapses into rationalization, as Cash's Saul in Man in White shows. Forgiveness proper requires repentance and a solvent character, like paying off a debt; the shame of the "Long Black Veil" woman shows why; and because we see "through a glass darkly," nobody, not the Reno murderer, not Joe Bean, is beyond hope, which is why the doors are never locked and why execution or life without parole claims a judgment of moral worth that is not ours to make.
- ch. 20 "From Cash to Kant—and Back Again" **·** Immanuel Kant — Kingdom of Ends; The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; The Golden Rule — Yolanda Estes pairs Cash with Kant as two duty-based moralists: Cash's Golden Rule in 'The Preacher Said, Jesus Said' parallels Kant's formula of humanity (never treating persons merely as means), and Cash's 'good heart' matches Kant's good will, since songs like 'Sam Hall,' 'Folsom Prison Blues' (the man shot in Reno 'just to watch him die'), and 'I Hung My Head' weigh inner motives over outcomes. Because earthly law never balances the moral scale, she reads Kant's kingdom of ends and Cash's apocalyptic kingdom of God in 'When the Man Comes Around' and 'God's Gonna Cut You Down' as parallel objects of faith: we must live as if perfect justice already prevails, working to improve this world rather than waiting for the next.
- ch. 21 "PART IV - Justice" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "The Good, The Bad, and The Ugly" **·** John Stuart Mill, Socrates — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Utilitarianism; The Harm Principle — Johnson and Schmitz argue that Cash, through songs like 'San Quentin,' 'Jacob Green,' and his cover of 'The Mercy Seat,' championed reforming prisons from a retributive to a rehabilitative model and abolishing capital punishment (songs like 'Joe Bean' and 'Long Black Veil' center executed innocents). They defend Cash with Mill: Socrates shows vengeful retributive sentiment is immoral, Mill's harm principle says government may coerce only to prevent harm, so punishment should be justified only by future deterrence and rehabilitation. Since the death penalty's deterrent effect is empirically weak (California, Missouri-vs-Iowa murder rates) and wrongful convictions are common, Mill today would join Cash in calling for abolition.
- ch. 23 "“Unchained” The Inspired Anger of a Religious Man" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Thomas Aquinas — The Golden Mean; Intentionality; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Dan Haggerty uses Aristotle's account of anger as a virtuous mean—felt at the right people, time, and degree—to show that songs like 'San Quentin,' 'The Man in Black,' and 'Oney' express justified anger at real injustices done to prisoners and workingmen, contra pop psychology's blanket rejection of anger. He then coins 'inspired anger' (drawing on Plato's Ion: the artist possessed and blown away by emotion): a painful, hopeless desire for a justice or restoration that can never be, exemplified by Cash's 'Hurt' video and its regret over irrecoverable time—mourning rather than self-pity or depression. The chapter closes with 'Unchained' as a prayer showing how a Christian like Cash can live with this futile desire rather than argue it away.
- ch. 24 "What Is Justice" **·** John Rawls, Plato, Mahatma Gandhi — The Veil of Ignorance; Justice as Fairness; Justice in the Republic — Johnny Huss extracts multiple theories of justice from 'Jacob Green'—desert (traced to Plato's Republic), equal treatment, conventionalism, and systemic injustice—and argues that desert collapses once responsibility is pushed back to factors beyond our control (Cash's God-given voice, the 'victim of the times'). Rawls's veil of ignorance supplies the standard for judging institutions: since anyone could be Jacob Green, we could not accept principles that let anyone 'get done like Jacob Green got done.' The chapter extends this to social justice for Native Americans via 'The Ballad of Ira Hayes' and 'As Long as the Grass Shall Grow,' including the flooded Cornplanter Tract, and closes insisting justice requires action, not just principles.
- ch. 25 "PART V - God" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "Memories Are Made of This" **·** Plato, Plutarch, Aristotle — Personal Identity; Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity (memory theory); Immortality and the Soul — Randall Jensen asks whether Cash's hope of reunion with the dead ('Meet Me in Heaven,' 'If We Never Meet Again This Side of Heaven') is even coherent, using the 'One Piece at a Time' Cadillac and a hypothetical 1954 Plymouth to retell the Ship of Theseus puzzle about identity through part replacement. He canvasses Platonic dualism from the Phaedo (Cash is his soul, the body a rusty cage), the materialist hope that God can reassemble a resurrected body despite dispersed matter, and the psychological-continuity view that persons are 'made of memories,' ending on the duplication problem: if God made two heavenly bodies both carrying Cash's memories, which one would be Cash?
- ch. 27 "Redemption" **·** Aristotle, Thomas Aquinas — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Problem of Evil; Cardinal and Theological Virtues — Stephen Bilynskyj tests John Hick's soul-making theodicy—that God allows suffering because virtues like courage, temperance, and compassion need pain to form—against Cash's life and songs. Using Aristotle on habituation by pleasure and pain (Cash's first amphetamine euphoria and the ostrich-attack painkillers as missed occasions for temperance) and Aquinas on innate aptitude and divinely infused virtue, he argues virtue can form without suffering (by genes or miracle), so soul-making alone cannot justify evil, as Cash himself implies about his mother's grief after Jack's death. Cash instead locates redemption in Christ's suffering ('Redemption,' 'Why Me, Lord?'), while the intervention by his son John Carter shows pain still occasioning real growth in temperance.
- ch. 28 "“A Boy Named Sue” A Cashian Theodicy" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Gottfried Leibniz, Plato — The Problem of Evil; The Divided Will; The Best of All Possible Worlds — Jamie Carlin Watson reads Cash's Christian worldview as Augustinian: evil is not a positive substance but a privation or corruption of good (contra the monism Leibniz risks and the Manichean dualism Augustine rejected), so humans alone bear blame for moral evil through freely corrupting good things—'Cocaine Blues,' 'The Wall,' the Folsom prisoner who admits he 'had it coming,' and the divided self of 'The Beast in Me' echoing Romans 7. Natural evil, as in 'Guess Things Happen That Way' and the flood of 'Five Feet High and Rising' (Cash's mother's 'blessing' of rich river dirt), serves punishment or greater goods. 'A Boy Named Sue' dramatizes the residual tension: the father's get-tough-or-die theodicy convinces neither Sue nor us that the sheer amount of evil is justified.
- ch. 29 "Christmas in Jamaica" **·** Alvin Plantinga, David Hume, Epicurus, Thomas Aquinas — The Problem of Evil; Compatibilism; Free Will vs Determinism — David Werther frames Jack Cash's saw death—which Cash told Nick Tosches he considered murder—and the 1982 armed robbery at Cinnamon Hill as tests of Epicurus's old questions (quoted via Hume): could a good, powerful God have prevented these evils? Distinguishing the evidential from the logical problem, he answers the logical problem with Plantinga's libertarian free will: not even God could ensure Cash freely 'walked the line' with Vivian, since guaranteeing fidelity would eliminate freedom. He rebuts the compatibilist counterobjection by arguing, with Aquinas, that nothing can be the cause of its own nature, and argues heaven can still fulfill Johnny and June's hope of meeting 'on the far side banks of the Jordan' even if the redeemed can no longer act wrongly.
- ch. 30 "About the Authors" *(notes)*
- ch. 31 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "Johnny Cash and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "ALSO FROM OPEN COURT" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "Johnny Cash and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Jurassic Park and Philosophy - The Truth Is Terrifying

- ch. 1 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — The editors' introduction argues that Crichton was doing philosophy rather than science: he imagined a possibility and probed its range because it could not be tested. It frames the whole book around Malcolm's question of whether we should, not whether we can, and around the gap between what we can do and what we should do with technologies like de-extinction. It connects the film to our illusion of control, insisting there are no ghosts or magic here, only nature, and that forgetting how edible we are turns us into 'bloody smears on the teeth of a T. rex'.
- ch. 9 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Existential Risk; Immortality and the Soul — Michaud and Watkins blame Crichton and Malcolm for frightening us away from science, arguing that most life does not 'find a way' and that science is the shield keeping vulnerable humanity a step ahead of extinction by meteors, plagues, and predators. They take up Rousseau's anti-Enlightenment longing to return to nature and reject it, arguing that humility comes from science itself (a 13.72-billion-year-old Earth, Sagan's 'star stuff' making us the universe become conscious of itself), not from abandoning it. Hammond, not science, embodies the real arrogance: he was so certain he could not fail that he tested the park on his own grandchildren.
- ch. 11 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** John Stuart Mill, Arne Naess — Utilitarianism; Deep Ecology — Littmann surveys real de-extinction efforts (George Church's passenger pigeon, plans to clone a frozen mammoth) and asks when reviving extinct species is morally permissible. He argues that Arne Naess's deep ecology, with its 'equal right to live and blossom', fails because it projects human desires onto organisms like plants that have none, and cannot say whether species or only individuals bear rights. He instead applies Mill's utilitarianism: bring species back when doing so maximizes happiness and minimizes suffering, which permits a Jurassic Park in principle but forbids dinosaur fights and trophy hunts like Roland Tembo's, and rebukes Sattler's claim that only the people we love matter.
- ch. 12 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Ship of Theseus — Freeman explains why Grant dismisses the clones as 'genetically engineered theme park monsters': a frog-DNA-patched genome is not dinosaur DNA, and nobody knows how much alteration turns a T. rex into something else, so as Wu tells Hammond the park has only reconstructed the past, never re-created it. Behavior cannot vindicate the clones either, since The Lost World's raptors grow up without elders to teach raptor behavior, and the animals must survive an ecosystem of grass, bacteria, and species that did not exist in the Cretaceous. The chapter's running puzzle of how much of an organism can change before it is no longer that organism is a Ship of Theseus problem applied to paleontology.
- ch. 13 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** David Hume — The Watchmaker Argument (design) — Shea stages the Wu-Malcolm dispute through Hume's Dialogues: Jurassic Park is an 'intelligent design' so botched that, like Philo's world of needless suffering, it points to ignorant or malicious designers rather than a perfect God, undercutting Richard Owen-style design arguments. He then explains Hammond's folly with Darwinian natural selection and memetics, treating the dinosaur-theme-park as a meme that infected InGen's investors by exploiting evolved desires for profit, fame, and importance. Hammond's fatal errors were assuming evolution 'wants' humans to survive, that complexity means better adapted, and that he could outsmart selection, which the all-female raptors breeding and life 'finding a way' quickly refute.
- ch. 14 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Plato — Aristotle on the Soul; Theory of Forms; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — A pub dialogue applies Aristotle's De Anima, where the soul is the first principle of living things, to cloning: since a living clone must have an emergent, unrepeatable soul, successfully cloning dinosaurs brings new life into existence, which is good, so cloning itself is ethical. It then argues that neither unguided natural selection nor a divine dragon-slayer who nonetheless allowed cloning technology gives us reason not to revive dinosaurs, against Malcolm's 'they had their shot'. But drawing on Plato's forms, it condemns Hammond for controlling rather than merely containing: the lysine contingency and enforced sterility leave the dinosaurs shadows of what they could be, denied their natural flourishing.
- ch. 15 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Stoicism; The Dichotomy of Control — Billard concedes the Stoic point that we control neither our passions nor nature ('life will always find a way', Sattler's 'you never had control'), as the bucardo cloning failure shows, but argues this proves the park rushed, not that de-extinction is impossible. Dangerousness is subjective, raptor intelligence is a logistics problem rather than a metaphysical objection, and since humans have already caused the sixth mass extinction we may have a duty to bring species back. Because revived animals would be 'neo-dinosaurs' built from fragmentary DNA, de-extinction is creation rather than resurrection, less like playing God than like the transhumanist enhancement humans already practice.
- ch. 17 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Theory of Forms — Edwards argues that gene banks assume genes are the essence of a species, which he reads as a modernized Platonism: unchanging forms written in DNA, the same craving Hammond expresses in wanting to show people 'something that wasn't an illusion'. Following Hans Jonas's reading of Darwin, in which conditions rather than essence explain what an organism is, he casts Wu as the Platonist and Malcolm's 'life finds a way' as the evolutionary rebuttal in their debate over breeding control. The moral is that freezing DNA cannot save species, because what makes an animal what it is includes the vanished environment, so we should preserve habitats rather than entrust preservation to 'the hands of engineers'.
- ch. 18 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Aldo Leopold, Peter Singer, Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill — The Land Ethic; Wilderness and Nature Aesthetics; Animal Liberation — Fitzpatrick argues that the awe Ellie feels at the brachiosaurs makes experiencing real dinosaurs so valuable that restoring them may be obligatory, and reads Malcolm's distrust of land managers as sharing Leopold's land ethic, under which firebombing Isla Nublar would be barbarism on the order of Dresden. Invoking Bentham, Mill, and Singer's expansion of the moral community (echoed in the park's dart guns), he weighs Elliot's 'Faking Nature' and Katz's charge that restored nature is a fake and an arrogance against Andrew Light's defense of respectful restoration. He concludes that Jurassic Park failed not because restoration is wrong but because Hammond restored dinosaurs for amusement, and that a careful, land-ethic-respecting de-extinction could one day be justified.
- ch. 19 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Karl Marx — Ideology and False Consciousness; Class Struggle; Commodity Fetishism — Sexton reads the park as capitalist ideology in action, quoting Marx and Engels on the ruling class controlling the means of mental production: Hammond's flea circus is the illusion we agree not to see, and Nedry's sabotage shows what happens when workers are told their poverty is their own problem. He applies Veblen's leisure class to Gennaro, who judges the night-vision goggles valuable because they are expensive, and Marx's general law of capitalist accumulation to Hammond's squeezing of wages once every other cost is beyond his control. The dinosaurs themselves figure as the exploited working class, held not by ideology but by electric fences, and when the fence falls they seize the park.
- ch. 20 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Michael Muniz argues the dinosaurs of Jurassic Park are real 'in the most important ways' - not cloned in a lab, but real whenever the film runs: they terrify, they occupy our dreams (his T. rex-at-the-window opening), and they re-actualize deep time on screen. He weighs whether the franchise is genuinely horror - preying on fear for a negative response - or something stranger, a tension between fear and excitement in which extinction itself is the source of the scare.
- ch. 21 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Nagel, Frank Jackson — What Is It Like to Be a Bat?; The Knowledge Argument (Mary's Room); Mind–Body Dualism — Stoody asks whether Hammond's dinosaurs are more than bones, blood, and DNA, and answers with Nagel's bat: there is something it is like to be eaten by a velociraptor, and even a complete neuroscience of a T. rex's brain would not reveal the bloodhound-rivaling smells it experiences. He reruns Jackson's Mary as Dr. Sattler raised in a black-and-white world who learns something new upon seeing violet West Indian Lilac berries, and rebuts the physicalist two-descriptions reply using Crichton's Michael Douglas pen name. He concludes that physicalism leaves out the what-it's-like aspect of consciousness, leaving a choice between substance dualism (dinosaur souls) and property dualism.
- ch. 23 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Jean Baudrillard — Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal) — Kempner argues that the featherless raptors confirm Baudrillard's hyperreal: our dinosaurs are 'a real without origin or reality', like blue raspberry flavor or the never-existent brontosaurus, so the more real the clones pretend to be, the more fake they seem. He reads Wu's 'there isn't any reality here' and The Lost World's fossil-album passage as Crichton's own warnings against mistaking images for reality, while Spielberg's rejection of feathers shows the franchise teaching audiences to prefer the fake. Baudrillard himself used the film's finale, cloned dinosaurs rampaging among their fossilized ancestors in the museum, as the image of our predicament between 'our fossils and our clones'.
