- ch. 24 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Tom Regan, Immanuel Kant, Aristotle — Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life); Respect for Persons — Karavitis maps the three films onto the history of animal ethics: Jurassic Park treats dinosaurs as property on Aristotle's ground that only rational humans matter; The Lost World adds Kant's indirect duties, where the sporting rules of Tembo's hunt express duties owed to other humans rather than to the T. rex; and Jurassic Park III moves to Tom Regan, whose 'subjects-of-a-life' with inherent value fits raptors that remember, plan, and finally choose to spare the humans. The cow and goat fed live to the raptors test how far each view protects animals we treat as mere means.
- ch. 25 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Immanuel Kant, René Descartes, Tom Regan — Speciesism; Language Games; Humanity as an End in Itself — King charges human exceptionality with being speciesism and asks whether dinosaurs are persons rather than humans, testing Kant's rationality criterion and Descartes' denial of animal thought against the raptor that opens the shed door and the T. rex that spins the Explorer searching for prey. He runs the dinosaurs through Mary Anne Warren's five personhood criteria, then plays his Wittgenstein trump card: animal language would be the language of a different life-form, so the raptors' calls expressing fear and summoning help count as real communication without words. He closes with Regan's rights view, arguing that the cow and goat feeding scenes, like past denials of personhood to Native Americans and women, should shame us into showing a little respect.
- ch. 26 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Jeremy Bentham — Utilitarianism; Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life); Anthropocentrism — Lisa Kadonaga stages a choice between two sealed vials - clone the T. rex or the extinct thicktail chub - and weighs purposes for de-extinction: education and fundraising, Veblen-style prestige and conspicuous consumption, making amends for human-caused extinctions (the chub's wetlands were drained, the tyrannosaur died out before us), and Bentham-style utility of ecosystem function. She argues the chub wins on utility because it could resume a real niche while a top carnivore would wreck modern ecosystems, and questions whether Hammond-style engineering that makes recreated animals dependent on humans (the lysine contingency, terminator genes) violates the animals' right to exist as themselves. Her verdict is 'Pister's Bucket': defer resurrection until habitat and resources exist, and spend the effort saving the species we are killing right now.
- ch. 27 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Nagel, John Stuart Mill — What Is It Like to Be a Bat?; Speciesism — Christopher Ketcham writes a mock chronicle in which velociraptors escape to inhabited Nava, deliberately avoid humans, and over decades learn to share a hunting language with a Congo tribe, asking whether we would ever grant such beings equal intelligence and rights. He reads raptor pack behavior and cooperation through Dawkins's selfish gene, and uses Nagel's 'What Is It Like to Be a Bat?' to argue we can never peer into raptor consciousness, just as we brand human races inferior without looking. Citing Plessy v. Ferguson's whiteness-as-property and Mill's point that mistreating animals belongs in the same category as racial discrimination, he ends with a senate-hearing parody and the Neanderthal precedent: a human 'superiority gene' means coexistence with smart raptors would likely end as it did for them.
- ch. 29 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Arne Naess, Peter Singer — Deep Ecology; Animal Liberation; Consequentialism — Seth Walker asks whether Nick Van Owen - the Earth First! documentarian who monkeywrenches InGen's camp and frees the caged dinosaurs in The Lost World - is a bastard or morally justified. He runs the consequentialist cost-benefit (the escaped T. rex slaughters a cargo ship crew and trashes San Diego; the sabotage costs the team their radio and rescue) against the ecodefense argument built on Arne Naess's deep ecology and biocentric equality, on which Isla Sorna is our 'home' and rescuing the injured baby T. rex Roland Tembo crippled is Self-defense. Singer's principle of equal consideration of suffering - a difference in ability never justifies a difference in consideration - bridges the gap for those who reject biocentrism, and Walker answers the consistency objection (must Nick accept InGen sabotaging him back?) before crediting Nick's defense of his larger home.
- ch. 30 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — Autonomy of the Will; Existential Risk — Tim Jones argues the films show why Kant's Enlightenment ideal - the courage to use your own understanding without others' guidance, embodied in Hammond's isolated, independently funded genius - has become dangerous now that one man's project can shape the world future people must live in. He tracks Hammond's arc from dismissing Malcolm's review to conceding 'I was wrong', but shows in the sequels that one convert to caution cannot contain a technology: Ludlow ships a T. rex to San Diego and paragliding entrepreneurs land on Isla Sorna anyway, so the damage to future people is done before anyone repents. Against this he proposes Rupert Read's 'Guardians of the Future', a super jury that would veto laws and technologies threatening future people's basic needs - meaning Hammond should have been stopped before he started.
- ch. 31 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — The Four Causes; Anthropocentrism — Daniel Kokotz tests Aristotle's border between natural things, moved by inner final causes, and technological artifacts, made for external human purposes, against the park's dinosaurs - and both fit. They hunt, grow, and breed on their own ('life finds a way', the sex-changing frogs-DNA population), yet they exist only because Hammond's scientists cloned them as paying entertainment, exactly like an artifact. Using Grant's different reactions to the incubator versus the wild nest, he shows we blame humans for technologically caused disasters while shrugging at natural ones; his resolution is that nature and technology are not two Aristotelian boxes but extremes of one spectrum, and biotechnology's hybrids prove humans never stood outside nature - we are still just prey in it.
- ch. 32 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Jeremy Bentham, Peter Singer, René Descartes — The Death of God; Speciesism; Utilitarianism; Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life) — Kenn Fisher reads the franchise as the extinction of the Great Chain of Being: from Plato's humans mid-way between gods and beasts, through Genesis dominion and Descartes's non-conscious animals, to Malcolm's 'God creates man. Man destroys God. Man creates dinosaurs' - with computers (Hammond's control room, Wikipedia, Google) replacing God at the top. He then tracks animals' ascent from property: Francione on welfare versus personhood, Bentham's 'Can they suffer?', and Singer's equality-as-prescription, applied to Dieter Stark tasering a compy (punished by the compys) versus the unexamined electric fences that keep InGen's 'zoo animals' caged. He closes with Carol Adams's meat-masculinity link - Roland Tembo will hunt only a male T. rex, the dinosaurs are all female - and holds up vegetarian Lex as hope that the Chain bends into a Circle.
- ch. 33 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Tom Regan, Martha Nussbaum — Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life); The Capabilities Approach; Speciesism — Roger Hunt argues the films show that whoever assumes human superiority or ownership of created life gets eaten: Dieter Stark zaps a compy and is devoured by a swarm of them, Ludlow claims InGen's patent and becomes a hunting lesson for the baby T. rex. He stages the real debate over dinosaur moral status between the 'value of life' view (Harding and Sattler: experiment on them if enough sentient beings benefit) and Tom Regan's rights view - anything sentient has basic rights from unconditional individual value, and grading lives by usefulness is speciesism like racism or sexism. He then prefers Martha Nussbaum's capabilities approach: a T. rex penned in San Diego cannot roam, hunt, or play, so it cannot flourish, and the point is not granting rights but recognizing we have no right to block other beings' flourishing for fun.
- ch. 34 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 35 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Gilbert Ryle — Personal Identity; The Ghost in the Machine; Immortality and the Soul — Nicolas Michaud argues that when you go extinct you do not come back, dismantling our three hopes: the soul, cloning, and an essential self. Cloning a T. rex yields a new individual 'Rex' with her own perspective, not the original; the soul cannot both be beyond science's reach and record memories and move bodies; and he deploys Gilbert Ryle's view that 'soul' is a language mistake - a mind is just what a functioning brain does, the way 'Jurassic Park' is just buildings, dinosaurs, staff, and money working together, ceasing when the functioning stops. A Hammond stripped by Alzheimer's and atom-replacement of every memory, trait, and particle still gets called John Hammond, so there is no essence over time: we already die constantly and never notice, which is why a second extinction holds no fresh terror.
- ch. 36 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Will to Power; The Übermensch — Jeff Ewing reads the park as Nietzsche's will-to-power in action: Hammond's 'creation is an act of sheer will', resurrecting extinct species because science enables it, is the drive to remake the world godlike in one's own image, and Malcolm's 'so preoccupied with whether or not they could' marks exactly the external mastery Hammond achieved. But the Nietzschean great man also needs internal mastery - control over himself before control over nature - and Hammond failed it: one man (Nedry) held the whole security grid, frog-DNA surprises were foreseeable, raptor intelligence was underestimated, and the storm was ignored. For Nietzsche, lying to yourself about your strength is not arrogance but weakness, so Hammond's disaster was not a moral crime but a failure of self-mastery - a lesson Ewing extends to modern cloning, drones, and weapons pursued without honest reckoning.
- ch. 37 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Francis Bacon — Existential Risk; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Jannelle Poetzsch introduces Hans Jonas's imperative of responsibility - 'Act so that the effects of your action are compatible with the permanence of genuine human life' - through Malcolm's charge that scientists never ask whether they should, since nuclear and genetic power now reach people no old 'love thy neighbor' ethic could imagine. Hammond is Bacon's 'knowledge is power' grown too successful: his 'thintelligence' embodies the gap between the power to act and the ability to foretell, so Jonas demands humility, worst-case rather than optimistic planning, and social institutions strong enough to check people who act merely because they can. Jonas also indicts capitalism's profit motive - Hammond risked his grandchildren and Costa Rican infants to manufacture a need for a dinosaur zoo - closing that the answer is not halting technology but thoughtfully restraining it, because some things cannot be re-caged.
- ch. 38 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Free Will vs Determinism; Scientific Realism vs Anti-Realism — David Morgan unpacks the chaos theory behind Malcolm's 'I hate being right all the time', starting from Newtonian causal determinism and Laplace's demon, according to which the future is fixed and calculable - then showing that non-linear systems like weather and the park show sensitive dependence (Lorenz's butterfly effect), so even a fully determined universe is unpredictable in practice because we can never know initial conditions exactly. He raises the philosophy-of-science question of whether chaos is a property of nature or only of our computer models, and reads 'life finds a way' as Malcolm extending the same contingency to Darwinian evolution: rewind the tape and you get different species. He ends by noting Malcolm's own paradox - a chaos theorist certain of one specific doom - and settles on chaos theory predicting unpredictability: ignore tiny factors and do not be surprised when they bite you.
- ch. 39 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Aquinas — Cardinal and Theological Virtues; Natural Law — Michael Stark and A.G. Holdier diagnose the park's collapse as vice rather than accident, using Aquinas's virtue ethics grounded in natural law: Hammond's imprudence (never weighing whether resurrection is right, shrugging off Malcolm's lunch-table warning, still planning 'corrections' over melted ice cream) and Gennaro's intemperance (admissions up to ten thousand dollars a day, 'coupon day', money tunnel-vision), both flowering from pride, the worst vice. Malcolm, despite the flirting and leather, is the only consistently virtuous character: prudence in situational judgment (waving a flare to lure the T. rex away from the kids), temperance about wealth and about Sattler, plus fortitude and justice - even lying to comfort Lex and Tim in the tree reflects character, not rule-breaking. Aquinas's point that virtues are lifelong course corrections, never a finished state, frames the closing Kierkegaard quote about never being finished with life's task.
- ch. 40 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" **·** Existential Risk; Ethical Egoism — Trip McCrossin takes Grant's 'they'll just have to evolve, too' as addressed to the audience and asks whether human behavior, not meteors, could cause our extinction, using Malcolm's toolkit: self-organizing life on fitness landscapes, the Red Queen arms race you must run to stay in place, and Gambler's Ruin - once things go bad they cluster and go to hell together. The deepest lens is the Tragedy of the Commons: like herders each adding a sheep to a shared pasture, we do what is best for ourselves without concern for the common good, and the films chart ever-failing 'fences' - the paddock, the coastline, the Navy, the Marines - until pteranodons simply fly off Isla Sorna. Since ours is self-destruction (we run as fast as we can, but really from ourselves), the real fence, he concludes, is daring ourselves to collective restraint.
- ch. 41 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 42 "Jurassic Park and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Justified and Philosophy - Shoot First, Think Later

- ch. 1 "Justified and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Justified and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Justified and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Justified and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Justified and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Justified and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Justified and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The introduction argues that Justified grips its audience because nothing in it is black-and-white: even Mags Bennett has some good in her, even Art gives Raylan a black eye, and Harlan's crime is bred by coal-industry exploitation while religion (Billy St. Cyr's Last Chance Salvation Church, Boyd's born-again congregation of ex-cons) may be salvation or a grift. Its central example is the pilot's Miami rooftop scene, where Raylan gives hitman Tommy Bucks twenty-four hours to leave town and then kills him when the clock runs out, raising the question the whole book pursues: is Raylan justified, or is it murder dressed up as cleaning up Harlan County? It closes by previewing the book's essays in ethics, political philosophy, epistemology, metaphysics, and logic.
- ch. 8 "Justified and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Socrates, Plato — Virtue Ethics (arete); Justice in the Republic — The chapter argues Boyd Crowder personifies coolness - composure, wily cleverness, unconventionality, intelligence, likability - as shown in the 'Cottonmouth' payroll-robbery scene where he turns his flip phone into a room bug, overhears Kyle planning to blow him up in the mine, and lets the plotters kill themselves. Jon Cotton borrows Wittgenstein's family-resemblance model to define coolness as an indefinable cluster of traits (with Dewey Crowe as the agreed uncool case), and reaches back to Socrates' defining of ethical virtues and the Republic's just, well-ordered soul to argue that coolness is a genuine ethical virtue - the excellence of a properly functioning person - in a scientific, post-religious ethics.
- ch. 10 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Deontology; Consequentialism — The chapter juxtaposes the 1992 no-knock raid in which 84-year-old paraplegic Annie Rae Dixon was shot in her bed with Raylan's pilot-episode ultimatum to Tommy Bucks, arguing both deaths count as 'justified' only because aggressive proactive policing manufactured the lethal encounter. It catalogues Raylan's tactics - provoking draws, kidnapping the cartel chief's niece and Dickie Bennett, the lucky-sniper standoff at the Bennett compound - and asks whether we want our police to be judge, jury, and executioner. On the deontological approach Raylan's routine lawbreaking cannot be justified whatever the results, and even on the consequentialist approach the war-on-drugs data (soaring murder rates in targeted neighborhoods, mass incarceration) suggest proactive policing fails on its own terms.
- ch. 11 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Herbert Marcuse — One-Dimensional Man; Class Struggle — The chapter argues coal made Boyd Crowder possible: company scrip, Bloody Harlan's union-busting terror, black lung (Boyd's would-be assassin takes the bridge job because mining ruined his lungs), and Black Pike's mountaintop removal leave Harlan no viable work outside the mines, so crime is the rational alternative. It reads this through Hegel's 'rabble' from the Philosophy of Right - civil society is never wealthy enough to prevent an excess of poverty, and the rabble loses the honor of self-support - plus Marcuse's affluent-society critique of an existence that 'violently defends its own servitude'. Boyd's outlawry, which sets its own code above mere criminality, is a rebellion against being pushed into the rabble, and Hegel's remedy of emigration explains why leaving Harlan is the only escape, though the coal train hauls coal out while the people stay.
- ch. 12 "Justified and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The chapter applies Critical Race Theory - Derrick Bell's theses that racism is ordinary and structural, advances white interests (interest convergence, illustrated by his reading of Brown v. Board), and that race is socially constructed - to argue Justified reinforces white superiority. Its Black characters are overwhelmingly criminals (bookie Arnold Pinter, enforcer Curtis Mims, bail jumper Jody Adair, drug-dealing butcher Ellstin Limehouse, who disciplines Bernard with the lye pit), law enforcement is white, and even the positive Rachel Brooks remains a bit-part mystery. Following media research on differential racialization and the cumulative effect of TV portrayals, it concludes the show is one more vehicle teaching viewers that whites keep African Americans in line.
- ch. 13 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Edmund Gettier, Alvin Goldman, David Hume — The Gettier Problem; Philosophical Skepticism; Problem of Induction — The chapter turns Raylan's claim that shooting Tommy Bucks was 'justified' into a question of epistemic justification, expounding Plato's Meno definition of knowledge as justified true belief, Gettier's 1963 counterexamples (rebuilt with Boyd truly but luckily predicting that a marshal will be arrested), and the repairs of Goldman's no-false-causal-link condition and Harman's inference to the best explanation. It then grades the show's reasoners: Raylan knows the Crowes rather than Boyd killed Wade Messer, Boyd knows Cassie milks the revival snakes' venom, Art Mullen does not yet know Raylan helped Winona return the evidence-room money, and gut-feeling victims like Ellen May do not know what they saw. Against Humean skepticism about cause and effect it defends inductive, detective-style knowledge - Raylan can know who done it without courtroom proof.
- ch. 14 "Justified and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** William James, William of Ockham — Pragmatism; Faith and Reason — The chapter asks whether Boyd's survival of Raylan's bullet in the premiere was a genuine sign from God: the atheist invokes Ockham's Razor and the promise of a naturalistic explanation, while the theist replies that a world-creating God would act through the laws of nature, making such explanations neutral rather than negative evidence. It then applies William James's pragmatist standard from his study of mystical experience - judge the sign by the fruits of the Spirit (Galatians' love, joy, peace, self-control) it bears - to Boyd's prison ministry, his urging Raylan to forgive Arlo, and his meth-lab crusade. Despite Boyd's eventual apostasy after Bo murders his congregation, the author concludes he passes James's test, since faith is a maintained journey that can be lost, and the born-again Boyd still struggles - like Jacob becoming Israel - rather than scheming coldly.
- ch. 16 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, G.W.F. Hegel — Teleological Suspension of the Ethical; The Leap of Faith; Faith and Reason — The chapter reads preacher Billy St. Cyr's snake-handling through Kierkegaard's Fear and Trembling: like Abraham, Billy performs the absurd double movement - fully resigning himself to dying by rattlesnake while believing he will be spared - which is why Boyd Crowder, who abandoned faith for Hegel's objective rational certainty, can neither buy him out nor understand him. Kierkegaard's teleological suspension of the ethical explains why Billy and Abraham may set obedience to God above self-preservation and social morality, while Boyd's showdown in 'Truth and Consequences', where he hands Billy an un-milked rattlesnake, exposes that Billy's certainty has curdled into the hubris de Silentio warns against, so Billy is no Abraham. De Silentio's three marks - love, humility, no desire to instruct others - separate genuine faith from cult leaders and violent extremists.
- ch. 17 "Justified and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Cast as a pub dialogue between philosopher Christian Cotton and therapist Anthony Palazzo over Ava Crowder's killing of her abusive husband Bowman at supper, the chapter asks when extra-legal violence is morally justified. It explains her entrapment through systems theory - homeostasis, learned helplessness, self-esteem destruction, false hope - and takes up Kant's debt metaphor for punishment, on which wrongdoers owe a penalty and retribution restores balance, to ask who may collect when the legal system treats domestic violence as a 'family matter'. They conclude Bowman did not strictly deserve death, but Ava's 'I did what I had to do' is best understood as acting from anger in pursuit of justice and self-reclamation rather than on anger for vengeance.
- ch. 19 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — Deontology; Consequentialism — The chapter maps the Raylan-Boyd rivalry onto two ethical theories: Raylan is a Kant-style deontologist who does his duty (tracking Tommy Bucks to Miami, weighing duty to the law against duty to Arlo, giving every opponent the choice to walk away), while Boyd is a Mill-style consequentialist maximizing good for himself and Ava, whose vigilante church and revenge plots follow from outcomes rather than rules. Their season-one team-up against Bo Crowder and the Miami shooters is read as contractarianism, two moral agents cooperating toward a right. Each man's choices become his undoing - Raylan's damsel-saving affairs, Mags's family loyalty, Dewey's impulsiveness - which the chapter takes as the show's thesis that what we do results in who we are.
- ch. 20 "Justified and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The chapter argues the show's refrain 'He pulled first; I was justified' never closes the question, using J.L. Austin's 'A Plea for Excuses' distinction between justifying an act (admitting it happened but denying it was bad) and excusing it (accepting it was bad but denying responsibility) - a line Raylan himself blurs when he confesses to Winona he may have wanted revenge for what Tommy did in Nicaragua. It deploys Austin's performatives from How to Do Things with Words on the pilot's rooftop scene: Tommy treats Raylan's countdown and dinner-chat as failed speech acts lacking authority and uptake ('supreme bullshit'), and Boyd's 'God made me do it' defense of the meth-lab bombing is likewise just an excuse, as Art's thrown Bible insists. Judge Reardon's collapse into a gray case, Ava's manslaughter plea, and Roland Pike's faith that kindness and retribution are systemically owed round out the claim that in a performative world justification is an impossible standard.
- ch. 21 "Justified and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Justified and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The chapter transplants Edward Said's Orientalism to Appalachia as 'Appalachianism': reality shows like Here Comes Honey Boo Boo and Mountain Monsters, and the hillbilly-redneck archetype, construct the region's people as ignorant savages so their land can be strip-mined 'for their own good' - the same logic by which colonizers claimed the Orient needed civilizing. Mags Bennett becomes the subaltern given voice, her 'The Spoil' speech about slurry impoundments and poisoned valleys speaking for generations of Appalachian women, while Boyd's taunt to Detroit's Robert Quarles - 'You're a conquistador... Only, we are not your savages' - names the colonial dynamic outright. The chapter contends that Appalachianism hides the contest for coal behind our amusement at 'those people', and that the show mostly reproduces the stereotype it occasionally resists.
- ch. 23 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Catharine MacKinnon, bell hooks — Feminism; Objectification; Intersectionality — The chapter surveys Harlan's gendered violence - Ava's killing of Bowman, the routine roughing-up of Audrey's working girls, Robert Quarles's childhood prostitution - and uses Catharine MacKinnon's account of patriarchal power imbalance to explain victim-blaming, underreporting, and police disbelief of trauma responses in domestic-violence and rape cases. On prostitution it weighs sex-positive feminism against the objectification worry, concluding via Teri's line about 'sucking hillbilly dicks for money' that Audrey's women have little real choice even under Ava's management. Finally it brings in bell hooks's third-wave point that oppression cannot be split from race and class, plus Peggy McIntosh's invisible backpack of white privilege, to read Noble's Holler's isolation and the bombed black church as evidence that patriarchy in Justified is always also racial and economic.
- ch. 24 "Justified and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Sigmund Freud — The chapter argues Justified populates both sides of the law with failed patriarchs: Arlo beats his wife and son, sets Raylan up at Bo's motel, replaces Raylan with surrogate son Boyd (and dies shooting at the man he wrongly thinks threatens him); Bo hangs Boyd's congregation as 'Crowder gospel'; Theo Tonin passes over Robert Quarles for his biological son Sammy, creating a monster who in turn phones his own ignored son beside a bound hostage; and Raylan grows into a deadbeat dad who Skypes his daughter from Harlan. Reading the fathers in the Freudian terms borrowed from film scholar Janet Walker - the father as the son's potential killer - it shows only Art Mullen does the work of a real father, repeatedly rescuing, forgiving, and finally punching the son who disappoints him, making the show a baroque inversion of the Western's promised happy family.
- ch. 26 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Aristotle, Plato — Man as Political Animal; The Philosopher King — The chapter reads season two's families through Engels's Origin of the Family, Private Property and the State: Mags performs the patriarch's role of accumulating and bequeathing wealth (the Black Pike payout 'sufficient to provide for my kin for generations'), Arlo fails it by hoarding nothing for Raylan, and Ava joins Boyd's crime precisely when he hands her the mine money for her mortgage - a moment the author calls borderline prostitution, as Engels predicts. Hegel's Philosophy of Right, where the family exists for love and for forming free moral beings who must leave it, exposes the Bennetts' failure on both counts, while Aristotle's Politics (the polis is prior to the family) and Plato's Republic (rulers without private families, children raised in common) ground the claim that feuding clans need the state. The Kentucky-fried twist: when government and coal companies treat mountain families as spoil, family is sometimes all you've got.
- ch. 27 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Sigmund Freud, Arthur Schopenhauer — Philosophical Pessimism; The World as Will — The chapter reads Harlan's family bonds through Freud's Oedipus Complex - Raylan bails Arlo out only at stepmother Helen's request, and Coover whimpers 'I'm sorry, Mamma' while cradled in Mags's arms after she hammers his hand, the submissive son destroyed by his need for his mother's approval. It then applies Schopenhauer's pessimism to Winona's pregnancy: if life is suffering, as On the Vanity of Existence argues, bringing a child into Harlan is selfish vanity, and Mags's dynasty-building - grooming Loretta, bequeathing the weed business - is the will-to-live seeking immortality through the family name. Only Boyd and Ava's unsentimental care for the demented Arlo, the chapter suggests, escapes both the Oedipal trap and the vanity of lineage.
- ch. 28 "Justified and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Jeremy Bentham — The Hedonic Calculus; Utilitarianism — The chapter argues Ava Crowder lives by hedonism - from Aristippus's pursuit of pleasure to Bentham's utilitarianism - with Bentham's censorial principle (weighing everyone's happiness) stripped out: she kisses Raylan moments after shooting Bowman, takes over Audrey's, orders the murder of Ellen May, and in Season Five has Penny dislocate her arm for infirmary access, kills Judith in the chapel, and finally informs on Boyd to Raylan for her own release. Bentham's seven-dimension hedonic calculus (intensity, duration, certainty, propinquity, fecundity, purity, extent) supplies the framework for each choice, and the verdict is that Ava's pleasure-maximizing left her directly or indirectly responsible for three deaths - which, for her, is all that matters.
- ch. 30 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** The Male Gaze; Feminism — The chapter argues Mags Bennett is the show's 'outlaw mother', read through feminist media theory: she escapes Laura Mulvey's male gaze as an unkempt older woman viewers are never invited to look at pleasurably, defies the institution of motherhood described by Adrienne Rich (hammering Coover's hand as tough love), and literalizes Andrea O'Reilly's outlaw mothering - questioning the 'shoulds' of motherhood and not always putting children first. Stuart Hall's encoding/decoding model explains how audiences can hold her simultaneously as the poisoned-apple-pie murderer of Walt McCready and the maternal folk hero of the whoop-de-do. Her Black Pike reversal - the community speech followed by selling out the mountain for Doyle's sons - seals the portrait of a mother whose love is indistinguishable from strategy.
- ch. 31 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Virtue Ethics (arete) — The chapter diagnoses Raylan with Aristotle's akrasia (lack of self-restraint): Art's 'lost cause' lecture, Arlo's boast that he has never shot anyone, and Boyd's 'you wake up in the morning pretending that you are not the bad guy' all charge him with acting on passion against his own calculations - sleeping with witness Ava, resuming adultery with Winona when her ring comes off. Because Raylan feels regret, the Aristotelian mark of the unselfrestrained person who knows his condition, he is curable and lacks self-restraint by habit rather than nature (Nicky Augustine's limo verdict: 'it's not who you are'). The chapter reads Raylan's signature move - forcing men to draw first so their deaths count as justified - as a perversion of justice by an akratic who is not wicked, and the series arc as his slow acquisition of self-restraint.
- ch. 32 "Justified and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Augustine of Hippo — Doctrine of Double Effect — The chapter assembles real contested self-defense cases beside Raylan's shooting of Tommy Bucks - the OK Corral and Wyatt Earp's far-from-clean vendetta ride, Curtis Reeves killing a texting moviegoer over thrown popcorn, George Zimmerman and Trayvon Martin, Ossian Sweet's mobbed house, Bernie Goetz's subway shooting, Gary Plauche executing his son's abuser on camera, and the Texas father who beat his daughter's rapist to death. Its philosophical machinery is Aquinas's principle of double effect from the Summa Theologica (self-defense may have two effects, saving one's life and slaying the aggressor, only the first intended), set against St. Augustine's condemnation of private self-defense as inordinate self-love. Florida's stand-your-ground law - where the dead man cannot tell his side - supplies the legal gray zone the show's title exploits.
- ch. 33 "Justified and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Justified and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## KISS and Philosophy - Wiser than Hell

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "KISS and Philosophy Wiser than Hell" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "KISS and Philosophy Wiser than Hell" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Corrupting the Youth Since 1974" *(no index entry yet)* — Editor Courtland Lewis's introduction argues that KISS — the make-up, pyrotechnics, anthems, libido-centered songs, and even the 'Knights in Satan's Service' rumor — makes the band irresistible material for philosophy, quoting Gene Simmons's claim that KISS's original philosophy was 'to create magic and to encourage the suspension of disbelief.' It lays out the volume's structure (sections for the Demon, Space Ace, Starchild, Catman, and Fox) and previews each contributor's chapter, from Siler on the Message to Rentmeester on Kondoms and Kaskets. Lewis closes with personal testimony that KISS's message of perseverance kept him from ever giving up.
- ch. 8 "I The Demon" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "1. The Message of KISS" **·** Self-Reliance — R. Alan Siler argues that KISS's deepest and most consistent philosophy is a message of self-belief: work hard, believe in yourself, never give up. He traces it from 'Shout It Out Loud''s 'treat yourself like Number 1' and 'Flaming Youth' through The Elder's oath narrative ('only you are the answer') and its closing declaration 'I believe in me,' to Eric Carr's 'Little Caesar' and Carr's final backing vocals on 'God Gave Rock'n'Roll to You II,' reading Carr's scrappy rise and death as the Message incarnate. Siler contends the Eighties albums are the Message's peak and closes by showing how adherence to it organized his own life accomplishments.
- ch. 10 "2. KISStory and Kulture of the Hottest Band in the World" **·** Personal Identity; The Meaning of Life — Historian and lifelong fan Matt Alschbach argues that the history of KISS is the history of its fans: his police-officer father taking him to the 1979 San Diego show, schoolyard debates over Alive! versus Alive II, rubbing pencil shadings over the Double Platinum gatefold to guess the band's faces, and strangers' comments on his KISS T-shirts all show the band forging identity and community. He recounts how Alive! rescued Casablanca Records, how the 1983 MTV unmasking and 1996 remasking reinvented the brand for new generations, and how the KISS KRUISE, $50,000 Vault Experience, and Rock & Brews extend its cultural reach. The chapter's claim is that KISS fandom supplies camaraderie, family bonds, and life-meaning across generations.
- ch. 11 "3. Paul’s Lick It Up Philosophy" **·** Socrates, Plato, Albert Camus, Aristotle, John Locke, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The Unexamined Life; The Absurd; Eudaimonia (flourishing); The Golden Mean — Courtland Lewis reads Paul Stanley's autobiographies (Face the Music, Backstage Pass) and songs as a worked-out life philosophy. Stanley's turn from chasing approval to confronting his inner pain exemplifies Socrates' unexamined-life dictum; 'Reason to Live' is glossed through Camus's Sisyphus — ground your reason to live in something indestructible, your own self-chosen meaning — while Plato's Ion explains Stanley's mix of learned craft and divine-inspired performance. Aristotle's flourishing through moderation appears in the advice not to overplay 'Rock and Roll All Nite,' and Lewis adds Locke and Rousseau on how laws, boundaries, and education create rather than restrict freedom, closing with Stanley's 'questioning ourselves is not a weakness' as Socratic wisdom.
- ch. 12 "4. Knights in Satan’s Service" **·** Aristotle, Augustine of Hippo — Eudaimonia (flourishing); The Golden Mean — Donald Presley debunks the claim that KISS are 'Knights in Satan's Service' by showing the pitchfork Satan is a pop-culture invention — tracing the Adversary from the Book of Job through Zoroastrian influence, Dante, and Milton — and arguing that branding Gene's Demon persona or songs like 'God of Thunder' and 'Unholy' as evil commits the informal fallacy of missing the point. He then argues, ironically, that KISS's actual values — hard work, self-empowerment, sexual freedom, indulgence without harming others — overlap substantially with the real LaVeyan Satanism of The Satanic Bible and with Aristotle's ideal of moderately indulging natural goods (sex, alcohol, success) for the sake of flourishing. The conclusion: KISS is not Satanic, but its life-affirming message coincidentally mirrors Satanism's actual teachings, and Christians, KISS, and Satanic virtues can coexist.
- ch. 13 "5. Greatness or Nothingness" **·** William James, Friedrich Nietzsche, Blaise Pascal — The Will to Believe; Pascal's Wager; Truth as What Works; Will to Power — Courtland Lewis uses KISS's career gambles to compare theories of decision-making. After Tolstoy's twig-over-the-dragon allegory of mortal life, he tests W.K. Clifford's shipowner (never act without sufficient evidence) against KISS's counter-evidence bet on the Alive! live album, models the choice with Pascal's Wager, and uses the Prisoner's Dilemma to explain the flop of the four 1978 solo albums. He then argues William James's pragmatism — beliefs as rules for action, the living, forced, and momentous option that 'KISS is the best' — best explains why acting on the band's self-created narrative was authentic, with Nietzsche completing the picture: greatness requires leaping, living dangerously, tightrope-walking toward self-defined success.
- ch. 14 "II The Space Ace" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "6. The KISS Franchise" **·** Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity — Peter Finocchiaro takes Gene Simmons's and manager Doc McGhee's suggestions that KISS could become a franchise — new musicians stepping into the Demon and Starchild roles — and works out what band identity could mean. Using the Ship of Theseus, he distinguishes qualitative from numerical identity, invokes collective intentionality to explain how a band without a mind can perform intentional actions, and contrasts the 'originality connection' (being the actual founding members) with the 'continuity connection' (day-by-day chained succession), mirroring the bodily-versus-psychological debate about Gene Simmons's personal identity. His conclusion is that bands are governed by social rules fans collectively create — like the rules of basketball or musical genre — so in his imaginary two-KISS scenario, and for any future franchise, the fan base would decide which band is the real KISS rather than discovering a pre-existing fact.
- ch. 16 "7. Destroyer Unmasked" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — The Dialectic — L.A. Recoder's densely dialectical essay presents KISS as a 'philosophical toy' that taught him creativity through destruction, pivoting on Paul Stanley's line that 'we loved you until we strapped on our guitars, and then we were there to destroy you.' He reads the oral history of KISS upstaging Black Sabbath — the 'KISS! KISS! KISS!' chant that got them thrown off the tour — and Ted Nugent's challenged escalation as the destroyer's veneration-through-destruction, then reads Chuck Schuldiner's death-metal band Death covering 'God of Thunder' as Hegel's tarrying with the negative: the KISS of Death turns round upon itself to become the Death of KISS, through which KISS is reborn.
- ch. 17 "8. Philosophy and the Personae" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Epicurus, Plato, Aristotle — Eternal Recurrence; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); The Übermensch — Randall Auxier builds a characterology of the four personae using Aristotle's methods (endoxa and aporia) and the band's wealth hierarchy as his map. The Demon is Nietzsche — an uninhibited cosmopolitan beyond good and evil who philosophizes with a hammer and whispers eternal recurrence; the Starchild is Epicurus — Paul's microtia and bullied childhood formed the detached soul that Plato's Republic ties to being a philosopher, yielding ataraxia, aponia, therapy, and disciplined long-term pleasures; the Catman has Kundera's lightness of being around 'Beth'; and the Spaceman's otherworldly detachment explains both his guitar genius and the Nazi-regalia incident. The chapter's thesis: KISS succeeded because Gene and Paul's detached, centered self-knowledge let them give fans exactly what they secretly wanted.
- ch. 18 "9. KISS the Ship of Theseus Goodbye!" **·** Plutarch, Thomas Hobbes — Ship of Theseus — Shane Ralston asks whether KISS, after constant lineup changes that left only Stanley and Simmons fixtures, is still the same band. He lays out Plutarch's Ship of Theseus and Hobbes's harder variant — a ship rebuilt from the salvaged original planks — formalizing three assumptions (endurance through gradual change, identity with parts, transitivity of identity) and their conflicting conclusions, then applies a fifty-percent-plus-quality reading: keeping the two powerhouse founding members preserved KISS's core identity through the Carr, Vincent, St. John, Kulick, Singer, and Thayer eras. For the imagined duplicate-KISS case he deploys Chisholm's 'loose and popular sense' of identity and Ted Sider's four-dimensionalist 'space-time worm,' concluding the dispute is practical — best settled by courts of law, not armchair metaphysics.
- ch. 19 "III The Starchild" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "10. KISS Was Made for Lovin’ You" **·** Philosophy of Love; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Courtland Lewis categorizes virtually the entire KISS catalogue by kind of love. Using Nicholas Wolterstorff's distinctions, he sorts erotic-activity songs ('Uh! All Night,' 'Deuce'), erotic-attachment songs ('I Still Love You,' 'Calling Dr. Love'), and erotic-attraction songs ('I Was Made for Lovin' You,' 'Hotter than Hell'), flags the disturbing near-statutory trio ('Christine Sixteen,' 'Goin' Blind,' 'Domino'), and adds non-erotic attachment, philia ('Childhood's End'), agape ('I Will Be There'), and the thirty-three percent of songs advocating a way of life ('Lick It Up' as an anti-suicide anthem of self-reclamation). He concludes with Aristotle and Plato on flourishing and Wolterstorff's claim that lives are 'shot through with love,' inviting readers to re-sort the lists with better reasons.
- ch. 21 "11. Excess (I Love It)" **·** Cicero — Philosophy of Love — Robert Vuckovich reads KISS's sex songs through Aristippus of Cyrene's hedonism: immediate bodily pleasure is the highest good, so the 'urge to merge' of 'Fits Like a Glove,' the whips of 'Sweet Pain,' the persuasion of 'Nothin' to Lose,' and the stewardess encounter of 'Room Service' are candid validations of pleasure — morally acceptable where desire and consent are mutual. Aristippus's cautions (the prostitute visit, the three courtesans he declined, his non-exclusive arrangement with Lais) ground the chapter's limits on excess, mirrored in Gene passing girls to Point Blank's guitarist and Paul's shift to quality over quantity. The Cyrenaic alternation of work and play matches KISS's 'God Gave Rock'n'Roll to You II' ethic — sweat first, then indulge — and Cicero's report that the body is 'nature's canon and rule' closes the case.
- ch. 22 "12. An Intimate Look at Paul Stanley’s Love" **·** Plato, Thomas Aquinas — Platonic Love (the ladder of love); Philosophy of Love; Objectification — Robert Grant Price maps Paul Stanley's catalogue onto four loves. Eros as soulmate-love follows Aristophanes' split-beings myth from Plato's Symposium ('Forever,' 'Every Time I Look at You'); self-love follows Aquinas's demand that it be 'rightly ordered,' so Stanley's machismo songs pass while Gene's 'I' fails as disordered egomania; bodily love is defended as bawdy dramatic monologue — foreplay between lovers rather than instruction in reducing women to sex objects; and love of life is secularized agape praise in 'I'm Alive,' 'Shout It Out Loud,' and the very title Alive!. Price then asks why love drives art at all, weighing the transcendentals (true, good, beautiful) against the Scruton-versus-Craig-Martin can-of-shit debate, and reads 'Reason to Live' as the claim that only rightly ordered love can ground a life.
- ch. 23 "13. You Wanted the Best, You’ve Got the Right to the Best" **·** Utilitarianism — Mikko Puumala invents a 'Preservationist' who argues that future generations have a right to the chance of experiencing a live KISS show, by analogy with preserving the Colosseum and Niagara Falls, and then dismantles the argument. A reductio ad absurdum shows the principle would oblige preserving nearly every rock band; the Castle thought experiment exposes the problem that after the End of the Road tour there is no fact about which historical KISS (masked Seventies, unmasked Eighties, reunion era) should be restored; and the museumization worry shows that freezing KISS would contradict the adaptability — disco, glam, grunge — that defines KISStory. He concludes there is no duty on Simmons and Stanley to keep KISS alive, though a modest utilitarian case favors the show going on.
- ch. 24 "IV The Catman" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "14. The Joyful Relativity of Kondoms and Kaskets" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Casey Rentmeester reads KISS through Nietzsche and Mikhail Bakhtin. The music — 'Rock and Roll All Nite,' 'Love Gun,' and the Alive! spectacle of blood-spitting, fire-breathing personas — embodies Nietzsche's Dionysian, the raw instinctual side that Socratic rationalism suppressed, with concerts supplying the Dionysian fusion of the crowd. The band's transformations into the Demon and Starchild (which Gene and Paul describe as real, not acted), the face-painted audiences, and the taboo-breaking merchandise — KISS Kondoms that helped destigmatize condoms, the KISS Kasket that turned death into celebration as at Dimebag Darrell Abbott's funeral — enact Bakhtin's carnival, whose 'joyful relativity' decrowns the serious, with Zarathustra's 'free death' framing the Kasket as festival rather than mourning.
- ch. 26 "15. The Rashomon Effect in KISS’s Autobiographies" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Bad Faith; Philosophical Skepticism — Michael Forest and Matthew Mitchell read the four original members' autobiographies as a Rashomon effect, focused on the conflicting accounts of recording Destroyer and of who really wrote 'Beth.' Bernard Lonergan's 'scotosis' — the intellectual blind spot that censors evidence against our preferred view — explains how each memoirist constructs memories protecting his self-image, while Sartre's bad faith from Being and Nothingness (distinguished from lying via the woman-on-a-date example) explains how they may have deceived themselves rather than lied. Comparing Gene's, Ace's, Peter's, Paul's, and Lydia Criss's five accounts, the chapter concludes that no one is a reliable narrator and no one a sheer fabricator — and that fans reading the memoirs must guard against their own blind spots and bad faith.
- ch. 27 "16. KISS Goes Disco and Boogies on Simmons’s Bad Faith" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Bad Faith; Authenticity; Existence Precedes Essence — Christopher Innes defends 'I Was Made for Lovin' You' and KISS's disco turn on Dynasty against Gene Simmons's own scorn. Insisting that KISS is essentially and only hard rock treats the band's facticity as an in-itself, like Sartre's waiter who becomes nothing but his role — the bad faith of Simmons and rockist fans alike — whereas human transcendence and nothingness mean the band can authentically choose disco, grunge, or anything new, as Donna Summer's 'I Feel Love,' Pink Floyd, and Queen exemplify. The twist: bad faith is also a comfort, and the chapter ends by conceding that Simmons's hard-rock constancy may itself be an authentic filling of his own nothingness — so who is to say he is wrong?
- ch. 28 "17. Forgive and Make Up" **·** Forgiveness — Courtland Lewis tests philosophical theories of forgiveness against KISS's feuds. Jeffrie Murphy's account (overcoming vindictive resentment on moral grounds) shows Ace has not forgiven Gene's fired-three-times claims; Charles Griswold's twelve-condition 'paradigmatic forgiveness' is likely too demanding for them; Margaret Holmgren's compassionate character-forgiveness fits Bruce Kulick's and Eric Singer's graceful exits; and Geoffrey Scarre's utilitarian reading makes forgiveness a calculation of benefits. Lewis then applies his own rights-based peace-ethics (eireneism) to a thought experiment in which Gene steals Ace's drugs to save his flourishing — no wrong done, so no forgiveness needed — and closes by likening Paul and Gene to a married couple whose ongoing obligations model forgiveness, endorsing Wolfendale's refusal to call anyone unforgiveable.
- ch. 29 "V The Fox" *(front)*
- ch. 30 "18. What Does It Mean if the Band Plays Forever" **·** Marcus Aurelius — Stoicism; The Meaning of Life — Robert Grant Price treats KISS's repeated farewell tours — the 2000 Farewell Tour followed by twelve more tours, then the End of the Road Tour — as false endings that keep reshaping the band's story, running two counterfactuals: folding in 1982 would have made a tragedy of celebrity, quitting after the 2001 Farewell Tour a comedy of graceful closure, while the actual open-ended career risks farce. He then asks whether an aging rock star can be wise, contrasting Stoic virtue — Marcus Aurelius's Meditations on self-control and humility, the cardinal virtues of Prudence, Fortitude, Justice, and Temperance — with the hedonism and libertinism of rock's party ethos, from 'Uh! All Night' to Bernini's Ecstasy of St. Teresa. Knowing when to place an ending, he concludes, is ultimately the philosophical question of what one's life is for.
- ch. 31 "19. Beauty of The Elder" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer, Immanuel Kant, David Hume, Plato — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; The World as Will — Courtland Lewis uses KISS's reviled concept album Music from 'The Elder' as a case study in aesthetics. He contrasts objective theories of beauty (Plato and the Golden Ratio, tested against the 4/4 stomp of 'Rock and Roll All Nite') with Hume's taste and Kant's immediacy thesis that beauty is judged without inference, adds the disinterestedness of aesthetic pleasure, and applies Noel Carroll's moderate actual intentionalism (with Jerrold Levinson on what a musical work is) to argue that fans and band co-create the album's meaning. Peter French's collective memory explains why 'The Elder sucks' hardened into fan identity after Casablanca reordered the track listing and wrecked the boy-hero story, and Schopenhauer's claim that music expresses the in-itself of the world redeems the fans who find the album beautiful.
- ch. 32 "VI Kisstory" *(front)*
- ch. 33 "Band Configurations" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Official Album Releases (Studio, Live, and Compilations)" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "Movies and Other Video Releases" *(notes)*
- ch. 36 "Books" *(notes)*
- ch. 37 "Contributors from The Elder (AKA “The Destroyers”)" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "Index" *(notes)*

## LEGO and Philosophy - Constructing Reality Brick by Brick

- ch. 1 "Series" *(front)*
- ch. 2 "Title page" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Table of Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Notes on Contributors" *(notes)*
- ch. 6 "Introduction Play Well, Philosophize Well!" *(no index entry yet)* — Editors Bacharach and Cook argue that LEGO is not really a toy but a building medium, closer to bricks and paint than to toy-aisle items, from which toys, practical artifacts, and gallery art (Sean Kenney, Nathan Sawaya, Ai Weiwei) are all made — and that this open-endedness is what makes LEGO philosophical. They frame the volume with Aristophanes' Cloud Cuckoo Land from The Birds, noting that Unikitty's rule-free kingdom in The LEGO Movie inherits the same pointed satire of social life. The introduction asks why LEGO in particular (Serious Play, Mindstorms, Friends, the Greenpeace video) invites hard questions about how we construct ourselves and our world, closing with the Danish motto 'leg godt' — play well, and philosophize well.
- ch. 7 "Part I LEGO® AND CREATIVITY" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Notes" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Immanuel Kant, Zhuangzi — Wu Wei (effortless action); Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Willard poses the paradox of creativity: how can a toy that promises creativity come with rigid instructions and a purist fan culture that polices what counts as legitimate building? She reads The LEGO Movie as staging three rival models — the Master Builders' unfettered recombination (Plato's Ion on poetic madness and Kant's mysterious free-playing genius), Emmet's 'thinking outside the box' that yields the mocked double-decker couch, and Finn's father's meticulous vision. Following Aristotle against Plato that poetic skill is a teachable craft, plus psychology of jazz-improvisation studies, flow states, and Zhuangzi's butcher Cook Ding, she argues creativity is the joyful mastery of a process, so following instructions is the etude-stage that builds the database of moves from which free creation grows.
- ch. 9 "Notes" **·** Plato, Sigmund Freud, John Locke, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Democritus — Atomism — Shores opens with Sophie's World comparing LEGO bricks to Democritus' indivisible, endlessly recombinable atoms, and argues LEGO is a system of play and an 'object to think with' that models how ideas and worldviews are assembled, disassembled, and rebuilt. He contrasts Plato's Republic-era suspicion that children's play must be regulated with the Socratic playfulness that treats philosophy itself as serious play, and enlists Locke and Rousseau on play-based learning and Freud on the child at play as a creative writer rearranging its world. Lord Business' Kragle shows play taken too seriously becoming inert, while Caillois' ludus/paidia distinction frames the difference between themed narrative sets, free building, and the goal-directed rigidity of the LEGO video games.
- ch. 10 "Notes" *(no index entry yet)* — Using three scenarios — describing the Eiffel Tower by shape alone, Frank Lloyd Wright's Fallingwater welded to its site, and original-brick houses imagined on Mykonos versus Milwaukee — Fisher defends a moderate formalism about architectural aesthetics: in the pre-1976 'original LEGO world' of standardized, uniform bricks, aesthetic properties like beauty arise from the forms themselves and their hallmark features of versatility, backwards compatibility, and ease of disassembly. Contextualist and functional-beauty accounts of the anti-formalists fail for LEGO architecture because any original-brick design can be built anywhere, at any time, for any function or none, so context cannot fix the structure's aesthetic identity. He extends the point to real-world architectural models, which likewise bear no actual functional loads and are appreciated outside context.
- ch. 11 "Notes" **·** Aristotle, John Locke, Thomas Hobbes — Ship of Theseus; Labor Theory of Property; Personal Identity — Gettings asks whether LEGO set #21103, the Cuusoo DeLorean Time Machine, is numerically identical to Team BTTF's original fan design — and why the fans deserve credit and royalties. Neither matter (his 1982 attic spaceship with bricks replaced one by one) nor form (a license plate added here or there) settles identity, as the Ship of Theseus puzzle and Hobbes' reassembly variation show, analyzed through Aristotle's matter/form distinction for substances. He adopts the causal/historical view: the released set continuously descends from the fans' submission just as the adult author descends from the boy who built the spaceship, and this same historical origin, in Lockean fashion, is what grounds the designers' labor-mixing property right — the musician-and-producer analogy explaining why Team BTTF keeps the larger share of credit.
- ch. 12 "Part II LEGO®, ETHICS, AND RULES" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Notes" **·** Immanuel Kant — Deontology; The Categorical Imperative — Robson argues that The Man Upstairs' real moral flaw is neither stinginess toward Finn nor mere instruction-following, both of which he defends, but 'rule worship': treating LEGO construction instructions — rules with at best conventional status like chess or etiquette rules — as sacrosanct commandments to be followed for their own sake. The contrast is with Kant, who does claim absolute status for central moral rules such as the prohibition on lying, and with the moral/conventional distinction from psychologists like Kelly and Stich that The Man cannot grasp. Emmet's rule-following differs because it serves goals like fitting in, and the flaw scales from office Micromanagers and Velma Staplebot to Inspector Javert and the mafioso, showing how misdirected rule worship becomes genuinely vicious.
- ch. 14 "Notes" **·** Immanuel Kant, Harry Frankfurt — Humanity as an End in Itself — Quanbeck reads The LEGO Movie as a battle between two theories of the value of persons. President Business rules Bricksburg through extrinsic accounts — Harry Frankfurt's view in The Reasons of Love that caring and loving confer value, plus an economic model on which worth equals social contribution — which make Emmet's self-worth swing wildly with others' judgments, from Bad Cop's 'blank slate' recording to Wyldstyle's flattery and dismissal. The philosophical turning point comes with Vitruvius' ghost: Emmet's Kantian enlightenment that rational persons are ends in themselves with intrinsic, mind-independent value, so that 'The prophecy is made up, but it's also true. It's about all of us' — a belief that empowers his self-sacrifice and Wyldstyle's broadcast to the citizens.
- ch. 15 "Notes" **·** Immanuel Kant, Simone de Beauvoir, Jean-Paul Sartre, Christine Korsgaard — Autonomy of the Will; Woman as the Other; The Look (hell is other people) — Chelstrom uses the progression from guided DUPLO builds to free design to argue that autonomy develops in degrees and is relational rather than purely individualist. Kant's self-thinking agent — for whom precepts and formulas are the 'ball and chain' of immaturity — explains Emmet's dependence on Octan's instruction booklets and Lord Business's profit from deference, and Korsgaard's definition frames the individualist autonomy the Master Builders embody. Yet their lone-wolf autonomy fails, and Sartre's look plus Beauvoir's account of woman forced to assume herself as Other explain Wyldstyle/Lucy's deference to others' expectations; Catriona Mackenzie's relational autonomy recasts 'Everything is Awesome' as peers supporting each other's self-development, with Emmet's friendship letting Lucy accept herself.
- ch. 16 "Notes" **·** Martin Heidegger — Authenticity; The They-Self (das Man); The Question Concerning Technology — Miller reads Emmet's arc from 'Tell me what to do, and I'll do it!' conformity toward Vitruvius' 'embrace what is special about you' as a Heideggerian journey from das Man — the anonymous 'one does not do that' of the they-self, named in her notes — toward authenticity. LEGO play exemplifies the ready-to-hand engaged dealing with equipment that is more primordial than detached present-at-hand analysis, moods like boredom disclose the exhaustion of Lord Business's 'calculative thinking', the technological enframing that treats everything as standing-reserve, and art (Heidegger's Van Gogh shoes, a #KeepBuilding ad) reveals hidden cultural truths. Heidegger's 'Only if we are capable of dwelling, only then can we build' grounds her claim that LEGO's 'new toy everyday' recyclability sustains the meditative, sustainable building the book's ethos demands — even while admitting the movie is itself a masterful advertisement.
- ch. 17 "Part III LEGO® AND IDENTITY" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "Notes" **·** Kwame Anthony Appiah — Cook traces how LEGO's 1978 all-yellow 'smiley' minifigure — intended as racially neutral — broke down after the 1999 Star Wars license forced the question of how to mold Lando Calrissian, answered in 2003 with flesh-toned NBA and licensed figures. Using Tak Toyoshima's Secret Asian Man comic (a brown Kobe Bryant minifig confronting a yellow castle soldier and yellow ninjas) and his own Brickworld 'Seven Smiley Samurai' vignette experiment, he shows that introducing racially specific figures retroactively racialized the yellow ones, so the same plastic reads differently by context. The chapter argues for racial constructivism over biological essentialism and over the eliminativist views of Appiah and Naomi Zach: categories like 'black in New Orleans, white in Brazil' are built from conventions and attitudes — we don't treat people differently because of race; they are racialized because we treat them differently.
- ch. 19 "Notes" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, Mary Wollstonecraft, John Stuart Mill, Plato — One Is Not Born a Woman; Feminism; The Subjection of Women; A Vindication of the Rights of Woman — Gutwald takes seven-year-old Charlotte Benjamin's viral letter ('all the girls did was sit at home, go to the beach, and shop... but the boys went on adventures, worked, saved people') to argue the LEGO Friends theme is Wollstonecraftian 'friendly sexism' — revering girls as pretty play-things in a house-bound Heartlake City rather than oppressing them outright. Following Beauvoir's 'One is not born, but rather becomes a woman' and her point that men describe the world from their own perspective, plus Mill's Subjection of Women observation that argument alone cannot beat a 'mass of feeling', she explains how pinkified toy segregation feeds implicit bias, schemas, and stereotype threat in both girls and boys. Her fix is structural: make Friends bricks and mini-dolls compatible with the rest of the system ('Let the Friends go to Space!') and return to the gender-neutral 1981 'What it is is beautiful' ad.
- ch. 20 "Notes" **·** Feminism — Grant and Wainman assess the fan-designed Research Institute set (#21110, with its paleontologist, astronomer, and chemist) — created by geoscientist Ellen Kooijman and the first LEGO set with women scientists — as a genuine step for women in STEM that still markets girls' play as passive role play with lipstick-wearing figures. Drawing on gender socialization research (parents describing babies differently from birth, the draw-a-scientist test, Emily Martin's analysis of gendered egg-and-sperm descriptions in biology texts), they argue that LEGO's gendered coding of minifigures and themes — masculine-coded City police and astronauts, salon-and-bakery Friends — is one brick in the wall steering girls away from science and engineering. They call for sets that embed real science and engineering skill rather than narratives, and for boys to extend their range to the Heartlake Hair Salon too.
- ch. 21 "Notes" **·** John Locke, Søren Kierkegaard, Derek Parfit, Thomas Reid — Personal Identity (memory theory); Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity — Mentyka uses minifigure puzzles — can 'Eliza' and 'Beth' be distinct characters when built from identical pieces, and is Eliza the same character after every piece is swapped out, a minifig Ship of Theseus — to introduce the philosophy of the human person. He runs through supervenience on physical parts, Locke's memory theory and its Butler–Reid controversy (Reid in the taxonomy), the Christian Resurrection-of-the-Body background, and Parfit's despairing deflation that identity matters less than what survives us. Against these he defends personalism, citing Max Scheler and Kierkegaard's call in The Single Individual to become one rather than a crowd: a person is both the sigfig and the AFOL building it, constituted by free choices and actions — which is also why the LEGO Movie's 'Where Are My Pants?' mass society is the enemy.
- ch. 22 "Part IV LEGO®, CONSUMPTION, AND CULTURE" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "Notes" **·** Climate Ethics — Bacharach and Das use Greenpeace's 2014 'Everything is NOT awesome' video — a pristine LEGO Arctic slowly drowning in Shell oil until only a polar bear on a Shell-flagged iceberg remains — to ask what exactly was wrong with LEGO's Shell partnership, and whether a company trading on a wholesome image incurs stricter moral duties. They audit the reality behind the brand: Shell's complicity in Nigeria's repression of the Ogoni and the hanging of Ken Saro-Wiwa, LEGO's outsourcing layoffs in Denmark and the US, and the fact that only about 10 percent of LEGO's carbon emissions come from its own plants, the rest along the supply chain. Their verdict: LEGO is ethically better than most for-profit companies and its fictional Octan satirizes the very consumerism it profits from, but image itself is a fair ground for holding it to a higher standard.
- ch. 24 "Notes" **·** Food Ethics — Van Pelt argues the LEGO Farm theme depicts an agricultural utopia — sparkling tractors, no concentrated animal feeding operations, no manure lagoons, and animals raised only for eggs, wool, and milk, never meat — in line with the vegetarian, small-is-beautiful ideals of activists Michael Pollan and Bill McKibben. The troubling side is the farm-to-fork gap: no butcher shop, no slaughter, pigs whose purpose goes conveniently unexplained — a silence that, he argues, reflects consumers' own refusal to know where food comes from rather than a LEGO conspiracy, though food's everyday urgency means toy farms should be held to a different standard than Hot Wheels. The redeeming idealism is not in the sets but in the Master-Builder freedom to build better realities, like solar-powered crop drones and trains delivering unsellable food to the city.
- ch. 25 "Notes" **·** Jean Baudrillard — Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal) — Lueth maps Baudrillard's four stages of the image from Simulacra and Simulation onto LEGO history. Early basic-brick town sets reflect a profound reality; the late-1980s shift to cops-and-robbers conflict play masks and denatures it — prison-striped convicts presuppose a justice system that never appears in the sets; sky-fi MOCs like the Savoia S.21 from Porco Rosso, which borrows a real WWII-era designation for a fictional plane, mask the absence of reality; and ApocaLEGO — MOCs riffing endlessly on Metro 2033, Fallout, The Walking Dead, and each other — is the pure simulacrum, images referring only to images with no real-world counterpart to check against (via Borges' 'Tlon, Uqbar, Orbis Tertius'). If Baudrillard is right that images now precede reality, ApocaLEGO is, culturally, literally building the apocalypse.
- ch. 26 "Notes" **·** Jean Baudrillard — Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal) — Boardman reads Miniland USA at LEGOLAND California and Florida as the nation itself rendered a 'Fan Developed Theme': an archipelago of tourist destinations — NYC, Washington DC, Las Vegas, New Orleans, 'New England' — connected not by geography but by the visitor's body performing a simulated cross-country tour that skips the flyover states entirely, with Bachelard's Poetics of Space on miniaturization and Debord's spectacle (footnoted) framing the effect. The California Freedom Tower reproduces a rejected architectural design, a literal Baudrillardian simulacrum, a copy for which no original exists, while Florida's version simply deletes Lower Manhattan and swaps Coke for Pepsi per sponsorship deals. Comparing the two parks' itineraries shows that 'America' here is constructed from circulating tourist imagery and corporate logistics — Carlsbad itself erased from its own park's map.
- ch. 27 "Part V LEGO®, METAPHYSICS, AND MATH" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "Notes" **·** Laozi, Plato, Socrates, Aristotle — The Tao (the Way); Wu Wei (effortless action); Yin and Yang — Bein reads the LEGO brick as the Daodejing's 'uncarved block': a single brick is an invitation rather than a toy, and precisely because it is empty of meaning (and mostly empty of plastic — the stud-and-tube marriage of yin and yang) it can become anything, whereas printed, specialized pieces 'are diminished by being added to'. Against the Greek obsession with definition — Socrates refusing sophistry while insisting one cannot rule justly without defining justice, Plato's definition dialogues, Aristotle's inheritance of the precision ideal, and William James's 'think clearly' — Daoist imprecision is defended as a philosophical stance: dao is a way, an activity, not a thing. Wei wu wei ('doing without doing') explains Emmet's winning plan of building the utterly ordinary Octan spaceship, why Lord Business's penny-throw is genius without sagacity (coercive, not water-like), and why governing should be like cooking a small fish — minimal, attentive intervention.
- ch. 29 "Notes" **·** The Buddha — Impermanence (anicca); Non-Self (anatman); Buddhism — Kahn argues LEGO play embodies the Buddhist teaching of impermanence that the Buddha states in the Digha Nikaya ('Impermanent are all component things...'): his childhood urge to superglue creations and Finn's father's Kragle dramatize the clinging that produces anxiety when things change, from brick formulations to discontinued sets and even funerals' wilting flowers. The LEGO Star Wars Death Star illustrates non-self — no Death Star exists apart from its 3,803 pieces, and no self apart from shifting aggregates — and the five aggregates (form, sensation, perception, formations, consciousness) are unpacked through building experiences like discovering a daughter has opened the mint Taj Mahal set. Tibetan sand mandalas, painstakingly built then ritually dissolved, model the ceremonial demolition of LEGO creations, teaching acceptance of change without sliding into a nihilistic 'why bother building at all'.
- ch. 30 "Notes" **·** David Lewis, David Hume, William of Ockham — Possible Worlds; Ockham's Razor — Leuenberger argues that a LEGO MOC is a surprisingly faithful model of David Lewis's Humean supervenience: the world is just a vast mosaic of local matters of particular fact — homogeneous, arbitrarily recombinable atomic bricks (fundamental properties at space-time points) with no necessary connections, Hume's 'greater denier', versus gunky Play-Doh or jigsaw pieces. Ockham's Razor motivates the view (while also able to cut against a divine master builder, whom Lewis's world lacks, though LEGO-world builders likewise stand outside their creations), and Lewis's concrete possible worlds make every build a replica rather than a creation, answered to the 'incredulous stare' by quantitative parsimony. Four-dimensionally stacked creations model change as variation along temporal parts, while the causation objection — nothing in one LEGO creation brings about the next — marks the fundamental Humean/non-Humean divide.
- ch. 31 "Notes" *(no index entry yet)* — Fischer stress-tests LEGO's slogan that 'you can build whatever you can imagine' as a piece of modal epistemology: the imaginable-but-unbuildable three-headed minifig shows imagination overgenerates, while the arch that seems sound in imagination but collapses in plastic shows it undergenerates, and tightening the slogan to complete brick-by-brick imagining leaves us knowing almost nothing. He replaces imagination with 'working knowledge' — the tinkerer's implicit feel for studs, balance, and lateral pressure — which becomes explicit theory when hard questions arise, as when engineers calculated a 3,591-meter maximum tower by combining brick-crushing force with material science: a theory-based epistemology of modality. Purism, oddly, is vindicated, because what AFOLs love is not just the possibilities but the specific impossibilities of the LEGO system, whose limits spark the determined engineer in every builder.
- ch. 32 "Notes" **·** Plato, Ludwig Wittgenstein, Gottlob Frege, Bertrand Russell — Theory of Forms — Tanswell takes Plato's side against Plato: where Republic Book VII mocks geometers who talk of 'squaring' and 'applying' as if math were practical doing, and Platonist foundationalism (Frege and Russell, ever-revising) treats proofs as revealing the eternal abstract structure of unchanging mathematical objects — modeled as a mighty load-bearing LEGO tower built from the base up — picture proofs show the activity is the point. Two diagrammatic proofs (the infinite series of triangles yielding 1/3, and the Pythagorean theorem via inflated, rotated, recombined triangles) work exactly like LEGO instruction booklets: sequences of pictures guiding a series of actions we must perform. With Wittgenstein's 'The mathematical proposition says to me: Proceed like this!', he proposes a 'LEGO account of diagrammatic proofs' on which mathematics, like LEGO, is fundamentally about what we do — building, playing, proving — not just about objects.
- ch. 33 "Glossary" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "EULA" *(notes)*

## Led Zeppelin and Philosophy - All Will Be Revealed

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Led Zeppelin and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Popular Culture and Philosophy Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Foreword" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Acknowledgments" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "And If You Listen Very Hard . ." **·** Aristotle — Editor Scott Calef's introduction argues that the critics who dismissed Led Zeppelin as hype, blues thieves, and purveyors of mere excess were wrong: Page and Jones had long apprenticeships, Plant spread asphalt while struggling, and the band released four albums in two years while touring nonstop. It contends the band invites philosophy because Page (occultism, the Hermit tarot figure on Led Zeppelin IV) and Plant (mythology, the feather-of-truth symbol) were serious spiritual seekers, and because Zep's music resists tidy genre categories just as philosophy challenges our everyday categories of creativity, recognition, and value. It closes by invoking Aristotle's claim that philosophy begins in wonder, prompted by Plant's 'it makes me wonder' in 'Stairway to Heaven'.
- ch. 8 "From Low to High Culture Where’s That Confounded Bridge" **·** Immanuel Kant — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — John Stone-Mediatore argues that Led Zeppelin's music, especially in concert, is a quintessential experience of the sublime. He applies Burke's catalogue of sublime qualities (loudness, darkness, rudeness, the artificial infinite) to Bonham's cavernous drum sound on 'When the Levee Breaks', Page's rough overdriven tone and 'sloppy' technique, and the dissonant mandolin intro and nocturnal imagery of 'The Battle of Evermore'; Longinus's inspired, thunderbolt-like genius explains Plant's overnight writing of the 'Stairway to Heaven' lyrics at Headley Grange. Kant's account of imagination, understanding, and reason then shows why the impossible stairway to heaven, 'our shadows taller than our soul', and 'when all are one and one is all' frustrate concepts and delight us through reason's superiority. The chapter concludes that once rock attains the sublime, the high/low culture distinction is mere aesthetic snobbery.
- ch. 9 "Bring It On Home to You" **·** Andy Clark, Plato, Immanuel Kant — The Extended Mind; Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Jeanette Bicknell argues that the two classic models of creativity—Plato's Ion, where the rhapsode is a divinely possessed conduit, and Kant's solitary genius who issues new rules—cannot explain a four-piece rock band, since both are individualistic, purely mental, and end in mystery. Her positive account uses Clark and Chalmers's extended mind (Page describes his guitar as 'an extension of you', instruments and notation as cognitive props) and Keith Sawyer's collaborative emergence: Zeppelin's creativity happened in rehearsal and performance, as with the stairwell-recorded drum groove of 'When the Levee Breaks' that framed every later musical decision. The transformation of Joan Baez's 'Babe, I'm Gonna Leave You' into a whisper-to-cry hard-rock template shows the band creating something no member could have produced or predicted alone.
- ch. 10 "Authentic Rock" **·** Immanuel Kant — Authenticity; Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Theodore Gracyk asks whether Zeppelin's uncredited borrowings—Willie Dixon's 'You Need Love' into 'Whole Lotta Love', Spirit's 'Taurus' into 'Stairway to Heaven', Jake Holmes's 'I'm Confused' into 'Dazed and Confused'—undermine their status as an authentic rock band whose value supposedly rests on sincere self-expression and original songwriting. Using Hanslick's thought experiment as staged by Little Roger and the Goosebumps's 'Gilligan's Island (Stairway)' parody and Handel's recycling of his own music into the Messiah, he argues a song's expressive power comes from the interaction of words and music (guarding against the fallacies of division and composition), and that by itself music is neither angry nor spiritual. Since Zeppelin's instrumental arrangements diverge radically from their sources and the lawsuits turned on Plant's lyrics, the chapter concludes that transformation, not narrow originality, grounds their genius—so the rock-versus-pop authenticity standard must be loosened.
- ch. 11 "Tell Me No Lies, Make Me a Happy Man" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Martin Heidegger, Arthur Schopenhauer — Intentionality; Phenomenology — Luke Dick gives a phenomenological analysis of what it is to be moved by 'Black Dog', introducing affectivity through a fantasy in which a stranger insulting Zeppelin instantly provokes anger. Sartre's account of emotions as unreflective, 'magical' transformations directed at intentional objects explains how fast the insulted fan's world changes, while Heidegger's claim that we are never moodless grounds the idea of a distinct 'Zeppelin mood' that the band's sound articulates better than language could.
