Schopenhauer's misogynist essay 'On Women' is set beside the song's predatory canine sexuality, and the chapter ends by asking which of the three 'males'—Schopenhauer, Plant's character, or the author with his carefully chosen six-song mix CD—a father could endorse as a son-in-law.
- ch. 12 "I’m Gonna Crawl" **·** Karl Marx — Objectification; Commodity Fetishism — Richard Wilson and Emma Rees apply Freud's theory of fetishism—the boy's disavowal of castration displaced onto a substitute object—to Zeppelin's lyrics and imagery. 'Trampled Underfoot' continues Robert Johnson's 'Terraplane Blues' car-as-lover convention, 'Custard Pie' and 'Black Dog' turn women into consumable food (Freud's cannibalistic pregenital sexual organization), and 'The Lemon Song' and 'Whole Lotta Love' avert the castrating vagina, with the Seattle red snapper episode read through the vagina dentata myth. The band's misogyny ('Soul of a woman was created below' in 'Dazed and Confused', echoing Lear), the voyeuristic die-cut windows of the Physical Graffiti cover, and Plant's cock-rock persona in The Song Remains the Same all illustrate how fetishistic substitution neutralizes the threatening female body and explains the band's compulsive doomed-relationship songs; the authors briefly borrow Marx's language of commodification for the woman-car-drugs as status objects.
- ch. 13 "Singing of the Good Things" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Socrates, John Locke, Thomas Hobbes — Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; The State of Nature; Free Will vs Determinism — Scott Calef asks whether Led Zeppelin's lives of wealth, groupies, and no-questions-asked liberty were genuinely free and therefore enviable. Locke's distinction between weak freedom (doing what you want, with Page happily locked in his suite at the Riot House) and strong freedom (being able to do otherwise, defended via beer-spilling concert excuses) is destabilized by the observation that no one ever does other than what they did. Rousseau then dominates: the state of nature versus LA, freedom as the capacity to resist natural impulse, which explains both Zep's libertine promiscuity and the paradox that heroin and alcohol enslave the very freedom that took them (the post-Physical Graffiti decline, Bonzo's death), while the band's violence—Bonham's assaults, the Oakland beating—shows society's suppression of natural pity, not natural aggression as Hobbes would have it. Socrates gets the last word: doing what we please is not doing what we truly want, so only the temperate person is free.
- ch. 14 "True Selves Found or Made" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Arthur Schopenhauer — Existence Precedes Essence; Second-Order Desires (personhood); Free Will vs Determinism — Theodore Schick reconstructs Aleister Crowley's philosophy of Thelema to explain Jimmy Page's devotion—Boleskine Manor, The Equinox bookstore, the Zoso sigil, 'Do what thou wilt shall be the whole of the law' etched in Led Zeppelin III's run-out groove—and reads 'Stairway to Heaven' stanza by stanza as an allegory of awakening to the True Will. He then dismantles the framework: no spell has ever worked under controlled conditions (post hoc fallacy), Sartre's existence precedes essence denies any divinely written True Will or human nature, and Schopenhauer (via Einstein) argues we can do what we want but cannot want what we want. Using second-order desires and autonomy, the chapter concludes that selves are made, not found—so even if a True Will existed, following a program we did not write would make us 'loyal plastic robots', not free.
- ch. 15 "As We Wind on Down the Road" **·** The Rhizome — Randall Auxier develops a theory, drawn from Ernst Cassirer's work on myth and magic, that distinguishes magic (the generalizing of will so that past, present, and future connect through symbols and co-operating forces) from myth (the consciousness that experiences the world as wills and archetypal symbols). Page embodies the magical path—the zone musicians enter where playing exceeds conscious control, the Faustian need to master it that led him to Crowley, and the risk of being drained (Page in 1977)—while Plant embodies myth, supplying archetypal lyrics ('The Battle of Evermore', 'Kashmir', 'Stairway' as 'a song of hope') and the Welsh pastoral. Zeppelin's blues borrowing is re-enactment rather than repetition, a rhizome-like reinvention of old roots, and the four unpronounceable symbols of the fourth album are genuine creations: symbols that store concentrated will for the future.
- ch. 16 "I Ain’t Gonna Tell You One Thing that You Really Ought to Know . . . Ooh!" **·** Plato, René Descartes, Martin Heidegger, David Hume, Socrates, Ludwig Wittgenstein — Theory of Forms; Bundle Theory of Self; Being-Toward-Death; Immortality and the Soul — Scott Calef reads the black 'Object' on the Presence cover—which Storm Thorgerson calls not a presence but a hole—to argue that absence permeates Zep's album art (the cut-out wheel of III, the die-cut windows of Physical Graffiti, the spindle holes of Coda) and that holes are a genuine metaphysical puzzle: immaterial yet extended, seemingly causal, and hard to see on a causal theory of perception. Descartes's immaterial soul is compared to holes (both non-physical, though holes suspiciously have size and location), Plato's Forms explain how the Object and we ourselves partake of both Being and Non-Being, and Hume's failure to find a self in introspection makes the self 'a hole'. Kierkegaard and Heidegger connect nothingness to the anxiety of death—fitting an album made after Plant's near-fatal crash—Socrates's two alternatives about death frame the end, and Wittgenstein's 'not a something but not a nothing either' caps the Object's dual nature.
- ch. 17 "Close the Door, Put Out the Lights" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle — Utilitarianism; The Categorical Imperative; The Golden Mean; Humanity as an End in Itself — Scott Calef tests three ethical theories against Led Zeppelin's off-stage destruction: TV-tossing, samurai-slashed hotel rooms, food fights, sharks hung in closets at the Edgewater Inn, the red snapper incident, and Jimmy Page's relationship with fourteen-year-old Lori Maddox. Classical utilitarianism weighs Bonzo's glee against the traumatized chambermaid, displaced guests, and unpaid-for sleep loss, generally condemning the pranks while leaving the supposedly consensual snapper episode uncomfortably open. Kant condemns across the board: Plant's fake tour itineraries and the stolen 'Black Beauty' Les Paul fail the universalizability test, trashing hotel rooms is self-defeating when universalized, and treating the Portland woman as a mere means violates her dignity as a rational agent; Aristotle's golden mean finds the band's excess nowhere near virtue, whatever Caligula would say. The chapter closes by separating moral judgment of the men from appreciation of the music.
- ch. 18 "Coda" **·** Plutarch, Aristotle, John Locke — Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity — Gimbel asks what makes a band remain the same band over time: Led Zeppelin survived replacing drummer John Bonham with his son Jason, yet Page and Jones insist no singer could replace Robert Plant. Working through Plutarch's Ship of Theseus, Aristotle's essential properties, and Locke's continuity account of identity, he uses Peter French's distinction between aggregates (groups defined by membership) and conglomerates (defined by role structure) to argue a band is a conglomerate. The twist is that a reunion would be a monument like Theseus's preserved ship rather than a working vessel: Plant is an Aristotelian essential of what Zeppelin was, while Jason Bonham's family link supplies the reverence and facsimile-fidelity a monument needs.
- ch. 19 "When the Levee Breaks Excess as Truth" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Arthur Schopenhauer, Heraclitus — The Apollonian and the Dionysian; The World as Will; Everything Flows / Logos — Flynn reads the Swan Song logo (Rimmer's falling Apollo) and songs like 'Kashmir,' 'Whole Lotta Love,' and 'When the Levee Breaks' through Nietzsche's Birth of Tragedy: individuals are temporary manifestations of a chaotic primordial unity (Schopenhauer's will), and Dionysian art redeems that suffering by celebrating the very excess that undoes us. The sandstorm of 'Kashmir,' the relentless sexual longing of the traveling blues, Plant's death-moan-as-ecstasy in 'In My Time of Dying,' and Bonham's levee-crushing drums all enact 'excess revealed as truth' — joyous laments in which words bleed into music. This is not hedonism, Flynn argues, but ecstatic awareness that the self's boundaries are artificial and its dissolution is a kind of reunion.
- ch. 20 "Bring the Balance Back Led Zeppelin and the Hegelian Dialectic" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — The Dialectic; Geist (World Spirit); History Has a Direction — Macan argues Zeppelin's contradictions — 'cock rock' versus androgyny, blues versus avant-garde sound collages, acoustic versus electric, East versus West — are Hegelian dualities driven toward monist synthesis by a World-Spirit working through the band's generation. He reads the physical riff sections and shadowy middle collages of 'Dazed and Confused' and 'Whole Lotta Love' as interpenetrating Primitive/Modern, African-American/European, and Male/Female poles; 'Stairway to Heaven''s seamless growth from recorder-and-lute evocations of Elizabethan England into full hard rock reconciles past and present (via Tolkien, the Hermit gatefold, the Lady and the Piper); and 'Kashmir' versus 'In the Light' stage Jung's East/West problem, with Crowley's Schopenhauerian will supplying the turn from wisdom sought without to wisdom found within.
- ch. 21 "Led Zeppelin and Art" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — Struggle for Recognition; Master–Slave Dialectic — Shain uses Hegel's master-slave dialectic and the struggle for recognition to explain the band: Page and Jones formed Zeppelin because recognition as top session players wasn't enough, and the drive for critic and peer recognition — which varied by hierarchy, as when Bonham said 'If Buddy Rich says I'm shit, then I'm shit' — pushed the band from the bombastic early albums to the achievement of Led Zeppelin IV and Physical Graffiti. Recognition extends to groupies (association and gratification, sharpening feminism's empowerment-versus-objectification dilemma about Des Barres) and to the high-art question: claims like 'Stairway as landmark artistic achievement of the twentieth century' misfire, Shain argues, because using high-art elements such as improvisation or minimalism can simply make better pop culture without lifting Zeppelin into the art tradition.
- ch. 22 "Sneaking In through the Back Door" **·** Walter Benjamin — The Aura of the Artwork — Seng asks whether The Song Remains the Same can relive the concert experience, using Benjamin's account of the artwork's aura. The ritual of queuing for tickets, the stable set list, Plant's city shout-outs (compared to Homer's rhapsodes naming local warriors), and Page's unrepeatable improvisations give each show a unique aura, while recording 'pries the event from its shell,' makes experiences interchangeable, and turns viewers into 'absent-minded examiners' who judge by price and camera angle. He concedes the movie's camera angles may open new appreciation unavailable even from a Garden seat, but insists being there is a different kind of experience no DVD duplicates.
- ch. 23 "Coda" **·** John Locke — Labor Theory of Property; Utilitarianism — White asks whether fans who trade or sell Led Zeppelin bootlegs wrong the band, given how little official live material it released. He weighs Locke's labor-mixing account of property (the band creates the performance, but tapers 'mix their labor' too), the legal view of property as a bundle of rights, and rule-utilitarian arguments that bootlegs promote the band without cutting into sales of studio albums. He concludes with Ronald Dworkin's point that rights trump utility: if the live performance is the band's property, the band's choice to withhold it binds fans whatever happiness wider distribution would produce.
- ch. 24 "Is Led Zeppelin, after All, the Most Philosophical of Rock Bands" **·** Gilles Deleuze, Friedrich Nietzsche, Immanuel Kant — The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Surber asks why we really listen to Zeppelin when the lyrics carry no philosophy. Kant's Critique of Judgment ranked music lowest among the arts — a 'mere play of sound sensations' at the zero point of philosophy — while Nietzsche reversed him by rooting philosophy in Dionysian rhythm; Deleuze's 'surface' philosophy (height, depth, surfaces; flows, intensities, blockages, repetition-with-difference) then resolves the question. Zeppelin enacts the bodily surface flow — 'Stairway''s gradual build, the flow-blockage-new-flow segues of the 25-minute 'Dazed and Confused' on How the West Was Won, Plant dropping lyrics to use his voice as an instrument — syncing us with lived experience, which makes them the most philosophical of rock bands precisely without saying anything philosophical.
- ch. 25 "Many Times I’ve Wondered 77 Zep Facts, Anecdotes, and Trivia" *(no index entry yet)* — A back-matter list of 77 facts and anecdotes: chart trivia, album-cover stories, rival readings of the four runic symbols (Saturn/666 versus Thor's hammer), Page's Crowley and Lucifer Rising connections, bootleg history, and touring debauchery. It advances no philosophical argument of its own but supplies background the essays draw on, such as the occult symbolism behind the magic chapters and the Willie Dixon credit behind 'Whole Lotta Love.'
- ch. 26 "Top Eleven Contributor Top Ten Lists" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Arthur Schopenhauer, Immanuel Kant — The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Eleven contributor top-ten lists, several applying the book's philosophical tools in miniature: Stone-Mediatore ranks ten songs by how they exemplify Burke's, Kant's, and Longinus's theories of the sublime ('Stairway to Heaven' first), Flynn ranks ten by Nietzsche's Dionysian criterion of celebrating the natural forces that undo us ('When the Levee Breaks' first), and Dick pairs ten songs with Schopenhauer's misogynist essay 'On Women.' The remaining lists — resources, sideman appearances, cover versions, oral fixations, gigs attended — are fanzine fare without philosophical content.
- ch. 27 "Elders of the Gentle Race" *(notes)*
- ch. 28 "With a Word She Can Get What She Came For" *(notes)*
- ch. 29 "Led Zeppelin and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 30 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Leonard Cohen and Philosophy - Various Positions

- ch. 1 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Jason Holt's introduction argues there is a genuine paradox in Leonard Cohen: his songs count both as high-art poetry and as popular folk/pop music, challenging the assumed absolute divide between high and popular art. Holt considers whether film auteur theory - the way Hitchcock elevates genre thriller - resolves the paradox by making Cohen a transcendent auteur, or whether the songs remain gloriously popular without any supra-popular transubstantiation. He rejects mentor Louis Dudek's charge that taking up the guitar betrayed Cohen's poetry, insisting the literary lyrics and pleasing melody are perfect complements.
- ch. 10 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Socrates, Epictetus, Epicurus, Sextus Empiricus, Lucretius, Marcus Aurelius, Zeno of Citium, Democritus, Pyrrho — The Unexamined Life; Stoicism; Pyrrhonian Skepticism (suspension of judgment); Death Is Nothing to Us — Brendan Shea reads Cohen through the three Hellenistic schools. Socrates's trial in the Apology mirrors the persecuted singer of 'A Singer Must Die,' with 'Everybody Knows' and 'Democracy' voicing a Socratic mix of empathy and pessimism about whether democracy can deal rationally with its critics. Stoics such as Epictetus ground the present-focused, resigned acceptance of loss in 'Hey, That's No Way to Say Goodbye' and 'If It Be Your Will'; the Pyrrhonian skepticism of Sextus Empiricus illuminates the narrator's calming, unresolvable ignorance in 'Famous Blue Raincoat'; and the Epicurean atomism of Epicurus and Lucretius - death is nothing to us, empty desires are unsatisfiable - underlies 'A Thousand Kisses Deep.' Hypatia's murder and Cohen's 'Joan of Arc' close the chapter: the examined life is an all-consuming, sometimes fatal commitment that is nonetheless worthwhile.
- ch. 12 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Albert Camus, Søren Kierkegaard, Jean-Paul Sartre — The Absurd; The Myth of Sisyphus; The Leap of Faith; Despair (sickness unto death) — Agust Magnusson argues that Cohen's 'crack in everything' credo from 'Anthem' is an existentialist celebration of brokenness against a culture demanding an unattainable perfect offering. He reads 'Waiting for the Miracle' alongside Camus's Sisyphus - the narrator stops waiting for a transcendent Answer and accepts the absurd, while 'The Future' and 'Democracy' dramatize both the fright of relativism and its resolution in humanistic hope. Kierkegaard's Fear and Trembling then explains 'Hallelujah': Abraham's leap beyond the ethical into the religious models a resignation in which broken Hallelujahs are still worth singing, and The Sickness unto Death shows despair overcome by letting go of the ego so that suffering begets compassion and love.
- ch. 13 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Judith Butler — Gender Performativity — Wieland Schwanebeck surveys the many masculinities in Cohen's songs - spies, soldiers, cowboys, rabbis, captains - and argues they mostly appear broken, mocked, or failing (the losing partisan, the captain whose ship leaks or crew deserts), which puts the 'real man' ideal in doubt. He shows how Cohen's Jewishness plays against stereotypes of the bookish, unathletic Jewish man, usually embraced and then inverted with Socratic irony, as in 'Is This What You Wanted' and 'Lover Lover Lover.' Following Judith Butler's Gender Trouble, he reads Cohen's stage persona - the tailored suit, kneeling, clowning, and erotic ambiguity - as a repeated performance demonstrating that masculinity is 'an imitation without an origin.'
- ch. 14 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Seneca, Jacques Derrida, Marcus Aurelius, Baruch Spinoza — Phenomenology; Living in the Present — Gary Shapiro treats The Future as a 'lyrical phenomenology' of time that stages multiple temporalities rather than one theory of time. 'The Future' evokes an apocalypse where the katechon - the Biblical restraining force holding back the end - no longer works, while 'Waiting for the Miracle' anatomizes passive deferral in a way Shapiro finds sharper than the Stoic critique of waiting by Seneca and Marcus Aurelius and Nietzsche's 'problem of those who wait,' opposing wasted chronos to the seized kairos of 'First We Take Manhattan.' 'Anthem's' crack that lets the light in gets a Gnostic reading, 'Democracy' is glossed through Derrida's 'democracy to come' as messianic, always-arriving futurity, and 'Closing Time' and 'Always' add honky-tonk eschatology and the vow of eternal love.
- ch. 15 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** David Hume — Category Mistake — Jason Holt defends the serious question of whether Cohen is a good singer against both anti-fans and fandom, arguing that judgments of taste purport to objectivity and cannot be settled by polls or Juno Awards. Borrowing David Hume's standard of taste - ideal critics with strong sense, delicate sentiment, practice, comparison, and freedom from prejudice - he judges Cohen in his 'weight class' as a singer-songwriter and vocal stylist, ranks him between Paul Simon and Bob Dylan, and offers the 'great singer-songwriter argument' that excellence in both crafts implies being at least a good singer. Much dismissal, he argues, is really style prejudice or a category mistake, and he ends by confessing that Cohen's voice saved him from teenage suicide, biasing him in the best possible way.
- ch. 17 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Adam Auch asks why the 'born with the gift of a golden voice' line of 'Tower of Song' is a joke in Cohen's mouth, a lament in Marianne Faithfull's, and a boast in Tom Jones's. Using indexicals, performer persona, and Paul Grice's conversational maxims, he argues interpretation depends on who sings and how: audiences fill the lyric's 'I' with the persona they associate with the performer, and Cohen's bald-faced flouting of the maxim of sincerity forces the ironic reading. The signature 'L. Cohen' in 'Famous Blue Raincoat' ties that song so tightly to Cohen's persona that cover artists must either inhabit the role (Tori Amos) or rewrite the lyrics (Jennifer Warnes), while post-boom 'Hallelujah' covers by Rufus Wainwright and Alexandra Burke perform it as a straight hymn, losing the tension between earthly and heavenly that Cohen and Jeff Buckley preserve.
- ch. 18 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Immanuel Kant — The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Edward Winters answers his sister's question - why listen to such depressing music - through Lorca's duende, the 'black sounds' of deep song that, as Nick Cave says, every true love song must contain. He argues duende resists definition yet is recognizable in Cohen, and connects it to Nietzsche's Dionysian art of participation (war dances, anthems, 'Dance Me to the End of Love') as against Kant's disinterested aesthetic distance, so music can re-enchant a disenchanted world. Via Kant's account of artistic genius and Cohen's testimony that Lorca and six flamenco chords gave him his voice, Winters concludes Cohen's dark, honest beauty has duende - which is why it lifts and tears at the heart rather than depressing.
- ch. 19 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Socrates, Plato — Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Socratic Method (elenchus); Philosophy of Love — Christopher Lauer argues Cohen's lyrics seduce through Socratic irony as Kierkegaard analyzes it in The Concept of Irony: ostentatious self-concealment that makes hearers aware of the gap between words and intentions, building 'personality' and promising intimacy. Songs like 'Closing Time,' 'Everybody Knows,' and 'Democracy' oscillate between cynical third-person verdicts and sudden second-person address, offering a hidden sincerity only to withdraw it - an erotic improvement on Plato's Socrates that dissolves its own ironic distance. 'Hallelujah' and 'Anthem' make the program explicit, treating brokenness as the space in which love develops, while 'Tower of Song' shows the bleak side: irony collapsing into pure cynicism, the singer speaking sweetly from the Tower long after all promises of genuine intimacy have been disavowed.
- ch. 21 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Maurice Merleau-Ponty, Edmund Husserl, Jean-Paul Sartre, G.W.F. Hegel, Plato, Aristotle — Embodiment (the lived body); The Look (hell is other people); Struggle for Recognition; Platonic Love (the ladder of love) — Lisa Warenski reads 'Suzanne' as staging a phenomenological account of self-awareness: with Husserl and Merleau-Ponty the body is not an object but the lived first-person perspective - so 'touch a perfect body with a mind' is literal - and with Sartre's keyhole example from Being and Nothingness we become aware of ourselves through the gaze of the other. Developmental psychology's mother as 'magical mirror,' who reflects the child's emerging mind, is connected to Hegel's paradox of recognition, and to Aristotle's and Neera Badhwar's friends as mirrors of the soul. On this basis Suzanne's mind-touch has epistemic value: love reflects you truly and shows you what you might become, whether as Jesus's agape or Suzanne's appreciative philia.
- ch. 22 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Richard Rorty, Plato — Language Games; Philosophy of Love — Simon Riches argues that Cohen fuses the erotic with the spiritual - worshipping female beauty as religious experience in 'Light as the Breeze' and 'Night Comes On' - and that this produces conflicted, self-deceiving relationships in songs like 'Famous Blue Raincoat' and 'Chelsea Hotel #2.' Against the classical theory of concepts embodied in Socrates' demand for essences, he deploys Wittgenstein's language games and family resemblance, Wittgensteinian views of religious belief, and Davidson's divided mind to deny that love has a rationally coherent essence. Following Richard Rorty, Cohen emerges as an ironist who rejects any 'final vocabulary' of love, and this very contingency and irony ground his authenticity as a singer of romantic love.
- ch. 23 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Simone de Beauvoir, G.W.F. Hegel, Martin Heidegger, Heraclitus, Parmenides, Jacques Derrida, Plato, René Descartes — The Ethics of Ambiguity; The Dialectic; Being-Toward-Death; Woman as the Other — Babette Babich traces the shifting 'you' of 'Hallelujah' and 'Suzanne' through Hegelian thesis-antithesis-synthesis and Empedocles's cycle of Love and Strife, reading the songs' ambiguity (David, Samson, the bound champion) as Cohen's sovereign having-it-both-ways. Beauvoir's Ethics of Ambiguity frames the human condition of being what one is not, while Nietzsche - on broken promises, on women and distance, on the Antichrist - supplies both the critique of Cohen's courtly projections onto the feminine Other and the model of singularizing solitude. 'Who by Fire' is read against the Rosh Hashanah prayer Unetanneh Tokef as a song of fate, judgment, and atonement whose closing 'who shall I say is calling?' is quasi-Augustinian and Heideggerian: death singularizes each 'who' at the moment of its claim.
- ch. 24 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Steven Burns rejects both the Obscenity Reading and a merely Poetical Reading of Beautiful Losers in favor of a Political Reading: the four losers - Saint Catherine Tekakwitha, the constipated Anglophone Historian, the French sensualist F., and the Aboriginal wife Edith - represent Canada's founding peoples on the eve of the 1967 centennial. Defending his view that richer works admit a single 'best reading' (after Anthony Savile's aesthetics of best explanation), he reads F.'s Quebec separatism, the statue bombing, and the novel's orgasm-imagery as allegory of a nation inhibited and failing, in dialogue with George Grant's Lament for a Nation. The epilogue's blonde in the Oldsmobile who says 'I am Isis' signals Cohen's dream of a transformed, liberated Canada - Catherine updated - rather than Grant's lament.
- ch. 26 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Theodor Adorno, Hannah Arendt, Sigmund Freud — The Banality of Evil; The Unconscious — Paweł Dobrosielski and Marcin Napiórkowski read Flowers for Hitler as Cohen's answer to Adorno's dictum that to write poetry after Auschwitz is barbaric - Cohen writes poetry that self-consciously knows it comes after the end of poetry. Using Freud's distinction between the vocabulary and grammar of dreams, they show the collection is assembled from a small repertoire of recurring images (ovens, ashes, telephones, radios, green grass, Nazi names) whose awkward normality expresses repression: life goes on as if nothing had happened. Arendt's banality of evil, born of the Eichmann trial, is directly alluded to in 'All There Is to Know about Adolf Eichmann,' and the book ends by universalizing Holocaust memory - the poet waking with a number on his forearm, feeling like victim and perpetrator alike - until the grass itself is grown on ashes and every flower is a flower for Hitler.
- ch. 27 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer, The Buddha — The World as Will; Philosophical Pessimism; Ethics of Compassion; Buddhism — Liane Heller pairs Cohen and Schopenhauer - born and died on September 21st, the autumnal equinox - as two explorers of the world's irrational negativity who both retreat to 'a room' to create. Schopenhauer's directionless Will, his pessimism about a life of constant suffering, his notorious misogyny in 'On Women,' and his ascetic denial of the will-to-live (with his contempt for Judaism and preference for the Upanishads and Buddhist compassion) are set against Cohen's affirmation that love is 'the only engine of survival,' his five-year Zen retreat that could not hold him, and his return to the Judaism of the 'little Jew who wrote the Bible.' A closing imagined dialogue has a humbled Schopenhauer confronted with Cohen's self-compassion and ordinary joys, admitting the limits of his own field of vision.
- ch. 28 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger, Jean-Paul Sartre, Sigmund Freud — Being-Toward-Death; Thrownness (Geworfenheit); Authenticity; The Death Drive (Thanatos) — Christopher Ketcham narrates a night listening to Dear Heather during a café blackout, in which darkness becomes Eugene Minkowski's 'lived space' - more personal than light, with auditory space as its own dark space - and Cohen's voice leads him down into a shared funk of loss and depression. Through Heidegger he diagnoses his own shrunken, past-bound, thrown existence and reinterprets being-toward-death not as a downward spiral but as being toward his own remaining possibilities, and through Freud's death drive he names the 'unobtrusive death wish' he had let dwell unchallenged in him. The essay closes with a Heideggerian resolve toward resoluteness and future possibility, glimpsing a street-worn 'Suzanne' in the dark as hope rather than absence.
- ch. 30 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Nagel, Aristotle, René Descartes — What Is It Like to Be a Bat?; The Four Causes — Rachel Haliburton asks what metaphysics underwrites Suzanne's world, where a mind can touch a perfect body. The materialist world inherited from Descartes reduces thoughts, meanings, and purposes to purposeless physical processes in which minds never touch bodies; Aristotle's teleological world, refurbished by Thomas Nagel's Mind and Cosmos, builds purposes into nature and treats subjective experience - what the tea and oranges are like for you - as real in its own right. She argues Suzanne's world, and our own ordinary mall-food-court world, is teleological: consciousness is 'biased towards the marvelous,' and we touch one another's bodies with our minds all the time.
- ch. 31 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** William James, Martha Nussbaum — Liam Dempsey uses Cohen's songs and the TV piece I Am a Hotel to test somatic against cognitive theories of emotion. William James's James-Lange thesis - emotions are the perception of bodily change - captures the embodied metaphors everywhere in Cohen (icy and broken hearts, trembling, hunger as lust, pain like iodine), while cognitivists such as Martha Nussbaum, working from the Stoic tradition, explain judgment-laden emotions like lifelong love, jealousy, and betrayal in 'Chelsea Hotel #2,' 'Famous Blue Raincoat,' and 'Paper Thin Hotel.' Following Jenefer Robinson's and Ronald de Sousa's process accounts, he concludes a tear is an intellectual thing only as a complex exchange of parallel systems - bodily arousal interpreted and reassessed by appraisal - so that emotions are, finally, written in the flesh.
- ch. 32 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 33 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Forgiveness; Immortality and the Soul; Faith and Reason — Timothy P. Jackson argues Cohen is the closest thing today to a Biblical prophet - a 'strong poet' in Harold Bloom's sense, wearing a coat of many colors drawn from Judaism, Christianity, Buddhism, Taoism, and Sufism, sustained by an 'epistemic humility' that lives between dogmatism and doubt. Cohen leads Christian texts back to their Hebraic origins, critiques every arrogant nationalism including Israel (Book of Mercy, psalm 27), and gave his Tel Aviv concert profits to Israeli-Palestinian reconciliation. Jackson reads Cohen's equanimity at his business manager's embezzlement trial as forgiveness overcoming resentment, weighs his hesed/agape-style unconditional love against his failures in erotic love, and ends with Cohen's reticence about personal immortality - a prophet for the living.
- ch. 34 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Maimonides, Thomas Aquinas, Plotinus — Emptiness (sunyata); Zen Buddhism; Neoplatonism (the One) — Bernard Wills takes the reference to The Cloud of Unknowing in 'The Window' as the key to Cohen's lifelong fascination with apophatic, negative mysticism - God sought beyond every image in a 'naked intent of love,' as in the medieval Cloud, Dionysius, Maimonides, and Kabbalah - against the kataphatic way of affirmation. He reads Recent Songs as an album of exile and the play of union and separation ('Un Canadien Errant,' 'Ballad of the Absent Mare'), where the incarnate Word is 'stuttered' and 'frozen,' a passion without resurrection. Cohen's later Zen turn is thus prefigured: the emptiness of sunyata and the Neo-Platonic descent from and return to the One are positive nothingness - self-overcoming in the service of compassion rather than the black-romantic nihilism of self-annihilation.
- ch. 35 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" **·** Daniel Dennett, Niccolò Machiavelli — Falsificationism — Peter Stone applies Dawkins's memetics and Dennett's Breaking the Spell to the rise of 'Hallelujah' from the troubled release of Various Positions to ubiquity via John Cale, Jeff Buckley, Shrek, and Rufus Wainwright. On Dennett's account religious memes thrive through hyperactive agency detection, doctrines rendered unfalsifiable and thus immune to evidence, and 'belief in belief' - and 'Hallelujah' is religiously ambiguous enough, from David and Bathsheba to Buckley's 'hallelujah of the orgasm,' to be as religious as any singer or congregation wants it to be, whether as Rabbi Kagan's hymn of the heretic or the Holy Trinity Church's 'Amazing Grace' lyric swap. Machiavelli's maxim that fortune governs half the outcome frames the song's lucky trajectory, and its carefully engineered ambiguity makes it a set of very 'happy memes' likely to keep replicating.
- ch. 36 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 37 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "Leonard Cohen and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Louis C.K. and Philosophy - You Don't Get to Be Bored

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "vi" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "A Note on “Louis” and “Louie”" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "The “Other Shit” that Louis C.K. Wanted to Talk About" *(no index entry yet)* — Editor Mark Ralkowski's introduction argues that Louis C.K. moved from absurdist joke-writing to 'personal revelation comedy,' the 'other shit' about love, death, boredom, sex, and parenting that he now builds his specials and Louie around. Framed by Trevor Griffiths's play Comedians, it stages the choice between comedy as entertainment ('coloured sweeties') and comedy as medicine that dares to see the truths audiences fear, and claims Louis chose the latter - developing each hour by the samurai-sword process of constant folding and reworking, opening with his strongest bit, and trading money to FX for total creative control. The chapter maps no individual philosophers; it sets up the book's thesis that Louis's bits are worth philosophical reading.
- ch. 11 "I. Everybody Has a Competition in Their Brain" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "1. Everything’s Amazing and Nobody’s Happy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Death of God; Amor Fati (love of fate) — Max Elder reads Louis through Nietzsche's Gay Science claim that all philosophy is unconscious memoir, arguing Louis's confession-like comedy reveals dark truths we would rather flee - the Conan 'everything's amazing and nobody's happy' bit, the 'I know the darkness' exchange with the Christian woman, and the Subway/Pamela scene where a homeless man bathes behind a subway violinist. Using Nietzsche's line that man alone laughs because he suffers so deeply, and The Birth of Tragedy's comic as the artistic discharge of the nausea of absurdity, the chapter argues Louis's laughter performs a gestalt switch that ties the ugly to the beautiful and so redeems it. It reads the religions-supply-meaning argument - God's mockery-laughter versus Nietzsche's holy laughter - as the reason redemption now happens in comedy clubs rather than churches, and closes with Louie's 'Country Drive' scolding of Jane ('you don't get to be bored') as Nietzschean embrace of the amazing fact of being alive.
- ch. 13 "2. Sex Has Something Behind It" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer — The World as Will; Philosophical Pessimism — John Heaney pairs Louis with Schopenhauer: both see human existence as error-shaped misery, but more importantly both treat sex as the will-to-live tricking the intellect into serving the species while we mistake the species' good for our own. He reads the 'Come on, God' stand-up (every beautiful thought about a woman followed by a disgusting one) and Louie's 'So Old/Playdate' speech that 'sex is really trying to have a family' as comedic versions of Schopenhauer's metaphysics of love, with episodes like 'Bummer/Blueberries' (a decapitation killing Louie's lust mid-non-date), 'Elevator Part 4' (the civil divorce flashback ending in a possible pregnancy), and 'Eddie' (a man losing the will-to-live along with the will-to-love) enacting the oscillation between sexual arousal and suicidal despair. Even Louis's devotion to his daughters fits Schopenhauer's claim that the sexual delusion transfers to offspring, which is why 'Eddie' ends with Louie refusing to name his reasons to live - he has to pick up his kids in the morning.
- ch. 14 "3. When to Hate and When to Be Irate" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — The Golden Mean; Respect for Persons; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Daniel Alloy opens with Aristotle's demand that one be angry 'as and when and for as long as one ought' and builds a theory distinguishing anger (a response to a specific offense, seeking proportional payback) from hatred (a response to a thing's very existence, seeking its destruction). He tests the theory on Louis's bits - hating deer as a class and fantasizing about giving them AIDS, the six-year-old 'Jizzanthapuss,' the Pig Newtons fight with a three-year-old, the Cinnabon clerk, the 'Awesome Possum' hipster, and the justified driver who yells 'suck a sack of dicks.' He argues justified hatred is impossible, since it would require a pure-malevolence being that could not be an autonomous person, so hatred is always wrong while properly proportioned anger remains a necessary part of the moral life. Kant supplies the self-respect dimension ('one who makes himself a worm cannot complain if people step on him'): failing to get angry at real wrongs done to you or your children signals a character deficiency, not virtue.
- ch. 15 "II. Misery Is Wasted on the Miserable" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "4. On Being a Dead Person Who Hasn’t Died Yet" **·** Marcus Aurelius, Epictetus, Epicurus — The View from Above; The Dichotomy of Control; Stoicism; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — Mark Ralkowski argues that Louis's 'dead people who haven't died yet' bit sounds like Marcus Aurelius, and that Dr. Bigelow's Season Four advice about back pain, the three-legged dog, and heartbreak works as the Stoic spiritual exercises Pierre Hadot described - above all the 'view from above' that re-describes things to strip them to their true value. Epictetus's dichotomy of control explains Bigelow's core advice: pain is not 'up to us' but our judgments about it are, so Louie should adjust his expectations to nature ('every second without back pain is a lucky second') instead of demanding a pain-free life. The apparently un-Stoic counsel to savor heartbreak ('misery is wasted on the miserable') is defended as genuinely Stoic: viewing his lost love from the perspective of a dead-person-who-hasn't-died-yet, Louie can accept sadness as precious and impermanent rather than being crushed by it. The chapter concedes Louis is no philosopher, but suggests his bits - contrasted with Seinfeld's 'polished' version on Talking Funny - can be read as newly discovered Meditations for comedy audiences.
- ch. 17 "5. Just Want a Shitty Body" **·** Epictetus — The Dichotomy of Control; Stoicism — Bekka Williams argues that Louis's 'just want a shitty body' bit restates Epictetus's central Stoic teaching - want events to happen as they do happen, because only judgments, efforts, and desires are 'up to us' while bodies, reputations, and outcomes are not. She reads the Late Show arc (Tardigan's non-offer of the job, then Louie's triumphant 'Hey Letterman, I did it!' when Letterman stays) as Epictetus's cardplayer ideal: total effort on what is controllable, calm acceptance of what is not, alongside the bits about disgusting bodies, 'do the shit out of your job,' Jane and the mango pop, and Louis's self-trained habit of intercepting impulsive desires like the motorcycle urge. Like Epictetus, Louis is presented as a truth-teller who corrects mistaken impressions even when it is unpleasant, and the chapter uses him to rebut the charge that Stoicism means not caring.
- ch. 18 "6. You’re Gonna Die" **·** The Buddha, Epicurus — Death Is Nothing to Us; The Four Noble Truths; Impermanence (anicca); Atomism — Ethan Mills uses the Buddha and Epicurus to show that Louis meets death with laughter instead of fear. The Buddha's diagnosis of dukkha ('dissatisfactoriness') explains the Blu-Ray player and cellphone bits - consumer desire just restarts the craving cycle - and impermanence frames both the Amia heartbreak that Dr. Bigelow reframes as 'misery is wasted on the miserable' and Louie's 'life isn't that long... it's happy math' reflection, while rebirth would only make things worse since you would have to repeat junior high forever. Epicurus's argument that death is nothing to us (where death is, you are not) grounds Louie's 'lots of things happen after you die, just none of them include you,' backed by atomism - at death our atoms scatter - and by gods who, like Louie's maybe-God with no heaven, guarantee no immortality. The USO duckling episode models the payoff: comedy defuses our deepest anxiety about annihilation the way it defuses the soldiers' tension in Afghanistan.
- ch. 19 "III. Raising the Grown Up They’re Going to Be" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "7. Because Nothing Can’t Be!" **·** Parmenides, Zeno of Elea, Plato — Being and Changelessness; Zeno's Paradoxes — Joseph Kirkland takes Louis's 'Why? Why? Why?' routine from One Night Stand - collapsing into 'things that are not can't be!' and 'giant ants with top hats dancing around' - as an accidental encounter with the Pre-Socratics' question of why there is something rather than nothing. Louis, he argues, rejects the Principle of Plenitude (a finite world has no room for every possibility), whereas Parmenides argues from pure logic that nothingness is impossible and change therefore illusory - his student Zeno 'proving' it with paradoxes such as the infinite halfway points between Louis and Ben's Pizzeria. Framing the bit with Plato's claim that wonder is the beginning of philosophy and Socrates' unexamined life, the chapter ends on the parental punchline 'bread first, then ethics': feeding the kid trumps metaphysics, and Louis's frustration comes from failing that duty while his daughter probes being and non-being over her French fries.
- ch. 21 "8. Should Jane Get to Be Bored" **·** Aristotle, Plato — Socratic Method (elenchus); Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Daniel Addison defends Louie's 'you don't get to be bored' admonishment of Jane in 'Country Drive' against Mathew Remski's charge that it transfers adult anxiety, pointing to Louis's Conan 'forever empty' monologue and reddit advice as proof that Louis insists on facing despair rather than fleeing it. The real aim, he argues, is Socratic: Louis wants Jane and the plane-complaining generation brought to aporia, the perplexity Plato's Socrates induces in the Meno's slave boy so that, no longer thinking he knows, he 'would be glad to find out.' Aristotle grounds the optimism - every human by nature desires to know, philosophy begins in wonder, and our deepest happiness lies in contemplating - so Louis's 'everything's amazing' tirade is a demand that we become philosophical rather than 'kinda satisfied' consumers. Boredom, on this reading, is failed wonder, and Louie is playing Socrates to his daughter.
- ch. 22 "9. What Fuckin’ Chance Does a Kid Have" **·** Augustine of Hippo — Potentiality and Actuality; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Joseph Westfall argues that Louis's parent-hating bits - the baby he doesn't know who 'might fuckin' hate Jews,' the four-year-old 'asshole,' 'I love her, but I wish she was never born' - voice the real ambivalence of parenthood while showing that children are developmentally incapable of responsibility, so parents must not blame kids for being kids. He contrasts Louis's unknowable, selfish infant with Augustine's doctrine that unbaptized babies are simply evil, and reads Louis's parenting maxim - 'you're not raising the kid in front of you, you're raising the grownup they're going to be' - through childhood as a state of becoming: Jizzanthapuss is on the road to Hitler thanks to his weak mother, and Louie berating little Jane realizes 'I'm her first asshole,' shaping the men she will later attract. Good parenting is keeping kids alive plus forming the future adult, which is why Louis denies his daughters cell phones (they prevent learning empathy face-to-face) and puts his daughter on the smelly city bus while his Mercedes waits.
- ch. 23 "IV. That Knowledge that It’s All for Nothing and You’re Alone" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "10. On “Crying Like a Bitch”" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, G.W.F. Hegel — The Stages on Life's Way; Anxiety (the dizziness of freedom) — Roberto Sirvent and Joel Avery read Louie through Kierkegaard's claim that wherever there is contradiction the comical is present: Louie is a walking contradiction - the devoted dad who does masturbation bits - and the show stages incongruities like the homeless man showering behind a subway violinist and the decapitation in an episode called 'Bummer/Blueberries.' Kierkegaard's aesthetic sphere maps both of Louie's failures in 'Something Is Wrong' and 'Moving': the instant-gratification aesthete (the impulse motorcycle, the unaffordable townhouse fantasy) and the overthinking aesthete who cannot break up with his girlfriend or park his car, while his girlfriend shows what it is to actually choose and act. Eddie, who spits abstract truths but does nothing with them, 'plays Hegel to Louie's Kierkegaard,' embodying the gap between abstract philosophizing and lived existence that Kierkegaard attacked in Hegelianism. The closing distinction between irony (spotting the contradiction) and comedy (living with its permanent implications) is what Louis's Conan confession - crying like a bitch and finding happiness rushing in to meet the sadness - gets right.
- ch. 25 "11. Louis C.K.’s No-Bullshit Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Bad Faith; Authenticity; Radical Freedom and Responsibility; The Look (hell is other people) — Marie Snyder uses Sartre's existentialism to unpack Louis's Conan monologue about the 'forever empty': with God gone we are condemned to be free, thrown into a world with no ready-made purpose and alone with our choices - which explains both the cellphone distraction economy and the Live in Portland bit that 'even if you're with someone, you're alone.' Authenticity means ruthlessly honest self-awareness and owning every choice with no excuses - Sartre's waiter example, the claim that there is no cowardly temperament, and Louis's refusal to let 'a flaw in the human male' excuse the tits bit or porn masturbation. Since in choosing for ourselves we choose for all humanity, Snyder pairs Sartre's universalized responsibility with Louis's 'act in a way that, if everybody acted that way, things would work out,' and reads intimate others, as in the Sartre-Beauvoir open relationship, as mirrors that reflect us back to ourselves against the connectionless 'we're just cummers' porn culture. Bad faith - self-deception, distraction, blame - is the fallenness Louis keeps diagnosing in his audiences and confessing in himself.
- ch. 26 "12. Confronting the “Forever Empty”" **·** Martin Heidegger, Jean-Paul Sartre — Authenticity; Bad Faith; Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Existentialism — Argues that Louis's 2013 Conan 'forever empty' confession—welcoming the sadness of listening to Bruce Springsteen alone in his car instead of texting—is an exemplary case of existentialist authenticity: Heideggerian anxiety discloses the nothingness beneath our projects, and Sartre's radical freedom, facticity, and bad faith explain why texting while driving and other distractions are escapes from self-creation. It then contrasts Eddie in Louie's 'Eddie' episode, whose suicide plan is a bad-faith loss of care and passion, with the 'Late Show' trilogy, where Janet exposes Louie's bad faith over the Letterman audition and he ends by shouting 'I did it!' at the Ed Sullivan Theater after Tardigan's scam.
- ch. 27 "V. Did You Look in the Downstairs Bathroom" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "13. Why Why Why" **·** Aristotle, Socrates, Plato — The Four Causes — Reads Louis's 'Why?' routine from One Night Stand as a compact philosophy of science: explanations are answers to why-questions (Hempel and Oppenheim), and the daughter's regress climbs through increasingly abstract answers ('some things are and some things are not') that echo Aristotle's definition of truth in the Metaphysics and Socrates in the Cratylus, while Aristotle's Physics claim that we do not understand until we have the why of it grounds the whole exchange. Louis's two regress-stoppers—'eat your French fries' as brute fact and 'Why not?' where no because-answer exists, drawn from the shitty-parents and dishwasher bits—supply the chapter's proposed solutions.
- ch. 29 "14. Louis C.K.’s God" **·** Paul Tillich, Theodor Adorno — Faith and Reason — Argues Louis's God material is a negativistic, immanent critique of religion in Adorno's sense of 'damaged human life': the 'God' episode's crucifixion doctor who makes young Louie hammer a nail shows faith wrecking children with guilt until his mother's skeptical 'malarkey' speech and donuts rescue him. His SNL 'did you look in the downstairs bathroom?' bit becomes an evidentialist ethics of belief aimed at over-certain atheists, his 'shitty girlfriend' Abraham bit and body/food/shopping jokes illustrate Tillich's functionalist test of what we treat with ultimate concern (echoing David Foster Wallace's 'everybody worships'), and the chapter closes that comedy here works like Ignatian Catholic reflection, keeping faith and doubt together.
- ch. 30 "15. God Started in 1983" **·** Blaise Pascal, Epicurus — Pascal's Wager; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Faith and Reason — Uses the SNL bit of a God who has been standing in a room since 1983 to argue Louis is an 'apathetic agnostic': he cannot know whether God exists, lambastes atheists who claim certainty, and thinks the question barely matters for our lives. The chapter weighs Pascal's Wager against Epicurus's four-part cure ('Don't fear god, don't worry about death'), faults Louis's 'downstairs bathroom' bit for putting the burden of proof on atheists, reads the 'Heaven' glory-hole sketch through Clifford's ethics of belief as a rejection of faith without evidence, and ends with Louie's mother's line that you have to be good to people whether or not God is watching.
- ch. 31 "VI. Having a Lot of Beliefs and Living by None of Them" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "16. You’re Not Starving" **·** Peter Singer, Hannah Arendt — The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); The Banality of Evil — Argues Louis's 'my life is evil, I drive an Infiniti' bit and 'we make them die with our cars' capture Singer's drowning-child argument that famine relief is a duty of the affluent rather than charity, since the obligation to prevent bad outcomes at little cost holds across distance. It then amplifies the harm claim with Pogge's account of a rigged global institutional order (WTO protections, sweatshop-made smartphones) and frames unthinking consumer participation in that system as Arendt's banality of evil—the 'terrifyingly normal' thoughtlessness of Eichmann repeated at the checkout.
- ch. 33 "17. Louis’s Little Believies" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Builds a three-part definition of moral failure—acting against one's own moral beliefs, freely chosen—and applies it to Louie's Infiniti bit, his stopping the country girl from helping the homeless man, and his refusal to give a soldier his first-class seat while feeling proud of merely considering it. The chapter concludes Louie acts unselfishly only when someone might judge him (the sheriff's kiss, the limousines for his daughter's stranded field trip), so without the threat of judgment his 'little believies' stay inert, and a world where such hypocrisy goes unremarked would lose the power of moral criticism altogether.
- ch. 34 "18. Believies are Not Motivaties" **·** David Hume — Reason Is the Slave of the Passions; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Uses the 'believies' bit, the 'I think it's wrong to eat meat... I eat them all' confession, and 'of course, but maybe' to place Louis in the meta-ethics of moral motivation: he is a cognitivist and partial success theorist, but a Humean who treats beliefs as causally inert without desire and an externalist who denies a necessary link between moral judgment and motivation. That combination, the chapter argues, lets Louis sincerely judge and not be moved without incoherence—making the amoralist conceivable and avoiding 'the moral problem.'
- ch. 35 "VII. Take Some Responsibility for the Shitty Words You Want to Say" *(front)*
- ch. 36 "19. The Playful Thought Experiments of Louis C.K" **·** Plato, Daniel Dennett — Allegory of the Cave — Argues Louis's bits function as playful philosophical thought experiments in Ernst Mach's and Tamar Gendler's sense: the white-people time machine, sitting 'in a chair in the sky,' and the 'Indians' naming skit are make-believe scenarios that reconfigure our frames and draw out truths we already knew, just as Plato's cave and Nathan's parable to King David do. Pairing the cognitive 'Aha!' with the mirthful 'Ha-ha' (via Hurley, Dennett, and Adams's Inside Jokes) makes his humor an error-detection reward loop that takes audiences to upsetting territory and makes them glad they went.
- ch. 37 "20. Feminists Can Take a Joke" **·** Feminism — Argues Louis performs feminist philosophy by writing marginalized voices into his work: Rick's poker speech about what 'faggot' means to gay men who hear it during beatings, Vanessa's seven-minute 'So Did the Fat Lady' confrontation that leaves Louie silenced, and his Daily Show reflection on learning that rape polices women's lives. The chapter does not excuse his slurs ('cunt,' 'nigger,' 'faggot'), but contends his admitted fallibility is what keeps a broad, mixed audience listening—the guy who came for masturbation jokes leaves having heard about rape culture—so feminism and comedy are not natural enemies.
- ch. 38 "21. If You’re Not White, You’re Missing Out" **·** Kimberlé Crenshaw — Intersectionality — Treats Louis as a 'comedic philosopher of race' whose time-machine, 'white people problems,' and 'if you're white and you don't admit that it's great, then you are an asshole' bits teach identity privilege as unearned, systemic advantage. It credits him with an intersectional grasp of white-plus-male benefit, contrasts his 'but for now, Weeeeeee!' complicity over smartphone suffering with Barbara Applebaum's argument that the privileged cannot simply opt out and should mobilize against systems rather than police individual guilt, and concludes his job is descriptive: showing us privilege and leaving the remedy to us.
- ch. 39 "VIII. Of Course but Maybe . ." *(front)*
- ch. 40 "22. Sometimes Cartesian" **·** René Descartes — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); The Cogito — Takes the character name 'Never Cartesian' as a clue for reading Louie through Descartes's Meditations: the show's dream sequences, hallucinations, and continuity-breaking strangeness (Jane stepping off the subway to test whether she is still in her 'nice dream,' the model's 'maybe it is not really happening,' Liz's possibly dreamed death) make it a sustained exercise in the dream argument and distrust of the senses. The chapter concludes Louis C.K. breaks with Descartes at the rebuild—with no God to anchor the cogito, Louie's world stays suspended between waking and dream, a doubt Louie can live with.
- ch. 41 "23. Louis C.K. Meets the Unconscious" **·** Jacques Lacan, Sigmund Freud — The Unconscious; The Symbolic, the Imaginary and the Real — Reads the 'of course, but maybe' bit as a wrestling match between Lacan's big Other—society's unwritten rule book of queues, taboos, and approval—and the Freudian unconscious that betrays itself in the therapist's 'the climax results in the death of the woman' slip and other parapraxes. Jokes then work as sublimation and a release valve: Louis's nut-allergy and slavery bits ('you all clapped for dead kids... you don't get to cherry-pick') let audiences laugh at their disavowed 'I know very well, but...' contradictions, though comedy remains confession without absolution.
- ch. 42 "24. An Hour" **·** Jacques Lacan, Jacques Derrida, Sigmund Freud — The Symbolic, the Imaginary and the Real; Deconstruction — Argues Louis's shift to an hour-a-year of digressive storytelling—spurred by George Carlin's 'dig deeper' tribute speech and forced by fatherhood—is 'secular revelation' in Adam Phillips's sense: his wandering bits work like the free play of the signifier and free association, so the stage becomes staged therapy in Lacan's return-to-Freud mode. The chapter reads the trumpet purchase as anxiety speaking through parapraxis, the street-sign and 'suck a bag of dicks' bits through Saussure's arbitrary sign and Derrida's catachresis, and Louie following Tarese home as desire that self-consciousness cannot account for.
- ch. 43 "References" *(notes)*
